# Almanac

Puzzle, logic and knowledge games for the phone, alone or with a partner, online or off. It began as Crates, the first game below, and keeps that address; on the home screen it's Almanac, a sextant for its icon (the manifest, every page's home-screen title, and `icon.svg` with the PNGs drawn from it, `icons/` and `icon.png`).

Play: https://kmrring.github.io/Crates/ (on iPhone: Share → Add to Home Screen)

## The look

Brass instruments on an almanac's page, with an Art Deco hand: the precision of a sextant or an astrolabe (brass on navy, engraved scales, enamelled detail) set the way the 1930s set gold on black, in wide geometric capitals and fine double rules, as in an ocean liner's chart room or Griffith Observatory. Light is the page: parchment (`--yard`), cream paper for cards and dialogs (`--sheet`), navy ink (`--ink`), brass for lines, frames and scales (`--brass`). Dark is the instrument itself: a night-navy dial, parchment lettering, bright brass. The four crate colours are enamels: ochre, verdigris (copper's green patina), cobalt and oxblood, and every game's own accent is retuned to an enamel of its colour, with its soft fill mixed from it and the paper (`color-mix`), so both schemes follow from one value.

All of it lives in `style.css`'s tokens; a game names colours only for what is its own. Headings (a game's name in its header, dialog titles, menu headings, crate names, the tiles on the games screen) are Archivo at its widest (`--wide`, 125%), in spaced capitals: the font the suite already serves offline, so the look costs no download. A header sits on a graduated rule, a hairline with ticks every nine pixels, like an instrument's scale. Buttons are brass-framed with square corners; a primary action is a plate, navy lettered in gilt on the page and brass lettered in navy on the dial (`--plate`, `--plate-ink`), with a gilt hairline inside its edge, and every game's main action button is that plate. Tiles are paper with a brass edge, and a picked tile wears a brass frame. A game's stage (the card it plays on: Chart's map card, Quote's question, Manifest's floor, a tasting note in Brut) is a panel with square-ish corners framed in a double rule, a brass edge and a hairline four pixels inside it, drawn as an inset shadow so it sits under the content and never crosses a map or a board; Cartel's felt table has a gilt rim and a gilt line inside it. Category labels are tags, not pills: small rectangles in the game's enamel, lettered in the same wide capitals; boxes, options and inputs take a tile's corners, and the only round things left are dots, discs and round icon buttons. Every game's logo is its object drawn as the instruments are (README, the games screen): navy, brass, parchment and its enamel. Each logo's contour is drawn at its width times `--wire`: 1 in a game's header and on the games screen, 1.5 on the selected game (the one you're in, or a tile under your finger or pointer), the contour thickening as it's chosen while the engraved detail keeps its fine weight.

The logo is a sextant whose frame is the A: its brace the crossbar, its graduated arc the foot, the index arm reading the angle (`logo.svg`; the app icon is it on a navy tile). Loading is drawn the same way (`loading.js`). Whenever a page or a long task loads, the astrolabe's rete turns large in the middle of the screen, the task's words under it (`rete.svg`): while a page loads (it fades in only after a moment, so a page that's ready at once never shows it; it lets taps through, so a page can never be locked behind it), while Delta deals a hard board, while Crates sets up or fetches a synced run, and while Almanac updates itself. A short wait inside a line or a box shows the sextant, small, its arm swinging (`sextant.svg`): a dictionary lookup, a reconnecting room, a toast that says it's still working (one ending in "…"), and a picture on its way. `busy(label)` returns the function that ends a wait; `sextant()` is the small one as an element. Both drawings are animated SVG (SMIL), so they run as plain image files, offline, with nothing in any page's markup.

Four themes, chosen in Settings (More, Settings), each with a day and a night that follow the phone unless set: Almanac (above, the default), Modern (Almanac as it was before its Art Deco dress), Kontor (dense and technical in an office's green, with line icons) and Office. Office looks like Outlook: Microsoft's Fluent design, light greys and white surfaces by day and charcoal at night, one blue (`#0F6CBD`, `#479EF5` at night) for what can be pressed and what's selected, Segoe UI in sentence case at 14 px, hairline borders, 4 px corners and dialogs with a soft shadow, no ornaments and no marks: the games screen is names with thin monochrome line icons, and each game's board (Pipes' tiles, Harbour's chart and 3D skin, Cartel's table, the chess board) has its Office colours by day and by night. The themes beyond Almanac live in `themes.css`; `theme.js` sets the theme on the page before it draws.

## Bigger screens

Almanac is drawn for a phone held upright, and a phone looks exactly as it did before bigger screens were filled (October 2026: every page, and every game a little way into play, compared pixel for pixel at three phone sizes in every theme by day and night). A bigger screen is filled in two ways (`style.css`, "Bigger screens"):

- **A tablet held upright draws the phone's layout larger.** Sizes are in rem (a rem is the root's font size, 16px on a phone), and on an upright tablet the root grows until the screen holds the layout as a large phone does, 35rem across and 50rem down: 1.46 times on an 11-inch iPad, at most 26px a rem on a 13-inch. The board, the type, the buttons and the gaps grow together, so a game fills the screen instead of a phone's column standing in its middle. Borders and hairlines stay in px, and so does text inside an SVG drawn to a viewBox, which grows with its drawing already. `tests/wide.mjs` fails on any other size of 3px or more in px (n px is n/16 rem, the same on a phone).
- **Held sideways** (a tablet, a computer's window, a phone turned over: wider than tall and at least 700px across), each game puts its parts beside its board: the header across the top, the board, map or picture on the left with all the height, and the game's information and buttons in a column beside it, the buttons at the bottom where a thumb is, and boxes to type in high, clear of a tablet's keyboard. The root grows with the height alone (50rem down). `#app.beside` sets that frame, a grid of the board's column and a side column `--beside` wide (or `--beside-cols` of a game's own), and each game places its parts in it in its own stylesheet; a game whose card divides itself (Quote's question and market) takes `#app.wide`, the whole width, and lays the halves out itself. A `.fit-half` is such a half: on a phone it isn't a box at all, its parts lying in the column as they always have. A phone on its side is too short for every part, so there what doesn't fit scrolls inside the app, as it always has there.
- Sizes set in script (Slate's board and keys, Cartel's dice) are multiplied by `scale()` from `wide.js`, and a game that needs to know which layout is showing asks `sideways()`, whose query every stylesheet uses for its side-by-side rules (`tests/wide.mjs` checks they agree).

## Crates
Sixteen words, four hidden crates. Group the four words that belong together, then name the country or commodity behind them.

Topics are tapped round three states, the same control as Punt's: out, in, more (twice as often). An old "Less" counts as in; the presets (Balanced, Trader, Culture night) use in and more. Balanced has every topic in, and more of each except the four specialist ones (watches, skiing, the art market, etiquette), which are in once.

Held sideways (October 2026; README, Bigger screens), the crates and the tiles stand on the left, as wide as the height lets four rows of tiles be and lettered in proportion, and the brief, what's happening, your guesses so far and the buttons stand beside them, the buttons at the bottom where a thumb is.

## Boards
Every board is drawn fresh from a word bank: 69 countries and 90 commodities. Clues and answers map many-to-many: Copper points to Chile, the DR Congo, Zambia and eight more; the Nile to Egypt and Ethiopia; Commonwealth to the UK, Poland and Lithuania. Each clue-answer pair has its own explanation and its own difficulty from 1 to 3, because the link is easy for some answers and hard for others (about 5,500 pairs over 3,700 clues). Hong Kong's clues sit under China. Clues also carry topics. The generator picks four answers, then four clues each, and only keeps a board that has exactly one solution given every answer each clue points to. Each crate must also name exactly one answer: the only answer in the whole bank that all four of its clues fit. That matters for close relatives such as FAME and its variants (UCOME, RME, SME, PME, TME): clues true of all biodiesel sit under all six, so a crate needs at least one clue specific to its answer. No tile contains the name of an answer on its board, so White gold never appears alongside Gold. Red herrings (clues that also point to another crate on the board) follow the difficulty: none on Easy, at most one on Mixed (on roughly two boards in five), one or two on Hard (two on roughly one board in five). Each herring fits exactly one other crate, and no two herrings link the same pair of crates. A solved crate lists its red herrings but names the other crate only once that one is open too; from then on the herring shows in both crates ("Amazon also fits Brazil" in Peru, "also fits here, but belongs to Peru" in Brazil). Crates are coloured yellow to red by the average difficulty of their clues.

The dropdown in the header picks what the boards deal: Countries, Commodities, or Mixed (each board one or the other). It is the suite's own dropdown rather than the phone's: a button in Crates' logo colours (a deep tint on a dark screen, where the light one glared), as wide as its longest choice so it never changes size, opening onto a card with a tick on the current choice and a line under each saying what it deals. A hidden select still holds the choice, so links (`?cat=`), rooms and synced runs read and set it as before. Any game can take it up (`dropdown.js`, `dropdown.css`): link the stylesheet, call `dropdown(select)`, give the select `data-accent` with the game's logo prefix, and its options `data-note` for the lines under them.

## Settings
Menu (No. button) → Settings. They apply from the next board.
- Preset: Trader, Balanced or Culture night.
- Difficulty: Easy, Mixed or Hard.
- Topics: out, in or more for each (README, The knowledge base: every knowledge game deals its topics this way). Countries and commodities have their own lists, and Settings shows the ones your pool deals from. Country topics (19): Film, TV & games covers films, series, video and board games; Craft & style covers textiles and weaving, tailoring and menswear, shoes and watches. Commodity topics (10): Origins, Production, Uses, Specs & science, Markets, Trade & logistics, Policy, Companies, History, Culture & language. Money & economy is what an informed newspaper reader knows (currencies, central banks, household-name banks and indices, famous crises, sovereign funds); Finance is what someone working in markets knows (benchmarks and crude grades, bond markets, trading jargon, rogue traders and blow-ups). Benchmarks also carry their sector topic, so TTF comes up under Energy as well.
- Regions and sectors: switch whole groups of answers on or off (for example only European countries, or no chemicals). Commodity sectors: Energy; Biofuels & low-carbon; Metals & minerals; Grains & oilseeds; Softs, fibres & livestock; Chemicals; Circular & recycled; Credits & freight.

If the settings are too narrow to build a board, that board falls back to Balanced and says so.

## Learning modes (solo)
Settings → Learning mode has four settings:
- Off: boards are dealt at random, so a clue can come back.
- Learn: missed clues come back (below).
- No repeats: no clue is dealt twice (under any answer) until you've seen every clue your settings allow; then that category starts over. It counts every board you've played, not just those since you switched it on.
- Clue learn: only tiles you open with ? come back, 3 to 8 boards later in a new crate, even if you skip that board. Solve one without opening it and it's learned. Nothing else on the board is recorded. Tiles you opened in Learn mode count here too.

Learn mode in detail: every clue-answer pair is its own card: knowing that Copper points to Chile doesn't mark Copper→Zambia as known. For each pair on a board the question is: did you tie that clue to that answer?
- Yes: its crate was found and named, or a "one away" guess put it among the three that belonged together (in a one-away with Matches, Rotten eggs and Gunpowder, those three count as known even if the Sulphur crate is never found).
- No: it was the odd one out or the one left out in a "one away", you opened its clue, its crate was found but not named, or its crate was never found and nothing showed you placed it. A pair in a plain miss (two and two, or worse) proves nothing either way.
- A missed pair comes back 3 to 8 boards later in a crate of the same answer, next to companions it wasn't missed with, and usually on a board without the crates it was missed next to. In a Mixed pool, pairs of the other category wait for a board of their own kind, so they can take a little longer.
- A pair you get right retires to the bottom of the deck. Unseen pairs are preferred, so the same clue turns up under its other answers as you go.
- Solved crates flag clues "↻ back soon" or "✓ learned" (in Learn and Clue learn). Progress stays in the browser (or follows a synced run); Settings has a reset for the learning cards and, in No repeats, a way to start the clues over.

## Slate
(Called Glyph until October 2026: glyph.html forwards to slate.html, and saves, rooms and the switcher id still say glyph, so nothing in play broke.)

Tap the title (Crates or Slate) to switch games. Slate lives at slate.html.

Fill a small crossword so every across and down run is a real word. Coloured fields carry secret letter rules, and the colour family says what kind:
- Blues and greens judge each letter on its own (a tick or cross on the letter).
- Yellows and oranges judge neighbouring pairs, read left to right and top to bottom (the mark sits on the join).
- Purples and pinks, dashed, judge the whole field once every cell is filled (one mark for the field).

When a board ends, the result shows placements, checks, clues (rules revealed) and time (time on screen for solo boards; from dealing to the end in a together game). On Easy, checks, suggestions and rule reveals are unlimited; otherwise two of each per board. Check and Suggest judge against every winning board, not our fill: any fill of real words from the full list (repeats allowed) that keeps every rule, including the whole-field rules that can't be judged yet. Check asks which letters must change for a winning board to exist, keeping all the others: letters nearest the highlighted word are protected first (rings counted outward through crossings; no nearer letter is marked while changes further out would do), then fewer letters, then typed letters before placed ones. Those letters get an orange outline until they change, and while the cursor is on one its key is outlined too; a clean board says "All of this can still win". The answer is exact when the search finishes, and says so when time ran out first. Typing: tap a cell and type; the cursor moves along the row (the default) or, after tapping the same cell again, down the column, and Place (bottom left of the keyboard) puts the word down. Letters typed but not placed belong to their word: moving within it (a tap or the arrow keys) keeps them, moving to another word drops them. Retyping one letter of a word and pressing Place counts as a new placement. Delete empties the cell under the cursor (typed or placed) and the cursor stays; on an empty cell it steps back one and empties that, so repeated presses walk back along the word. On a keyboard the arrow keys move the cursor, as in the New York Times crossword: pressed across the word being typed where another word crosses, the first press turns to that word; otherwise the cursor goes to the next cell that way, over gaps, and typing then runs that way wherever a word does. A complete word that isn't in the word list can still be placed: you're told, it's underlined in red, and like a frame it isn't judged. Placing letters that don't complete a word is allowed, to build a frame: they show greyed with a dotted underline, aren't judged, don't count as placements, and the first Clear removes them; a letter is judged once it's part of a complete real word. While the cursor is in a single-letter field, the keyboard shows the letters that field has taken (green) and rejected (red). In a pair field, once a neighbour in the same field has a letter (placed or typed), the keyboard shows the letters known to go with it (green) and known not to (red), from the pairs tried so far, each read in the field's direction (left to right, top to bottom); with two lettered neighbours, green needs both known to pass and red needs one known to fail. With the field's rule revealed, every letter is coloured. Clear (between Fields and Check letters) escalates: the first press empties letters that aren't part of a complete real word, the next also words that break a field's rule, the next the whole board; clearing never counts as a placement. Fields opens a sheet with everything each field has passed and failed, and a ? per field: two per board (the "2 × ?" beside Close counts them down) reveal a field's rule (for a single-letter field the keyboard then shows every letter green or red). In a together game a revealed rule, and that field's marks, are shown to everyone at the table. New board is in the menu. The board and keyboard size themselves to the visible screen, clear of Safari's bars. Our fill uses only very common words; any fill that obeys every rule and makes real words wins too. Every rule is revealed when the board ends.

Boards come in 36 shapes (12 per level) in which every horizontal and every vertical run of two or more cells is a word of three to five letters: there are no two-letter runs, so rows stacked on each other are always joined by real down words. Each board turns or mirrors its shape at random. Easy shapes have five or six words, Medium six to eight, Hard eight to ten with rows stacked into blocks. tests/shapes.mjs checks every shape in every orientation. Difficulty comes from the rules, each rated easy, medium or hard; every level mixes all three field types. The rule catalogue (slate-gen.js): vowels, consonants, alphabet halves and thirds, straight or curved letters, enclosed spaces, keyboard rows, typing hand, mirror symmetry, odd alphabet positions, rarer letters and hidden keywords for single letters; ascending, descending, vowel-consonant, same class, same shape, same keyboard row, same hand, within three letters and double letters for pairs; no duplicates, no vowels, exactly one vowel, a repeated letter, one shape, rearranges into a word, one keyboard row and an even alphabet sum for whole fields.

Together: a pair's match (asked for from the partner sheet on the games screen) or a party's table of up to six (README, Party), each on their own phone, everyone filling the one board at once and sharing everything: the placements, the checks and clues, and every field's marks (October 2026; until then two, in seats 0 and 1). Each letter shows in the colour of whoever put it there (a word laid through someone else's letter leaves that letter theirs; a frame's letters paler), and the word each of the others is on has a bar of their colour along its cells' feet, their initial in the corner of the cell they're on, so you can see who is working where and not type over them; the line over the board names everyone in their colours, dimmed while their phone sleeps or they've gone. Checks, rule reveals and suggestions are the team's (two of each per board between everyone, as alone), and a teammate's check or reveal is said in a line as it comes, since its orange letters or coloured keys would otherwise just appear. The result counts the words each placed. Emptying the board (Clear's third press) asks first when some of the letters are someone else's. Each request said yes to (`?new=`, a party host's or your partner's; a party names Slate by its branch, glyph, the partner sheet by its name, slate) deals a new table, whoever arrives first dealing it at their own level and seating as many as it asked for (`sitDown` in rooms.js); a reload, or your other device, sits you back in your own seat, letters and all. Opened in a room, the page shows the rete until the table's board is drawn, never your own meanwhile (whose clock used to run while the room was joined), and your own only if the join doesn't come off; Back to solo (the menu) returns to it as you left it. Joining reads only Slate's part of the room, not the whole room, with that part held while joining so the joining transaction takes one round trip, not two. A table dealt as you sit down is at your own board's level, Easy before you've had one. Each player's cursor is written to their own seat (`players/<uid>/at`) as it moves, at most every 150 ms; the board's redraw for someone else's news waits while a finger is on it, since a cell redrawn under a finger loses its tap. The table is `slate-room.js`, pure; `tests/slate-room.mjs` seats parties of three and six and a pair, deals for either kind of request, gives a player's other device their seat, and fills a board with six through the database's round trip, every letter keeping its placer.

Words: SCOWL word lists (sizes 10-20 for our fills, up to 70 with British and American spellings for what you may place), packed in slate-words.js.

Suggest: a word for the highlighted slot that lies on a winning board keeping every letter entered, placed or typed; our fill's word where it can be, else common words first. It's typed into the slot for you to place with Enter. If no winning board keeps your letters it says so ("No winning board keeps your current letters", or "No solution found" when the search ran out of time) and isn't spent: Check shows what has to change. A full slot has nothing to suggest. Both are worked out off the page (`slate-worker.js`, `slate-solve.js`): a depth-first search over the full word list, the slot with fewest words left first, with the rules propagated as letters go in (a single-letter field narrows its cells, a pair narrows the partner of a placed letter, a whole field narrows as letters arrive and exactly at its last cell); Check goes ring by ring outward, learning small sets of letters that can't all stay and dropping the fewest that break them all. `tests/slate-solve.mjs` plays simulated boards and checks that clean boards (ours or another winning one) are called clean, that the orange letters are enough and each is needed, and that Suggest's words win. Definitions: double-tap a cell to see the complete words through it defined; the double tap leaves the cursor and any typed letters as they were. The dictionary is Slate's own (`slate-defs.js`), so it works offline: up to three senses for each of the 10,510 words players may place, from Open English WordNet 2025 (CC BY 4.0, credited in the dialog), the first sense of each part of speech first. An inflected form shows its base word's senses (BILLS: plural of BILL), and a word that is also a form of a common word says so (FOUND: also the past tense of FIND). The 1,500 or so words WordNet lacks (function words, slang, abbreviations, dialect and archaic words) were defined for Slate; 35 of the rarest have no entry. It is 877 KB (329 KB as served), loaded on the first lookup rather than with the board, and replaced dictionaryapi.dev, a free service that was often down and lacked common words. One entry per line, so edit it in place; `tests/slate-defs.mjs` checks every fill word is defined and every pointer lands on a definition.

On a narrow phone the four buttons under the board share the row equally, so the last one never runs off the edge.

What's left of Check and Suggest on a board that limits them shows as a small brass badge on each button's corner, not as "(2)" in the label, which ran out of four buttons to a row.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the board takes the left column under the header, its cells up to 100px rather than a phone's 84 (`fitLayout`), and the dock stands beside it, the keys at the bottom as tall as the column lets them be; on an upright tablet the board, the keys and their limits grow with the page (`scale()` from `wide.js`).

## Your run on several devices
Menu → Your other devices → Sync this run. The run gets an 8-letter code and a link; open the link on your other device (or type the code into Join there) and both devices follow the same run: the board you're on, your history, settings and learning cards. Changes go up a moment after each move and when you put the page away, and a device that was left open with old progress catches up instead of overwriting newer progress. Each change also carries the size of the word bank it was made with. A device still on an older version of the app reloads itself onto the new one instead of dealing boards of its own (it couldn't read boards that use newer words), and a device that is only following, untouched, doesn't run the clock or write anything. "Stop syncing on this device" keeps a local copy and lets go of the run.

## Delta
A field of hexes with gaps. Some hexes hold plain numbers, some hold operations (+4, −2, ×3, ÷2), the rest are blank. Three rules:
1. Join the numbers in pairs. Drag from a number to draw a path; paths can't cross, share a hex or pass through another number.
2. A path runs from the number you draw it from: the value starts as that number, each operation on the way changes it in turn, and it must arrive exactly at the other number. Values can become fractions (5 ÷ 4 = 1¼). Which end to start from is yours to work out.
3. Every operation is used exactly once. Blank hexes are optional.

Drawing through another path cuts it there; tapping either number of a path removes it. Arrows show the way each finished path runs.

Levels:
- Easy: two pairs, + and −, the pairs are marked; the running value follows your finger and finished paths are marked right or wrong at once.
- Medium: three pairs, adds ×; running value and immediate marks as on Easy; two clues per board (a clue shows two numbers that belong together).
- Hard: a small, crowded field (the 37-hex hexagon with five gaps), adds ÷. No running value: you work the values out. Paths are only marked right or wrong once every number is joined.

Numbers and operations keep the mental maths light at every size: times tables on small numbers, round numbers times small ones, shifts by 10, 100 and 1,000, round steps on big round numbers (+300 on 1,200), and simple halves, thirds and quarters. Big numbers (up to 100,000) only come from those easy steps, so a big number is a hint at the operation that made it (13,500 wants the ×1,000), never a sum like 259 × 13. Medium adds the big numbers; Hard also has fractions, shown as 9½ or ¾. Operations are often reused across the board, so another pair could take one: that feeds the lures below.

Boards are built backwards from random paths, then checked by a solver in two stages: first every pairing and share-out of the operations the arithmetic allows, then whether those paths can actually be drawn at the same time. A board is kept only if exactly one can be drawn (the one it was built from) while the arithmetic alone allows others, and only if it is full of lures: single paths that add up and could be drawn on their own but belong to no answer, because they block another pair or use an operation another pair needs. Lures must touch most of the numbers (all six on Hard), so the "this works, but it cuts off that" moments are all over the board. The same seed always gives the same board; if a seed's first tries don't meet the bar, the lure and decoy targets relax step by step, never the one-answer rule. `tests/delta.mjs` checks every level.

Boards are dealt in a worker, and a spare board per level is dealt in the background and kept between visits, so New board and changing level are instant even on Hard (a Hard board can take a few seconds to find). Where the worker can't run (its script won't load, or there are no module workers), boards are dealt on the page instead; a board asked of a worker that failed used to never come.

Together: a pair's match or a party's table of up to six (README, Party), everyone drawing on the one board at once (`together.js` handles the room side: joining, seats, moves as transactions, presence; a party's request seats as many as it asked for). A path takes the colour of whoever drew it, and keeps it when another's path cuts it; a path being drawn on another phone shows as it grows, thin and dashed in that player's colour, their initial at its head (each player's path under the finger is written to their seat as it changes, at most every 90 ms, and cleared once the drawn one is on the table), so two people don't set off for the same pair. The line over the board names everyone in their colours, dimmed while away. Clues and the result are the team's, and the result counts the paths each drew. Clear asks first when some of the paths are someone else's. Opened in a room, the page shows the rete until the table's board is drawn, never your own board meanwhile (until October 2026 your own board was drawn first, its clock paused, and the shared one replaced it seconds later), and your own only if the join doesn't come off. The table is `delta-room.js`, pure; `tests/delta.mjs` seats parties and a pair, keeps paths' drawers through a cut, shows the others' paths only for those here, and has three solve a board through the database's round trip.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the board takes the height and most of the width, and Clue and Clear stand beside it, low.

## Punt
Trivia from the Crates bank and the subject banks, with a stake on every answer. Each question shows a clue, a country or a commodity, and four options, filling the 2×2 grid; sometimes more than one is right ("China" goes with many commodities): then the question says how many, and you must pick all of them to win. You pick and stake a share of your pot (slider or 0/5/10/25/50%/all-in), or pass. Every bet pays 2×: a right pick wins the stake again, a wrong one loses it. You start with 1,000. Runs are a standard length (12 or 15 questions by level), 100 questions, or endless (dealt 20 at a time; end it from the menu). The score that compares runs is the average return a question, compounded: the steady rate that would have turned 1,000 into the pot (a pass is 0%, a bust −100%). The best per level and length is kept.

Runs are dealt in balanced batches so they're comparable: a fixed-length run is one batch, an endless run a batch of 20 at a time, and within a batch the mix of question kinds and clue difficulties is exactly the level's. Runs differ in which questions they get, nothing else.
- Easy: 12 questions, easy clues.
- Medium: 15 questions, easy to hard clues.
- Hard: 15 questions, hard clues, more questions with several right answers.

Questions come in two kinds: a clue with answers to choose from (any answer carrying the clue is right; wrong options come mostly from the same region or sector), and an answer with clues to choose from. A clue that names one of the options is never used. The same seed always deals the same session, so a link (#s=…&d=…) lets two people play the same questions. `tests/punt.mjs` checks every level.

Maths: a fourth entry in the level dropdown deals from `maths-bank.js` instead of the clue bank: 1,108 multiple-choice questions from GCSE to first-year university with a finance tilt, each with a stage (GCSE, IB SL, IB HL, Y1 Uni), a difficulty from 1 (routine GCSE) to 10 (hardest first-year university), four options, one or two right answers and an explanation. Choosing Maths shows two pickers under the header, stages and difficulties, each a sheet of toggles that counts how many questions match; a new choice starts a new run. The bank loads only when Maths is chosen. Questions come in the order the seed shuffles the filtered pool (round again once it runs dry). Links carry the choice (`&st=` stages, `&df=` difficulties), stats and bests keep Maths as its own level, and flags work on maths questions too.

Refining: a fifth entry, on `refining-bank.js`, the same machinery as Maths: 139 questions on how a refinery works (basics, units, specs, renewables, economics) and, in two stages of their own, the chemistry of gasoline and diesel blending: molecular classes and octane, reforming and alkylation reactions, RVP and distillation, EN 228's limits, oxygenates, stability, cetane and cold flow, EN 590, what each component brings and costs, difficulties 1–10, with explanations, and the same two pickers. It pairs with the Refinery game: the game teaches by routing, the questions check what stuck. Quote has a Refining category too (two dozen refinery numbers), and Chart's trade places include refineries and hubs.

Reasoning: critical reasoning on `reasoning-bank.js`, 41 questions in trade and market settings: which statement weakens or strengthens an argument, what it assumes, what would help evaluate it, the flaw by name (false dilemma, argument from ignorance, equivocation, affirming the consequent), what follows and what doesn't, correlation against causation, base rates, selection and Simpson's paradox, regression to the mean, overfitting, sunk costs and expected value. Five stages for the picker.

Words: a word, pick its synonym among four, 236 of them from advanced to obscure with a gloss each, with a trade and law tilt (contango, demurrage, estoppel, subrogation). Cities: every capital (197), which country a notable city is in (about 200 cities: ports, refinery towns, industrial and financial centres), and what a city is known for (115 questions: BASF's Ludwigshafen, the ECB, Cushing, the Canton Fair, Rosario's crushers), stages by region with the weight on Germany, the UK, China, the rest of Europe and Latin America. Flags: 197 countries (the UN's members, Vatican City, Palestine, Kosovo and Taiwan), the flag as its emoji, distractors from the same region at a similar obscurity, in three stages from well known to the Pacific micro-states. Patterns: 61 what-comes-next questions, each standing on its own and most with a drawing: number series and the odd one out as boxes, functions as pairs, letter and word series, and figures (a painted cube cut 3×3×3 in isometric, a die, points on a circle and a grid, a clock, a folded sheet, shape rows, a chessboard). A bank question may carry an `svg`, which Punt shows under the prompt. All four use the Maths machinery and pickers.

The textbook banks, each on the same machinery, with explanations on every question: Economics (`eco-bank.js`, 73: markets and firms with drawn supply-and-demand diagrams, macro and money with the yield curves and the Phillips and Laffer curves, trade and finance, the history of ideas); Physics (`phy-bank.js`, 62: motion and energy, electricity and waves, heat, gases and nuclei, the modern picture, formulas in LaTeX); Chemistry (`chm-bank.js`, 58: atoms and bonds, reactions, organic chemistry and fuels, industry and the environment); Code (`cs-bank.js`, 68: data and algorithms, code to read in Python, JavaScript, SQL, regex and JSON shown as a snippet, object-oriented and functional paradigms, systems and the web); Philosophy (`phil-bank.js`, 60: the ancients, the moderns, ethics and politics, knowledge and mind); Religion (`rel-bank.js`, 59: Islam, Judaism and Christianity, India and East Asia, and what to know at the table and in the calendar). Switzerland (`swiss-bank.js`, 44, written October 2026 for the move to Geneva): Geneva (the Escalade and Mère Royaume's soup, Calvin's Protestant Rome, the Reformation Wall, the Jet d'Eau, Dunant and the Red Cross, international Geneva, CERN, the Geneva Seal, the private banks, the trading houses), the Swiss state (the Federal Council and the magic formula, initiatives and referendums, the double majority, the Sonderbund War, neutrality, the cantons, the UN and the EU) and Swiss life and money (the languages, the Gotthard, Swiss made, chocolate, banking secrecy, the franc shock, Credit Suisse, the militia, fondue); wrong options are real alternatives where they can be (Tell's apple and Winkelried's spears beside Mère Royaume's soup; the Paul Scherrer Institute beside CERN). Art (`art-bank.js`, 797) shows the painting: who painted it, where it hangs, which movement, over 201 works from Giotto's Lamentation to Hockney's A Bigger Splash (the build writes these from `kb/`: The knowledge base, below), and Stories, 68 of the art world's without a picture: what hides in a painting (the skull across The Ambassadors, the king and queen in Las Meninas' mirror, 'Jan van Eyck was here' over the Arnolfini mirror), thefts, forgeries and rescues (the Mona Lisa in 1911, the Just Judges never found, van Meegeren's Vermeers, Beltracchi's titanium white, the salt mine at Altaussee), pigments and restorations (ultramarine, Prussian blue, Klein's blue, the Sistine ceiling, Il Braghettone), and paintings in novels, films, music and on album covers (Ferris Bueller, Titanic, Bean, Doctor Who, The Thomas Crown Affair, Woman in Gold, Pictures at an Exhibition, Viva la Vida). Wine (`wine-bank.js`, 162) is Brut's cards. Movements as ideas (written October 2026): knowing which pictures belong to a movement isn't knowing the movement, so each of the 34 movements has a card in kb/entities.js (where and when, what it rejected, what it sought, how to spot it, what lay behind it, what came next) and the build asks four questions from it: which movement was reacting against this, which was seeking that, how to spot it, what lay behind it. Wrong options are other movements and their own lines, nearest in time, so every wrong answer is true of something; on 'against', never one of the same family (the Renaissances, the abstractions), whose reasons overlap; never a movement the question names, and no question echoes its answer's name. A miss shows the card.

Rich questions: any prompt, option or note may carry maths between `$…$`, rendered to MathML by Temml (`vendor/temml.min.js`, MIT, loaded on first use; `rich.js`); a question may carry a drawn chart (`svg`), a code snippet (`code`, shown in a monospace block) or a picture (`pic`, the English Wikipedia page title of a painting or landmark, whose lead image `pics.js` fetches from Wikipedia at display time and the service worker keeps). Deck shows all three on review. Quote dates the same 201 paintings from their picture, with the painting's story in the note once it's settled, and Chart shows a museum's famous painting on its card. Maths is told from money as Pandoc does: an inline $ opens maths only before a non-space and closes it only after one, never before a digit, and \$ is always a dollar, so 'spent $3 million … needs $2 million' stays two prices (`mathParts` in `rich.js`; `tests/rich.mjs` also checks that no maths span in any bank reads like a sentence).

Pictures (paintings in Punt, Quote and Chart, and Deck when it brings them back) are named by their English Wikipedia page (or, where the page won't do, a file on Commons) and fetched at display time by `pics.js`: Wikipedia's own API gives the page's lead image (`prop=pageimages`, fair-use images included, since most 20th-century paintings are) at a standard thumbnail width, the asked width rounded up to Wikimedia's ladder (120, 250, 330, 500, 960, 1280, 1920 px). Since 2026 Wikimedia refuses thumbnails at any other width, which is why every picture once went blank: the old code built 640-pixel URLs by hand. If a thumbnail is refused anyway, the original file is shown instead; only a page without an image is remembered as such (a failed fetch is tried again next time), and the old cache, which had remembered the refusals, is dropped. The service worker keeps fetched pictures, including thumbnails from thumb.wikimedia.org, for offline play.

Keep pictures offline (Settings on the games screen, October 2026) downloads every picture any game can show, once, at 500 pixels: the 707 in `pics-list.js`, which the build writes from every bank's `pic` (paintings, buildings, places, watches, Who am I's people), about 50 MB, into the cache the service worker keeps shown pictures in, which app updates and the Update button leave alone. It's off by default, since that's 50 MB of a phone for pictures you may never open, and every picture you've seen is kept anyway. They aren't shipped with the app because many are Wikipedia's fair-use copies (most 20th-century paintings), which aren't ours to hand out; this way each phone fetches them from Wikipedia, as it would by showing them. The addresses come fifty to a request, the images four at a time; a download cut short (the connection gone, the app closed) carries on where it stopped the next time the app is open online, turning the switch off stops it, and Settings says how far it has got. Offline, a game asking for a bigger picture (Punt and Quote ask for 960 pixels) gets the kept copy. The switch belongs to the device (`suite:pics`, not synced). `tests/pics.mjs` runs it against a stand-in for Wikipedia and the browser's caches.

A question about "this" picture is no longer asked beside an empty space (October 2026). Quote would ask for the year "this" was painted with no painting, every picture downloaded, for three reasons. Some titles gave Wikipedia's API no picture: Nighthawks named a disambiguation page, Composition VIII, Travelers Among Mountains and Streams and the Unité d'Habitation named pages that don't exist, and a museum or a building whose page puts its logo before its photo showed the logo, since PageImages (the API's choice of a page's picture: the lead section's first image unless it's tiny or wider than 3:1) takes it; Settings counted a page with no image as kept, so it said all were kept with them missing. A weak connection was waited on: Punt and Quote ask for 960 pixels, and a lookup or image that hung left the space empty while the 500-pixel copy sat on the device. And an earlier question's picture, still on its way or failing late, could hide the next one's or put itself back. Now every title was checked against its page (that it exists, that it's the subject, what it leads with), and 44 point elsewhere: the right page (Nighthawks (painting)) or, where the page won't do, a file on Commons named outright (`File:…`, which gives exactly that image: Composition VIII, the Sydney Opera House, the Centre Pompidou, the Reverso, and museums whose logo came first). Water Lilies (its page leads with a 6:1 panorama of the Orangerie's) and Lavender Mist (its page has no image) still have none, and a dozen museums whose page may still lead with a logo (the Louvre Abu Dhabi, Te Papa) are as they were: Chart only shows them, and no photo of them could be confirmed. The copy kept on the device shows once the bigger picture's address hasn't come in 2.5 seconds or its image in 4, and a picture that isn't kept is given up after 10; only the latest picture asked of a box touches it. When a picture can't be shown, its question names the thing in place of "this" ("The year Water Lilies was painted", "Which style is the Seagram Building?"), from the name the build writes beside every picture (`title`: a painting's as titled, a building's with a small "the"), and says "this" again once it shows; not when the name is one of the options, which would give the answer away (`askedWith` in `pics.js`; Punt, Quote and Deck). Settings says how many pages have no picture instead of counting them kept, and the service worker finds a kept picture by its address alone (`ignoreVary`), whatever headers the image's request carries. `tests/pics.mjs` covers the waits, the takeovers and the naming.

Flags: after any question, "Disagree with this answer? Flag it" lets you say what's wrong (a right answer marked wrong, a wrong one marked right, an unclear clue, something else) with an optional note. A flag is kept on the device first, so it works offline, and sent to the database (crates/flags/punt) as soon as there's a connection (`question-flags.js`). Menu → Flagged questions lists this device's flags or everyone's, with a button that copies them as text to hand over for review. The database only accepts flags once the rules in `firebase-rules.json` are published (Firebase console → Realtime Database → Rules → paste the file → Publish); until then they wait on the device, nothing lost.

Stats (Menu → Your stats, also from the end of a run; this run, or all runs at the level on this device) score how well you judge what you know, two ways. Expected outcome, the view for a fixed run: how often your bets were right against how often their prices needed you to be, the edge a bet at equal stakes (what each chip earns, ignoring compounding), and the return on the chips you actually staked. Kelly, the view for the long run (Kelly is only optimal as the number of bets grows without limit): your bets grouped by stake size (a bigger stake says you're surer), how often each group came off, what Kelly would stake at that hit rate and those prices, your stakes as a multiple of Kelly's (over- or under-betting), and the growth a question you got against Kelly on your own hit rates (with hindsight). If you pick an option and then pass, the pick is kept, so the stats also show how your passes would have gone. Every settled question is kept on the device (the latest 5,000).

At a table (October 2026): a pair's match from the partner sheet, or a party of up to six, everyone on their own phone with the same question. Against, the default, each has a pot of their own, 1,000 to start, and stakes on it as alone, all in included; Together, the table shares one pot, each staking up to an equal share of it (half for two, a third for three, a sixth for six), so the stakes can never come to more than the pot. The menu's Together part chooses (Pots: Against or Together) at a table of any size; the other kind of pot is a new run, asked first while one is under way. A question settles once everyone at the table has staked or passed, and until then the Bet button says who it waits for; once everyone else has staked and half a minute has passed since the last of them, it reads Go on without Tom, and anyone may: the slow one's stake is nothing. Nobody away is waited for (their phone asleep, or on another page), nor anyone bust (Against, a pot at nothing: they watch); until the first question settles the table also waits, with the same half minute, for seats not yet sat in, since a party's phones arrive one by one. The reveal shows the right answer, a dot in each player's colour on the option they picked, your own result, then everyone's line, ranked by pot: their pick (ticked or crossed), their stake, what it won or lost and their pot now (Together, ranked by what each has added to the pot over the run). Next waits for everyone at the table the same way. At the end of the set (its length as dealt) come the final standings: Against, who won, what each gained or lost, their bets won and their average a question; Together, the team's pot and what each added. Play again deals a new run for the table, everyone's pot back to 1,000; Back to solo returns to your own run as you left it. The line under the header shows the others in their colours, with their pots and a tick once they've staked (at the reveal, once they've pressed Next). A table's run touches no best, no daily and no solo run; your stats and the review pile still learn from your own bets. The pair's record: Against is head to head, won by the sole first; Together is the team's pot, as before. Opened in a room, the page shows the rete, Joining your partner (at a party, Joining the table), until the shared run is drawn, and starts your own run only if the join doesn't come off (October 2026): it used to draw your own run first, so for the first seconds, ten on a slow connection, each of you was on a question of your own. `punt-table.js` is the table's rules, pure, and `tests/punt.mjs` plays them through the database's round trip: a round settled for three, who's waited for, the half minute, Next, the bust, the reveal's order, the shared pot's share, a seat taken over on another device.

The next question no longer shows the last one's pick and stake (October 2026): the choice was cleared only after the new question had been drawn, so Bet straight away would have staked on an option you never chose.

The page fits the screen: the slip takes the room left and scrolls inside itself when a question is long (its torn edge stays put), and at the reveal the result sits under the slip with its notes scrolling inside, so Next stays in reach while the slip keeps the right answer in sight.

The level dropdown in the header is the suite's own, as in Crates (`dropdown.js`): a button in Punt's logo colours, and the levels on a card with a tick on the current one, scrolling inside itself on a phone. A hidden select still holds the level, so links and rooms work as before.

The slip never scrolls. A painting takes only the room the question and its answers leave (contained, with a shadow on its own edges), down to a small picture on the shortest screens; the stages and difficulties pickers of a bank and the running average are in the menu (Content and About), bank questions carry no stage-and-level marker, and there's no "choose a stake, or pass" line. Pass is as narrow as its word, and the Bet button carries the rest: the stake and what comes back if right ("Bet 100 · pays 200"; "Pays double" before a stake; "Waiting for Sarah" in a duo). Checked over full Art runs at 360×640, 375×600, 390×664 and 390×844: nothing scrolls inside the slip.

Name it turns a clue round: its own hint, with the country or commodity it belongs to and its topics ("Name it · France · Culture & arts: A masked genius haunts the Paris Opéra"), and the clue to name, typed: recall rather than recognition, nothing to choose from, the strictest test of having learned something. Three modes (Menu, Content, Name it; a change deals a new run): Off; Known, the default, where two in every seven questions may be name-it, but only for clues this device has already recognised the other way round (a crate solved and named in Crates, or a right answer you staked on in Punt's clue and answer questions: `known.js` keeps that record, keyed by the knowledge base's entities), clues not named yet coming first, and until there are some those slots are ordinary questions; and All, every question name-it, any clue. Being able to pick something out doesn't mean being able to produce it, so recognising comes first and naming is the test after. A typed answer pays 2× like any other bet: it is only harder. Case, accents, punctuation, a leading article and a part in brackets don't matter, and a slip of a letter in a name of five letters or more (two from nine) still counts, the reveal showing the right spelling (`typing.js`, shared with Deck and Parley, tested in `tests/typing.mjs`). A name you miss goes to the review pile as a name-it card, and Deck asks it the same way: the hint, a box to type in, Check. Naming one right marks it named in the record; missing it unmarks it. In a match the questions are dealt from the starter's record, and a device with name-it off chooses from four of the answer's clues instead of typing.

A subject bank's options (Maths, Art and the rest) come in a new order every run, with "all of the above" and its kind kept last; they used to keep the bank's order, which gave answers away.

A pass is a bet of 0%: Pass waits for an answer like Bet does, the answer is kept, and every count treats it as a bet (the stats' hit rate and Kelly view, with a "passed (0%)" band; the Ledger, where a pass believed no more than an even chance, what a bet at 2× needs, and shows as "Passed (0%)").

A run deals from topics, not a level: Countries and Commodities (Crates' clue bank, each its own topic) beside the subject banks (Maths, Refining, Reasoning, Words, Cities, Flags, Patterns, Wine, Art, Economics, Physics, Chemistry, Code, Philosophy, Religion). In the menu each topic is tapped round out (dashed), in (white) and more (highlighted, dealt twice as often), with three presets (Balanced, Trader, Culture night, as in Crates); one difficulty range (1–10) applies to every topic, and each subject bank keeps its own stages. Every question is on the same 1–10 scale (a clue question's from how obscure its clue is: 3, 6 or 8; `mixQuestions` in punt-gen.js), and every one pays 2×. Easy, Medium and Hard are gone as choices (their runs still play to the end). A run's link carries its topics (`&t=countries.1,maths.2&df=…`); in the pile a question keeps its own topic's key.


Wrong options are real alternatives, never padding. In Philosophy each wrong answer is another thinker's or school's actual idea, stated correctly (Plato's Forms beside Aristotle's form-in-matter, Democritus' atoms and Heraclitus' flux; Zeno's Achilles beside his own arrow, the sorites and the liar), so a question asks which idea is whose; in Reasoning the wrong answers are plausible considerations that don't do the job (most strengthen an argument the question asks you to weaken). In Refining each wrong answer is something real that belongs to another unit, stream or spec: what HVO brings to a diesel pool sits beside FAME's, LCO's and coker gas oil's own profiles; cloud point beside pour point, CFPP and flash point; gasoline's induction period beside existent gum, copper strip and the Rancimat (FAME's test). None is secretly true of what's asked: LCO's density, which really is too high for EN 590, isn't offered against it. Options are of matching length: once the right answer was the longest every time (four times the wrong ones'), which gave it away. `tests/fair-options.mjs` holds those banks (Philosophy, Reasoning, Refining) to it (the right option the longest in at most 40% of questions, on average at most 1.2 times the wrong ones' length); a bank joins it once its options are evened out. Still to do: religion, chemistry, economics, code and physics have the same tell; wine a milder one.

Every bet pays 2× (October 2026), and every question has four options. The odds used to run from 1.1× on the easiest question to 3× on the hardest (4× for a typed answer), set by how hard the house reckoned a question was for a typical player, so a bet weighed the price as well as what you knew; now a bet is only about how sure you are, and the stake is the whole skill: at 2× a bet pays on average once you're right more than half the time, and Kelly's stake (the share of the pot that grows it fastest) is twice your chance less one, 60% sure staking a fifth. A clue question used to have two options when its clue was easy; four for every question makes a pure guess one in four, a known answer the only way to an edge. Two things went with the house's prices: its noise and its scale (`priceBatch`, `scaleOdds`), and the odds row under the slip (hidden since the Bet button carried the odds). And a fault in the deal: a block's kinds and difficulties were dealt as the first of the level's list, so a topic with one question in a block of a mixed run (most of them, with every topic in) always dealt the easiest clue, as a clue question, never a name-it or a harder one; a block now draws them from the whole list. Bests from before keep their scores: the old odds averaged about 2× on a long run, but easy questions paid less than they do now.

No subject question gives its answer away. Every bank was audited (442 of 3,067 questions did: the right option clearly the longest, or the only one with a formula or a number) and those questions rewritten, every option as long, specific and plausible as the answer: often a neighbouring idea (Veblen for Giffen, contango for backwardation, Bellman-Ford for Dijkstra, the expected shortfall for VaR, jihad as a false fifth pillar) or the answer with one thing changed; detail that only the answer carried moved to the explanation. `tests/banks.mjs` holds every bank to it.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the ticket stands on the left with its picture as large as the column lets it be; where you stand (the question, the pot) is beside it at the top, and the stake or the result at the bottom, where a thumb is. The ticket's torn edge is drawn in rem, so its teeth grow with an upright tablet's page.

## Cartel
Coup and Liar's Dice built from dice. Everyone has gold dice (lives and roles; two each) and plain dice (money and weight in the bidding; three to start, at most eight), all hidden. A face is both a number and a role: 1 Fixer, 2 Banker, 3 Trader, 4 Auditor, 5 Legal, 6 Regulator. Only gold dice give powers; every die counts in bids, 1s (Fixers) as any face.

Tap any gold die you can see (yours, or one of theirs that's been shown) for what its role does. There's no Pass: taking a die is always at least as good, and rerolling a face-up die covers the rest. A turn goes: before your move, if you like, claim Fixer to reroll one of your dice (free, once; say one the table has seen); one move; after it, if you like, ask a player a question about their whole hand (free, once; they learn the same about yours); then a bid. The question comes after the move, so it shapes the bid but can't make a steal or a sanction safe; the Fixer comes before it, so dice that land face up from the bank or a steal stay seen for a round. Moves: take a die from the bank (it lands face up, so taking costs secrecy); reroll any of your dice secretly (gold ones change your roles); hit (pay 7 dice and they lose a gold die; with a full hand of 8 you must); or claim a role your gold dice show, true or not: Banker takes 3 dice from the bank, Trader steals 2 from a player, Auditor looks at all of a player's dice, and Regulator has two abilities: an inquiry (learn how many dice on the table show a face) or a sanction (pay 4 dice and a player loses a gold die; the dice are spent even if it's blocked, as Coup's assassin pays). Using a role leaves the gold die as it is. Legal blocks steals and sanctions (Coup's Captain and Contessa in one). Any claim, blocks included, can be challenged by the player it's aimed at (the next player, for claims aimed at no one; the blocked player, for a block), and whoever is wrong pays the other 3 dice: the bluffer if caught, the challenger if the claim was true (the proof is shown); short of 3, they pay what they have and lose a gold die. A claimant whose claim (or block) is proven then chooses what becomes of the proof: keep it shown, and nobody will challenge that role while it stays, though everyone knows one of their gold dice; or put it back, rerolled unseen, so their gold dice are hidden again (and it may come up as another role). Then the claim takes effect, or the block stands. The bid raises "at least N dice show F" or calls it; the referee says only whether it held, and whoever was wrong pays the other 3 dice. A player who can't pay in full loses a gold die as well, and with none left is out. Information persists: nothing is revealed or rerolled unless someone makes it so.

Balance (October 2026, thousands of AI games; `RULES` in the engine switches each rule so variants can be compared). With questions as an action and challenges costing 2 dice, asking was a trap (a player who asked a lot won 9% against three Quants, where a fair share is 25%) and bluffing didn't pay (never bluffing won 30%, heavy bluffing 13%); Banker was 43% of all actions. Questions as a free move get used about 14 times a game with no effect on balance. Coup-style stakes (a lost challenge costs a gold die) halved the gap between honest and bluffing play (24% against 16%) and cut Banker to about 30% of actions, but felt too drastic; challenges now cost 3 dice (a gold die only if you can't pay), which plays like the old stakes: about 23 turns, Banker about 42% of actions, never bluffing 27% against heavy bluffing 9%. `RULES.goldStakes` switches the Coup version back on. Seats are fair (25% each), the opening roll barely matters (except two 1s on the gold dice, back when the 1 had no power: about 1 player in 36), the lead changes about 7 times a game and the winner had been last at some point in most games. Roles (400-game studies; win rate when a gold die shows the role at the start, 25% fair): with Auditor and Legal blocking steals, Banker 21%, Trader 25%, Auditor 31%, Legal 29%, Regulator 23%, and steals landed only 28% of the time; with Trader blocking steals instead (now the rule), Banker 28%, Trader 25%, Auditor 30%, Legal 26%, Regulator 23%, and steals land about 38% of the time. Holding a role is worth most when it blocks something (a bluffed block is risky; any other ability can be bluffed as easily as used), which leaves Regulator, with nothing to block, the weakest to hold; a cheaper sanction, an inquiry counting every face, and Regulator blocking Banker didn't change that. The AIs now value an inquiry by what it does to the turn's bid (with a bid standing, the exact count of its face turns the call or raise into a sure thing), so they use it about 2.5 times a game and win about 80% of the calls they make after one. An inquiry was almost as easy to bluff as to use (only about 15% of claims get challenged), so holding a real Regulator added little; now the referee answers only a real Regulator (an Auditor's look can still be bluffed: the other player shows their dice, and can't check), which brings the Regulator to 24% and every role with a power within 24–30% (Legal highest), and the 1 (then Wild, with no power) 20%. Then the free reroll moved from Legal to the 1 (renamed from Wild to Fixer, so every face has a power) and the steal block from Trader to Legal (so Legal, left with only sanctions, isn't thin): two independent 400-game samples put every role at 23–26%, the most even of everything tried. Still open: called bids hold only about 30% of the time, because the forced raise each turn leaves the last raiser stuck. The question moved after the move and the Fixer's reroll before it (October 2026; `RULES.askAfterMove`, `RULES.fixerFirst`): on the same 400 seeds with four Quants, the old order and the new one play alike (30.4 turns either way; every role 21–27% before and 20–29% after, the Auditor up two points and the Banker down two; Banker 23% of actions in both). The computer players never used the old order's tricks (asking whether a target holds Legal before stealing from them, or hiding a die that had just landed face up), so the change is aimed at human play.

Keeping or putting back a proven die (October 2026; `RULES.provenReroll`: "may" is the rule, "keep" the old one, "must" Coup's, where a proven card goes back in the deck), 400 games each on the same seeds, four Quants and mixed tables: game length, the Banker's share, how often players bluff and every role (20–30%) didn't move beyond chance. A player whose claim was proven won 32% (four Quants) and 36% (mixed) when the proof stayed shown, 37% and 38% when it went back: a shown gold die is more a liability than an asset, the safe repeat claims it buys being only 3–4% of claims while the whole table knows one of your roles all game. Challenging pays a little less (35% of challenges caught a bluff with four Quants, 31% with the proof put back; 50% and 43–45% at mixed tables), which takes 3–5 points off the Sceptic. Choosing and always putting back played alike; the computer players keep a proven Banker (income nobody will challenge) or Legal (a block nobody will test) and put back the rest, about three proofs in four.

Two gold dice, not one: with one you'd hold a given role only 1 time in 6, so challenging would nearly always pay and bluffing would die out (with two it's about 1 in 3), and a single bad payment would knock you out.

You play against two or three computer players (choose them in the menu), or with friends at a table (below). Each keeps a Mind that follows the game exactly as its seat sees it: every die's owner, every face seen (public, audited or its own), every answer, proof, caught bluff, call verdict and inquiry count for as long as each stays true, and everyone's habits (how often they're caught bluffing, how often they challenge, how honest their bids have been). It samples tables consistent with all that, weighted by what bids suggest, and decides from the probabilities: its action, its free move, whether to challenge, block (honestly, or a bluffed block when the thief seldom challenges) or challenge a block, and how to bid. Personalities: Quant (rational: plays the probabilities, tuned to what the tournaments reward), Wildcat (somewhat random), Gambler (risk taker: bluffs over half its claims, calls late), Hedger (conservative: hardly bluffs, builds and hides its hand), Sceptic (suspicious: challenges and asks a lot). In 200-game tournaments with shuffled seating the Quant and the Hedger win about a third of their games each, the Sceptic and Wildcat about a fifth, the Gambler least; `tests/cartel.mjs` plays a tournament and checks the rules one by one on hand-built games. Your own view is built the same way, so the table shows exactly what you could know: a dark cup is a die you can't see, a dashed edge is one only you know, a dot is one everyone has seen.

Computer turns come at the pace chosen in the menu: Fast (each move shown for 0.7 s), Steady (1.6 s) or Tap to continue (each move stays until you tap anywhere on the table). Once you're out, the rest of the game plays itself quickly.

`cartel-engine.js` is the referee, `cartel-ai.js` the players, `cartel.js` the table.

At a table with friends (October 2026), up to four, each on their own phone: Invite players opens a party with Cartel chosen (README, Party), and the computer plays the seats nobody has. Cartel runs on `seats.js` now, solo too (README, Seats): `cartel-rules.js` is the game as a few seats' game, the referee's state kept as it is but for its random stream, a function, which is kept as the number it has reached (`rstate`) and made a stream again for each move, so any phone can make the next one; a move is the Fixer's reroll, the turn's move, the question, the bid or call, or an answer (to a claim, to a block, to a proof), and whose decision it is follows the referee (a claim's challenger, a proven claimant, else the player whose turn it is). A computer player's move is its personality's player following the whole game as its seat sees it, from the first event (any phone can make it, the one that ran the table before having gone), its free moves first as at the solo table; should the referee refuse one, the plainest move stands in, so the table never stalls. Each seat has a personality, the opponents chosen sitting after you and yours another, for when the computer plays your seat (your phone away for half a minute). What the database drops comes back as the referee wrote it (`restore`: a hand that's out, a banker's empty take, no bid standing, maps of faces given back as lists with gaps). Your answers come up as they always have (a claim against you, your proof to keep or put back) when the decision is yours; at a table the others' names are theirs, and a game you join part-way you watch until the next deal. Tap to continue holds the computer's move solo only (seats.js's `hold`); the streak counts each game once, for the seat you played (`cartel:counted`). A game kept before this (the referee's state alone) carries on as the runner keeps one. `tests/cartel.mjs` plays whole games at tables of three and four through a model of the database, every move the referee's; five phones in one browser played a party of three with the Wildcat in the fourth seat, each answering the claims made against them.

Targets are chosen on the table: when a move needs one (a steal, an audit, a sanction, a question, a hit), the seats that can be chosen glow, the chosen one is ringed and labelled, and tapping another seat changes it; the move panel keeps only the move.

Your turn's panel: on your move, four tiles (Take, Reroll, Claim, Hit); Claim opens six cards three to a row, the Fixer's free reroll first (dashed), then Banker, Trader, Auditor and the Regulator's inquiry and sanction, each role's name green when your gold dice show it and red when claiming it would be a bluff, with what can block it in small print on the button. On your bid: the faces, the count, and Ask, Call and Bid side by side; Ask opens the question (its kind, a face or a total, and a button that names the player and the question). The screen never scrolls: the turn panel keeps one height whatever it shows (the tallest move, a claim, fits it on a 664-pixel phone), and the table takes all the rest, so it keeps one size all game (326 px on a 664-pixel phone, 482 px at 844). Each seat's dice take two rows and shrink as hands grow, yours one row; what you know is one line along the table's near edge that scrolls sideways; the panel scrolls inside itself only on the smallest phones.

Each seat keeps its player's whole last turn until their next turn starts, so a round played fast can still be read: their move and what came of it ("Trader on you: stole 2", "Banker: a bluff, caught", a quiet Fixer reroll riding on the move's line), their question, with both answers when you're one of the two ("Asked you: how many 4s?" then "Quant 2 · you 1": what each learned about the other's hand; between two others the answers stay theirs, so there's no line for them), their bid or call, and any gold die lost. The log is seven lines tall; when a turn has more than fits (a long line wraps onto two), whole lines drop, so nothing is ever cut off part-way: a Fixer reroll first, then the bid (the middle of the table shows it too), then a question together with its answers; the move and any loss stay longest. Your own last question and both answers sit under your hand; a call's verdict sits where the bid stood until someone bids again.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the table takes the left, all the height under the header, and your turn's panel stands beside it, low (a long one scrolls from its top). The dice are sized to their seats in script, so they're sized again when the screen turns, and grow with an upright tablet's page.

## Spot
Sums fly across a dark field: thrown up from below in an arc, flung in from a side and falling away, or dropping faster and faster with a sway, each on its own path. Three lives; every 5 right in a row raises the score multiplier by half, up to ×3.

The pace is a budget of difficulty: every sum has a cost (1 for a small addition, 2 for a times, 3 for a division, +1 for bigger numbers, +1 under a modifier), and the run has a budget of difficulty a second that rises slowly (0.45 at the start, 1.3 at four minutes, then gently on). A cheap sum is followed quickly by the next; a dear one buys a few seconds, so you might get four easy sums in a row or one division with time to think. Lifetimes scale with cost (5.3 s for a small addition, 12 s for a big division) and shorten by a fifth over four minutes. Times tables fade in from 12 s, division from 35 s, bigger numbers from 60 s; the level shown goes up one every 30 s.

Modifiers: from 45 s, a badge in the header shows an operation to apply to every result before anything else (+1, +2, +3, −1, −2, +10 or ×2), changing every 20 s; a bit over a third of the time there is none. Each sum is tagged with the modifier it came under, so a change mid-flight is never ambiguous. Alone, the shelf shows modified answers, and the unmodified answer is always planted among the fakes. Versus, everyone has the alone game's modifiers, the same for all. Co-op, each player has their own modifier, and a pair is two sums that make the same number after each player's own modifier, so the numbers you call are the modified ones.

Alone: the answers wait on a shelf of eight round tokens below, among fakes. Tap an answer before its sum runs out; a wrong tap or a sum that runs out costs a life. Fakes are the slips you'd make: off by one or ten, swapped digits, a neighbouring times-table answer, adding instead of multiplying, forgetting the modifier. No two sums on screen show the same value, no fake matches one, and no number shows twice on a shelf. Play a random run or today's run (the same for everyone that day); bests are kept.

Each number comes onto the shelf at a moment of its own, 0.3 to 3.5 seconds before its sum flies in, into a free slot picked at random (October 2026). The answer used to arrive with its sum, its fakes beside it, in the first free slot, which was the first slot nine times in ten: the newest number in the first place was the answer, so it could be tapped without working the sum out. Now the shelf holds answers to sums still to come among the fakes, every number arriving on its own, so neither when nor where one appears says which sum it answers (`tests/spot.mjs` measures it: the answer as its sum appears is spread evenly over the eight slots, and the newest number on the shelf about one time in four, as likely as any of the few that came in just before). An answer tapped before its sum has come answers nothing on screen: a wrong tap, costing a life once however fast the taps, and the number stays for its sum. The moments come from a stream of each sum's own, apart from the sums'. Numbers that can share the shelf are kept apart over the longer stretch the early ones make (11 seconds and a tenth, plus the 3.5), which changes a seed's sums from the first time it rules a number out, today's run included.

At a table (October 2026): a pair's match from the partner sheet, or a party of up to six, everyone on their own phone, in one of two modes, chosen in the lobby before a run starts (or in the menu, once the last run is over for everyone). The lobby shows who's at the table in their colours, and Start deals a run to everyone there, starting four seconds later on the database's clock. Opened in a room, the page shows the rete (Joining your partner, or Joining the table at a party) until the room's lobby is drawn, never the solo start meanwhile, where a tap on Play started a run of your own (October 2026).

Versus, a party's to start with: everyone plays the alone game on the same seed from the same start, so the same sums are thrown at the same moments at everyone, each answering on their own shelf. The others' scores run live in the line under the header; once your lives are gone the standings show who's still playing, and once everyone's are, who won (the most points; equal scores share a place, and a draw wins nobody the pair's record), with Play again and Back to solo. Nothing but taps travels: each phone replays every player from their own taps on the one map (`versusStandings` in `spot-table.js`), so the scores agree everywhere, and a phone that leaves runs out of lives on its own as its sums go unanswered, holding nobody up.

Co-op, a pair's as it always was: there's no shelf, only sums, and each of you sees only your own. Most come in pairs: a sum on one screen and a sum making the same number on another, the second arriving 0.6–4 s after the first. You call out what yours make; whoever has the other taps the one of theirs that makes a number someone called, now or when it arrives (a tap clears the pair, even a partner still to appear). Some sums have no partner: tapping one costs a life. A pair that leaves both screens untapped costs a life. Lives and score are the table's, and no number means two things at once. For more than two the pairs are dealt across everyone at the table, each pair on two different screens and every screen its share, and a little faster than for two (√(n/2) times: 1.2 for three, 1.7 for six), since each call has more ears but must stay something a table can follow; two are dealt exactly as before. A run is dealt to those at the table when it starts: one who sits down after watches it out and plays the next.

How it's built: everything comes from a seed and the taps. `spot-engine.js` makes the timeline from the seed alone (the budget, the modifiers per seat, the pairs) and replays the taps over it to get the state at any moment (`play()`), so two phones with the same seed and taps see the same game, and only taps travel through the room (each with its time on the database clock, which `sync.js` provides as an offset). The page replays the run every frame; flight paths come from each sum's id. Solo runs pause while the app is in the background (a table's clock is everyone's and never stops), and the screen stays awake while you play. `spot-table.js` is the table's part, pure: the room's state, the seats a run is dealt to, the standings and the result. `tests/spot.mjs` checks the timelines, the pace (over twenty runs: one run's thirty seconds hold only a dozen sums), the modifiers, the pairs (for two to six), the replays, that the shelf gives nothing away, and Versus: the same map for every seat, the standings, the end and the draw.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the field takes the left, all the height under the score, and the shelf of answers stands beside it, two across and low; Co-op, which has no shelf, gives the field the whole width (it used to leave the shelf's column empty).

## Quote
Make a market on a number you don't know: a bid and an ask. Every question carries documented tiers, the radius a market may have around the truth for each grade: SS (exact, where exactness can be expected), S, A, B; wider is a C, a miss. Marie Curie's birth (1867): the 1860s is an S, 1850–1880 an A, 1850–1900 a B, 1800–1900 a miss. A market's radius is the distance from the truth to its farther edge, plus the miss again when the truth lies outside, so a confident wrong market grades harder than a wide right one and a near miss (1870–1880) still earns an A. The payoff is stepwise by grade (300 at exact, 250 at the S edge, 180 at A, 100 at B, 0 at twice the B radius, then a loss growing towards −300) with a continuous run between the steps, so a touch tighter earns a touch more, all scaled by the question's difficulty (1 to 5: a 1 pays 0.7 of the curve, a 5 pays 1.3). The card shows the tiers; the shortcuts set a market of the S, A or B radius around what you've typed; the verdict names the grade. Only a B or below banks the question to the pile: an A is adequate knowledge. Tiers and difficulty are in `quote-bank.js` (`tiers`, `d`), derived from each question's range (years: S 2.5× the range, A 2.5× S, B 4.5× S; magnitudes: S 1.5×, A 3.5×, B 7×) and meant to be edited by hand. Know a figure exactly? Give it as both bid and ask: a market on one figure, drawn as a dot on the tape, and the only way to an SS on a question that can be known exactly.

Every question has its own range for "close" (`tol` in the bank): ±1% on Everest's height, ±15% on a city's population, ±40% on Picasso's output; 2 years on a modern date, 20 on an ancient one; whole units on a count. The card says it ("close means ±15%").
