The payout is continuous: inside the market it is 300·e^(−v/2), with v the width in ranges, so a point market exactly right pays 300, one a range wide about 180 and one five ranges wide about 25; outside, that fades by e^(−u) with u the miss in ranges while a loss of 300·(1 − e^(−u/3)) grows in its place, so a miss by a third of a range still pays about 60% of exact, a miss by a whole range about breaks even, and far out costs up to 300. It's continuous across the market's edge. Widths and misses are in doublings for magnitudes (log2 of the ratio, the range converted the same way) and plain units otherwise. Shortcuts under the fields set a market of half, one, two or four ranges around what you've typed. After you quote, a tape shows your market and where the truth fell, with a note on the basis of the figure.

Together (October 2026): a pair's duo match, or a party of up to six, each on their own phone. Ranges, the default: everyone makes their own market on the same question, in private, scored exactly as a market alone is; a line under the Quote button shows who has quoted (ticked) and who's away. The reveal comes once everyone at the table has quoted: your verdict, then every market drawn on one scale in its player's colour with the truth through them all (one that missed it dashed) and what each earned, the best first; then the standings (standings.js: each book from the 1,000 everyone starts with, this question's change, and under each name their market and grade, a C said as wide or a miss); then the note. The table moves on once everyone at it has pressed Next, by Chart's rules for people away and the slow: nobody away holds it up, and once those who have quoted are at least as many as those still to and half a minute has passed since the last market, they may reveal without the rest (a missing market scores nothing); Next the same way. The last question's reveal ends the set, its result final there and then; the closing bell shows who won, every book with how many markets held the truth, your result on each question, Play again and Back to solo. Trade, the other way a pair can play (the menu's together part: Ranges or Trade, a fresh set either way; a table of three or more always plays Ranges): one makes the market without seeing anything else; the other then sees it and hits the bid (sells to the maker at it), lifts the offer (buys at it) or passes. A trade settles between the two by how far the traded price was off the truth, in the question's ranges, on the same smooth curve (up to 300 each way); a pass settles the maker against the house as when alone. Ten questions, five markets each, and either one's Next moves both on. Either way the higher book counts for the pair's duo record. The closing bell's list names a painting or building where the question said "this" ("The year The Kiss was painted"), as the list has no pictures to point at. Opened for a table, the page shows the rete (Joining your partner, or Joining the table at a party) until the table's set is drawn, never your own set meanwhile (October 2026; your own set used to be drawn first, so for the first seconds you and your partner were on different questions); Back to solo returns to your own set as you left it, and nothing at a table touches your own run, bests, today's, the Ledger or your pile. The table's rules, both ways, are pure functions in `quote-table.js` (everything kept by seat and by question, never in a list by number), tested in `tests/quote.mjs` through the database's round trip; three phones in one browser played a set of Ranges through (the reveal on each, Next from all three, one leaving mid-set, the grace for a slow quoter, the closing bell, Play again), and a pair from the partner sheet's address played Ranges, switched to Trade in the menu and traded a set through to its record. From 6 October 2026 the maker's screen failed to draw at a table together (it still asked for the width shortcuts after they'd gone): fixed in October 2026, when a run joining every duo game the way a request does found it.

Topics (October 2026): a random set deals from your mix of the bank's categories (Trade, Cities, Wine, Art, People, Geography, Markets, Refining, Scale), each out, in or more (twice as often, and up to four of it in a set rather than two), from a preset (Balanced; Trader: trade, markets and refining more; Culture night: art, wine, people and cities more) or your own, in the menu (README, The knowledge base). Balanced is the set as it always was, at most two from a category, and today's set is always Balanced. A set on a preset counts for your best (with learning mode off); your own mix doesn't. One topic in is that topic's set, all ten from it, with a best and a link of its own (`&f=refining`), as refining sets always were: European fuel specs (EN 228, EN 590, EN 15940, EN 14214 limits), the octane, vapour pressure and density of each blendstock, cetane and cold flow by component, energy and oxygen contents, conversions, RED III and the THG-quota, fuel taxes and duty, buy-out prices and crack spreads: 111 questions, so a player who crushes them has the numbers of the European road-fuel trade. Any other mix goes into the set's link topic by topic (`&t=trade.2,art.1`). At a table, a fresh set comes from the mix of whoever deals it.

`quote-bank.js` holds the questions (796 across trade, cities, art, wine, people, geography, markets, refining and scale), `quote-engine.js` settles markets and picks sets (seeded, at most two questions from a category, or by your mix), and `tests/quote.mjs` checks both.

The page fits the screen: once a market is settled the bid and ask boxes and the width shortcuts make way for the result (the tape shows your market against the truth), and a long note scrolls inside. Widths are rounded where they're made (0.1 × 3 used to show as 0.30000000000000004).

A question shows its unit only: the grade widths ("S ±3 ppm · A ±7 · B ±15") and the width shortcuts gave away the scale of the answer, so you work out your own range. A range that holds the answer never loses money, however wide, and always earns something: past the curve's B, 30% of B's payoff out to twice the B radius, shrinking as the range widens (100 to 1,500 on Switzerland's GDP of 885 earns about +15). Its verdict is yellow, "Wide, but in range", never red; red is for a range that missed. Below A it still goes to the review pile. A range that misses never earns, by however little: it's a C and loses at least 10, more the further the answer is beyond it against the question's B radius (a year beyond on a ±15-year question loses about 14; five beyond about 34), up to the old losses for big misses.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the card opens like a ledger's facing pages: the question, its unit and its picture on the left, the market beside them, level with the question and above where a tablet's keyboard rises (the card's two halves are `.fit-half`, which on a phone aren't boxes at all).

## Manifest
A stack of coloured containers (columns of varying height) drops into one isometric stage, container by container with a bounce, holds, and is gone; then you're asked about it. A run is twelve rounds, or until three lives are gone.

The order book: each round opens with the orders for its stack, the questions to come posted by kind and not by detail, each with a price. How many of one colour (which colour isn't said), how many in all, how many on tier 2, the colour at a spot on tier 2, the top of a column, the most common colour, a spot with the stack seen from the side or from behind (from round 3), and which of four stacks is yours, turned (from round 5). You choose what to watch. The stack comes in by the round's view (tier by tier; all at once, inner containers hidden; slice by slice; or two tiers and then the rest) and holds. Ship it ends the hold early: the share of the hold you didn't use pays up to half as much again on the round's answers. Then the questions come one at a time, in the order that gives least away first: the counts, then colours, then the turned spot (whose grey stack shows the shape), and which-stack last (its four stacks show nearly everything). Each card has its answers in view, so one tap answers it, or Pass this one moves on; the questions still to come show only their kind and price, and nothing is judged until the round's review, so neither a later question's options nor an earlier verdict helps. A question about a place on the stack carries its own picture beside its answers (the floor with the spot marked, or the stack turned and grey with one container lit). A right answer pays its price, a wrong one costs a life, a pass costs nothing, all settled at the review. Number answers are five evenly spaced values with the truth at a place drawn evenly, so the middle one is no safer a guess.

Prices grow 12% a round and follow your record for each kind (a hit rate that weights recent answers): a kind you usually get right pays down to 0.6 of its price, marked ↓; one you usually miss up to 1.4, marked ↑. The strategy that pays keeps moving.

Turning: a quarter turn puts the containers' long side the other way, and the stack is drawn so. The turned spot shows the stack turned and grey, one container lit: which colour is it? The which-stack question shows four stacks turned the same way: yours, its mirror image (left out when the stack is its own mirror under some turn), and yours with a container recoloured or two swapped.

Change rounds, every fourth: the stack holds, fades to an empty stage for a second and comes back all at once with something different, nothing marking it. Round 4: a container recoloured. Round 8: the stack turned a quarter, with a container recoloured. Round 12: two containers of different colours swapped, so counts can't give it away (tap both).

Stacks grow from 3×2×2 with three colours holding for 9 s to 4×4×3 with five colours for 5.7 s; three orders a round, four from round 6. The review after each round shows every answer and the truth; tap a question to light what it asked about. Random runs or today's run; bests kept; a run resumes where it was (a showing starts again from the beginning). `manifest-engine.js` makes the rounds and prices, `tests/manifest.mjs` checks 720 rounds: every order fair, turning consistent, four different stacks with one yours, and change rounds changing what they say among containers you could see.

The page fits the screen. The stage is there for the stack coming in, for a change round (where the stack itself is the answer: tap it) and for the review; while answering an ordinary round the questions have the room. They scroll inside themselves only in the busiest rounds on a small phone (four questions, two with pictures, and the four stacks).

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the stage's halves stand side by side: the containers (or the round's tiers) on the left, large, and the questions with the buttons on the right, the buttons at the bottom.

Together (October 2026): a table of two to six, the pair from the partner sheet or a Party, plays the rounds in step, in one of two modes, Against or Together, chosen in the menu's together part for the next game (before round 1, for this one too). The room keeps the round and the moment its order book opened, on the database's clock, and every phone runs the order book (six seconds, its bar running down), the drop and the hold from that, so everyone sees the stack at the same moment; a phone that reloads or comes in late joins at that moment, the containers already down drawn landed. A lobby lists who's at the table, and round 1 opens once everyone there has said Ready (two at least); after each round's results, the next opens once everyone at the table has pressed Next. Nobody away holds the table up (a phone asleep or closed isn't at the table), and once all the others are done, anyone may go on without the last one 30 seconds later (in a round, 30 seconds after the questions opened at the soonest: their answers stand, the rest are passed). Prices are plain for everyone: your record moves your own prices, not a table's, though it still learns from the answers you give. A table's clock doesn't stop for the menu, and the screen stays on. The line under the round says who else is at the table and how they stand (✓ done or ready, … answering, away). Your solo run, bests and today's are never touched.

Against: everyone sees the whole stack and answers their own questions, one at a time as alone, and Ship it ends only your own hold, for your own bonus. Once everyone at the table has finished, the round's results: the standings (standings.js: each player's answers this round as ✓ ✗ –, their lives, what the round paid them, the score), the questions with the truth, and, on a tap, the stack lit and what everyone gave. A player out of lives watches the rest, the stack and then the others answering. The game ends after the twelfth round, or once nobody has a life left: the final standings, Play again (dealt by whoever asks first; a later ask is a Ready for it) and Back to solo. For the pair's record, a win is first place alone.

Together: one run for the table, its score and three lives. Each player sees only their part of the stack, so the table has to talk. The stack is shared out in whole columns of the floor, a stretch of it each, the columns taken slice by slice in a snake and cut where the parts come out most even (never further apart than the tallest column, three containers): a part stands on the floor as it was stacked and reads the same in every view. Not by tier, though a stack comes in tier by tier: the bottom tier has every column and the top one a third of them, so one player would get three times another's, and a tier above the floor would hang in the air. The parts go round the table a step each round, and one who sits down mid-round has a part from the next. All at once, each part is drawn alone, so the table sees more than the whole stack shows (nobody else's containers stand in front of yours), and "you could see" means any of you. A change round shows each their part and brings the whole stack back to everyone: its owner can tell what changed. The questions are the team's, one at a time: anyone answers, the first answer counts (one a moment later does nothing, and says who was first), anyone passes. Ship it is everyone's: the hold ends, and the bonus is set, once everyone with a part has shipped. The review shows everyone the whole stack, and who answered what. For the pair's record, the team's score, won if it came through the twelve rounds.

The table's rules are pure, in `manifest-engine.js` (Ready and Next, the round's clock, replies, settling a round for everyone, who the table waits for, the split), each change made in the room's transaction, keyed s<slot> and q<i>; `tests/manifest.mjs` splits 3,600 stacks among two to six, checks teams' rounds as fair as alone, and plays tables of three in Node, every change read back as the database gives it. (Before this, a match was a race on `duel.js`: the same twelve rounds for everyone at their own pace.)

## Chart
Pin a place on the map: a city, a country, a port or chokepoint, a mine or oilfield, a wine village, a museum (or where a painting hangs), a birthplace, a mountain or a strait.

Every question says what it asks: Pin the Hermitage, Pin Balkh with what the name carried as a line of its own (Where Rumi was born; In Rioja; The town, for Guernica), and for an outline or a line what counts (Anywhere inside it counts; Anywhere on it counts); at the reveal it takes its full name again. An instruction rather than a question, so the pyramids of Giza and the Alps read right. Deck asks Chart's places in the same words.

What a set deals is your mix of topics in a region, both in the menu (October 2026: the topics were a dropdown of six until then; README, The knowledge base). The topics are Chart's categories: Cities, Countries, Commodities (ports, terminals, refineries, mines and oilfields), Geography (peaks, islands, deltas and the far places), Physical (the rivers, ranges, deserts, plateaus and lakes drawn on the map), Art, Museums, Architecture, Myth, History, People, Wine, and Local places, a region's own (Bavaria's towns, the Alps' resorts, Lake Geneva's sights), dealt only in that region. Each is out, in or more (dealt twice as often, and up to twice as many of it in a set), from a preset (Balanced; Trader: commodities, cities and countries more; Culture night: art, museums, architecture, myth, history, people and wine more) or your own. Balanced deals exactly the sets Everything did, at most two of a kind worldwide; a mix of only in and out (one topic, or the old Nature's two) deals the sets the dropdown's topic did, a little over a category's share each and one category all ten, topped up so a set is short only when the selection is. The region: Worldwide, or Europe, the Middle East, Asia, Africa, North America, South America or Oceania, each listed with how many places it holds for your mix (a short set below ten). In a region every question opens zoomed on its map window. A place counts in its country's region, by the flags bank's regions with a few made to match what the windows show: Turkey is Middle East and Europe, Cyprus Europe and Middle East, Egypt Africa and Middle East, Russia as a country both Europe and Asia, and a place in Russia or Turkey by its side of the Urals or the Bosphorus (Norilsk is Asia, Istanbul Europe); Antarctica and the far ocean islands are in no region. A new region deals a new set in it at once (asking first if one is under way; together, a fresh set for everyone); new topics apply to the next set. A set's link carries both (#s=…&t=art.2,wine.1&r=europe; a link from before the mix, &t=nature, deals the set it always did). Bests are kept per selection, a preset's, a single topic's or an old topic's in each region (Balanced worldwide keeps Everything's best); your own mix of several keeps none. The games screen's best counts today's set (Balanced, worldwide) and a preset worldwide, learning mode or not.

A country's outline is its target: anywhere inside it scores in full (1,100), anywhere outside nothing, with the distance to its border shown. The outlines (`chart-countries.js`, 168 countries) are Natural Earth's 1:110m, named as the flags bank names them and rounded to a tenth of a degree as `world.js` is, so the outline is the border drawn on the map; the inside test is even–odd over all of a country's rings, so a hole is outside (Lesotho isn't South Africa); Northern Cyprus and Somaliland are inside Cyprus and Somalia (the recognised borders); rings across the antimeridian (Russia's east, Fiji) are cut there; French Guiana is part of France, as in the source. Microstates aren't in the 1:110m set, nor territories the flags bank has no flag for. A country's clues can't name it, so they are its land neighbours' count (with its region), then two of its neighbours, then its flag (an island nation: its flag, then its first letter). A pin outside a country, or any clue, sends it to the pile, carrying its knowledge-base id, so the other games' learning modes deal it too. One map: drag to pan, pinch (or the wheel) to zoom about your fingers, from the whole world down to 1.5° across, with a graticule that tightens as you zoom in; tap to place the pin, tap again to move it, then pin it. The reveal frames your pin and the truth together, with a line and the distance, and a description of the place. Points fall off smoothly with great-circle distance: 1,000 on the spot, about 600 at 1,000 km, 135 at 4,000 km, plus 100 within 100 km. Physical features too, 172 of them, drawn on the map rather than pinned to a point: rivers as lines (the Nile, the Rhine, the Yangtze, the Paraná), mountain ranges, deserts, plateaus and basins, and lakes as shapes (`chart-geo.js`, from Natural Earth, simplified), each with a note. Pin anywhere on the feature for full marks; the distance is to its nearest point, and at the reveal the feature is drawn and the map frames it with your pin. Clues, at a price: the first names the region and keeps 70% of the points, the second the country (45%), the third shows the place's description with its own name hidden (25%); in a room each player's clues discount their own score. Ten places a set, random sets or today's set (for the selection), bests kept, links to a set.

Together (October 2026): a pair's duo match, or a party of up to six, each on their own phone. Everyone pins the same place, seeing only their own pin, and takes their own clues, which discount only their own points; a line under the map shows who's at the table, who has pinned (ticked) and who's away. The reveal comes once everyone at the table has pinned: the map shows every pin in its player's colour with their initial, each joined to where it should have been, and under it this place's results, the most points first (each one's distance, any clues, the points), then the standings (standings.js: each total and this place's change), then the place's note. The table moves on once everyone at it has pressed Next, and the hint says who it's waiting for. Someone away (another game open, the phone asleep) never holds it up, and nor does someone slow: once those who have pinned are at least as many as those still to, and half a minute has passed since the last pin, they may reveal without the rest (a missing pin scores nothing); Next the same way, so a phone put down at the reveal doesn't stop the table either. The last place's reveal ends the set, its result final there and then (the pair's record counts it); Final standings shows who won, every total with how many places each was best on, every pin of the set on one map, Play again (a fresh set for the table) and Back to solo. The menu at a table holds your topics and the region: a new region deals a fresh set for everyone at once, and every fresh set comes from the mix of whoever deals it. A pair plays the same way with two seats, and the higher total counts for their duo record. Opened for a table (a request, Join, a party), the page shows the rete (Joining your partner, or Joining the table at a party) until the table's set is drawn, and deals your own set only if the join doesn't come off (October 2026): it used to draw your own set first, so for the first seconds, ten on a slow connection, each of you was on a place of your own. Back to solo returns to your own set as you left it, and nothing at a table touches your own run, bests or today's. A set dealt as you sit down is worldwide, from the mix of whoever deals it, the menu changing it for the next. The table's rules are pure functions in `chart-table.js` (everything kept by seat and by question, never in a list by number, which the database would read back with holes), tested in `tests/chart.mjs` through the database's round trip: scoring a place for several, the reveal's order, who the table waits for and the grace, people coming and going, the standings and the record. Three phones in one browser played a set through (each pin private until the reveal, the reveal on each, Next from all three, one going back to solo mid-set without holding the other two up, the grace for a slow pinner, the final standings and Play again), and a pair from the partner sheet's address played one with a reload on each side and its record written.

The map is drawn on canvas from `world.js`: Natural Earth's country polygons (with their holes, each drawn on its own so nothing wraps the antimeridian; the rings that crossed it, Russia's far east and Fiji, are cut there) and interior borders at 1:110m (public domain, via the world-atlas package), rounded to a tenth of a degree, 160 KB, so it works offline. Mercator clipped at ±78°. `chart-countries.js` holds the countries (outline, flag, regions, land neighbours from the source's shared borders, knowledge-base id) and every flags-bank country's regions; `chart-bank.js` holds 415 places with coordinates and, for each, a description of what it is and why it matters (shown at the reveal), with the trade angle where there is one; `chart-engine.js` does distance, scoring (a country by its outline), sets, topics, regions and their views, and the projection; `tests/chart.mjs` checks them, the countries' outlines (each holds its own anchor; Paris is in France and Madrid isn't; Maseru is in Lesotho, not South Africa; Chukotka past 180° is Russia) and the regions (Ras Tanura is Middle East, Norilsk Asia).

The page fits the screen: the map takes the room left and keeps its shape, and under it a fixed space holds the clues and buttons or, after the pin, the result (its note scrolling inside), so the map keeps one size from pinning to the reveal. At a table the map keeps the world's shape at the card's width (smaller only where the screen is short) and the space under it, all that's left on a tall phone, holds the clues and buttons and then the results and standings, scrolling inside; held sideways they take the side column under the place.

The end of a set shows every pin on one map, each joined by a dashed line to where it should have been (for a river, range or lake, the nearest point of it, with the feature drawn), numbered and framed so all ten fit; under it a row a place with its miss and points. Tap a row to frame that place's miss alone (the others fade); tap it again for all of them. At a table everyone's pins are drawn, each in their seat's colour, and each row gives a pair's both misses, or a bigger table's best and yours. A region can also lie within a country: Bavaria (October 2026) is played on its own map window, with its outline, the Danube and Lake Constance drawn faintly for bearings. Its places are those marked with its state in kb/ (Munich and Neuschwanstein as much as Landshut), and its own category of towns, peaks and sights is dealt only there, never in a round of the world. Britain and Scotland (October 2026) are sub-regions too: Britain takes every place in the United Kingdom, Scotland the places marked with its state; Scottish towns are dealt in both. They're drawn on a detailed coastline of the British Isles (chart-detail.js, Natural Earth at 1:50m), in place of the coarse world map, so the Hebrides, Orkney and Shetland are there to pin; with the Anglo-Scottish and Anglo-Welsh borders, the Thames and the Severn. China (October 2026) is a sub-region the same way, by country, drawn with the Yangtze and the Yellow River. The Alps (October 2026) cross five countries, so their places join by a mark in kb/ (marks: Alps) rather than a state or a country; Garmisch and Oberstdorf are marked Alpine and Bavarian, and turn up in both. The range is drawn as a dotted outline. Lake Geneva and the Watch Valley (October 2026) are drawn with 1:10m lakes and borders (chart-detail.js), the fine borders in place of the coarse ones; Lake Geneva is played at city scale, so a small region opens, and reveals, at its own width (regionSpan) rather than the 6° a continent needs. The map's night palette (October 2026) sets the land well apart from the sea, every coastline is outlined, and the islands the coarse world map leaves out come from Natural Earth at 1:50m (chart-islands.js, written by kbwork/islands.mjs): oceanic ones (the Azores, Cape Verde, the Maldives, Tonga…) show as a speck at any zoom and as land once they're big enough, coastal islets only then. On the iPhone, Safari's own pinch is refused on the map, so a pinch zooms the map, not the page. The map listens to pointer events alone (October 2026), as every other map in the suite: it once also cancelled a second finger's touches, and a browser given both can lose that finger's lift, after which a one-finger drag pinched against the lost finger, zooming and hardly panning, and a tap no longer placed the pin. `fingers()` in `chart-engine.js` counts the fingers on the map and starts again with the first finger of every new touch, so a finger the browser lost can't outlast its gesture; `tests/chart.mjs` checks a pan, a pinch, a tap and a lost lift.

Museums: the world's most significant museums, art and otherwise, are all in the knowledge base with a position and a note (196 of them). Art museums are pinned under Art (141 pins); the rest under Museums (67): history and archaeology (the Egyptian and Grand Egyptian Museums, the Acropolis Museum, Mexico's anthropology museum, the Terracotta Army), science and technology (the Deutsches Museum, the Science Museum, Air and Space, the Computer History Museum), natural history (London, New York, Paris, Berlin, Chicago), ships and polar voyages (the Vasa, the Mary Rose, the Fram, the Kon-Tiki) and memorials (Anne Frank House, Auschwitz-Birkenau, Yad Vashem, Hiroshima). Where a painting hangs is asked among art museums only.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the map takes the card's left side, all its height, and the place to find, its picture and the buttons stand at the right, the buttons at the bottom.

## Survey
A 3D puzzle (October 2026; it was a budgeted guessing game about a mining concession before, which Korbi found weak). A block of rock, 3, 4, 5 or 6 cubes each way, hides a body of ore. Every straight row of cubes through it, in all three directions, has a reading printed on the face it comes out of: how many of that row's cubes are ore. With the seams marked (the default) the plate also says how the ore lies: a plain plate is one seam (one unbroken run), a ring is two seams, a box is three or more; Counts only leaves that off and is the harder reading. Your job is to chip away all the waste. Only a cube with a side open to the air can be chipped, so you work from the outside in, and each hollow you cut lets you reach, and see into, what lies behind it. A cube that turns out to be ore stays, shows in gold and costs a strike; Mark keeps cubes you are sure are ore safe from the chisel (free, and only a cube you could chip can be marked); Hint names a cube the readings force right now, with the row that says so, and lights both.

Your score is your time in seconds plus 20 for each strike and 30 for each hint; lower is better. A row whose cubes left are all ore has nothing more to chip and its plate dims; one with nothing left at all disappears. The last waste cube ends the block (counted the moment it goes, as for every daily), and the ore left is shown in a wave of gold while the block turns on its own for a few seconds.

Every block has exactly one answer, and it can be reached by reasoning along rows alone, never by guessing: `survey-engine.js` generates each one and checks that a solver which only ever uses what one row forces (the rows that fit its reading and what is already known) settles every cube. That also proves the answer is unique. The generator grows one to three lumps of ore to about half the block, breaks them up a little (a tenth of the ore removed, a few waste cubes added beside it), keeps the layout only if ore is between 28% and 60% of the block and no waste cube is sealed inside (one you could never reach), and takes the first that needs at least as many rounds of reasoning as the size asks (Small 2 with the seams marked and 3 counts only, Medium 3 and 4, Large 3 and 5, Vast 4 and 6; a round uses only what the round before settled), else the hardest of up to sixty it tried. With the seams marked every random shape is solvable this way; counts alone are, in a measured run, about 98% of Small, 92% of Medium, 69% of Large and 40% of Vast layouts, so the larger counts-only blocks take the generator more tries (a few milliseconds, never a wait). The puzzle is made from its seed, size and readings alone, so the same link is the same block anywhere. These numbers come from the generator's statistics, not from play: whether Medium is the right default and the depth targets are right is for Korbi's thumb to say.

The block is drawn by `survey-3d.js` on a canvas (three.js, kept on the device): every cube is one instance of a rounded cube, so a whole block is one draw; a chipped cube pops and shrinks away with a burst of shards and leaves a hollow with its shadows; a strike cube swells and turns gold; a marked cube is tinted blue with a diamond on each face. The camera goes round the block under a finger (a drag; it tips from 14° to 80° above the horizon and the view is kept) and is a perspective one fitted so the block and its plates fill the room at every angle. The readings are small plates of the page laid over the canvas, each at the face where its row comes out of the block on the side turned toward you, so they stay upright and legible while the block turns; a plate fades out as its face turns edge-on and the other end of the row takes over. A tap works the tool on the cube under the finger (the nearest cube still in the block: ray against the cubes' boxes); pressing a cube lights its three rows' plates, which is the way to read it. The light turns with the camera so every face you see is lit the same from any side. The light, the plinth and the gold follow the theme and day or night.

Tools and the line under the block follow the tool: tap a cube; Chip, Mark, Hint. A marked cube cannot be chipped until it is unmarked, so a wrong mark costs nothing but a tap; a wrong chip costs 20 seconds. Hints use only what is certain (cubes chipped, ore shown), never your marks. If every cube a hint could name is still hidden inside, it lights the row and says to chip towards it.

The page fits the screen: the block takes all the room under the score, the tools and the line under it; at 390×600 and up nothing scrolls. Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the block takes the card's left side with all the height, and the score, the line and the tools stand at the right, low where a thumb is. Where there is no WebGL the page says so (there is no flat version).

Sizes Small, Medium, Large and Vast in the header; Readings (Seams marked, Counts only) in the menu; a random block or today's, which is the same for everyone (Medium, seams marked) and counts for the games screen's tile as a time, the first clear's score (`survey:today`). Bests, one per size and readings, are in `survey:times`, not `survey:best` (the games screen reads a best as highest-wins). The block in play is `survey:run` (saved at every change and every five seconds, so a partner watching sees it), your view `survey:view`, the readings `survey:readings`; the old game's `survey:best` and `survey:daily` were cleared once (`survey:era`). Links are `#s=SEED&z=SIZE&r=READINGS` (`#d=` for a day's). The clock runs while the page is on screen and stops with the menu, the rules and the games screen.

Together: the same block for everyone at the table, the same size and readings, each chipping it on their own phone from when the table starts (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six; the header's size deals a new match at that size. The others show under the score as the rock they have left (sent every second and a half, not every chip), and as their score once they have cleared it. The lowest score (time plus penalties) wins. Your own block, bests and today's stay as they were. `tests/survey.mjs` checks the rules, the solver, the generator (a puzzle of every size and reading), uniqueness by exhaustive search on small ones, chipping, marks, strikes, hints and scores.

## Blend
The blender's job. An order comes in, a product with its specification and a sales price: summer or winter E10 (EN 228 class A or F), Super Plus 98 E5, winter E85, summer or winter B7 (EN 590 class B or F), arctic class 1, paraffinic diesel (EN 15940), Jet A-1 with SAF, VLSFO 0.5% (ISO 8217 RMG 380), ECA marine gasoil 0.1%. You have a tank farm of components with real properties and prices (Eurobob, reformate, alkylate, isomerate, FCC naphtha, light straight-run, butane, ethanol, ETBE, toluene; straight-run gas oil, hydrocracker diesel, LCO, kerosene, arctic and summer HVO, RME, PME, UCOME, heavy gas oil, GTL, diesel slops; jet kerosenes and HEFA; residues, marine gasoil, cutter stock) and set each in steps of 5% until the blend adds up to 100 and every line of the specification passes, at the best margin you can. Renewable content earns the compliance credit (UCOME double). Par is the exact best blend on the 5% grid, found by trying every one. A spec line turns green or red as you go; tap it for what the property is and which components move it; tap a component for its note and full properties. Hint names the best 5% move: the one that fixes most of the shortfall, or, once you pass, the one that earns most. Certify when every line passes; the order is done within €5 of par.

The maths is the practice: most properties blend linearly by volume, octane by blending values (ethanol's RON of 120), vapour pressure by blending RVP (ethanol's 130 kPa at low blends), and flash point, cold flow (CFPP, freeze point) and viscosity by index, so the component that is worst in that property dominates: 5% of slops at a flash point of 24 takes straight-run diesel from 66 to 45 °C, and 20% kerosene takes it to 54; lubricity saturates with about 2% FAME. The orders teach the constraints that bite: the vapour-pressure ceiling on summer E10 against ethanol, the floor in winter and butane's 358 kPa, aromatics and olefins caps against reformate and FCC naphtha, the 5% ethanol limit on Super 98, LCO against cetane and polyaromatics, kerosene against cetane, viscosity and flash point in winter diesel, the density window against HVO, palm ester's +10 °C CFPP, a slops cargo's flash point, the arctic class, EN 15940's aromatics ban, the aromatics floor that caps HEFA in jet, sweetening residue to 0.5% at distillate prices. Figures are textbook-typical. `blend-engine.js`; `tests/blend.mjs` checks every order has a passing blend, the blending maths, and that hints lead to one.

The page fits the screen: the order in two lines (its story behind About), a compact margin card with the verdict beside Hint and Certify, the specification as chips in the spec's order (red off spec; tap one for the limit, this blend's value and why), and the tank farm taking the rest and scrolling inside itself, each tank's figures on one line (tap its name for the rest).

A partner watching (Rooms) sees the blend as you set it. Blend has saved under blend2: since its rebuild (its stars under blend:), and watching copied only blend:, so a watcher saw the tank farm as the order opens; both go across now (October 2026).

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the tank farm, where you blend, takes the left, and the order, the margin with its buttons and the specification stand beside it.

Together (October 2026): the same order for both of you, from the same starting blend, each blending on their own phone (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet; the header's order deals a new match on it. Par shows as ever; the hint doesn't. Certify is your answer, asked once and final: the highest margin wins, then the quicker, so it's a race to par as much as a hunt for it. The others show where the cost line was, blending or certified at their margin. Your own blends, bests and stars stay as they were. A party doesn't offer it (October 2026): a long, quiet game makes a poor round at a table of friends.

## Pipes
Oil is already on its way. The board is laid with pipe tiles in random rotations (straights, bends, crossings whose two channels don't mix, pumps, and rock); tap a tile to turn it a quarter clockwise, hold it (or right-click) to turn it back. With Hydraulic model, a rare boon (October 2026: before, everyone had it), a red "dry" marks where a line, as the board stands, would run out of pressure; without it you count the tiles yourself. After the planning countdown the flow leaves its source: HVO crosses a tile every 2.8 s at the start of an act, crude and used cooking oil more slowly, crude eight seconds behind when it shares the board; each tile entered is locked. A dead end, the edge of the field, a pipe that's already full or the wrong terminal is a spill. Pressure lasts 8 tiles after the source or a pump and only a pump refills it, so every board has a direct line that runs dry and a detour through a pump that doesn't. A delivery's netback is at the day's prices: the terminal's price, moved by the market, less 10 a tile of pipe and 40 a pump the flow passes, plus time (the tenths of a second left of a 90-second window once the oil goes); it scores that netback times the mult its boons make (below). On two-terminal levels the far one always shows the higher price but costs more to reach; either can be the right pick, and the note after delivery says which was. Fill it now runs the flow at once and counts the time as if pumped at ×4. Three lives; a spill costs one and the level comes back as a fresh board. Leaving the app mid-level starts the level over on a fresh board too (no life lost), so leaving can't buy time to plan.

The campaign runs in acts of five levels, each bringing one new idea onto a gentle board and ramping within itself; the next act starts gentler again, and gives a life back. Act 1, crude alone on a 5×7 field (levels 3 and 4 with a near and a far terminal); act 2, crude from its wellhead and HVO from its plant to their own terminals on 6×8, crossing only at a crossing; act 3, the HVO unit on 7×9: used cooking oil from its tank into the unit, which hydrotreats it, HVO out one side and bio-naphtha out the other, each to its own terminal, as a renewable diesel plant really does; then the open field, the three in turn. Speeds ramp within an act and never pass a floor: HVO at least 1.9 s a tile, at least 15 s to plan, rock at most 12% of the spare cells.

A run is a string of jobs (October 2026), a roguelike in Balatro's spirit. Before every level, three doors, each a job's twist and the boon that delivering it earns (Hades' doors, more or less). Level 1 offers three standard jobs for common boons; after that, one standard job for a common boon and two twisted ones (a rush with 25% less planning, oil 20% faster, twice the rock, no tools, or pressure a tile shorter) for an uncommon one or, a time in three, a rare. A spill retries the same job on a fresh board; the reward comes with the delivery.

Boons sit in five slots, in the row under the header with the score and the lives, each edged in its tier's enamel (a tap says what it does, and what a counting one has come to). A delivery scores netback × mult: the mult starts at 1, boons add to it and multiply it (all the adds, then all the multiplies, so the slots' order never matters), and most of them fire as the oil enters the tiles they care about, so the route you lay is the hand you play. As the oil goes, each boon's effect rises off its tile (verdigris what it adds to the netback, copper what it adds to the mult, gold what it multiplies it by), its slot pulses and the mult climbs beside the status line. Delivered, a ledger covers the board: the netback's lines (the terminals, less pipe and pumps, plus time and what boons add), the mult's (each boon's share), their product counting up, then the quota, Interest's share and the boon earned; See the board puts it behind. With the five slots full, the boon earned waits: before the next doors you tap the one it replaces (and what that one had counted goes with it) or leave it.

The forty-five boons fall in families that cross. Bends: Serpentine (+0.15 mult a bend), Elbow grease (+20 netback a bend), Hairpin (×1.2 for every three bends in a row), Winding river (rare: +0.02 for every bend since you took it, counting across the run). Straights: Trunk line (+0.3 a straight), Express (+1 for each straight after a straight). Crossings: Junction (+1 a crossing), Spaghetti junction (×1.5 for each crossing carrying two products). Pumps: Booster (+100 netback a pump), Compressor (×1.2 a pump), Bypass (×2 with no pump at all), Pump jack (rare: ×0.05 more for every pump since you took it), High-pressure pumps (pressure two tiles longer in play, though the board is built for the usual eight, so some direct lines stop running dry). Length: Long haul (+1 for every eighth tile of pipe), Golden pipe (rare: ×0.04 more for each tile beyond 20), Cheap steel (pipe at 7). Time: Early bird (Fill it now pays +0.1 mult a second of planning left), Clockwork (rare: +0.4 per 10 s of the bonus left), Survey team (5 s more planning), Long window (the bonus from 120 s), Choke valve (oil 15% slower), Fast fill (Fill it now at ×6). Tools: Thrift (+2 on a level with no tool used), Toolsmith (+1 a tool used), Surveyor's marks (+0.5 for each ✓-marked pipe the oil passes), Supplier discount (tools 30% less), Spare parts (Auto-turn always on offer), Wide kit (four tools), Restock (a use more), Pre-laid (rare: each route's first two pipes start turned right, ✓-marked). The market: Interest (5% of your points after each delivery, up to 300), Premium buyers (terminals 15% more), Green premium (HVO and bio-naphtha pay double), Speculator (+2 while crude trades below list), Hedge (crude and HVO never below list), Integrated refinery (rare: ×1.5 for each product delivered beyond the first). The ground: Wildcat (+1 for each rock beside the oil's path). Risk: Clean record (+0.3 for each level in a row without a spill), Contrarian (rare: ×2 on any twisted job, finales included), Insurance (rare: an act's first spill costs no life and keeps the record clean), Spare crew (rare: a life, and room for four while held), Leverage (rare: terminals 50% more, a spill two lives). The run: Broker (four doors), Big contracts (finales at triple), Hydraulic model (the dry warning). Builds come from crossing them: Serpentine, Hairpin and Winding river want long winding routes; Trunk line and Express want straight runs; Compressor, Booster and Pump jack want pumps on the way where Bypass wants none; Thrift and Toolsmith pull against each other; Contrarian pays for taking the twisted doors.

An act's fifth level is its finale: a big contract paying double (triple with Big contracts), its doors three rare boons, with a boss bending one rule (Storm, the oil 30% faster; Drought, pressure two tiles less, built and played; Strike, no tools; Quarry, three times the rock; Audit, pipe at double; the Regulator, your first boon sits it out, greyed in its slot from the finale's doors) and a quota the delivery must reach: 1,200, 12,000, 36,000, 90,000, 220,000, then ×2.5 an act. Short of it, the buyer pays nothing and it counts as a spill: a life (two with Leverage, none once an act with Insurance), and the clean record broken. The six bosses come in an order dealt by the run's seed, none twice in six acts, and the note between levels says which is coming and what it will take. The quotas are set against simulated runs (every level played on its designed route with 30 s of the bonus left): a build of commons alone gets through act 2 and fades over acts 3 to 5, doors taken at random fade over acts 4 to 6, and a build chosen for what it multiplies reaches act 6 or 7. The first draft's quotas (1,000 rising to 60,000 at act 5) were passed through act 4 by commons alone 98% of the time.

Boons are rewards only: points stay the score and buy the tools. The rules are pure, in `pipes-run.js`, seeded by the run: today's run deals everyone the same doors and bosses for the same choices, and nothing carries over between runs. The doors cover the board's box like the tools' brief, edged in their tier's enamel; the run's summary lists the build and its best delivery. A run saved before boons keeps its last five upgrades as boons.

Tools, bought with points as you play, in planning or while it flows: each map offers three of six (four with Wide kit; none on a no-tools job), at its own prices and with a few uses each (clear a rock into a random pipe, add a pump to a straight or a bend, make a tile a curve, make it a crossover, pause everything for five seconds, or Auto-turn a pipe). Auto-turn (October 2026) is the cheap one, 20 to 40 a go: tap a pipe and it turns the way the level's designed route runs through it (where a near and a far terminal's routes part, towards the better pick), marked ✓ until you turn it by hand; a pipe no route uses gets ✕ instead, and a route straight that another tool made a curve gets neither. It charges either way, because a free refusal would show the route tile by tile; a tap on a pipe already marked is free. A level opens on its tools (October 2026): a card over the board lists them, each with its price, uses and what it does, and the clock waits until you tap Start the clock (or anywhere on the card), so taking them in costs none of the planning time. The board stays covered meanwhile, so leaving the app then doesn't deal a fresh board as it does once the clock runs; a partner watching sees the card too. There's no card when none of the three is affordable (level 1, with nothing yet to spend). Between levels crude and HVO prices drift a few per cent either way (within 70% and 140% of list). Random runs and today's run (whose maps offer the same tools to everyone); bests by score and level. `pipes-engine.js` builds levels, moves the flow and traces the routes for the dry warning; `tests/pipes.mjs` checks 200 levels across the acts: repeatable, routes deliver, direct lines run dry, two-product levels cross, HVO-unit levels split, the trace of every solution delivers, either terminal can be the better pick, scrambled boards don't work by luck, the netback and the pressure rule, Auto-turn (every route pipe turns to join its route, a board turned that way delivers to the better terminal, a pipe off every route and a curved route straight have no right way), and the run (the doors over 30 runs, swapping when the slots are full: repeatable, never a boon held or twice on offer, the right tiers and twists, cargo once all are held; every job's terms, every boss and every rule boon as its card says; boards under rocky ground, low pressure, Drought and Quarry built and delivering; High-pressure pumps letting direct lines deliver; every scoring boon as its card says, alone and together, and with none the netback as ever; and the quota curve against twelve simulated runs, commons alone against boons picked for what they multiply).

The page fits the screen: the board takes the height left and keeps its shape, and the buttons and the note keep their room whether or not they're showing, so the board never changes size mid-level. The boons share the score's row (the level moved to the status line), so the board kept its size when they came; the status line stays one line, and three products' pressure gauges shrink their bars rather than wrap (they wrapped once ticked, moving the board). The ledger's lines come in one by one and a short box tightens it, measured in script, not by a container query: in Chromium a delayed animation on an element styled by one restarts at every layout of the page, and the score counting up lays it out every frame, which once left a line invisible until the count stopped. In Modern, Kontor and Office the quiet buttons (Fill it now, See the board) keep the ink by day; they were lettered white on the light page.

The level as it stands is kept in the run's save (October 2026): at every turn of a tile, every tool, and a few times a second as the oil enters tiles. A partner watching sees your board and the oil moving on it, run on by the moment since your last move (it used to see a board of its own, dealt from the run's seed). A finished level comes back as it ended, its result and its button, where a reload used to deal the level again and pay for it twice.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the board takes the stage's left side, as tall as the stage; what's happening, the tools and the note stand at the right, the buttons at the bottom.

Together (October 2026): the same seeded run for everyone at the table, for an act, two or three (the menu's Length, for the next match; two to start with), each building their own run on their own phone (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six. The same seed deals the same first doors, and the same boards and bosses for the same choices; a match's prices drift between levels alike for everyone (seeded, where a run of your own drifts at random). The others' scores and levels show under yours. A match's clock doesn't stop for the menu, as your own run's does, and a level left mid-way starts over as ever. The highest score once the last level is delivered, or the lives are gone, wins, then the quicker; the result lists your build and best delivery. Your own run, bests and today's stay as they were. (The menu's title no longer stays "Out of lives" after a finished run.)

## Refinery
A flowsheet puzzle that teaches what every refinery unit eats and makes. Each level gives you a crude with its assay (API, sulphur, the cut yields), a set of units and a price deck; the crude unit's cuts appear as streams and you route each one to a unit or a product pool. Units turn their feed into outputs by yield tables (the vacuum unit by the crude's own residue split, the hydrocracker swelling the barrel), hydrotreaters carry the feed's octane or cetane through and set its sulphur, the reformer makes hydrogen and everything else that treats or cracks with hydrogen uses it; the shortfall comes from the hydrogen plant, or at three times the price without one. Pools blend what they're given by volume and must meet their spec (gasoline RON 95 and 10 ppm; diesel cetane 51 and 10 ppm; jet only jet-grade streams; fuel oil 0.5% sulphur) or they sell as something cheaper: off-spec gasoline as naphtha, off-spec diesel as fuel oil, sour fuel oil as high-sulphur fuel oil. The margin is revenue less crude, bought-in renewables, operating cost and hydrogen, in $/bbl of crude, shown against par, which a solver finds by local search with pair moves (pools have cliffs: two streams often have to move together). A level is done within $0.6/bbl of par with gasoline, diesel and jet in spec (fuel oil falling to high-sulphur fuel oil is priced, not a failure). Help: every option in the destination picker shows what it would do to the margin, judged with any streams it creates sent to their best places (so heavy naphtha to the hydrotreater is judged with the treated naphtha going on to the reformer), and choosing it routes those streams the same way; the Hint button names the single change worth most, with the follow-on routings, or, when no single change helps because two streams have to move together, where par differs; the fifth hint shows par's whole routing with the differences marked and a button to take it. Sulphur in the straight-run cuts scales with the crude's assay, so a sweet crude's residue makes VLSFO and a sour one's doesn't.

Fourteen units with cards (purpose, three paragraphs, feeds, yield table, hydrogen, operating cost, and what it's running at now): crude and vacuum distillation, the naphtha hydrotreater, the reformer, isomerisation, the FCC and its naphtha post-treater, the hydrocracker, alkylation, the diesel and kerosene treaters, the coker, visbreaker, bitumen blowing, the hydrogen plant, the sulphur plant and an HVO unit; some 40 streams with their blending properties and a line each; six crudes from Bonny Light to Maya. The campaign is the syllabus, one unit at a time: hydroskimming; the vacuum unit and the FCC; sulphur (Urals); octane (isomerisation); the hydrocracker and the hydrogen plant (Arab Heavy); alkylation; the coker, visbreaker and bitumen (Maya); renewables (HVO, ethanol and FAME bought in, with the ticket value); and full complexity with the crude yours to choose. Yields, properties and prices are textbook-typical and labelled as such. Routing is saved per level, with bests and completion in the menu. `refinery-data.js`, `refinery-engine.js`, `refinery-levels.js`; `tests/refinery.mjs` checks the data, that par beats the default on every level, and the chemistry.

The page fits the screen: the level in two lines (its story behind About), a compact margin card, the units in one row that scrolls sideways, and Streams or Pools in two tabs taking the rest and scrolling inside; the Pools tab says how the pools stand ("Pools · 1 off spec" in red), so you see it while routing.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the streams take the left and the brief, the margin and the units stand beside them; on a screen 1000px across or more the pools stand between the two and the tabs go, so a routing and its result are seen together (both lists are drawn every time, so showing both needs no script).

Together (October 2026): the same level for both of you, the same crude (or the same choice of crudes), units and prices, from the same starting routing, each routing on their own phone (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet; the header's level deals a new match on it. Par shows as ever; the hint doesn't. Submit is your answer, asked once and final: the highest margin wins, then the quicker. The others show under the margin bar, routing or submitted at their margin. Your own routings, bests and stars stay as they were. A party doesn't offer it (October 2026): a long, quiet game makes a poor round at a table of friends.

## Deck
The pile, reviewed: what you didn't know, brought back until you do. Every knowledge game records an item when you show you don't know it: Punt when you answer wrong, pass, or stake 20% or less; Quote when the truth is outside your market or the market is wider than three ranges; Chart when a pin is over 500 km off (unless it's within 600 km inside the right country, as Deck counts it) or you took a clue; Crates when a clue's crate is got wrong (through its own learn deck); Gambit's puzzle rush when you miss a puzzle. Items move through six piles with growing gaps, Leitner's boxes on Pimsleur's clock: ultra-short (due at once), short (10 minutes), medium (12 hours), long (a week), longer (a month), longest (three months); right in the longest and it's learned. Chess puzzles skip the short-term piles: a tactic seen ten minutes or twelve hours ago is still in mind, so they go ultra-short, then a week, then three months, then learned. (Until October 2026 the week was the last pile; what had been learned by then came back once, a month after it was learned, so nothing skips the long checks.) Right moves an item up a pile, in Deck or in the game that banked it (a confident right answer in Punt, a tight quote, a clean pin, a solved puzzle); wrong drops it to the first; right in the long pile and it's learned. Deck shows the piles and what's due per game, and a review asks each due item back in its own form: Punt's questions as options (drawings included), Crates' clues as which-country-or-commodity, Chart's places as which-country in the first pile and then on the map of their region (right within a share of the map's width that narrows up the piles, about 500, 310 and 190 km on Asia's map, or within 600 km anywhere inside the right country, since a lot of the countries are vast; a map whose region doesn't reach a place, as Asia's doesn't reach Norilsk, widens to take it in), Chart's countries on the map, anywhere inside the outline, Quote's numbers as a number judged in the question's own range, Gambit's puzzles on the board. Up to 40 a review, the most revised first: the highest pile first (what's lasted longest is what a lapse would cost most), the longest overdue first within a pile, so moving a new miss up from the ultra-short pile waits until every longer check is done. The games' learning modes deal what's due in the same order.

Learning mode, on by default and switchable from Deck's, Punt's, Quote's or Chart's menu: Punt leads each block of a bank level with what's due (up to half the block, spread through it) and fills the rest with questions you haven't seen before any you have; Quote and Chart lead their sets the same way. Off, the games deal as they always did and still bank what you miss. Crates keeps its own learn mode, which feeds the pile. `pile.js` is the store and scheduler; `tests/pile.mjs` checks promotion, demotion, gaps and learning.

No clicks needed. A typed card (Quote's numbers) has its field ready to type in, and the field stays focused from one typed card to the next (on a phone the keyboard stays up): type, Enter to answer, then either Enter to move on or simply type the next answer, which starts the next card with it. On a card with options, 1 to 9 pick one and Enter moves on.

Parley's cards say their word as they appear, in the course's own language, with a loudspeaker beside it to hear it again. The voice is the phone's own (`voice.js`, shared with Parley). Browsers let a page speak only after a tap, and Review is that tap, so even the first card is heard; the first touch anywhere also primes the voice with a silent word.

No card can be learned by its position: a card's options come in a new order every deal ("all of the above" and its kind stay last), and a Punt card whose options come from Crates' bank gets fresh wrong answers each time (other countries or commodities of the kind; other clues the answer doesn't carry). A Chart place is a which-country card only in the first pile; from the second pile on it's plotted: tap where it is on the map of its region, right within a share of the map's width that narrows as the card climbs (a 25th, a 40th, a 60th: on Asia's map about 500, 310 and 190 km), with the distance and the true spot shown after.

Language words come in a block per language (Chinese together, French together), where that language's first due word would have come, and are shuffled within the block rather than kept in chapter order; a chapter is Parley's to drill.

The map cards move: drag with a finger or the mouse, pinch, scroll the wheel or press + and − to zoom around the pointer; a tap that barely moved places the pin. The tolerance is set by the first view, so zooming in to aim doesn't tighten it, and the reveal redraws as you move the map.

The pile keeps each question as it was when it went in. Deck refreshes Punt's (October 2026) from their banks when it opens, so a question rewritten since (a giveaway fixed, its maths typeset, its diagram redrawn) comes back rewritten; its place in the pile and when it's due are untouched (pile.refresh), and a question gone from its bank keeps what it stored.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), each screen's halves stand side by side: what's due and the piles on the left, the lists and Review beside them; a review's question and picture on the left, its answers beside them.

## Parley
Three beginner courses from an English speaker's side: Chinese, French and German, on the pile's schedule. A course is units of about 14 words, each unit opening with its grammar pattern (three genders and *sein*; the accusative *den*; verb second; the partitive *du / de la*; *aller* and *au*; measure words and 块; 在 and 去; 了) and closing, once all its words are met, with a short text written only from words learned so far, read aloud by the phone, with three questions. Every word card carries the word with its article (French and German) or its characters and pinyin with tone marks (Chinese), the meaning, an example sentence, and a button that speaks it; a new word is shown on its card, spoken, and drilled at once. Study brings what's due first, then up to 12 new words a day, unit by unit. Every unit is open: Learn on its row teaches all of its words not yet met, whatever the day's count (choosing a unit is choosing the pace), its pattern opens from the row any time, and Read appears once its words are all met; a reading reached by jumping ahead says it may use words from units not yet finished.

A word is drilled harder as it climbs the piles: recognise it (word and voice, pick the meaning); pick it by ear (voice only), or for Chinese name the tone of its first syllable; produce it (type the word, accent-tolerant, and tap its article for a gendered noun; for Chinese type the pinyin with tones, ni3 hao3 or nǐ hǎo, and the sounds right but the tones wrong is a miss with a note); fill it into its sentence from four choices. The right answer sits anywhere among the choices, and no wrong one means the same or sounds the same (a Chinese homophone can't be told apart by ear). Right moves it up a pile, wrong drops it to the first, as everywhere; Deck reviews Parley's cards too. Six units each for now: hello, numbers and time, eating and drinking, places and going, shopping, work and trade (oil, prices, the boss). `parley-courses.js` is the content, written by hand and meant to grow; the German course is for a learner from English.

The page fits the screen: the units list, a drill's card and a reading (its passage and questions together) scroll inside themselves. The voice lives in `voice.js`, which Deck uses too when it reviews Parley's words.

A study session is kept as it goes (October 2026): a reload, or another device on your solo code, opens the card you were on, and a card already answered comes back with its verdict and Next, its answers locked so it can't count twice. A partner watching sees the same card (it used to see your home screen), silently. Changing course ends the session.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the course and Study stand on the left and the units beside them; a card on the left and its answers beside it; the reader keeps to a reading line's width.

## Brut
Wine. The name is the driest common Champagne and the French for crude oil (*le brut*).

Blind: a flight of six classic wines (39 in all, from Chablis and Marlborough Sauvignon Blanc to Barolo, Amarone, Vintage Port and Dézaley Chasselas), each with what a taster finds at each stage. You see it first: the colour in a drawn glass (bubbles rising for sparkling wines) and the words for it; taste further for the nose, then the palate (sweetness, acidity, tannin, alcohol, body and finish as dots), then the conclusions (Old or New World, climate, oak). Name it from six candidates, drawn from the same colour by likeness: the same grape from elsewhere, then the wines nearest on the palate. A right call pays 100 on sight, 70 on the nose, 45 on the palate, 25 on the conclusions, 15 off for each wrong call; the reveal gives the tell ("Pale in the glass but huge in tannin and acid, tar and roses: Nebbiolo"). A new flight or today's flight (the same for everyone); best flights kept; grapes on the candidates can be hidden from the menu for a harder flight. A wine you couldn't name before the palate goes to the pile.

Study: the wine bank (`wine-bank.js`), 162 cards in twelve units: grapes; Bordeaux (the banks, 1855, the first growths, Saint-Émilion's 2022 reshuffle, Sauternes); Burgundy and Beaujolais; Champagne and sparkling (the method, the sweetness scale, the houses); the Rhône, Loire, Alsace and Provence; Italy; Spain and Portugal (Rioja's ageing, sherry, Port, Madeira); Germany, Austria and Switzerland (Prädikat, Franken, the Wachau, Chasselas, Lavaux, the Valais, Geneva's Satigny); the New World; making wine; service, pairing and faults; the trade (en primeur, the Place de Bordeaux, Liv-ex, in bond, the Lafite bubble, grape marc in Annex IX). A new card shows the fact and then asks a question on it; due cards and missed wines come first; up to 15 new a day. The same cards are Punt's Wine level. `brut-data.js` holds the wines, `brut-engine.js` the flight, candidates and points; `tests/brut.mjs` checks them.

The page fits the screen: in a tasting the glass and its note take the room left (a long note scrolls inside, so the options never move); the units list and a study card scroll inside themselves.

Every game's header dropdown (the level, mode, size or course) is the suite's own, drawn by `dropdown.js` in the game's logo colours rather than the phone's plain list: Crates, Punt, Slate, Delta, Survey, Parley, Brut, Blend and Refinery (its crude picker too). (Rush had one for its clock, until its clocks went into the menu as every rush's are.)

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the glass, larger, and its notes stand on the left and your guess beside them, the buttons low; in study, the course and its units, and a card and its answers, stand side by side.

Together (October 2026): a blind flight for both of you, the same six wines in the same order with the same candidates, grapes shown or not as the one who dealt it had them (the menu's setting deals the next match), each tasting and calling on their own phone (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet. The others' scores and which wine they're on show beside yours. The highest total wins, then the quicker; the result lists your flight with each wine's tell. A miss goes to the pile as in any flight; your own flight, bests and today's stay as they were. A party doesn't offer it (October 2026): a long, quiet game makes a poor round at a table of friends.

## Harbour
Plan, then run. A harbour of hexes, ships you place where you like, and a program for each: one instruction per hour. Ships steer as ships do, relative to where they're heading: ahead; port, a 60° turn to the left and a hex on; starboard, the same to the right; astern, a hex back still facing the same way; and load, discharge, wait. A ship can't turn on the spot, so jetties sit on the channels round islands, and a dead end means backing out. A program written that way runs the same turned to any of the six directions, or mirrored with port and starboard swapped, so a group of ships and their programs can later be reused facing another way. Every program loops, the longest setting the loop for all of them, and they run in lockstep, so the whole plan is a machine you watch go round. A ship that would end on land or off the map runs aground; two that would end on one hex, or pass through each other, collide; either ends the run. A ship may follow another onto the hex it's leaving. Ships come in classes, the same in every level: a coaster holds 4 and hires at $20k, a Handy holds 10 for $30k (cheaper for what it carries); each level's fleet offers so many of each, shown under the map. The sizes aren't multiples of each other on purpose: a ship of N units blends in steps of 1/N (a coaster in quarters, a Handy in tenths), so a spec can suit one and not the other, and a fleet of both reaches totals neither does alone. Targets count units delivered. A jetty works a parcel an hour, in full or not at all, and one that can't happen does nothing: a load needs the parcel on hand and room for it aboard; a discharge takes the jetty's parcel out of the ship's blend, every product in its share, and needs at least that much aboard and the blend on spec (a refused cargo stays aboard, and the status line says why). What a discharge leaves stays aboard and blends into the next load. A refinery's tank fills at its rate up to what it holds, and the refinery waits when it's full. The levels to come build their logic on the rule that an impossible transfer is no transfer, the way Opus Magnum's machines rest on an arm that can grab nothing. The aim is Opus Magnum's depth over time: storage tanks ships both fill and draw from, plans tested against several runs of arrivals, ship-to-ship transfers and tides, stamped and reusable modules, and compliance tickets.

Any plan that delivers counts; tightening it is the second game. Four measures, each kept on its own: cost (ships hired, each at its class's rate, and product lifted at its price), hours (to the last delivery), water (every hex a ship was on) and instructions (each written instruction once, a loop's body once however often it plays, empty hours free). They pull against each other, so no plan is best at all four. Each level carries a par on each measure and the plans that reach it (found by searching every plan of up to three ships on each level's routes, from every starting point); `tests/harbour.mjs` runs those plans and holds the par there, so a rule change can't quietly make a level easier, harder or impossible. A par met is a drop (October 2026: what were a level's stars are drops now, the regions' diesel, below): a run's line marks each par it meets with a drop, and Menu shows your best beside the par, a drop after each one met. The plan that met a par is kept (under `harbour:v3:kept:` keys; a later run that does better on that measure replaces it), so a drop in Menu is a button: tap it to watch the plan that earned it. Short of a par, a level keeps only your plan as you left it. A drop earned before plans were kept is kept from your plan as it stands, if it still meets that par. Tap a par to watch the plan that reaches it. A plan watched, yours or the par's, takes your plan's place until Back to yours, runs and steps like yours, and you can pick and copy from it to paste into your own, but not change it, and running it keeps no bests; Use this plan makes it yours. The plans live with the levels in `harbour-levels.js`, written compactly (one character an hour, a loop as its count and body in brackets: `(2S)`), and the tests run those same plans.

The campaign comes in chapters, and a new player meets it a piece at a time (`harbour-campaign.js`, pure: the page tells it what's finished). Opening Harbour the first time puts you on First cargo, the picker showing the Baltic's chapter alone, every step after the first greyed with why ("Finish Up the creek"). A finish opens the next step, and a banner in the brief's place says so with a button to go there ("Up the creek is open. Go to Up the creek"). Rundown finished opens the first region, the Baltic. North-West Europe opens when the Baltic reaches its long-term contracts, which need more diesel than the plant and ORLEN can bring (so you build a terminal in ARA, and its harbours come first: First blend, then Two refineries); Two refineries opens ARA, the world and Trickle, with Blend wall and Heels after Trickle; the Straits open when the Baltic runs on its own. Menu's Continue goes to the first open step not done, passing over a region that's waiting on another (the Baltic in its long-term season with no lane bringing it ARA's diesel). Menu lists each chapter reached, every step done (✓, a level's drops beside it), here, open or locked and why, and under them a glimpse of the next chapter and what opens it. The campaign's progress lives under `harbour:v3:` keys: everyone started fresh when it came in, earlier plans and bests left unread, and Menu's Start Harbour over clears them all (Start this region over clears one region). The cheat is the par: on a level, tap a par in Menu and Use this plan makes it yours (one edit, so Undo takes it back); in a region, tap the engine's par to watch the par run week by week, and Use this week makes the region the par's as it stands that week, its moves made, ready to play (the other regions and the lanes stay yours).

A region is a season board (`harbour-season.js`, the rules, pure; `harbour-season-ui.js`, the screen): one picture of the region, not to scale (the sea, your terminal and its tank, the plant or refineries feeding it, the towns with a bubble of the drops they want this week and a face for how last week went, the roads, rails and rivers with your vehicles on them, the ships at sea), this week's three offers, and Play the week. A tall screen shows more of the sea. Every week you pick one offer (a truck, a train or a barge into the yard, more tank rented, a supply ship hired, or a cheap cargo now), tap a town to send it vehicles from the yard or call them back, and play the week: supply comes into the terminal first, as much as its tank holds (the rest is turned away and not paid for), then contracts are served, then the lane is loaded, then spot orders, each town getting what its vehicles carry and the tank holds. A week with every town supplied in full earns a star; a contract left short costs one, and 2 a drop. Stars win contracts: a town with one flies a flag (★2 deal), filled when your stars reach it (and, in the Baltic, what else it wants is there), and its sheet has Take it. A season is four weeks: spot orders, then medium contracts (spot orders half as big), then long-term ones, bigger and dearer to keep; an event comes once in a region's life (Tallinn's holiday rush, ice in the gulf halving ORLEN's ship, the plant's turnaround; the Rhine's low water halving barges, a refinery tripping). A region runs on its own, solved, when all its long-term contracts are held and kept on what comes in each week, not on stock that will run out. The Baltic's contracts want more than stars (October 2026): its medium contracts want 6 of its harbours' 12 drops (2 of 4 a harbour on average) or ARA's diesel by the world's lane, and its long-term ones 8 (2.5 a harbour) and the lane both, however much else comes in. So a player with few drops takes the medium contracts once ARA's lane runs, and finishing the Baltic takes ARA and the puzzles both. A contract's button says what it still needs ("Needs 6 drops or ARA", "Needs ARA and 8 drops"), its town's sheet says what its contracts want and what the Baltic has, and its flag fills only when it can be taken. Running on its own also wants the lane's cargo landing that week; a week that would have done it otherwise says what it's short of. Its engine is what it then makes a week (the next four weeks averaged, as everything stands), against a par.

A region's score is its net a week (October 2026): the header shows last week's net, what the week took in less what it bought, hire, rent and penalties, and not a balance that grew with every week played, which rewarded simply playing on. Tank beyond the terminal's own 12 is rented, 3 a week for each 6 more, so room pays only while it's used: everything comes into the terminal before it goes out, so a terminal wants room for a week's throughput and no more, and room beyond that is rent and stock bought only to sit in it (a free tank was once worth picking every week). The terminal's sheet shows the room and its rent and gives a tank back, when what's in the terminal still fits without it; a vehicle left in the yard costs nothing, so a week whose offers add nothing can take one rather than a tank.

The harbours feed the regions, a drop a week for every drop earned in them (October 2026; it was the pace of one harbour's fastest plan, which was hard to read): every par met in the Baltic's three harbours (First cargo, Up the creek, Rundown) is a drop a week from the plant into Muuga, twelve at most, and every par met in North-West Europe's five a drop a week from the refineries into Maasvlakte (eight at most when ARA opens, after First blend and Two refineries; Trickle, Blend wall and Heels add theirs as they're played, twenty in all). The Straits' drops wait for their own region. That's the reason to go back to a puzzle: on eight drops a week the Baltic's first season ends with ★3, on six with ★2, a star short of Rakvere's contract. The terminal's sheet lists its harbours with their drops and a button to each with pars still to meet, and a run that earns a drop says what it now feeds. With eleven of its twelve drops the plant and ORLEN alone would bring the 19 a week its long-term contracts want, which is why the Baltic asks for ARA's lane outright. The Baltic: Muuga holds 12 (6 more for each tank rented); Tallinn and Rakvere by road (a truck carries 6 and 4 a week, hiring at 1.5), Tartu by road (2) or rail (a train, 6 a week at 2.5); ORLEN's ship from Klaipėda, 8 a week at 2.1–3.2 a drop and 4 to hire; medium contracts 6, 4 and 3 a week (★2, ★2, ★3), long-term 9, 6 and 4 (★5, ★5, ★6). ARA: Maasvlakte holds 12; Rotterdam by road, Antwerp by road or barge, Duisburg by barge up the Rhine (a barge 6 or 8 a week at 2); the US Gulf's ships from Houston, 10 a week each, two at most; long-term contracts 6, 8 and 4 a week.
