Every region runs on the company's clock: a week played in one plays every region you've opened, importers first, so a cargo lands before the next one loads, and a solved region earns while you play elsewhere. The world (`harbour-world-ui.js`) is a chart of the oceans with your regions lit, the regions still to come marked (the Med, West Africa, the US Gulf, Brazil, India, Singapore), and the lanes between yours: ARA to the Baltic, up the North Sea, round Skagen and through the Sound. Its MRs (medium-range tankers, 8 drops a week each, 3 a week to hire, two at most) load ARA's diesel after its contracts are served and land it at Muuga the week after, booked at 2.5 a drop; a cargo that doesn't fit Muuga's tank waits aboard. With the lane running, the Baltic's long-term contracts can be kept, and the Baltic is solved; so is ARA, its lane counted as one more promise. Each region's card shows its season, stars and last week's net, or its engine once solved. The par run is the intended path, 22 company weeks played by a player with eight of the Baltic's twelve drops and seven of the eight First blend and Two refineries offer (the Baltic's two seasons alone, then ARA from the week it opens, the lane, both regions solved, each engine tidied), renting two tanks in each region, the room that makes most: the Baltic is solved in its 14th week, ARA in its 13th, engines 25.1 and 21.3 a week. `tests/harbour.mjs` holds the par run to those numbers, checks what the Baltic's contracts want (six drops or the lane for a medium one, the lane and eight drops for a long-term one, stars first), that without the lane even all twelve drops can't take its long-term contracts, nor seven drops with it, while eight take all three and it runs on its own, and that a week kept with no lane cargo landing, or on seven drops, isn't solved and says why; that the par's room is the best size in both regions (a tank fewer or more makes less a week, once its stock has settled); that rented tanks cost their rent and go back only when what's in the terminal fits; and the rules case by case and the campaign's gates.

The levels, chapter by chapter. The Baltic, learning to move: Level 1, First cargo: sixteen units from the refinery's jetty (L) to the customer's (D), four coaster loads, round an island. One coaster is cheapest ($20k, 43 hours); four, spaced so none reaches a jetty another is still at, are fastest (par 13 hours). Level 2, Up the creek: the refinery's jetty (L) is at the head of a creek too narrow to turn in, so a ship backs out (Astern keeps its heading), and the customer (D) is on the shore straight on from it. One coaster shuttling, ahead to D and astern home, never turns at all: cheapest, least water, fewest instructions (par $20k, 7 tiles, 4 instructions). Faster are two coasters on that same line: each backs from the refinery down the shore to the customer and comes home ahead, ducking into the ring at the creek's mouth for an hour (starboard, astern, port) while the other backs past (par 37 hours, found by the level lab); the creek holds one ship at a time, so a third only gets in the way. Level 3, Rundown (a tank's rhythm: two ships taking turns): the refinery's tank fills a unit an hour and holds ten, and both jetties pump two units an hour; twenty-four units within forty hours. No ship can do it alone (the best is 49 hours): the cheapest answer is two coasters on one loop, half a turn apart, taking turns at the tank, one lifting while the other delivers ($40k, 39 hours, the cheapest of 174 two-ship fleets that make it); three coasters are quickest (par 31 hours). North-West Europe, learning to trade and blend: Level 4, First blend: B20, the customer taking two units an hour with at least 20% FAME; gasoil (G) lifts two at $1k a unit, FAME (F) one at $5k; twenty units. A coaster's four can't be 20% FAME with those lifts (its best on spec is half FAME); a Handy's ten can, exactly: eight gasoil and two FAME, five parcels on spec. So the bigger ship is the cheaper plan (par $66k): the size that suits the spec blends to it without giveaway. A coaster's richest cheap try, two gasoil and one FAME with its heel topped up every lap, is on spec but gives away FAME ($69k); FAME is priced at $5k so that the giveaway outweighs the coaster's lower hire (at $3k, the level lab found, the coaster won). Level 5, Two refineries: the near refinery (N) is closing with 12 units left in its tank, the far one (F) makes two an hour, and forty units are wanted within 100 hours. The long haul wants the Handy and the near lap a coaster: the other way round takes 111 hours, the Handy alone 117, two coasters 122 (par $50k; three ships, 56 hours). The expert's coaster does a near lap and a far run every loop, and keeps earning after the near stock runs dry. Level 6, Trickle (found by the level lab): two refineries, G beside the customer (D) selling oil at $1k a unit that trickles into its tank half a unit an hour, one a lift, and L down the channel at $2k, four a lift; the customer takes four an hour; thirty units. Every measure is won a different way. One coaster that never leaves G and D, two hexes side by side, is cheapest (par $52k, 2 tiles). A Handy parked at the trickle takes four instructions. The fastest plan pairs a coaster working the trickle with the Handy hauling dear oil from L (par 35 hours), and no fleet of three or four does better. Level 7, Blend wall: E10, at most 10% ethanol; gasoline (G) lifts three at $2k a unit, and ethanol (E), up a creek, one a lift, its tickets paying $4k a unit. Pure gasoline is the easy plan and keeps the hours, water and instructions pars; the cheapest is a Handy carrying exactly one ethanol and nine gasoline, by way of the creek (par $72k): cheap bio costs time. Level 8, Heels: two customers, A pure diesel and B at least 30% FAME, twelve units each (a customer can set its own target, and a plan must meet both). A coaster for each grade works ($76k); a Handy delivering six pure to A, then blending the four left aboard up with two FAME for B, is cheapest (par $62k, 41 hours). The other way round, the FAME left aboard is refused at A. The Straits, mastering timing: Level 9, Roundabout (found by the level lab): a ring round a one-hex island, both berths dead ends off it, the refinery's (L) lifting two an hour and the customer's (D) taking four; sixteen units. One coaster takes 50 hours, its cheapest plan backing out of each berth with a duck on the way (par $20k, 10 instructions). Two take 27. The golden plan is a carousel: three coasters on one route, five hours apart, each backing out of a berth and swinging round the island the same way, one holding an hour so the gaps fit, a berth coming free just as the next ship comes in (par 23 hours; 196 of the 5.8 million three-ship fleets that fit reach it). Level 10, Queue (drawn in the level lab): the refinery (L) fills a ship a unit an hour, the customer (D) takes four; one channel between them with room to pass halfway and a little basin at each end; twenty-four units. One coaster takes 146 hours, two 70. The golden plan is three ships on three routes, queueing in the basins while the slow berth works, ducking aside and passing at the wide spot (par 58 hours; 2 of the 2,164 three-ship fleets that fit reach it); a fourth can't help. Level 11, Passing places (drawn in the level lab): one channel between the refinery (L) and the customer (D), both two units an hour, with three wider stretches; twelve units. One coaster shuttles, astern one way and ahead the other (47 hours); two take 30; three take 25, each in turn ducking aside into a wide stretch and holding four hours while the others go by (par 25 hours; 8 of the 12,110 three-ship fleets that fit reach it). Level 12, Lockstep (drawn in the level lab): one straight channel, the refinery (L) at one end and the customer (D) at the other, a hex to spare past each; twelve units. One coaster shuttles in 42 hours. Two can't pass anywhere, so they move as a pair: at each end one works the jetty while the other waits on the spare hex beside it, then both step together so the second gets its turn (par 35 hours; 8 of the 160 two-ship fleets that fit reach it). A third has nowhere to step. Fewest instructions is a coaster hauling pure FAME and never visiting the gasoil jetty: dear and slow, and seven instructions.

The harbour is drawn in 3D (`harbour-3d.js`, with three.js kept in `vendor/` like every other file, so it plays offline): hex tiles, shore tanks that fill, little ships carrying their cargo in its colours, gliding from hour to hour. It wears the suite's theme: Almanac a chart in brass and navy, Modern an architect's white model, Kontor an inked drawing office (at night a green trace on black), Office flat light greys and Fluent's blue (at night charcoal and navy), each by day and by night, and at night windows and jetty lamps are lit and ships show their sidelights. Where a device can't draw in 3D, or when Menu says so, the harbour is drawn flat from above (`harbour-flat.js`); both answer the same calls, so the game doesn't know which it has. Under the map is the fleet: tap a class to make the next ship that class, or, with a ship picked, to swap it to that class if one's left. Tap water to put a ship there, facing east; tap it again to turn it 60° to starboard; drag it to move it, onto land to scrap it. Pick an instruction from the tools and tap a program's hours to paint it (the same instruction again clears an hour). The square at the left of the tools switches to picking: tap an hour, then another, and everything between is picked, across ships too (a ship's number picks its whole row; with a mouse, drag or shift-click). Then Copy and Paste (pasting goes in before the picked hour, shifting the rest later), Insert empty hours, Delete, Loop, Back (copies the picked hours reversed with port and starboard swapped: the way home, once the ship has turned round), and the two arrows, which shift the picked rows an hour earlier or later inside the loop and keep going while held. A whole route pasted into an empty ship's row at hour h becomes the same loop h − 1 hours later, so a second ship on the route is one paste.

A loop plays its hours more than once. Loop makes the picked hours play twice; tap the count (×2) for one more pass, hold it (or right-click) for one fewer. Hold an instruction (right-click, with a mouse) and it becomes a loop of its own, that hour played twice. The tape still shows every hour, the body boxed and its later passes faded, so the timing between ships stays in view; painting a faded hour paints the body, and deleting faded hours takes passes away. Loops don't nest. The edits live in `harbour-tape.js` and are tested in `tests/harbour.mjs`.

A tool can also be dragged onto the programs: onto an hour it takes that hour's place; onto the edge between two hours (an hour's outer quarters and the gap) it goes in there for that ship, the hours after it shifting later; onto the hour numbers between two hours it goes in for every ship, a new column squeezed in. A finger aims a little above itself (2.1rem, about a row of hours), so the mark showing where the tool would go (an outlined hour, or a line between two) sits just over the fingertip rather than under it, and the tool floats a row higher still, clear of the mark: a line between two hours, a cell's width from the next, can only be placed if it can be seen (October 2026). A mouse aims with its tip. Each drop is one edit for Undo, and a tap still just picks the tool. The pen in the tools draws routes: with it on, drag on the map and the picked ship's route goes on from the dot where its program leaves it, an hour a hex (Ahead, Port, Starboard or Astern, whichever reaches it; a hex that would need a 120° turn is skipped, so draw a smoother line, and going back over the last hex takes that step off), added to its program on release, one edit for Undo. While you plan, every ship's route over a loop is drawn on the water from its own program (a ship never decides, so its route doesn't depend on any other ship). Under the tools: Undo and Redo, back to the start, one hour, run or pause, and 1×, 4× or 16× speed. A run keeps every hour it has played: the scrubber above the controls goes back to any of them and forward again as far as you've watched, B steps back an hour, and a crash is marked in both ships' programs at its hour; tap it for the hour before, where the status line says what's coming. Sound is made on the device (a horn leaving a berth, the pump, a chime for a delivery, a thud for a refusal, the sea under a run), and Menu turns it off. Any edit goes back to the start. On a computer, Ctrl or Cmd with C, X, V, A and Z copy, cut, paste, pick everything and undo; Delete deletes the picked hours, Escape stops picking; space runs and pauses, S steps, R goes back. Each level keeps its own plan and bests on the device, under `harbour:v3:` keys. Harbour took Survey's place among the first tiles on the games screen.

A game stands still while you're in its menu, its rules or the games screen (`menu.js`: `onPause(pause, resume)`, resume told how long it stood): Crates', Slate's and Delta's board clocks stop, a puzzle rush's clock stops and its start moves on by the pause, Pipes' oil holds where it is, Spot freezes, a running Harbour stops and runs on after, and Cartel's computer waits with its next move. A duo match's shared clock doesn't stop.

Flags are written as emoji (🇮🇪 is two "regional indicator" letters), and some systems, Windows above all, have no flag pictures and show the letters ("IE"). `flags.js` checks on every page whether the system draws a flag; where it doesn't, a font of just the flags (Twemoji Country Flags, MIT, 78 KB in `fonts/`) goes in front of the page's own font for the flag letters only. Systems that draw flags, like the iPhone, keep their own.

Vision, settled. The campaign is a chart of the world's real supply chains, as global and exotic as they come, not a list of regions. It starts with micro-puzzles (one harbour, one rhythm, one clever timing) and grows by layers, each built from the one below: a chain (harbours linked by voyages, with a plant converting feedstock between them), a network (chains sharing ships, terminals and contracts), the world (everything running for a month, each solved harbour a lit diorama on the chart). The rules stay the same at every scale (hours in a harbour, days at sea). Each layer brings one idea from real trade, taught first as a micro-puzzle: aggregation of many small origins (CNSL from West Africa and India, UCO from China and Southeast Asia), conversion with yields (HVO plants, cardanol), laycans (date windows to load), seasons (the Brazilian cane crush, the monsoon, low water on the Rhine at Kaub), decay in transit (LNG boil-off), production when the wind blows (RFNBOs, e-methanol: where seeded randomness comes in), certification (segregated against mass balance), a product's value set by where it's delivered (THG quota, RTFCs, RINs, LCFS credits: arbitrage as routing), and chokepoints with timing (Suez convoys passing in the Great Bitter Lake, Panama locks and drought drafts, the Bosporus's one-way windows). Design rules, settled. Ships never decide: every program plays exactly as written, hour by hour, whatever happens around it, and the only thing that depends on the harbour is whether a transfer happens. That keeps a plan readable as clockwork and keeps the timing puzzles alive (Rundown is timing ships to a tank). Conditions come later, and only with randomness, when they're needed: as equipment placed in the harbour and paid for, never as a free instruction. Lighthouses, gates, meters and signals that hold ships or plant until something is so, every hour of holding costing demurrage. Pure plans stay possible beside them, and the measures choose between them. Randomness, when it comes, is seeded: a level runs a fixed set of test runs, so every plan replays identically and every score can be verified.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the harbour (or the region) takes the left, all the height under the header, and the brief, the fleet, the programs and the controls stand beside it, the run's buttons at the bottom.
## The games screen
The app is Almanac, and it opens here: the site's front page (`index.html`, `home.js`) is the screen of games with no game open and nothing to close back to. Every game is a page of its own; Crates, which used to be the front page, is at `crates.html`, and a link made for it back then (a shared board, a room, a run, a pool: `?b=`, `?room=`, `?run=`, `?cat=`) is forwarded there. A link carrying codes (`?solo=`, `?duo=`) stays home and takes them on.

Over it, the sextant and ALMANAC on the same graduated rule as a game's header. Under that, the head is one line: A one-person icon with your **solo** code (8 letters: every device with it is in the same state in every game), a two-person icon with your **partner** code (4 letters), then what your partner is playing (tap it for the partner sheet); on the right Settings (in Almanac the rete, every game's menu button, in the same ring as refresh and ×, turning while Settings is open as a game's does while its menu is; a pair of sliders in the other themes), a refresh icon (load the newest version; it turns while it fetches) and an ×. Tapping a code copies this page's link with it: the solo link carries both codes (open it on another of your devices and it follows your games and your partner), the partner link only the partner code (your partner opens it and you're linked). Holding a code (or tapping one that isn't set) lets you type one: a code that already holds games or a pair is followed or joined, what's there winning; a code nobody has yet starts fresh, from what this device has now (a new partner code is an empty room), so you can pick your own, any letters (codes the app makes avoid I and O). Solo sync: each game's saved state, the review pile and your settings go up to Firebase a moment after they change (every change carries its time) and come down when a page opens or comes back into view, the newer winning key by key; a change from elsewhere to the game you have open reloads it once. Crates' run follows the same code through its own sync. `suite.js` does all of this, loaded on every page through `pwa.js`; the store lives under `crates/runs/` at the solo code moved on seven letters, so the database rules need no change.

The tiles, in four sections under small headings in the one list (October 2026, when the games passed forty: a phone showed fifteen at a time, and finding one meant scrolling past most of the rest): **Learning**, Crates, Punt, Quote, Origin, Order, Calibre, Chart, Deck, Arb, Blend, Refinery, Brut, Lexicon, Ledger and Parley; **Puzzles**, Slate, Delta, Spot, Manifest, Harbour, Pipes, Survey and Stow; **Board & card games**, Cartel, Hong, Tribute, River, Gambit, Boast, Big Two, Landlord, Banquet, Gammon, Trumps, Bones, Factor and Dandan; **Party**, Party itself, then each of its games with a tile of its own (Undercover, Who am I?, Echo, Decoy, Gauge, Fuse and Snap: each opens Party on that game, the page wearing its name and logo), Cue and Quest. Every game is in one section only (`sec` in `APPS`, `SECTIONS` in `apps.js`), so there's one place to look for it. They are headings in the one list, not tabs: a tab hides games behind a tap, as the "…" tile did until October 2026, when the eleven from Arb to Cue sat behind it in a popup with Settings (Settings is in the head now). Three across on a phone, four on an upright tablet, six on a screen held sideways. The screen itself never scrolls; the tiles do, in their own box under the head: on a phone a tile is 5.5rem high, so its logo and name keep their size, and on a tablet 5.4rem (they once shared a tablet's height so every game fitted it, which forty-odd games no longer can). The box's edge fades out where there are more tiles past it, top or bottom (`apps.js` marks which as it scrolls). Opening the screen from a game brings that game's tile into view, however far down the list it is.

Settings (at the top right, beside refresh) holds the theme, day or night, and, folded away, Start a game over (October 2026): tap a game, confirm, and every key it keeps goes (its levels, stars, saves and settings: Calibre's designs and progress, Harbour's campaign, a run under way), on this device and on every other with your solo code. A removal syncs like any change, carrying its time, so an older copy on another device can't bring the game back, and keys that only another device had are taken from the code and removed there too (offline, this device's go at once and their removals go up when it's back). The game open in the page starts again at once; until the page reloads nothing of the reset game can be written again, so a page still showing it can't save it back (`resetGame` in `suite.js`). Not listed: Crates, whose run is synced on its own and which resets its learning in its own Settings; Deck, whose pile is every game's misses; and Ledger and Lexicon, the records of the others. A game's keys are its own prefix, and the other names some keep: Slate's `glyph:` and `slate:`, Blend's `blend:` and `blend2:`.

A logo is the object at the heart of its game, so no two share a frame, drawn at the level of the instruments (the rete, the sextant): navy, brass, parchment and the game's enamel, edged twice (a navy contour with a brass line inside it) so it holds on the page and on the dial, its detail engraved in brass. Crates a slatted crate, its frame and brace darker, nailed in brass; Hong a mahjong tile showing the red dragon 中, ivory on its green back, a face-down one behind it; Tribute two cards fanned, a cobalt back and a heart under a brass crown; Stow a hull in section, its hold half full of cargo, an L-shaped piece swung in on the crane's hook to fill the gap that clears both rows; Cue a card held up, its word a bar of raspberry enamel, a brass burst behind it where it popped up; Party a speaking trumpet in brass, its sound in turquoise enamel and a little confetti, the phone in the middle of the table that runs the game and speaks for it; Slate a certificate of analysis with its table of results and a brass seal on slate ribbons; Delta a riveted hex plate with a verdigris inlay and the delta; Punt a racing-green casino chip, its edge inserts, a brass ring and an engraved P; Cartel a claret die showing five with a navy one behind; Spot a target dial, brass ticks round a vermilion ring; Quote a price tag on its string holding [•] (the market around the truth), a scale along its foot; Manifest a stack of corrugated containers, isometric; Chart a cobalt pin whose head is a globe; Harbour a tanker from above, the domes of its tanks along the deck, the manifold across it, bridge and funnel aft; Survey a block of rock with a cube of gold showing at its front corner; Blend a stoppered flask, olive inside, its meniscus and graduations in brass; Pipes a copper elbow with bolted flanges and a pressure gauge, the flow running through; Refinery a distillation column with its trays, side draws and ladder; Deck three cards fanned, the back one enamelled, the front one due for review; Parley A and 文 in two speech bubbles; Brut a gilt-rimmed glass of bordeaux; River a xiangqi disc on its brass rim, the general 帥 in cinnabar on ivory (Kontor's line icon the disc with the river's waves across it); Gambit a pawn, the piece a gambit gives, ivory on a malachite base. Boast a crimson dice cup tipped over a die; Big Two two cards fanned, a violet back behind the two of spades; Landlord the landlord's cap over two cards, the front one starred; Banquet a hand throwing two fingers beside a cup of blue-and-white porcelain; Gammon the corner of a board in its walnut frame, a terracotta and a navy point, a checker of each colour and a die; Trumps three cards fanned, the front one the Under of acorns; Quest a heater shield, half ivory and half purple, a sword down its middle; Bones two dominoes, one standing on one lying; Dandan a playing card in a cobalt frame with a fish swimming across its face, the water ruled under it (Kontor's icon the same card in line). Party's games, in its turquoise: Undercover a face in dark glasses and a moustache under a fedora; Who am I? a head in profile with a card on its forehead, a question mark on it; Echo a speech bubble with one word in it and its echoes behind; Decoy a carved decoy duck; Gauge the dial with its target and needle; Fuse a bomb, its fuse alight; Snap two round cards, the star they share. In a game's header the logo is 40 pixels, as large as the rete opposite it.

On an upright tablet the screen keeps four tiles across (six made them tall and thin, their names cut) and sideways six, the whole width; on a tablet the logos and names grow into the bigger tiles (README, Bigger screens).

## Origin

A hidden country, found from clues. The clues come one at a time, the most obscure first (difficulty 3, then 2, then 1) and, within each, trade and commodities ahead of the rest; they are the knowledge base's own clue links (the ones Crates and Punt draw on), every one of a country's, dropping any that names the country or a place in it. Guess whenever you like, any country on Chart's map by any of its names, typed (the app's own suggestion list opens as you type: names that start with what you've typed first, arrows and Enter work) or tapped on the world map under the clues (Chart's outlines; drag to move it, with a mouse or a finger, and pinch, scroll the wheel or press + and − to zoom, around the pointer; the map moves by its own view rather than by scrolling, so there are no scrollbars; a country's name shows once it's big enough on screen; one tap names a country, a second guesses it; guessed countries take their warmth, the answer turns green): a wrong guess shows only how warm it is, as a colour on its chip and on the map, blue for the far side of the world through to red next door (a log scale of the distance between Chart's centroids: 300 km and nearer fully red, 12,000 km and further fully blue), never the distance or the direction, and turns up the next clue. A country scores 100, less 10 for each clue after the first and 5 for each wrong guess (at least 5); giving up scores nothing. Five countries a run, random or today's (the suite's day, the UTC date, the same five for everyone); the run counts the moment its last country is decided, and after a finished run, or from its tile, today's comes first while it's still to play. 135 of Chart's 168 countries qualify (at least six clues, Crates' clue links or plain links with a reason, once those that give the country away are dropped, and a place on the map); `tests/origin.mjs` checks every answer, and that no clue in a deal names its country. The countries, the clues and the scoring are `origin-engine.js`'s, pure; the page draws them and the map.

Topics (October 2026): a random run's clues come from your mix of Crates' country topics, the aspects of the clue links they are, each out, in or more, from a preset (Balanced, Trader, Culture night, Crates' own) or your own (README, The knowledge base). Within each difficulty a clue of a topic more comes first (a clue counts as its likeliest topic), and a clue only of topics out never comes, unless its country would be left with fewer than six; which countries come up doesn't change. Balanced deals exactly as Origin always did, and today's run is always Balanced. A run on a preset counts for your best; your own mix doesn't. At a table, a new game comes from the mix of whoever deals it.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the map takes the card's left side, all its height, and the clues, the guesses and the box to type in stand at the right, high, clear of a tablet's keyboard.

Together (October 2026): one run for the table, played as a team, from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six, each on their own phone (`together.js`). Every phone shows the same country, the same clues and the same guesses, and anyone guesses (typed, or tapped twice on their own map: the first tap names a country on that phone only), takes another clue, gives up (asked first) or moves on with Next; the score is the team's. Each guess carries its player's initial on their seat's colour (README, Rooms), the strip shows who's at the table the same way, yours ringed and anyone away greyed, and a decided country says who found it or gave up on it. The first tap counts: every change is a transaction made only if the table is still as the tapping phone showed it (the game, the country and, for a clue, the clues showing), so two Another clue taps at once turn up one clue, two Nexts move on one country, and a guess that lands after someone else's right one does nothing and says so ("Too late: Sarah found it"). Nobody waits for anybody, so a phone that sleeps or leaves holds nothing up. The end, on every phone, is the team's total, with Another game and Back to solo; the menu's Together part deals a new game for the table (asked first while one is under way) and says who's there. The pair's record gets a team result, the total, the moment the last country is decided (a win with all five found); a Party's game isn't the pair's. Your solo run, best and today's stay as they were, and a table in the address is joined under the rete, with no solo run dealt beneath it. It replaced, the same day, a head-to-head on `duel.js`, everyone racing the same five countries on their own phone: Korbi wanted it friendly, anyone guessing. `tests/origin.mjs` plays a table of three through the database's own round trip (guesses by several players, the races above, the record, a new game); three phones in one browser played it end to end, and a pair from the partner sheet's address.

## Order

Put things in order. A card is dealt and you tap the gap where it goes in your line, which starts with one card face up and grows a card at a time; a wrong place costs one of three lives and the card goes where it belongs, so the line is always right, values showing. Dates deal 16 of Quote's 434 dated questions (a painting by its name, with its picture on the card, fetched once per card, and a small one beside it in the line; the name is the knowledge base's, which the build gives the question, since a Wikipedia page's title can carry the year: Number 1, 1950 (Lavender Mist)); Quantities deal one family a run, Quote's questions of one unit (20 families: %, millions, metres, kg/m³, km, °C and more), so the comparison means something. Quote's Scale questions, softball Fermi questions for calibration, give Quantities families in hectares, litres, kWh, MW, kg, km and euros. Random or today's, a daily for each mode by the suite's day (the UTC date, the same deck for everyone); bests by mode. After a finished run, or from its tile, today's comes first while it's still to play. The cards and the rules are `order-engine.js`'s, pure; `tests/order.mjs` checks the families and plays runs through. Stories (October 2026) deal one story's events instead of numbers, and what's ordered is the story itself: Heracles' twelve labours as Apollodorus gives them, Odysseus's voyage home, the Trojan War, Ragnarök, the deeds of Perseus, Jason and the Argonauts, Rome's seven kings (kb/items/sequences.js, checked by `tests/stories.mjs`). A placed card shows its place, 1st, 5th, and the end of the run says which source the order follows. Picture cards say what happened to the picture, from the question: a painting was painted, a building completed.

Topics (October 2026): Dates and Quantities deal from your mix of Quote's categories, the ones each holds (Dates: Trade, Wine, Art, People and Markets; Quantities: all nine), each out, in or more (twice as often), from a preset or your own, in the menu for a random run (README, The knowledge base). Dates draws its sixteen cards by their topic's weight; Quantities draws its family by the mean weight of its cards, among the families with six cards or more in the topics in, and deals only those. Balanced deals exactly as Order always did, and today's run is always Balanced. A mix with fewer than eight dated cards in, or no family, deals from every topic and says so. A run on a preset counts for your best; your own mix doesn't. History's stories have no topics. At a table, a new game comes from the mix of whoever deals it.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the card in hand stands on the left, its picture large (asked of Wikipedia at 960 pixels there, rather than a phone's 500), and your line beside it, a column you scroll down.

Together (October 2026): one run for the table, played as a team, from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six, each on their own phone (`together.js`). Every phone shows the same card in hand and the same line, and whoever taps a gap first places it, for everyone: the transaction places it only if that card is still the one in hand on the line that phone showed, so a tap made a moment after someone else's does nothing and says so ("Too late: Sarah placed it"). The three lives are the team's, and a miss is told on every phone ("Tom missed: 1886 goes last"). Each card in the line has a dot in the colour of whoever placed it (README, Rooms; the first, dealt face up, none), and a row under the strip shows who's at the table in those colours (initials for five or six), anyone away greyed. Nobody waits for anybody, so a phone that sleeps or leaves holds nothing up. The menu's Together part deals a new game for the table in Dates, Quantities or History (asked first while one is under way), says who's there, and has Back to solo. The end, on every phone: the count placed together and each player's right and wrong, by seat, not ranked, with Another game and Back to solo. The pair's record gets a team result, the count placed (a win with the whole deck placed); a Party's game isn't the pair's. Your solo run, bests and today's stay as they were, and a table in the address is joined under the rete, with no solo run dealt beneath it. It replaced, the same day, a head-to-head on `duel.js`, everyone building their own line from the same deck: Korbi wanted it friendly, anyone placing. `tests/order.mjs` plays a table of three through the database's own round trip (a stale tap turned down, the shared lives, the tallies, the record, a new game); three phones in one browser played it end to end, and a pair from the partner sheet's address.

## Arb

On the games screen with every other game, in its one list that scrolls (it once sat behind a More button, with Survey, Blend, Refinery, Brut, the Lexicon and Ledger, while the main screen kept a fixed number of games).

From one thing to another through the knowledge base's links, as cheaply as you can. Every step costs 1 + log2 of the links of the thing you step onto: an obscure thing costs 1, the United States about 9, so the cheap route runs through what you specifically know rather than the hubs. Par is the cheapest route there is (Dijkstra over the 4,329 linked things); matching it scores 100, more spent scores in proportion. Each choice shows its cost and kind, every hop the reason the two are linked, and the end the cheapest route. Dead ends aren't offered: a link appears only if it leads somewhere new (a thing linked only to where you stand, or only back onto your route, would force a step back), the goal always does. Routes run between things with 2 to 25 links, three to five hops apart and of different kinds; five a run, random or today's. The graph and the routes are `arb-engine.js`'s, pure; `tests/arb.mjs` checks them.

Topics (October 2026): a random run's routes come from your mix of the knowledge base's topics, Crates' country and commodity topics in two grids, each out, in or more, from a preset (Balanced, Trader, Culture night) or your own (README, The knowledge base). A thing is about the topics of its links: a clue link's aspects; a typed link's (a painting's painter, museum, movement and style, a building's architect, a book's author and lines, a style's successor: Culture & arts; standing in a place: Geography); a thing linked by none of those, its kind's (a person People, a company Companies & brands, a god or a hero Culture & arts). A route's two ends are drawn by their likeliest topic's weight, as Crates weighs a word, so a thing about a topic more is twice as likely and a thing only of topics out never ends a route; the route between them runs through whatever links there are. Balanced deals the routes Arb always dealt, seed for seed, and today's run is always Balanced; a mix that leaves no route deals from every topic. A run on a preset counts for your best; your own mix doesn't. A match carries the dealer's mix, so every phone deals the same routes.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), your route stands on the left with room to show all of it, and the links to choose from (or how it went) beside it.

Together (October 2026): the same five routes for everyone at the table, each finding their own way on their own phone (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six. The others' scores and how many routes they've done show in the strip. The highest total wins, then the quicker; the match counts the moment your last route is decided. Your solo run, your best and today's stay as they were.

## Crossword
(Being built, October 2026: a crossword on the knowledge base, in Learning.)

## Lexicon

Everything the games know, looked up. Search any of the knowledge base's 4,396 things, a country, a company, a painting, a river, a law, by its name or another of its names, accents ignored, names that start with what you typed first and the best-connected first among them. An entry shows what the thing is (its kinds, and its region or commodity family), its picture where it has one, what's said about it (its note, its country and region, a painting's year), the numbers Quote knows about it, the topics its links touch (energy, metals, history…), and every link in groups (clues to it, a clue to, painted by, paintings, hangs in, on its walls, movement, in, within it), each with the reason and a tap through to its own entry. `#e=<id>` opens an entry, so links can be shared and the back button walks back through what you've opened.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the search and what it found stand on the left and the entry beside it, wider, so one thing after another can be opened from the list without losing it (the list is filled even when the page opens on an entry); with no entry open the list takes the whole width.

## Ledger
Is it working? Ledger is the record of how well you learn, kept from what the games already do. Recall: every item from the pile that comes back, in Deck or in a game, right or wrong, sorted by how long it was away (within the hour, two days, a week or two, a month, three months); away a week or more and still known is the headline, since that's what lasts, with a weekly line under it and the six piles' counts. Confidence: every bet in Punt, read backwards through Kelly's rule (staking a share f of the pot at decimal odds o, you act as if your chance were (1 + f(o − 1)) / o), plotted against how often those bets won, a dot a tenth, the diagonal the ideal; the average belief against the win rate says whether you stake surer than you are, the Brier score (the mean squared gap between that chance and what happened) sums it up (0 perfect, .250 always saying evens), and the win rate by stake size shows whether bigger stakes win more often. Markets: in Quote, how often the truth lay inside your bid and ask, and the grades. The games report as they settle, to `ledger-log.js` (the pile on every item that comes back, Punt on every bet, Quote on every market), which keeps weekly totals under `ledger:stats`, a few kilobytes however much you play, synced with the solo code like everything else; a game that feeds the pile is measured with no change of its own. As the games change the record can be started afresh (Menu, Settings) without touching the piles. Tested in `tests/ledger.mjs`: ISO weeks across year ends, the gaps, Kelly backwards, the totals, and the pile's once-only move of what was learned under the old four piles.

Knowledge, read two ways: each Punt bet also records the chance a pure guess had (one in the number of options; one in the ways to pick several right ones; none for a typed answer). A stake's Kelly chance less that, over what's left (the standard correction for guessing), is the share of the answer the stake claimed to know: a pass claims none, all in claims all, and at a fair price it's the stake itself. Your hit rate corrected the same way is what you knew (right half the time on four options is knowing a third). The Ledger sets the two side by side, overall and by stake, says whether your stakes rise with what you know, and counts only bets made since Punt began recording the guess's chance.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the cards stand side by side, as many across as fit.

## Stow
Drag the shapes into the hold; a line full from end to end clears. Three pieces at a time, each placed once and never turned (Block Blast's rule; on triangles, turning would change which way a cell points), a new three once all three are in. A level is won when every consignment (the red cargo stamped in brass) has gone out with a line, and lost when nothing in hand fits. Before you let go the hold shows what the piece would do: its cells, and the lines it would fill, lit. A piece is dragged from the tray (held above the finger so you can see where it goes, snapping to the nearest place it fits) or tapped and then put down with a tap in the hold.

Eight chapters, 72 levels: The hold (8×8, rows and columns), Bays (9×9, where each 3×3 bay clears too), Hexagons (a hexagon of 61 cells, lines in three directions), Triangles (hexagons of 54 and 96 triangles, lines in three directions, and a piece only fits where its triangles point the way they do), Odd holds (a hull in section, a deck round a hatch, a cross of decks, hexagons round a well, triangles round a hub: a line is every cell on it, a gap counting for nothing), and three of holds in decks, in 3D (below): Below decks, Hexagon decks and The ship. The pieces: squares up to five (every shape of four and under, the lines and big corners of five, the 2×3 and 3×3 blocks), hexagons up to four and triangles up to six, the compact shapes and the bars, never a crooked snake.

Holds in decks (October 2026). The last three chapters are holds several decks deep, drawn in 3D: Below decks (square decks of 5×5 and 6×6, two to four decks deep), Hexagon decks (decks of 61 hexagons, two and three deep) and The ship (a ship's decks seen from above, narrowing fore and aft, or to the bow, two to four deep). The crane lowers each piece: you choose where over the hold, and it goes down as one until a crate stops it, so cargo can't be slid in under cargo, and a piece resting on a stack can bridge a gap beside it. A line along a deck (a row or a column of it; in a hexagon deck, any of its three directions) full from end to end clears; then the hold settles, every crate dropping until it rests on another or the floor, a bridge's crates into the gap under them, and the settling can fill another line, which clears in turn (a chain, a wave at a time). The pieces are cubes, or hexagonal prisms on hexagon decks, never turned: every shape of three or fewer (upright ones too), and of four those lying flat in a shape the flat game deals, the columns, the 2×2 squares standing up and the corner of a cube (three arms from one cube), with now and then the 2×2×2 block; a twisted four (a screw) is hard to read in a tray and never dealt, and a level's hold is dealt only what fits it. Two things were tried and left out: columns from the floor to the hatch clearing as lines (topping up a consignment's column freed it in a piece or two, so no hold made a puzzle of it), and decks of triangles (prisms pointing both ways, which the eye can't sort out in 3D).

The hold is drawn by `stow-3d.js`: seen from above at a slant, orthographic (parallel lines stay parallel, so a deck's rows stay true), each crate the prism of its cell, drawn back to front by the depth of their centres (a true painter's order for cells of one lattice), only the faces the eye can see; the near walls are left off so the eye sees in, the far walls ruled a crate apart (on square decks) and marked at each deck, the rim in brass. A drag across the hold turns it round and tilts it, and the arrows in its lower corners (where the hold never reaches) turn it by an eighth; the view is kept (`stow:view`) and the tray's pieces are drawn in it, so a piece in hand looks as it will in the hold. Once a piece held over the hold has a place to go it's drawn there, among the crates, and everything the move would clear, every wave of the chain, is lit before you let go in the colour of the piece in hand (Block Blast's own cue; lit in brass as the flat holds are, it was lost against the crates' wood), each crate followed through the settling by a number of its own, so a crate the second wave clears is lit where it stands now. What the cargo hides shows through it, dashed: a consignment, a crate the move would clear, and where the piece would come to rest. Let go and the piece is lowered from over the hatch, then each wave goes out and the crates over it settle. Its solver judges a hold by consignments gone, lines with consignments nearly full, room, and no hollows (an empty cell under a crate) or stacks near the hatch; cargo is stacked as the crane would have left it, nothing floating, and consignments are taken from under other cargo first.

Every level is a puzzle with a par. Its trays come from its seed, the same every time and for everyone. `tools/stow-levels.mjs` makes each level from a recipe (the field, how much cargo, how many consignments, the sizes of piece it deals, the par wanted): cargo laid in clumps, never a full line, the consignments on different lines; then a beam search (the solver in `stow-engine.js`, keeping the best boards at every step, judged by consignments gone, lines with consignments nearly full, room to move and no walled-in single cells) finds the fewest pieces it can win in. A level is kept when its par lands in the recipe's range and the same solver keeping only one board, a careless player always taking the move that looks best now, does worse or gets stuck, so the levels reward planning. Stars: three at par or better, two within about a third over, one for any win. Levels open one after another, and each chapter's first is always open. `tests/stow.mjs` replays every level's solution, and counts the pieces against the known numbers of polyominoes, polyhexes and polyiamonds, and of polycubes (1, 3, 15, 86) and hexagonal and triangular prisms; in a hold, it checks a piece lowered to the first crate it meets (a bridge, never slid under), one that would stand out of the hatch refused, a clearing that settles a crate into a gap and fills a second line, colours moving with their crates, nothing floating in a level's cargo, and deals that fit their hold. Rerunning the tool writes the flat levels byte for byte as they were.

Today's hold is made on the device from the date (the worker composes and solves, as the tool does, in integers, so every device makes the same one), a different kind of field each day, one day in five a hold of 6×6 three decks deep, its stars kept by the day.

Together: a pair's match (from the games screen) or a party's table of up to six (README, Party; until October 2026 two): one hold, and a hand for each, each of you seeing only your own; the others' hands lie face down in a row under yours, in turn order from you, a name in its player's colour and a card back for each piece still in hand. Turns go round the table in seat order, a piece at a time. Someone away (their phone asleep, or gone back to solo) is passed over until they're back, so a sleeping phone never holds up the table; the line over the trays says whose turn it is, and who's been passed over. Out of room on your turn and the level is lost for everyone ("nothing in Tom's hand fits"), so say what you're holding, and leave room for what the next player has; the turn that comes to you because the one before you went away counts too, and your phone says so itself. The hold and the score (pieces used) are the team's. Each request said yes to starts a new hold, at the level of whoever sits down first; starting another at the table asks first while one is under way, since it's everyone's. Your own hold stays under the rete while you sit down, as you left it. In a hold in decks another player's piece is lowered and its lines go out on your screen as they did on theirs (the room keeps the last move for it). A piece you've picked up stays in hand through news that isn't a move (someone sitting down or waking up dropped it). The table is `stow-room.js`, pure: hands kept by seat (`s0`, `s1`…; a list by seat comes back from the database with holes), the turn passing over the away, a move, out of room. `tests/stow.mjs` seats parties and a pair, turns a move made out of turn away, loses a level on the turn of whoever has nothing that fits, and plays the first level through with three, one asleep for three turns, every move through the database's round trip.

On an upright tablet the tray's three places keep to the tray's height: sized by their pieces, they grew taller than it and ran off the foot of the screen.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the hold takes the left with all the height, the tally and the three pieces stacked beside it.

## Hong
Hong Kong mahjong (香港麻雀) at a table of four: you and three computer players (Mei, Wing and Lok), or, together, you and your partner across the table with two of them between you. The rules are those played in Hong Kong homes and clubs (新派清章): 144 tiles with the flowers or 136 without, three fan to win (or one, or none, when a chicken hand will do), ten fan the most a hand pays, half-spicy payouts (the base doubles up to four fan, then every two), the discarder paying the whole of a win off his discard (4 × base) and a self-draw paid by all three (2 × base each), the head bump (of two who could win on one discard, the first after the discarder takes it), the pass block (pass a win you could take and you can't win off a discard until you next draw, 糊一唔糊二), liability (let go the tile that makes someone's fourth set, or third dragon set, and you pay all of their self-drawn win, 包), robbing a kong, play to the last tile, and the dealer keeping the deal on a win or a drawn hand; no seven pairs. A game is the East round, two rounds or all four. The scoring (fan, or no fan: below), the flowers, the minimum and the length are in the menu (they take effect with a new game); the Rules button has the fan table.

`hong-engine.js` is the referee: the wall, the deal and its flowers, the turn, the claims and kongs, the fan of a winning hand (every split of it tried), who pays what, and the next hand. `tests/hong.mjs` checks the scoring of the spec's example hands and the limits, the deal, the head bump, the pass block, robbing, liability and seven flowers, plays 400 random hands through (each ends, every tile accounted for, every payment balanced), then the computer players: their reading of a hand, the calls and the defence a good player makes, whole hands among themselves, and against plain efficiency players.

The computer players (`hong-bot.js`, thinking in `hong-worker.js` off the page's thread) see what a seat sees: their own tiles, what's been shown, the discards and the wall's count. A hand is read against the routes that reach the minimum (any hand, a half or full flush in each suit, all pungs, thirteen orphans), each with its shanten (how many tiles from ready) and its live tiles (unseen copies that would bring it closer). A route's chance of winning is worked out go-round by go-round: the stages still to make, each with the chance its live tiles come by draw or by call, then, once ready, the chance a winning tile comes by self-draw or off a discard, against the draws left and the chance someone else wins first. Its value comes from the chances of each fan it could show (flowers still to come, a value honour pair punged, the concealed hand while it lasts), so a plain hand that needs a self-draw to reach three fan is valued as one. A hand one tile from ready is worked out exactly: every tile that would make it ready, the best ready hand it makes, its waits scored by the real rules; and a hand that keeps a second route open counts a little of that too. Danger: each opponent's chance of being ready (by turn and calls, as measured in the bots' own play), whether they can win off a discard at all (from the fan they can still reach with what they've shown), the suit their calls point to, the tiles passed since they drew (safe, by the pass block), what dealing in costs beyond what their self-draw would cost anyway, and liability. A discard is chosen by value less danger, a call when the hand after it beats letting the tile go, a kong when it costs nothing. The first version played out a few hundred random continuations of each discard: four or five tiles from ready that was mostly noise, and among themselves its bots drew seven hands in ten. Calibrated against its own play, this one wins two thirds of its hands among itself, and over the same deals comes out about 22 points a hand ahead of three plain efficiency players, which win often but small and never defend. `tools/hong-arena.mjs` plays every deal four times with the seats rotated, so only the play shows, and pits versions and tunings of the bot against each other (`medium` for the two strengths).

The computer players play at two strengths (Menu → Computer players), Hard by default. Hard is the reckoning above with two additions, and over 2,000 deals it plays level with the players before them (within a point a seat-hand, the arena's precision). It passes up a discard win when waiting for the self-draw is worth much more: the common case is four concealed pungs waiting on two pairs, where the discard completes one pung as an exposed one and the hand is six fan (128 points), while the self-draw is the limit; waiting there is worth about twice as much, and the coach says so ("Pass the win…: waiting for it is worth about 272 points"). It counts the extra fan of the hand's last discard (海底) when judging who could win off it. Its discards vary at random only among those within a quarter of a point of the best. Medium takes every win, weighs danger at six tenths of Hard's weight and lets its discards wander among those within five points of the best, the better likelier; over the same deals Hard comes out about five points a seat-hand ahead of it (2,000 deals, every seat rotated). Four more expert reads were tried in October 2026 and left out, since none came out ahead of the reckoning above over 2,000 deals: an honour's danger by whether it's worth fan to that opponent (a dragon or the round's wind to anyone, his own wind to him), a flush read from the suit missing from someone's discards, a safe tile kept back in a slow hand, and a heavier weight on liability. The danger measure, calibrated on the bots' own play, already holds most of what they add, and each made the bots fold winnable hands more often than it saved. At a table together the strength is the room's (the device that deals brings its setting, either of you can change it), so both devices move the computer players alike; the coach is always Hard's, and gives the same answer each time it's asked.

The coach (Menu → Coach) speaks in one of four ways. Always, as at first: at each of your decisions, the computer's move in your seat and why ("Discard 7 dots: keeps a half flush in bamboo, one from ready, 12 tiles would help; 34% to win"), its tile marked in your hand with a jade bar. Check (October 2026; for its first day it was called If you differ): nothing until you've moved, and then only where it would have moved otherwise: what it would have done, how many points worse it values yours, and the two reasons that cost most, further from ready, fewer wins, a smaller hand and more danger before the rest ("Your 5 dots: about 2.3 points worse than the coach's 9 bamboo. A step further from ready (two from ready); wins less often (21% against 34%)."), its label in oxblood so it isn't taken for advice; Why? sets its move and yours side by side among the others. The points are what the hand is worth after the move on average (each win it could make, by what it pays and how likely it comes, less the danger of the discard), the measure the coach decides by. The word stays until a turn of yours it agrees with (a claim you pass on as it would doesn't end it, so it isn't gone before you've read it), and a move it values as well as its own, within a tenth of a point, counts as agreeing. Always says the same after a move it advised against, while the others play. Hints: only when you ask, Hint asking it about one decision (Check has Hint too). Off: nothing, and no Hint; until Guan Dan's coach came in, Off was what Hints is now, and a coach left there is now Hints. A coach that speaks unasked keeps its room under your tiles (four lines on a phone, a longer reason scrolling inside it), so the table no longer changes size each time it speaks and falls quiet. Every choice it weighs carries its value, so a kong it passed up, a win on your own draw let go for a discard (the win's points against the hand the discard leaves) and a discard win taken or waited on are measured too. Why? opens the choices in words, the coach's first, each with what speaks for it (+) and against it (−): for a discard, the way it keeps and how far along, a step nearer or further from ready than the coach's, more or fewer tiles to help, how often it wins (or, winning as often, how much less its wins would pay), a bigger or smaller hand, the danger and from whom, the hand's other way kept or closed, and how many points worse on balance (or "as good: either would do"); for a claim, letting it go against each call (the value honour's fan, the discard after it, nearer ready or not, a route given up for a smaller hand, the concealed fan lost and a set shown to read); for a discard win, taking it against waiting for the self-draw. Under them, the discards it weighed as numbers (the route each keeps and the next-best one, how far from ready, the tiles that would help, the chance to win, the danger) and what it makes of each opponent. At the end of each hand, unless it's Off, Your play: how often you and the coach agreed, and the three decisions where it would have gone another way that cost most, in the same words, with its own move's reason.

Copy turn and Copy game (Menu, October 2026) put the coach's reckoning into words to paste elsewhere, to check it. Copy turn copies your decision now (between your turns, your last one, while the page is open; after a reload, the table as it stands): the rules played, a key to the tiles (1m–9m, 1p–9p, 1s–9s, E S W N, Rd Gd Wd, F1–F8), the round, the hand and the wall's count, the order of play, then every seat from yours, its wind, score, tiles held, flowers, sets (whose discard each came off) and discards in order (those taken, and by whom), your own tiles with the one just drawn, and what you could do; then the coach's move and reason, yours beside it (the same, as good within a tenth of a point, or how much worse and why), every choice it weighed with what speaks for and against it and its points (to a decimal, so close calls show), the discards in numbers, and how it reads the others. Copy game copies every hand of the game so far, move by move as your seat saw it: your tiles after the deal (the flowers set aside), the others' discards, calls and flowers, and at each of your decisions your tiles (and the tile drawn, or the discard answered), your move and the coach's beside it (the same; its pick but yours as good; or its move, how much better and why), each hand's end (the winner's tiles and fan, what each paid) and how often you agreed. For that every decision of yours is now noted with the coach's verdict even with the coach Off, silently (the review and the word after a move still keep to the coach's setting), with your tiles then and where in the hand's log it fell; a finished hand is kept as words for the game (`hong:game`, cleared by a new game; at a table together, on the device until it closes). At a table together the coach's notes used to run on from one hand into the next, so a hand's review counted the hands before it; a new hand now starts them afresh. The clipboard is written within the tap; where a browser refuses, the words open in a sheet to select and copy by hand.

No fan (Menu → Scoring, October 2026) is the game as it's played at tables that don't count fan (雞糊, chicken hands). Any complete hand wins, and every win pays alike: self-drawn, 2 from each of the others; off a discard, 2 from the one who let it go and 1 from each of the others (robbing a kong counts as off a discard, its owner paying the 2). The dealer (東) pays and receives double: the dealer's own win takes 4 from each by self-draw, else 4 from the discarder and 2 from each of the others, and a dealer who pays pays twice. The dealer keeps the deal on a win or a drawn hand, as in the fan game. A kong takes 1 from each of the others as it's made (one added to a pung once no one has robbed it; a robbed one goes back to a pung and takes nothing), and if the first four discards are one tile, the dealer's and then the same from each of the others in turn with nothing called between (跟莊, following the dealer), the dealer pays each of the others 1; neither is doubled. No flowers, no minimum, no liability (包); the head bump, the pass block and robbing a kong stand. Your seat's line says "No fan: any hand wins", and the result says who paid what and why, kongs and the follow included. The best game is kept apart (`hong:bestNoFan`, in the menu's line), since its wins pay a few points where the fan game's pay tens; the games screen shows the fan game's. Together, the table plays the scoring of the device that deals it; the puzzle rush keeps the fan game's rules, its puzzles having been found there. `tests/hong.mjs` checks the payments (each way a win comes, the dealer winning, paying and looking on), the kongs (concealed, off a discard, added, robbed), the follow paid and broken, the deal kept, and that a no-fan deal has no flowers whatever the fan game's settings.

The computer players and the coach play it as it pays. Their routes are the plain hand and the thirteen orphans (a flush or all pungs is only a slower way to the same win), every wait is worth the same, anyone ready can take a discard, and dealing in costs twice one's share less what their win would likely cost anyway, doubled with the dealer at either end. A win is taken at once, self-drawn or not (Hard in the fan game sometimes waits for the self-draw); a kong is worth its 3 points besides the free draw; those after the dealer lean to following his first discard, the last of them for the point itself, and the dealer counts the chance of being followed against a tile everyone may hold. The margins counted in points (how much better a call must look, the discards alike enough to choose among, what the coach thinks worth saying) shrink in proportion, to 0.15 of the fan game's. A plain hand two or more from ready is reckoned slower than the fan game reckons it, each step's chance a go-round at a fifth of what its live tiles would give rather than nearly a half (0.47, 0.35, 0.25, 0.2 and 0.12 tried; 0.2 best). In the arena, over 1,600 deals with the seats rotated, the computer player that knows no fan comes out 5.5 points a hand ahead (± 0.16) of the fan game's playing these rules, and the slower reading alone is worth 1.2 (± 0.16); defence weighed at half or one and a half, the margin a call must clear, how often calls come, a second route's share, the others' speed and how often a wait comes off a discard made no difference within the arena's precision. Among the computer players the dealer is followed about one hand in two hundred. A concealed hand at no fan is reckoned as ready to call as an open one (October 2026): at the fan game calling costs a concealed hand its fan, so it seldom calls, and no fan had kept that reading, which made opening the hand look like speed. The coach once advised chowing 5 dots with 3 and 4 out of a made 2-3-4 (the chow then 28% to win, passing 23%), though a chow off the player before only takes the place of a draw, and this one brought nothing; it passes now, and still chows where the call completes a set. Over 12,000 deals in the arena, the seats rotated, the computer player reading it so comes out 0.06 points a hand ahead (± 0.03), winning 25.4% of hands against 24.6%; counting a chow as the draw it replaces as well made no further difference.

Puzzle rush (Menu, October 2026): find the best discard or call in positions from the computer players' own hands, as many as you can, with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush: Random or Today's; 3 min, 5 min or Survival; three misses end the run; until the settings were made one, five minutes and no choice). Each puzzle is a real table (the rivers, the sets, the flowers, the wall's count) at a decision that leaves the hand ready or one tile from it, where the coach's reckoning is exact (every tile that would make the hand ready, the waits it would leave, each scored by the rules, against the copies still unseen), and where one move beats the next by a clear margin: a point of expected winnings (what the hand is worth on average, each win's payment by its chance) and a tenth of the best's worth. Puzzles are rated 1 to 10 by what would have got them wrong: plain tile efficiency counting every copy as live (blind to the table), the same counting only what's left (blind to fan and waits), and how many discards keep the hand as near ready; each is marked by what it turns on (the live tiles, value, shape, defence, or a call). A run starts easy and rises, four puzzles at each rating, those you've seen lately last. The verdict comes at once: Best, or Not the best with the coach's move and its reason ("Discard 8 dots: ready, waiting on 3 dots, 6 dots (6 left), 3 fan; 80% to win"), what tells most against yours and how many points it gives up; in your hand the coach's tile has its jade bar and yours stays lifted. Why? lays out the options in words, the best first and yours next, then the discards as numbers with each one's points (what the hand is worth after it, less the danger). The clock stands while you read a verdict and while the menu or the rules are open. The bank (`hong-puzzles.js`, 400 puzzles, about 45 at each rating from 1 to 9, no more than a fifth of them calls) is found by `tools/hong-puzzles.mjs [hands] [first seed] [most]` in hands the computer players play among themselves; a puzzle is the deal and the moves before it, two characters a move (`hong-rush.js`), so it replays through the engine to exactly the table that was seen, and the verdict's reasons are the coach's, worked out again there. `tests/hong.mjs` replays every puzzle and checks its best is still the coach's choice: a change to the coach that changes an answer means running the generator again. The run is kept as it goes (`hong:rush`, so a reload finds it where it was), your bests by clock in `hong:rushBest` (a lone best kept before the clocks counts as a five-minute run's), today's runs in `hong:rushDaily`, the run and clock last started in `hong:rushNext`; while the rush is on, the menu is the rush's (Start a run, the run's results, Back to the game, the run and the clock, your bests by clock), and the game's About gives the bests in a line. Your game waits as you left it, and Back to the game returns to it. It's solo only.

Tap a tile to lift it, tap it again (or Discard) to let it go; when a discard can be claimed, the buttons say what you can do (Win, Pung, Kong, each chow, Pass). The computer players move at the pace you choose (Quick, Steady, Slow) and stand still while the menu or the rules are open. Your game is kept as you leave it (`hong:run`), and the games screen shows your best game's score (`hong:best`). Together (from the games screen), you sit across the table from each other, each seeing only your own tiles, with Mei and Lok in the other seats; the device that sat down first moves them while its player is there, and the other steps in only when a move hasn't come for four seconds (both used to work out every move and race to save it); each move is a transaction that checks the hand hasn't moved on since, so a computer player never moves twice. Your own discard shows at once, a round trip before the room confirms it (on your turn nobody else can move, so it comes back as you made it or not at all); a call waits for the room, since your partner may be answering the same discard. A seat kept for your partner until they sit down shows their name. A party of three or four (README, Party) sits at one table (October 2026): the first two to sit down across from each other as a pair would, the others in the seats between, as they come, the computer in any seat nobody has; the device of the first to sit down who's here moves the computer, and the menu's line says who's at the table and whom the computer plays. The room's copy is read back by `fromRoom` in `hong-engine.js`, and `tests/hong.mjs` plays hands through a model of the database move by move. Each request you say Play to deals a new table; your solo game isn't dealt or played on while you sit down (its computer players would have moved on without you), and Back to solo finds it as you left it. The pair's record counts a game played to its end, each of you for yourself.

The tiles are SVG symbols drawn once (`hong-tiles.js`) and used everywhere: characters as their numeral over 萬, dots and bamboo in the patterns of a real set (the one of bamboo a bird), the honours in their characters, each suited tile and wind with a small corner index (1–9, E S W N) for whoever doesn't read the characters (Menu → Numbers on the tiles). The table is one SVG drawn to its box: the player across at the top, the others either side, your discards at your feet, the round's dial between, an arc on its rim toward whoever is to move, the round's wind in it and the tiles left in the wall under that. It's drawn again when the rows under it change height as they're drawn (the coach's words arriving, a claim's buttons, a verdict): until October 2026 it kept the proportions of the box before, a little narrower than the cloth. Held sideways, the table takes the left with your tiles under it, your seat and your move beside it.

The discards and the sets on the table are drawn as large as the box allows (October 2026): on a phone a discard is a third larger than it was upright (half again on a tall phone) and half again larger sideways (390×664: 17 pixels wide before, 22 now; 390×844: 17 and 25; 844×390: 17 and 26), never larger than the tiles in your hand (as they'd be without sets), nor than 2.4rem on a tablet. The drawing is laid out in its own units (a discard 18 by 24, a set's tile 15 by 20), a unit as many pixels as the box allows, and of the ways to lay it out, the box gets the one that draws the tiles largest: upright, how many discards to a row in the middle (the player across and you, six or eight) and at the sides (six, four or three), the closest to a real table's rows of six taken among those within a twentieth of the largest (390×664 has fours at the sides, 390×844 threes, an iPad sixes or fours). Every river has its room from the start, 24 discards, the most on the table in 1,800 of the computer players' hands (if one ever ran longer, the table would draw smaller to hold it), so the tiles keep their size through a hand and the rivers never move; the player across has a row for his label and sets, beside each other, or his sets under his label where the row is too narrow, and his sets draw smaller rather than take a second row. A side column is its player's label, sets and discards, a set moving the discards down a row (a fourth set in a narrow column draws smaller). A label takes one line, or two or three in a narrow column (the score, or the count, under the name); it's measured with a four-figure score, so it keeps its lines as the score grows (past that it draws a little smaller), and the count of tiles held is a figure and a tile's back rather than "13 held". On a tablet the words stop growing at 1.6 pixels a unit, so the labels don't outgrow the tiles. `drawTable` in `hong.js` has the ways; at every size from 390×600 to 1180×820, with every river at 23, four sets each (kongs among them), eight flowers on one player, five-figure scores and a long name across, no tile lies on another, no word on a tile, nothing off the cloth or under the header's buttons in full screen.

A phone held sideways (October 2026) is a table of its own, since the iPad's layout left a phone's few hundred pixels of height to a header and tiles drawn too small to read. The cloth takes the whole height on the left and is laid out as the table lies: the four rivers round the dial, yours below it and the one across above it in rows of eight, the others' beside it in columns of four (six until the tiles grew, when the height they took was what kept the tiles small), each starting next to the dial and growing away from it; the player across has the top row, his label left of his discards and his sets right of them, and the others their own edge, label over their discards and sets under them; your tiles along the cloth's near edge, as if you sat at it. The extra width goes between the players. The round and the hand are said in the console beside the cloth, which holds the header (the logo stands for the name), your seat, the coach and your buttons, lowest, under the thumb. Nothing scrolls, from 740×330 up. It applies under its own query, so the phone upright, the iPad either way and a computer look as they did.

Full screen (Menu, October 2026) gives the tiles the room the header took. The header's buttons (the games, Rules, the menu) go onto the table, over the cloth's top right, and the drawing keeps the player across clear of them: his label left of them, its count after his score, his sets beside them, his discards and the column on the right below them. The header's row goes to the drawing, about 50 pixels on a phone, its tiles drawn larger with it. Held sideways on a phone, the cloth takes the whole screen; the console (your seat, the coach, your buttons) lies on it at the right with the header's buttons at its top, and your tiles take the whole width under both, about a fifth larger. Where the browser can, the page goes full screen too (an app on the home screen already is); the setting stays when the browser leaves it.

Your tiles, upright (Menu, October 2026): in one row, as they were; in two rows, filled down the columns (the first tile over the second, the third over the fourth), about half as large again; or by suit, three rows with the characters, the dots, the bamboo, the winds and the dragons each a section of its own, filled down its columns, with a gap between sections. In rows the tile just drawn is a section of its own at the right and your sets one more after it. The tiles' size comes from what the hand can hold, not what it holds this moment (seven columns in two rows, about eight and the gaps by suit, and never more than a quarter of the screen's height for all the rows), so they keep their size as tiles move between suits, and the table above gives up the height once. Held sideways the tiles stay in one row. The single row now keeps its size too: it was sized to the tiles in hand, so it shrank a tenth each time you drew and grew back as you let one go; it's sized to what the hand holds between calls, with room for the one drawn.

## Tribute
Guan Dan (掼蛋), the partnership climbing game from Jiangsu, by the official competition rules (《竞技掼蛋竞赛规则（试行）》, 2022): you and a computer partner across the table (Lin) against Wei and Fang, or, together, you and your partner against them. Two decks, 27 cards each, play passing to the right. Each side has a level, 2 to the ace; a hand is played at the level of the side that won the last, and its level cards rank above the ace and below the jokers; the two hearts of the level are wild (★), standing for any card but a joker in any play, and alone are just a level card. The plays: a single, a pair, three alike, a full house, a straight of exactly five, three pairs in a row, two triples in a row (the ace low or high, never round the corner; a level card counts as itself in a sequence); bombs beat them all: four alike, five, a straight flush, six to ten alike, and the four jokers on top. When everyone else passes, the last to play leads; if he has just gone out, his partner leads (接风). The side first out rises three levels if its second player is out second, two if third, one if last. From the second hand the last out pays the first his highest card (never a wild) and gets back any card of 10 or lower; after a one-two finish both losers pay, the higher card to the first out (alike: each pays the player before him); holding both big jokers, the payers pay nothing and the first out leads. A match is six hands (the higher level wins; another hand if they're level), or played to the ace, which a side passes by winning a hand at its own ace with its partner not last (Menu).

`tribute-engine.js` is the referee: the cards (a card is its id, 0–107), every reading of a set of cards (more than one when the wilds allow it), how plays compare, the trick, 接风, tribute and its return, the result and the next hand. `tests/tribute.mjs` checks every kind of play and the illegal ones (two pairs in a row, six in a straight, a small and a big joker as a pair), the wilds, the bomb ladder, the trick and the partner's lead, single and double tribute, resistance and returns, the levels and passing the ace; then plays whole matches of computer players (every card accounted for after every play) and puts them against greedy players.

The computer players (`tribute-bot.js`, thinking in `tribute-worker.js`) see their own cards, every play and pass, the counts and the tribute, and remember every card played. They play at two strengths (Menu → Computer players): Hard, the default, and Medium.

A hand is organised into the plays that cost it the fewest leads. Each play is worth the chance it gets made without a lead of its own (it holds when led, or goes on someone else's play): a low single nearly always needs one, a pair of aces often doesn't, the big joker almost never does; a bomb counts below nothing, since it wins the lead back. The split tries sequences in every combination up to four and straight flushes first, places the wilds where they cost least and has triples carry the cheapest pairs, and keeps the arrangement with the smallest total. That one measure gives an expert's habits without a rule for each: a wild makes the weak triple a bomb and leaves the strong one its full house (444★ and AAA55, not AAA★ and 44455); it fills the gap in a straight that would otherwise leave four singles; a level card goes into a sequence only when that saves plays; and a bomb is broken for a straight only when keeping it would leave more loose cards than a bomb is worth (一火保两单: 3 5678 9999 is played as 56789, 999 and 3; with one stray card it stays a bomb).
