Until October 2026 a hand was split by its number of plays alone, a bomb was never broken for a sequence, and the level cards went into runs as readily as threes.

Hard reads the table: how likely a play is to hold, reckoned for each opponent from the cards he could hold and how many he holds (below, Counting what's still out) and from the kinds he has passed on from the other side (a pass on your pair of 8s most likely means no higher pair). Leading: the play that sheds the most of what the hand still needs, longer ones first, shaped by the next opponent's count (京报: the kinds a hand of nine, seven or five cards can't easily follow), more cards than a short opponent holds (he can neither follow nor bomb), low singles and pairs for a partner close to out, and the kinds the opponents have shown they lack, a pair or a single taken out of a bigger combination when the table favours it; with two plays left, a bomb is kept to win the lead back and a sure play goes first. Following an opponent, it contests the trick with the play that costs the hand least, even one that breaks a combination, and keeps its controls from small plays early: the lead is worth more than a little of the hand's shape, and that alone made more than half a level a hand of difference against beating only when it cost nothing. A short opponent after it is blocked with a card he can't beat (顶牌); it bombs by the count of whoever played (炸五不炸四，打七不打八: at five he could go out on a full house or a straight, at four his four may be a bomb of his own, at seven he's two plays from out), or when the bomb and one more play take it out, and leaves the trick to its partner when he may well beat it without one. Its partner's play stands (让牌), unless taking it blocks an opponent about to go out, or holds and leaves its hand able to run out. Late in a hand (anyone within ten cards of out, or sixty left in all) Hard looks ahead: each choice worth considering, singles and pairs taken from combinations among them, is tried in up to 32 deals of the unseen cards that fit what the table has shown (each player's count; the tribute and return cards where they are; nothing above a payer's tribute card in his hand; the big jokers with the payers who resisted; deals where someone passed on a play that would have taken him out weighed down), the rest of the hand played out by these rules and scored by what it does to the levels (a rise capped at the ace, the ace passed winning the match, the tribute owed next hand); another play replaces the rules' choice only when it's clearly better over the same deals. Its choices vary at random only among plays it reckons alike. Medium leaves out the reading of the table, the blocking, the breaking of combinations to lead and the look-ahead, bombs by a plainer rule (an opponent down to seven, but not four), is slower to contest a trick, and now and then misjudges one or wanders among its leads.

In the arena (`tools/tribute-arena.mjs [deals] [greedy | medium | hard | other-bot.js]`, with ARENA_SKILL and ARENA_SEARCH for the bot's own strength and look-ahead; each deal played twice with the sides swapped, every second one from tribute at a random level) Hard with its look-ahead comes out about three quarters of a level a hand ahead of the players before October 2026 with theirs (its side out first in five hands in eight, one-two in a third of them against their one in seven), and about half a level ahead of Medium; its rules alone are worth about a level a hand against the old players' rules, and its look-ahead about a third of a level more than its rules. Medium is about half a level a hand stronger than the old players' rules; against greedy players either strength's side goes out first in nineteen hands in twenty. `tests/tribute.mjs` checks the organisation (the wild on the weak triple, the bomb broken for a straight or kept, the wild in a gap) and the judgement at both strengths (more cards than a short opponent holds, the lowest single for a partner on one, the bomb kept for the last play, the bomb at five and not four, the partner's pair left to stand, and Hard's 顶牌), and plays Hard against Medium. The return: the card that costs the hand least, low, from a rank held more than once (less likely to complete the payer's bomb) and, for Hard, not a 5 or a 10 (every straight needs one); to a partner, the most useful card the hand can spare.

Hint, at any decision (unless the coach is Off, below): Hard's play in your seat (whatever the strength of the others), its cards picked for you, its reason, and what told against the next best ("Pass: keep your bomb; West has 4 left, and four may be a bomb of his own. Next best: Bomb with four 4s; against it: spends your only bomb, your one sure way back to the lead."); tap again for the other plays it weighed, each with what speaks for it and against it. Why? lays them side by side: for every play, the plays your hand would need after it, its chance of holding (no opponent beating it except with a bomb, as the computer's players play: below), late in a hand what the look-ahead made of it (levels to your side on average), and its case, + for and − against, in the terms the computer weighed it: what it sheds or breaks, the controls it spends, the opponents' counts (京报, a short opponent who can't follow, one who could go out on it), your partner's count, the kinds the opponents have passed on, 顶牌, 火不炸四, and for a beat that seldom stands the price it sets the opponent after you (he must beat your J, not the 8). Pick takes a play's cards (for a day after the puzzle rush came in, Why? didn't open from the hint: it took your tap for the list of plays). While a hint stands (October 2026), its cards still the ones picked, your cards are set out as the coach plans them: its play first, then a column for each play it counts on to go out with the rest of your hand (in a row, held sideways, a group each), every other one shaded. That's the split behind "plays to go out after it", the hand organised in a vacuum into the plays that cost it fewest leads, so a strong combination broken for a hint shows where its cards went and what the rest then makes ("Your cards as it plans them: this, then 9 plays to go out"); tap Hint again and the next play's plan replaces it, pick a card yourself and your cards stand by rank again. Why? shows the same in small cards: your hand as it stands, as the coach splits it, and under each play what the rest makes after it (Then); after a check, from your cards as they were before your move. The hint's look-ahead runs a little longer than a computer player's (up to 0.7 s), since it waits on your tap. Your cards stand in columns by rank, the strongest on the left (jokers, the wilds, the level, the ace…), each showing its whole corner, the rank and the suit under it (until October 2026 a card behind another showed its rank alone, so a straight flush, five ranks in a row in one suit, couldn't be seen in the hand; a column of five or more of a rank draws its corners a little smaller, so it doesn't take the table's room); tap cards to pick them, and Play shows what they make before you play it; a return dims the cards you can't give. The card counter (Menu) shows how many of each rank you haven't seen. The computer players move at the pace you choose and stand still while the menu or the rules are open. Your match is kept as you leave it (`tribute:run`); the games screen shows your streak of matches won. Together, you and your partner are always one side, across the table at seats 0 and 2 (the first to sit down at 0), against Wei and Fang, each seeing only your own cards; the device that sat down first moves Wei and Fang while its player is there, and the other steps in only when a move hasn't come for four seconds (its partner's page asleep, or gone): both used to work out every move and race to save it, twice the work on each phone. Each move is a transaction that checks the hand hasn't moved on, and the room keeps each seat's cards under its own key so a seat with none doesn't shift the others. Your own play shows at once: the room's answer is a round trip away, a large part of a second from far enough, and only you move your seat, so it comes back as you made it or not at all (then the table is put back as the room has it). A seat kept for your partner until they sit down shows their name, and the news speaks to you as you ("You hold the turned-up 7♠, and lead"), not by your name. Until October 2026 a match together stopped at its first move: the room's copy of a hand was tidied by giving every log entry a list of cards, undefined for those that aren't plays, and the database refuses a whole write with undefined anywhere in it, so every move came back "Couldn't save that". `fromRoom` and `toRoom` in `tribute-engine.js` read and write the room's copy, and `tests/tribute.mjs` plays whole matches through a model of the database, move by move: every write one it accepts, every state back exactly as written. Their strength is the room's (the device that deals the table brings its own setting, and either of you can change it in the menu), so both devices move them alike. Each request you say Play to deals a new match; your solo match isn't dealt or played on while you sit down, and Back to solo finds it as you left it. The pair's record counts a match played to its end, as a team. A party of three or four (README, Party) plays one match (October 2026): the first two to sit down are partners, across from each other as a pair would be, the others the other side, in the seats between, the computer taking any seat nobody has; the device of the first to sit down who's here moves the computer, and the menu says who's with whom ("You and Mei against Bo and Ana").

The coach (Menu → Coach, October 2026) speaks in one of four ways, as Hong's does. Hints, the default and what there was before: nothing unless you ask, Hint at any decision (above). Check: Hint too, and after each move of yours it would have made otherwise, its word on it with its label in oxblood, so it isn't taken for advice: what it would have played, how much worse it reckons yours, and the two reasons that cost most ("Your K: about 0.7 levels a hand worse than the coach's pass. Breaks a pair of Ks, so your hand then needs 3 plays to go out, not 2; may be beaten in turn (it stands about 20%)."). Always: the same, and at each of your decisions its play and why without your asking, its cards ringed in your hand (Hint picks them for you). Off: nothing, and no Hint. How much worse is in the coach's own two measures. Its play and yours are played out in the same 80 deals of the cards you can't see (the rest of the hand by Hard's rules, scored by what it does to the levels, as the look-ahead does), and the difference is said in levels a hand when the deals can tell: at least a tenth of a level, and twice its standard error clear (the standard error: how far an average over that many deals may stray by chance). Otherwise it's said in plays to go out, what your hand needs after yours against after its, split as the computer would ("Your three 3s: a play further from out than the coach's full house, 3s (6 to go, against 5). Breaks a full house, 3s."). Early in a hand that's nearly always the measure: a deal's outcome varies so much then (a standard deviation of over two levels between the two plays' results) that 80 deals can't tell a few tenths of a level apart, and the rules decide by the plays there anyway. A move as good by those measures counts as agreeing: no further from out, or within a tenth of a level of its own with the standard error counted in. A return is measured by the plays alone, since the deals of a whole hand couldn't tell one card's worth. The word stays until a move of yours it agrees with. Why? sets its play and yours side by side, its first and yours next, each with its average over the check's deals (the look-ahead's own numbers, from other deals, are left out so the two never disagree); and the hand's result ends with Your play: how often you and the coach agreed, and the three moves that cost most, in the same words. The check works from the table as it was before your move, in a worker of its own (it takes up to three seconds, and the computer players, in theirs, never wait on it), and is kept in the run (`tribute:run`), so a reload finds its word where it was. A coach that speaks unasked keeps its room under your cards (four lines on a phone, three on one under 700 pixels tall, a longer word scrolling inside it), so the table doesn't change size as it speaks and falls quiet. `tests/tribute.mjs` checks it on a puzzle's worst play (a gap in levels, within the deals' error of the puzzle's own), on the coach's own play (nothing said) and early in a hand (said in plays).

Copy turn and Copy game (Menu, October 2026), as in Hong: the coach's reckoning in words to paste elsewhere, to check it. Copy turn copies your decision now (between your moves, your last one, while the page is open): the rules as the Rules button has them, a key to the cards (rank then suit, SJ and BJ, (w) for a wild), the hand and the match, both sides' levels and the level played, the order of play, the tribute, every seat's cards left and cards played in order, your own cards, the trick so far and what you could do; then the coach's choice and reason, every play it weighed with its numbers (plays to go out after it, how likely it holds, its levels where the deals gave them) and what speaks for and against it; and for your last move, yours beside it with how the two came out played to the end of the hand in 80 deals of the cards you couldn't see, the coach's ahead or behind by so many levels a hand with the standard error, which is how a move counted as as good can turn out the better one. The plays take the coach a moment (the check up to three seconds): the clipboard waits for them. Copy game copies every hand of the match so far, move by move: your cards as dealt, the tribute and its return, every play and pass, who took each trick and went out, and at each of your moves your cards, your move and the coach's beside it, then the order out and the levels. For that every move of yours is now noted with the coach's: checked as before under Check and Always, and under Hints or Off its pick alone (the plays it weighs for a hint, worked out silently in the coach's worker), said beside yours as unchecked; the review and the word after a move keep to the checked ones. A finished hand is kept as words for the match (`tribute:match`, cleared by a new match; at a table together, on the device until it closes). Where a browser refuses the clipboard, the words open in a sheet to copy by hand.

Counting what's still out (October 2026). The hint counts every card: every play, your hand, and what the tribute showed (a card paid or given back is with the player who got it, a payer holds nothing above the card he paid, wilds aside, and the big jokers are with the payers who resisted). Each other player is reckoned on his own, with the cards known to be his and a share of the rest by how many he holds, so the ace you paid West counts against your K in his hand only, and a payer whose tribute card was a Q can't beat it at all; before, the opponents shared one pool, and a card known to be one's counted against both. Bombs are counted the same way: four or more alike with the wilds standing in, straight flushes with a gap or two the wilds can fill, the four jokers. Where the count rules something out, and only then, the hint says so outright: "nothing left out there can beat it, not even a bomb" (with why: no rank has four cards left, the four jokers aren't all out there, no suit has five in a row left), "counting what's gone his four can't be a bomb: he needs two plays more", "no bigger bomb can be made"; "nothing unseen beats it but a bomb" only when no card that could beat it can be with them (it was said from 97%, and "can't follow it" from 90%). Why? opens with the count: the jokers, wilds, level cards, aces and kings still out (not in your hand, not played), and the chance one of the opponents holds a bomb, or why none can be left.

The chances are the computer players' own, since they're the opponents. The count alone takes the unseen cards as dealt at random among the players with room for them, and against what the players really held it was far out, always the same way: they keep their bombs, pairs and high cards while they shed the rest, and they don't beat everything they could. Where it gave a player of four to eight cards 1% of holding a bomb, he held one a third of the time; a full house it gave 1% of standing stood 41% of the time against Hard, since breaking a hand to beat it costs more than the trick. So each chance shown is the count as their play bears it out, by logistic regression (the log-odds of what happened fitted as a weighted sum of the count's log-odds and a few facts of the position) on what they held or did in their own play. That one opponent holds a bomb: by the log of his count and, for a bomb bigger than yours, its tier, from 89,000 positions in 4,500 hands of Hard's rules; fitted to two thirds of them, it was within 1 or 2 points on average on the rest where the count was 12 to 17 out, and within 2 or 3 against Hard with its look-ahead and against Medium. That either does: a fit of its own, since theirs aren't independent (taken as independent, 74% meant 59%). Holds, the chance no opponent beats a play except with a bomb: by the kind of play and whether it's led, from 8,000 plays against Hard (with its look-ahead, as it plays) and 55,000 against Medium, within about a point on other hands where the count was 16 and 23 out, so the numbers follow the strength you play against. What the count makes certain stays certain, and nothing less shows above 99%. The computer players still decide by the count, as the arena tuned them; the fitted chances are what you're shown. In the arena, reckoning each opponent on his own made no measurable difference to their play (+0.007 levels a hand over 6,000 hands, a standard error of 0.006), and a rule counting the bombs that could stop a run-out lost a little (−0.035 ± 0.02), so they don't use it. `tools/tribute-chances.mjs` plays the hands, records what happened and prints the fitted numbers with how close they come on games held out of the fit (`bombs`, about five minutes; `stands medium`, two; `stands hard`, half an hour, since Hard answers with its look-ahead): run it again whenever the computer players change.

Puzzle rush (Menu, October 2026): find the best play in late positions from the computer players' own hands, as many as you can, with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush: Random or Today's; 3 min, 5 min or Survival; three misses end the run; until the settings were made one, five minutes and no choice). A puzzle is kept only when one play is clearly best. The plays worth weighing (Hard's rules without the look-ahead, Medium's choice, a greedy player's, and the others the hint weighs: up to six different answers) are each tried in deals of the cards the player can't see, fitted to what the table has shown, the rest of the hand played out and scored by what it does to the levels, in batches of 30 deals until one play beats every other by 0.3 levels a hand or more, four standard errors clear (a standard error: how far an average over that many deals may stray by chance), at most 300 deals; about one decision in fourteen passes. The rating comes from who would have got it wrong (Hard's rules, Medium, the greedy player) and how close the call; a run starts easy and rises, four at each rating, those seen lately last. A play is the same answer whatever cards of its ranks make it (a pair of 9s is a pair of 9s in any suits); one the puzzle never weighed is tried against the best in 80 deals of its own while you wait, and counts if it comes within a tenth of a level. The card counter shows in a puzzle whatever its setting, since what's still out is part of the question. The verdict: Best, As good, or Not the best, with what each did in levels a hand, what tells against yours and for the best; Why? sets the plays side by side, the best first and yours next, in the hint's terms, and the best's cards are picked in your hand. The bank (`tribute-puzzles.js`, 193 puzzles from 320 deals, every other one a later hand from tribute at a random level) is found by `tools/tribute-puzzles.mjs <first deal> <deals> <out.jsonl>`, then `merge` to write it (two at once on different deals use both cores: 160 deals each took under half an hour); a puzzle is the deal and the moves before it (`tribute-rush.js`), replayed through the engine. `tests/tribute.mjs` replays every puzzle and checks that the plays it weighed are legal there and different answers, and that the best is the one worth most. The run is kept as it goes (`tribute:rush`), your bests by clock in `tribute:rushBest` (a lone best kept before the clocks counts as a five-minute run's), today's runs in `tribute:rushDaily`, the run and clock last started in `tribute:rushNext`; while the rush is on, the menu is the rush's (Start a run, the run's results, Back to the match, the run and the clock, your bests by clock), and the match's About gives the bests in a line. Your match waits as you left it, and Back to the match returns to it. It's solo only.

The cards are drawn in HTML: rank and suit in the corner, the jokers lettered down their side (red the big one), the wilds starred in brass, the level cards underlined in brass. Held sideways, the table takes the left with your cards under it, larger; the levels, the counter and your move beside it.

The plays on the table (October 2026) are drawn as large as your own cards, up to a quarter larger where the table has the room (a tablet, a phone held sideways), each card behind another showing its whole corner, rank and suit; they used to be two thirds the size, overlapped so far that a 10 showed as a 1. Your partner's play is across the top and yours at the foot, the other two side by side between, each up to half the table's width at its own edge, so a straight or three pairs has room; each name sits at its player's edge, yours under your play. The size is fitted to the table: three rows of plays in its height and six cards in each half of its width. It's worked out when a hand is first drawn and kept for the hand, so the plays don't grow as your hand shrinks and the table with it; only made smaller if the table must give up room (a hint's words). Where the table is short (a small phone, the card counter on, the coach's room kept), your partner's name and yours move beside the plays, to the table's left edge, so the plays keep more of their size. A bomb too long for its row draws its cards closer. Who led, and the tribute paid and given back, show between the two at the sides until the first play.

Picking your cards (October 2026): tap a card to pick it or put it back, or drag across your cards, along the row or down a column, to pick every card you pass (or put back every one, if the first was already picked); a return takes the last card you touch. Your picks stay while the others play (each of their moves used to drop them), so you can pick while they think; your own move or a new hand clears them. Your buttons keep to one row, so your cards don't move as you pick: the row used to wrap when the first card was picked (Play's name growing beside Hint, Pass and Clear), pushing your cards up a row's height under your finger. A play's name that doesn't fit now shows in two smaller lines, and on a phone Clear is ×.

Full screen (Menu, October 2026) gives the cards the room the header took. The header's buttons (the games, Rules, the menu) go onto the table, at the cloth's top right, and the levels at its top left, in a band along its top: upright that is the header's row and the levels' row given to the table, about 70 pixels on a phone, and the plays fit to the bigger table. Held sideways on a phone, the cloth takes the whole screen; the console lies on it at the right, the header's buttons at its top, and your cards in a row take the whole width under both, a tenth larger, so each shows more of itself. Where the browser can, the page goes full screen too (an app on the home screen already is); the setting stays when the browser leaves it. Held sideways, your cards (Menu): in a row along the table's edge, or stacked in columns as upright. A phone lays them in a row and a tablet stacks them, unless you choose; stacked on a phone, they keep a phone's size and the table above gives up the height.

A phone held sideways (October 2026) is a card table of its own: the iPad's layout gave a phone's few hundred pixels of height to the header and to your cards stacked in columns, and the rest scrolled. Here the cloth, a shade deeper, takes the whole height on the left, your partner across at its far edge, the others either side (a card's back beside how many each holds), the plays where they're made, and your cards fanned in one row along its near edge as if you sat at it: strongest on the left, each showing its corner, a little more room between ranks than within one, the wild's star moved to where the fan leaves it showing. Beside the cloth, a console: the header (the logo stands for the name), the levels, the counter, the hint, and your buttons, lowest, under the thumb. Nothing scrolls, from 740×330 up. It applies under its own query, so the phone upright, the iPad either way and a computer look as they did.

Your go (October 2026): as a decision comes to you (a play, or a card to give back), the table flashes verdigris, its cloth and frame lit green, and fades in a second and a half; once a decision, not each time the page redraws, and not in the puzzle rush, where every puzzle is yours. At a table together the coach and the card counter are the table's, like the computer players' strength: written on the table and the same on every phone, so the host who turns the coach off turns off everyone's Hint (a phone's own setting still governs it solo). At a party the host sets them, and the others' menus say what the host chose; at a pair's table either of you does. A new match at the table keeps its rules (it used to take the strength of whichever phone dealt it). The table's code is version 2 for it, so a phone still on older code updates before it sits down.

## Cue
A party game (October 2026): tap anywhere on the card and a simple word pops up, to act, draw or describe without saying it, or for someone holding the phone to their forehead to guess, with a countdown under it (below). The party keeps its own rules, teams and score.

The words (`cue-words.js`) are 553 simple, everyday, concrete ones in twelve kinds: animals, food and drink, things, clothes, nature, places, actions, people, sports and games, getting about, the body, feelings; British spellings, as the rest of the suite. The kind shows small over the word in the game's enamel, a raspberry, so whoever acts it knows what sort of thing to show; Menu, The kind over the word, turns it off for a harder game. Menu, Words, ticks the kinds in play (none ticked is all of them).

The deck (`cue-deck.js`) is the words of the kinds ticked, shuffled (Fisher–Yates: every order equally likely) and dealt one a tap, so no word comes twice until every one has; then it is shuffled again, never starting with the word just seen. The count on the card's foot says how far through you are (12 of 553, and the round after the first). Back steps to the word before, for a tap that came too soon, and a second tap within a third of a second counts as the same tap, so a double tap doesn't skip a word nobody saw. Menu, Shuffle them all again, starts a fresh round. The deck is kept on the device (`cue:deck`, the menu's choices in `cue:opts`) and syncs like every game's keys, so a reload, another of your devices or the next party carries on where it was; a deck kept from a list that has since changed is dealt afresh. On a keyboard, → (or Space on the card) is the next word and ← the one before.

The countdown (Menu, Count down 3, 2, 1; on unless turned off, October 2026): a word comes up alone for a second, so everyone has read it before anything is counted, and then a dial under it counts down three seconds, its numeral 3, 2, 1, a second each, and an arc in the game's enamel running out with the time. At nought the ring and the numeral turn raspberry and the card flashes once (a tint and a frame in the enamel, fading over most of a second), so the end is seen from across a room; the 0 stays until the next tap. For the first second the dial is only its empty ring, there from the start so nothing moves when the count begins; in Almanac three brass ticks on the ring mark the seconds. A tap at any moment is the next word with its own second to read and its own count, and Back gives the word before a fresh one. The count stands still while the menu or the games screen is open and goes on from where it stood (the suite's pause). The moment a word came up is kept with the deck (`when` in `cue:deck`), so a reload mid-count, another of your devices or a partner watching shows the same count; a page opened after a count has ended shows the empty ring, without a flash. Turned on in the menu, it starts with the next word: the one up has been read. The timing is in `cue-clock.js`, the time since the word came up in and the phase, numeral and share of the arc out, which the page draws each frame and tests/cue.mjs checks.

The word is drawn as large as the card holds it: at most a third of the card's height, no more than the kind and the dial leave of it, and nine tenths of its width. A long word (WEIGHTLIFTING) whose wide capitals would come out under 3rem takes narrower letters instead, which draw it about a quarter larger in Almanac's lettering. Held sideways the card takes all the height under the header, Back and the count lying on its foot, so a phone held to a forehead shows the word as large as the screen is high. tests/cue.mjs checks the words (each one word of small letters, none in two kinds, at least 20 of each kind, none over 13 letters, so every one fits large) and the deck (every word once a round, a new round not starting with the word just seen, back a step and on again to the same word, a kept deck read back, and dealt afresh when the list has changed under it, a fair shuffle) and the countdown (nothing counted for the first second, then 3, 2 and 1 a second each, nought at four seconds, the arc running out evenly).

## Party
A party is a room for the evening, each player on their own phone (October 2026): for when three, four or ten of you sit round a table, as on Korbi's trip to Hong Kong. Whoever opens it (Invite players) is its host and shows its QR code; the others scan it with their camera and sit down in their browser with a name: no app to install, no codes to type, no pairing (README, Tables). The host chooses what the party plays and starts it, and every phone follows; the others don't choose. A party plays any game that fits how many are in it: Party's own games (below), played on Party's page, where every phone shows its own player what's theirs (a word, a role, a vote to cast) and the host's phone runs the game and speaks for it, a narrator in the middle of the table, each step said aloud in the phone's own voice and written on every screen in a strip under the header tinted in Party's turquoise; the card, dice and board games (Boast, Big Two, Landlord, Banquet, Gammon, Trumps, Contract, Quest, Bones, Cartel, Dandan), each on its own page at a table in the party's room, the computer in the seats nobody has (README, Seats); and every game with a duo match: Crates, River and Gambit for two; Slate, Delta, Punt, Chart, Quote, Spot, Stow, Origin, Order and Manifest for up to six, everyone on their own phone (October 2026; until then all but Tribute and Hong were for exactly two); Tribute and Hong for up to four, the computer in the seats nobody has; and the head-to-heads on one deal (Gambit's puzzle rush, Arb, Survey, Pipes: README, Head to head) for up to six. How each plays at a table of more than two is in its section: in Chart, Quote and Punt everyone answers the same question on their own phone, and after each the reveal shows what everyone did and the standings; Slate, Delta, Origin and Order are one board or line for the team, anyone playing it; Stow takes turns round the table; Spot and Manifest are either, against each other or one team. So four can choose Who am I?, Bones, Cartel or Tribute, but not Landlord (three seats) or Crates (two).

Starting a game on another page writes its table in the party's room first (`tableFrom` in `party-core.js`: everyone in the party seated as they sit in it, two at a four-seat table across from each other as partners, each marked away until their phone sits down, so one who never comes is played for after the grace) or, for a duo match, a request the two have said yes to (the room's `ask`, as the games screen's Play writes it, so the game deals a new table for it: README, Rooms); then the party's choice (`playing`: the game, its page, the time, who was in the party). The two writes go at once, in that order (October 2026): the database applies one connection's writes in the order they were sent, so nobody arrives before the table, and the host waits one round trip rather than two. Every phone in the lobby goes there, the host's at once, and the host's page deals the first game with its own options (Bones' variant, Trumps' game) once its table is in. A game's page in a party (`?party=CODE&at=…` in its address, `party-link.js`) follows the party: its menu has the party's part at the top, Back to the party for the host (every phone goes back to the lobby) and Leave the party for everyone, and when the host chooses again it goes there. A game whose help is a rule of the table (Tribute's coach and card counter, Schafkopf's coach in Trumps) takes it from the host: the host's menu sets it for every phone at the table, and the others' menus say what the host chose (`partyHost()` in `party-link.js`). Someone who joins the party while a game is on goes to it if there's a computer's seat for them (a table game), else waits in the lobby: "Korbi and Mei are playing Delta: you're in the next game." A duo match in a party isn't the pair's, so it doesn't count towards the games screen's duo record. The seat games' Invite players opens a party with that game chosen, its QR code showing: it's the one way to play them with others (each used to open a table of its own). The games screen's Party tile opens Party's home: what a party can play, as tiles (a tap says what one is), and Invite players; a phone that left its party's lobby for another page finds Back to party CODE there.

Each of Party's own games also has a tile of its own on the games screen (October 2026): it opens Party on that game, the page wearing the game's name and logo, its rules on the screen and Invite players under them; the party it opens starts on that game. While one of Party's games is played the header wears its name and logo too, and Party's own in the lobby.

The lobby has the QR code and the party's four-letter code (tap it for a larger one, the link to copy or share, and who's in the party, where the host can ask someone to leave), who's in it with how many of Party's games each has won tonight, and the games: the one chosen at the head of the list, with its options for one of Party's own (the other games' options are their own pages'), then every game the party can play now, as the games screen's tiles, in its sections (Party games, with Quest; Board & card games; Learning; Puzzles). Only the host's taps choose, and only the host has Start; a guest's tap shows what a game is, and the guests see the host's choice and its options as they change. On a phone held upright that is one column, the chosen game and the tiles scrolling together in their own box (keeping their place as people come and go, back at the top when the choice changes, where its options are), Start below; held sideways, who's here on the left and the game on the right. A game over offers the host Play again (the same game and options, a new deal) or Another game, and the others wait for it. A player can leave the party from the menu; one who leaves mid-game is left out of what's still to do (their vote isn't waited for), and comes back to their own seat after a reload or on another device.

Typing with the keyboard up (October 2026). A phone's keyboard covers the bottom of the page without the page being told: the browser shrinks only the part in sight (the visual viewport) and slides it to the box being typed in, so the top of a game goes out of sight, and the page can stay slid once the keyboard goes. While a box has the keyboard, Party's page is made exactly the height in sight and placed in it (`fitKeyboard` in `party.js`, from the browser's `visualViewport`); the header, the narrator's strip and the game's name give way, each game's parts share what's left (scrolling in the game's own box if it's longer, the box being typed in kept in sight), and a toast shows at the top. When the keyboard goes, the page goes back to the top of the screen. Every game with a box gains, from Sketch's guesses and Gallery's titles to Decoy's lies and Echo's clues. The browser that checks the pages here has no keyboard, so one was stood in for (the part in sight shrunk and slid as a phone's keyboard does) at 390 by 664 with 374 in sight, on a smaller and a larger phone, and both ways round on an iPad: the box, its drawing or question and the clock in sight together, and the page as it was once the keyboard went.

The games are modules (`party-core.js` lists them), each with its rules, options, the steps it goes through and what each phone shows at each step; `party-kit.js` has what they draw with: a secret card that hides your word until you tap it and stays as you left it while others play, a grid of players to vote for, who's still to answer, a clock running down, scores. A game moves on on the host's phone from what the players send (their votes, clues, guesses, each tagged with the step it was for), in one transaction on the table, so the phones can't disagree and a vote sent for a step already closed counts for nothing; what a player does on their own turn ("I've said mine") they write themselves. A game can remember what it has shown the party (`shown` in the table, by game: Who am I's identities, so none is dealt twice): it's given the list as it starts and moves on, and adds what it deals; every phone keeps the lists too (`party:shown`) and brings to the next party it sits down at what that party lacks (`recall` in `party-core.js`), so a table starts out knowing what its people have been shown. Menu: the narrator's voice on or off (only the phone that runs the table speaks), and End this game. `tests/party.mjs` checks, beside the games, that every game a party can play elsewhere has its page, its tile and room enough, which fit how many, the table a party deals and the addresses its phones follow, and seats a party of three at Bones on the database in memory, each in their own seat, the host running it, a latecomer in the computer's. Five phones in one browser played it through: Bones for three and back, Delta, Crates, Slate and Gambit for two, Trumps for two (sitting across) and Quest for two with five computer players, a newcomer waiting out a duo match in the lobby, a seat game's Invite players, and the host finding the way back from Party's home.

### Offline party
No internet, as on a plane (October 2026; Korbi: "allow coop on plane"). Every phone joins the same Wi-Fi (on a plane, a pocket travel router: a plane's own network rarely lets passengers' phones reach each other, and an iPhone makes no hotspot without mobile data), then Party › Offline party, where one taps Host and the others Join. Each guest joins with two scans: its camera reads the code on the host's phone, its own code appears, and the host scans that (Add a player, Scan their code). One scan can't do it: each phone needs the other's keys. The phones then talk directly over WebRTC (the browser's own phone-to-phone channel, the one video calls use) across the Wi-Fi, and Party and every game it plays go on as they do online: the lobby, the host choosing, every phone following to the game's page.

How it works. The host's phone keeps the party's database in memory (`memdb.js`, with the Realtime Database's own rules for what it keeps, the same the tests' stand-in uses), and `lan.js` answers every page the same calls `sync.js` answers online, the same way: a transaction is tried on what the page has, then on the host's until the two agree (two taps at once settle as they do online); presence and sessions are undone when a page goes, when a phone's link closes, or when a phone has said nothing for nine seconds (asleep), and said again when it's back; the host's clock is the shared one, measured by pings. `lan.html` holds the links and runs Party in a frame over the whole screen, since a page can't take a link with it to another page: the pages move inside the frame, and `net.js` gives each its database from the page round it. It loads neither the games screen nor `pwa.js`, as an update taking over would reload it and drop every link. A code carries only what differs between two phones (the ICE username and password, which let each answer the other's connectivity checks; the fingerprint of the certificate its encryption uses; the addresses it can be reached at), packed in bytes, about 120 characters, and the other phone writes the rest of the description back round it (`lan-link.js`). Safari hides a page's local address behind a multicast DNS name (a `.local` name the network answers for) unless the page has been allowed the camera, which scanning asks for anyway: the phone that has just scanned shows its real address, and one direction that works is enough for the two to find each other. Safari has no barcode reader, so the camera's frames are read by jsQR (`vendor/jsqr.js`, Apache-2.0) in a worker (`qr-scan.js`, `qr-worker.js`). Only the party's room travels: a page's own syncing (solo saves, where you are for your partner, flagged questions) sees an empty database that keeps nothing, rather than send a phone's history to the host.

While the party lasts every phone keeps its screen on, since a phone that locks stops answering. A guest whose link is lost (its phone slept, or went out of reach) gets a panel over its game: the host opens Add a player, it scans in again, and carries on as it was, in its own seat; the same after its page reloads. The host's page keeps the database through a reload (this tab's own storage), so a reload loses only the links: everyone scans in again and the game goes on. A code answering an earlier one (a phone that scanned twice, the last player's still in view) is passed over and the camera keeps looking. The host ends the party from the menu, asked first, and the others are told. Pictures from Wikipedia need the internet: offline, the cards go without them.

Checked: `tests/lan.mjs` plays it over channels in memory (thirty transactions from two phones at once, presence through a page going and a phone falling silent, a lost link and a new one with what was made meanwhile, a value in pieces, a page kept to its party's room, the clocks agreeing, a table of four through `table.js`) and packs and reads back the codes. In a browser with the network shut, three Chromium phones joined through the screens, each camera showing another's code; they played Order, a guest's link was cut and scanned back in, the host's page and a guest's reloaded mid-game, a page outside the party found its way back, and the host ended it; nothing tried to reach the internet but Wikipedia's pictures. Not yet tried on iPhones: the camera in the home-screen app, Safari's local network permission (allow it when asked: Settings › Privacy & Security › Local Network otherwise), and multicast DNS on a travel router.

### Undercover
谁是卧底 (shéi shì wòdǐ, "who is the undercover"), the party game of Chinese dinners and the TV show of the same name. Everyone gets a word on their phone; most have the same word, the undercover a different word of the same kind (tea and coffee) and doesn't know it; with six or more there can be a blank (白板, báibǎn) with no word at all, who must bluff. In turn each says one thing about their word aloud, vaguely enough not to give it away to the undercover, clearly enough to show the others they have it; then everyone votes on their phone, and whoever has most votes is out, the phone saying what they were. A tie is voted again between those tied; a second tie puts nobody out. The civilians win when every undercover and the blank are out, the undercovers when they are as many as the civilians left; a blank voted out may guess the civilians' word, in either language, and wins outright if right. One undercover up to six players, two up to nine, three from ten.

The words are 125 pairs, each in English and in Chinese (simplified characters, as the mainland game is played), shown in either or both (咖啡 · coffee): the game's classics (牛奶 and 豆浆, 饺子 and 包子, 眉毛 and 胡子) and some from the table Korbi sits at with suppliers (白酒 and 啤酒, 茅台 and 五粮液, 柴油 and 汽油, 油轮 and 集装箱船). Your word shows open while everyone first looks, then hidden until tapped (a neighbour glancing over sees dots); the speaking starts with a different player each round, and the speaker taps "I've said mine" (the host can move it on for anyone). At the end every role and both words are shown, the winners starred.

`tests/party.mjs` plays whole games in Node on the database in memory, the host's phone moving each on as the page does (`tests/party-harness.js`): the right number of undercovers, and a blank only when asked; a game where the table finds the undercover and one where it doesn't; a tie and a second tie; a blank guessing right in either language; a player leaving mid-game without holding the others up; the evening's wins.

### Who am I?
我是谁, the guessing game of sticky notes on foreheads, played with phones: everyone is someone, and every phone shows each player everyone's identity but their own. First the cards are looked over (October 2026, after an evening of identities nobody at the table could have guessed): each card on the others' phones has Reroll, a vote to deal it again, and when most of the others have voted for a card (with two players, the other's one tap; with three, both others; with four, two of the three; with five or six, three) it's dealt again from the same deck, never a card already dealt, and the narrator says whose card it was without naming either. Its owner can't vote on it (they can't see it), and a vote on the card it replaced doesn't count for the new one. The questions begin when everyone at the table has tapped Ready for the cards as they are (a Ready from before a reroll counts no more), or when the host taps Start now; written cards (You write them) aren't rerolled, so that game starts at once. Then, in turn, each asks the table yes-or-no questions about who they are, aloud (Am I alive? Am I European?), and guesses aloud when they think they know; the table says yes or no, so nobody types anything. Every phone shows whose turn it is, how many rounds have passed and Next, which any phone can tap: it moves the turn on once, however many tap it, and the narrator says each turn, and the round as one comes round (Round 3: Mei's turn). A player who leaves is skipped. Menu: a clock for each turn (none, one minute, two), which passes the turn when it runs out. When the table is done, the host taps End: show every card (a second tap to be sure), and every phone shows who everyone was, the narrator saying it; no winner is counted, since the table, not the phone, heard who guessed.

Who you are comes from a deck (the lobby's choice): Famous people, People worth knowing, Gods and monsters, Countries, Cities, Food and drink, Brands, Commodities (for a table of traders), Paintings, A mix (of them all but People worth knowing), or You write them, where each player first writes who the next player at the table will be (someone the whole table knows: a friend, a colleague, a dentist), as the sticky notes go. The decks are built from the knowledge base (`kb/items/whoami.js` picks them: lists of the well known, or sets such as every country), 1,069 identities in all, and under each identity on the others' phones is its crib: what the knowledge base says of it, so the table can answer about a painting or a commodity it half knows (Zeus: a Greek god, the sky and thunder, the thunderbolt and the eagle, Roman name Jupiter; the Mona Lisa: painted in 1503 by Leonardo da Vinci, in the Louvre; Norway: Europe's biggest supplier of natural gas, Johan Sverdrup's barrels, the fjords). The one asking shows at the top of the others' phones with the whole crib, the rest below with their first lines and More; a painting or a person shows its picture when there's a connection. Your own card is a question mark with what you are ("a famous person", as the deck being played calls it) until the end.

The people were gone through in October 2026 so that a party is dealt people a table can guess, and the rest are there to learn: 278 of them, each marked for the party, for learning, or both (`PEOPLE` in `kb/items/whoami.js`). Famous people deals those for the party (194: Taylor Swift, Messi, Freddie Mercury, Mr Bean's Rowan Atkinson, Jackie Chan, Queen Elizabeth II); People worth knowing deals those for learning (192: Howqua, the Canton merchant who may have been the richest man of his day, Hu Xueyan, Lü Buwei, Jakob Fugger, Marc Rich, Wu Zetian, Gérald Genta, the Earl Grey of the tea), the ones few tables would guess but worth knowing; and someone any table could guess who's also worth knowing (Napoleon, Einstein, Shakespeare, Cleopatra, Gandhi) is in both. A mix leaves People worth knowing out, so a party's mix stays guessable, and deals someone in two decks once. Neither deals anyone a table would wince at: no founder of a faith, no modern dictator, no politician in office, no one famous for a crime. A person's crib is written by hand, four to six lines (what they were and where from, when they lived, then what they're known for, the most telling first), because what the knowledge base says of a person is what the other games ask about them, not what a table needs (Neil Armstrong was "a writer" there, of the words he said on the Moon), and their picture is their Wikipedia page's. A person the knowledge base holds keeps its id and its names; the 107 it doesn't (Taylor Swift, Howqua) are Who am I's alone, so Punt and the other games never ask about them.

Nobody comes round twice (October 2026). Once someone or something has been on a card, dealt or rerolled away, it isn't dealt again, at that party or at the next one any of the same phones are at, so nobody at the table has a face from an earlier game at the back of their mind; the game doesn't mention it. The party keeps what it has shown (`shown` in the table, oldest first) and every phone keeps a copy (`party:shown`), bringing it to the next party it sits down at. A deal takes what nobody there has been shown, in a random order; only once a deck has shown every card does it come round again, those shown longest ago first.

`tests/party-whoami.mjs` checks the decks and their cribs (the people dealt by their mark, both in both, their cribs the ones written by hand, those the knowledge base doesn't hold kept out of it; the mix leaving People worth knowing out and dealing each identity once), and plays games in Node on the database in memory: a card rerolled by most of the others and not by fewer, its owner's vote not counting, two players' one tap, five players' three of four, the new card from the same deck and never one already dealt, a vote on the card it replaced not counting, the narrator naming neither card; the questions beginning when all are ready and not before, a Ready from before a reroll not counting, the host's Start now; Next from any phone, two taps on one turn passing it once, the rounds counted as the turn comes round; the clock passing a turn; a player who leaves being skipped; the end telling every card with no win counted; the players writing each other's identities; People worth knowing dealing its own; a mix of the decks; and nobody twice: five games at one party with a card rerolled in each, none of the 25 coming round again, the party keeping them oldest first, a phone bringing what it was shown at other parties and taking home what this one showed, and a deck dealt out (Brands to twelve, five times) coming round again from those shown longest ago. Three phones in one browser (`tests/fake-sync.js` the database) played three games with a reroll, then a new party: nobody twice, and the phones' memory the party's.

### Echo
A word game played together, for the most cards won (Just One's mechanics, October 2026). Each card, one player guesses, in turn round the table, and everyone else sees the word on their phone and writes one clue: one word, without seeing anyone else's. Before the guesser sees them, the same clue twice cancels out, both copies, and so does a clue that is the word itself, of its family or more than one word: the phone finds these itself (by stem, so cat and cats, swim and swimmer, prince and princess are the same; in Chinese, a clue sharing a character with the word is of its family), and marks why. The clue-givers then look the clues over and tap any to cancel (a translation, a word that's the same in other words) or to keep one the phone cancelled, and show the guesser, who sees what's left, large, and has one guess, typed (either language, any case, a letter off in a long word), or passes. Right, and the card is won; wrong, and it's lost with the next card too (on the last card, a card already won); a pass loses only this one. A wrong guess that's right in other words can be counted by any clue-giver while the answer shows. The game is 8, 13 (the classic) or 20 cards, in English, Chinese or both (450 words, from animals and food to the trading desk's tankers and containers); a clock for the clues (none, a minute, ninety seconds) counts a clue not given as none. At the end the score and a verdict (seven cards in ten or better counts as a win for the whole table in the evening's wins), and the cards played, each with its guesser and how it went. A guesser who leaves mid-card loses that card, since the next guesser has seen its word.

`tests/party-echo.mjs` checks the words, the clues that cancel themselves (the same twice, by stem, the word and its family, more than one word, none, and in Chinese), the pile's tally (right, pass, wrong, wrong on the last card), and plays games in Node on the database in memory: clues waiting for everyone, the table keeping and cancelling, a right guess, a wrong one costing the next card and then counted by a clue-giver (never by the guesser), a pass, the end with its verdict, the clock, Chinese words, and a guesser who leaves.

### Decoy
The game of plausible lies (Fibbage's mechanics, October 2026). A question whose answer few at the table know: what a saying means ("The wall of worry", 鹤立鸡群 with its pinyin and "literally, a crane among chickens"), a number (the year OPEC was founded, world oil consumption in million barrels a day, Trafigura's headcount), or an odd fact with a blank (In 1932 the Australian army fought a "war" against ___). Everyone writes a lie that might pass for the truth. Then the truth and every lie are shown together, and each player picks the one they believe, never their own: finding the truth scores 1,000, and each player your lie fools scores you 500. The last question counts double; the most points wins, counted in the evening's wins, and the end names the best lie, the one that fooled the most players at once. A game is 5, 8 or 12 questions of sayings, numbers, odd facts or a mix (each kind in turn, so eight has every kind more than once); a clock (none, a minute, ninety seconds) closes the writing and the picking for whoever's slow.

The phone refuses the truth as a lie, and anything too near it, since a near-truth let through would make a right pick a wrong one: the same words in any case, plurals and articles aside; the whole truth and more ("emus and kangaroos" for emus); for a short truth, most of its words and nothing else ("Minnie" for Minnie Mouse) or its number ("38 mins" for 38 minutes); for a number, the same year exactly or any other within half a percent. A long truth (a saying's meaning) shares words with fair lies, so only the whole of it is refused there. The same lie from two players is one answer, both its authors scoring from it. A number's lie is written as the truth's are (2000 shows as 2,000) and every answer shows in capitals without a closing full stop, so nobody's typing gives a lie away; a saying whose meaning starts "to …" says so in the empty box. When the lies are fewer than one more than the players (four at least), the house adds its own, and a player who's stuck can tap Lie for me for one of the house's, a different one for each of the first three seats. The answers stand for six seconds and a second and a half each (twenty at most), long enough to read who fooled whom and hear it read out; everyone tapping Next moves on sooner.

The questions come from the knowledge base (`party-decoy-bank.js`, built from `kb/`): 432 sayings with their meanings and three wrong meanings each (those Punt's Sayings level asks about), 663 numbers from Quote (its refining questions and units too technical to lie about left out, and none under a tenth), and 63 odd facts in `kb/items/decoy.js`, each with three house lies that are false, never another true answer. A number's three house lies sit round it in steps (years in a span the question's own scale sets, never to come; a magnitude by ratio; the rest by steps of its scale), and how many lie above the truth comes from the question's id, so the truth is as often the least of the four as the greatest: with all its lies below, the latest year was always the truth.

`tests/party-decoy.mjs` checks the questions (a truth and three house lies each, none of them the truth; a number's truth in every place among its lies about as often), what counts as the truth and as the same lie, and plays games in Node on the database in memory: the truth refused, a lie for another question ignored, the same lie twice as one answer, the house making up the numbers, nobody picking their own lie, the scores, Next waiting for everyone, the last question double, the best lie and the winners, the clock closing the writing and the picking, a player who leaves, the house lying for a player, the mix and the numbers' years.

### Gauge
A clue placed on a scale (Wavelength's mechanics, October 2026). Each round one player gives the clue, in turn round the table: they choose one of two scales between opposites (Cold to Hot, Underrated to Overrated, Cheap wine to Fine wine, Bearish to Bullish; 154 written for the game, in English, Chinese or both) and see, on their phone alone, where the target sits on it, a band anywhere along a brass-rimmed dial; then they write a clue that sits there (“lukewarm tea” a little left of the middle of Cold to Hot) and say nothing more. Everyone else turns the dial on their own phone, a finger anywhere on it pointing the needle there (or the arrow keys), and locks it in. The target scores 4 in its bullseye (within 2.5 of it on a dial of 100), 3 beside it (within 7.5) and 2 at its edge (within 12.5); a target is placed where its bullseye is whole on the dial.

Two ways to play (the lobby's choice). Each for themselves: you score what your needle scored, and the clue-giver scores what the guessers scored on average, rounded, so a clue only one of them would get scores little; the most points after everyone has given the clue once (or twice) wins. Together: the table talks it over, every needle showing on every phone with its player's initial as it's moved, and the table's needle, dashed, is the middle of everyone's (the median, so one wild needle doesn't drag it as an average would); the table plays for its score, with a verdict on its points a round at the end (Mind readers, In tune, Close enough, Some static, Different stations), In tune or better counting as a win for everyone in the evening's wins. Two can play together; each for themselves needs three. The clue-giver watches the needles move, and sees the target under them at a tap. On the clue-giver's phone the target is covered as their turn begins and again the moment the clue goes out (October 2026): Show the target under the dial, or a tap on the dial, uncovers it and the next tap covers it, so a glance over their shoulder, or at their phone on the table while the others turn their dials, finds nothing; it stays as they left it through the redraws the others' moves cause. After each round the dial shows the target, every needle and the points for twelve seconds (everyone tapping Next moves on sooner), and the end lists the rounds: each clue, its scale and what it scored. A clock (none, a minute, ninety seconds) passes a round with no clue unplayed and counts a needle moved but not locked in where it stands; a clue-giver who leaves passes the round too. The dial on your phone keeps your needle where you put it through the redraws the others' moves cause, even mid-drag. Held sideways, the clue sits beside the dial, with Lock in and the round's points in the left column.

`tests/party-gauge.mjs` checks the scales, the target's bands, the table's needle and the verdicts, the clue-giver's target covered as each part of their turn begins and shown at a tap, and plays games in Node on the database in memory: the clue-giver's choice of scale and clue, needles moved and locked in, the points and the clue-giver's average, Next, the clue passing round the table, the end; together, the table's needle with a wild needle ignored, its score and verdict, two players; the clock for the clue and the dial, and a clue-giver who leaves.

### Fuse
The word game of a bomb passed round the table (the mechanics of word-bomb games, October 2026). The bomb shows two or three letters; whoever holds it types a word with them in it, together and in order (ING: bringing, kingdom), on their phone, and everyone else watches the word appear on theirs as it's typed. A word counts if it's in the dictionary and nobody has played it this game: it passes the bomb, with new letters, to the next player round the table. The bomb goes off after a time nobody can see (between ten and twenty-five seconds with the normal fuse, seven and fifteen with the short, fifteen and forty with the long), running on as it's passed; whoever holds it then loses a life, and after a moment (the words that would have done are shown, the shortest first) the next player gets a new bomb. Out of lives, you're out; the last player with a life wins, counted in the evening's wins. Use every one of the alphabet's common letters (all but k, w, x, y and z) across the words you play and you win a life back, never more than you started with; your letters show under the bomb, lit as you use them. The lobby sets the letters (easy, medium, hard), the lives (two, three, four) and the fuse. A phone buzzes when the bomb comes to it, where phones can; a word that doesn't count says why (no ING in it, played already, not in the dictionary) and stays said through the redraws; a holder who leaves passes the bomb on. Held sideways, the bomb and the box to type in sit on the left, the table and your letters on the right.

The dictionary is SCOWL's English, British and American word lists (Kevin Atkinson's, used under its licence, whose notice is in the file) to size 60, the size it recommends for a spell checker: 79,205 lower-case words of three letters or more, no names and nothing with an apostrophe, so both colour and color count. It's stored front-coded in `party-fuse-words.js` (each word a character for how many letters it shares with the word before, then the rest: 271 KB instead of 758) and decoded into a set when the game first needs it, in about 50 milliseconds. The letters a turn asks for are the two and three letters held by many of the common words (SCOWL to size 35): easy in 700 or more, medium in 200 or more, hard in 60 or more, so even the hardest are in at least 60 words of the whole dictionary.

`tests/party-fuse.mjs` checks the dictionary and the letters, what makes a word count, and plays games in Node on the database in memory: only the holder's word counting, words that don't, a word for another turn, a word passing the bomb on with new letters while its time runs on, the bomb going off, a life lost and the next bomb, the alphabet's life back, a player out and never handed the bomb again, the last one winning, a holder who leaves, and two players.

### Snap
The game of the one picture two cards share (Spot it's mechanics, October 2026). 57 cards of eight pictures, made so that any two cards share exactly one picture: the cards are the lines of the projective plane of order 7 (57 points, 57 lines, eight points on each line, any two lines meeting in exactly one point), the pictures its points, drawn by each phone's own emoji (an apple, an anchor, a trumpet, a panda…), which is all a player ever compares with. Your card is on your phone under the pile's, each a disc with its pictures at their own sizes and turns, the same each time the card is drawn; the first to spot the picture they share and tap it, on either card, wins the round. Taps reach the table a moment apart, so the first right one waits 0.4 seconds for any others, and the one made first wins, by the table's clock. A wrong tap greys your cards for a second and a half, so tapping everything isn't a way to win. The winner's name shows over the cards with the picture ringed, then the next round.

Two ways to play (the lobby's choice). The tower: everyone starts with a card; the pile's card goes to the winner of each round, their new card, and a new card turns up on the pile; when the pile is gone the most cards wins. The well: the cards are dealt out; the winner of each round puts their card onto the pile, and the first with none left wins. Short (24 cards on the tower's pile, six each in the well) or long (every card). Held sideways, the two cards sit side by side.

`tests/party-snap.mjs` checks the deck (any two cards share exactly one picture; every picture on eight cards) and the judging of a round (a wrong tap, a tap for another round, the first right tap waiting, the earliest tap winning though it arrived second), and plays the tower and the well in Node on the database in memory, to the end, and a table left with one player.

### Drawing
Party's drawing games (October 2026: Sketch, Whispers, Forger and Gallery) share a pad and the way a drawing travels (`party-draw.js`). The paper is the same light paper on every phone whatever its theme, the whole of it dimmed at night, so a drawing is the same drawing everywhere and the eraser, which draws in the paper's colour, still rubs out. On it a finger draws in eleven inks and three pens, with an eraser, undo, and clear, which asks for a second tap within a few seconds. A drawing travels as text: each stroke its ink and pen, then its points on a grid of 1,024 a side, the first in four characters and each after it as its step from the one before, in two characters where the step is short (most are) and five where it isn't. When the finger lifts, the stroke keeps only the points that shape it (Ramer–Douglas–Peucker: a point stays only where leaving it out would move the line by more than about half a pixel on a phone), and it's drawn point to point, since those points are the finger's path; smoothing them would round off the corners the finger meant. A minute and a half of drawing is two or three kilobytes. The one drawing sends it as they draw (at the end of each stroke, and every 0.3 seconds during one) to their own place in the table's inputs, which every phone watches. So the others see it drawn, and the game's own state, which every move of the table carries, holds a drawing only once its turn is over.

Party's page draws a game's screen afresh with every change at the table, and while someone draws that is several times a second. A drawing game is marked to keep its screen (`keepsView`), and `party.js` gives it its stage as it left it. Each screen is built once for its step and turn and then updated where it stands, so the pad under a finger, the drawing being watched and the box being typed in are never rebuilt.

### Sketch
The drawing game of guessing (Pictionary's, as phones play it; October 2026). In turn round the table, one player chooses one of three words and draws it on their phone, in a minute, eighty seconds or two minutes. Everyone else watches the drawing appear on their own phone and types guesses, as many as they like. A right guess scores 300 at once, falling evenly to 50 on the bell. The one drawing scores the guessers' average, so a drawing only one of them gets scores little. When everyone has it, the turn ends there.

Half way through the clock one letter of the word shows among its blanks, and three quarters through another (in a word of five letters or more). Wrong guesses show to everyone, newest last. A close guess (two letters off a word of five or more, or most of it) is told only to whoever typed it, in the box itself, and under it while the keyboard is down. The others see it only once they have the word themselves. After each turn the word stands for seven seconds with its drawing, who got it and what each scored.

Guessing with the keyboard up (October 2026). On a phone every guess meant scrolling: the keyboard covered the bottom of the page and the browser slid the page up to show the box, taking the drawing partly out of sight, and Guess took the focus from the box, which closed the keyboard and could leave the page slid. Now, while the keyboard is up, the page is the part of the screen in sight (Party's page does this for every game: README, Party), the blanks and the clock share one line, the drawing takes the height left, and under it the latest guesses and then the box sit on the keyboard; held sideways, the box moves to the bottom of the right-hand column, just above the keyboard. Guess leaves the box its focus, so the keyboard stays up from one guess to the next and nothing moves as you guess. On a phone whose keyboard leaves 374 of its 664 pixels in sight, the drawing is 221 across (331 with the keyboard down).

The lobby sets the words' language (English, Chinese or both), the difficulty (three easy words, three hard ones, or one of each), one, two or three rounds, and the time to draw. The most points wins, counted in the evening's wins. A guess in either language counts. So do other ways of saying it (fries for chips, 的士 for 出租车) and, in English, an article, a plural, or one letter off in a word of six letters or more. Nobody is offered a word twice in a game, and a word the party has drawn before comes round again only once the rest have (as Who am I's identities don't). Held sideways, the paper sits on the left with all the height. On the right are the word or its blanks and the clock at the top, then the box for a guess (high, where a tablet's keyboard leaves it), the guesses, and the pad's tools low where a thumb is.

The words are 720 things a hand can draw (`party-draw-words.js`), in sixteen kinds: animals, food and drink, at home, things to wear, getting about, places, nature, people, things from stories, sport and games, the body, things people do, music, tools and gadgets, festive things, and the trading desk's tankers, refineries and silos. Each is in English and Chinese and marked easy, medium or hard: easy is what a child could draw, hard an idea, a place or something done.

`tests/party-draw.mjs` checks a drawing's text (every point back where it was, steps short and long, a dot, the page's edges, text that can't be read left out), the thinning (a straight line kept as its two ends, a corner kept), the size of a minute and a half of drawing, guesses right and close, and the words: each once, in both languages, enough at every level, none a right guess for another of its kind. `tests/party-sketch.mjs` plays games in Node on the database in memory, checking the words offered at the game's levels, the choice made by the player or, when the clock runs out, by the phone; wrong guesses changing nothing, right ones scored by when they were typed, one too late or for another turn not counting; everyone having it ending the turn early, the drawing kept, the drawer's average, the clock; the rounds, one drawing who leaves, the end; Chinese words with an English guess; and the party's drawn words dealt last. Three phones in one browser played it at the four sizes, in the three themes, day and night.

### Whispers
Chinese whispers with drawings (Telestrations' game, as Gartic Phone plays it on phones; October 2026). Everyone writes something to draw on the first page of a book of their own: a few words, the odder the better, or a line from the game's book (Give me one, three to choose from). Then the books go round the table, all at once. You draw what's written on the page you're passed, without seeing the page before it. The next player sees only your drawing and writes what they make of it, the next draws that, and so on. Every book has every player's hand in it once and ends on someone's words. With an odd number at the table that works out by itself; with an even number each player draws their own first page too, so that every book still ends on words (a table of three makes books of three pages, four of five).

Each page has a clock (Quick, Normal or Relaxed: 30, 45 or 60 seconds for words, 60, 90 or 120 for a drawing). Done says you're happy, and Change takes it back. The next page comes when everyone's done or the clock runs out. It takes what each player had: words are sent as they're typed and a drawing as it's drawn, so nothing is lost to the clock. A first page nobody wrote is the game's own line, so the book still has something to pass on. A player who leaves leaves their pages blank, and the next to draw gets the book's last words instead. Then the books are shown to the whole table, every phone on the same page and the narrator reading each one out ("Korbi wrote: a cat stuck up a tree." "Mei drew that." "Ana took it for: a tent. And that's how it ended."). Anyone can tap Next, and a second tap on the same page moves nothing. There are no points, so nothing is counted in the evening's wins. At the end every book can be looked through again on your own phone. The lobby can deal the first pages from the book instead, and then everyone starts by drawing. The book is 150 lines, odd sights and sayings taken literally (raining cats and dogs; a bull in a china shop; a tanker stuck in a canal), and the party remembers which it has had, so a line comes round again only once the rest have. A book is a page per player, a drawing two or three kilobytes, so even ten players' books sit in the table comfortably.

`tests/party-whispers.mjs` checks how the books go round (at every table from three to ten, each page round every player has exactly one book in hand, every hand is in every book, and at an even table the owner draws their own first page), what each player is passed, and plays games in Node on the database in memory: first pages written, drawn and named, each page by the next player round; a page sent for another step counting for nothing; the books shown a page at a time by anyone's Next, a second tap moving nothing; the pages read out, to the end with no winner; the clock taking words as typed and a drawing as it stands, and the book's line for a first page nobody wrote; a player who leaves; and first pages from the book, none the party has had. Three phones in one browser played it through at the four sizes, in the three themes, day and night.

### Forger
The drawing game of the artist who doesn't know what's being drawn (A Fake Artist Goes to New York's mechanics; October 2026). Everyone sees the kind of thing (an animal), and all but one see the word (a giraffe) on their phone, hidden until tapped. The one without it is the forger. Your card has the same label and looks the same either way, so a glance at a neighbour's phone gives nobody away. In turn, twice round the table, each player adds one line to the drawing every phone shows, in their own colour (ten colours, one for each seat): pen down, one stroke, pen up, then Redo or Done, 25 seconds a line, the others watching it drawn. A dot counts as a line. The forger draws too, as if they knew. The first to draw moves round the table each round, never the forger, who'd have nothing to go on. Then everyone votes for the forger, never themselves. With the most votes alone, the forger is caught but can still win by naming the word, in either language. Otherwise, or on a tie, the forger gets away. The forger winning scores them 2, and the artists winning scores each of them 1. A game is three or five rounds, or once each as forger, each round's forger dealt so that nobody is forger twice before everyone has been once. The most points wins, counted in the evening's wins.

The word is dealt from Sketch's words (`party-draw-words.js`), from the easy and medium ones, of a kind a few lines can begin to show (not things people do, which are drawn as people), and the party remembers which it has had. A line reaches the table as the stroke it is, kept in its player's colour and the medium pen whatever was sent. A forger who leaves mid-round is caught and can't name the word, so the artists win it. The narrator says the kind aloud but never the word, then whose line it is, the vote, and who the forger was. The reveal shows the word, the forger, who voted for whom, and the points for fifteen seconds, or until everyone taps Next. Held sideways, the drawing sits on the left with the order of play, your card, Redo and Done beside it.

`tests/party-forger.mjs` checks the card in the table's language, a line kept as one stroke in its player's colour (a dot one, nothing none), and the vote (the most votes alone, a tie nobody), and plays games in Node on the database in memory: everyone looking at their card; one line each twice round, never starting with the forger; a line out of turn or still being drawn not taken; the clock passing a turn; the forger caught and naming the word in Chinese at an English table; getting away on a tied vote; caught and naming it wrong; the points and the rounds, each with a different forger; the end; a forger who leaves; and once each as forger. Four phones in one browser played it at the four sizes, in the three themes, day and night.

### Gallery
The drawing game of titles (Drawful's mechanics; October 2026). Everyone is given a strange title on their phone (a nervous lighthouse; a dog doing its taxes; a tanker that lost its way) and draws it, in a minute, ninety seconds or two minutes, keeping the title to themselves. Done says you're happy, Change takes it back, and the clock takes what's there. Then the drawings are hung one at a time, in an order nobody knows, each with its artist named; a blank page isn't hung. Everyone but the artist writes a title for it that might pass for the real one. The phone refuses the real title and anything too near it (all of its words, case, articles, apostrophes and plurals aside).
