Held sideways, the table takes the left and the counter, your cards and the buttons stand beside it. On `seats.js`: Invite players opens a party with Landlord chosen, friends taking the computer players' seats. `landlord:game` keeps the game as it stands, `landlord:opts` the menu, `landlord:record` the games won, and the games screen shows the run of wins. `tests/landlord.mjs` checks every kind of play and what isn't one, what beats what, the plays a hand can make, the deal and the face-up card, both biddings (rising bids, a 3 ending it, the cards thrown in and dealt again; each rob doubling, the caller's last word, no rob for one who didn't call), the trick, 要不起 and what it tells, the spring, the anti-spring and the rocket doubling the stake, the hand organised into plays, whole games of computer players at every strength and both biddings with every card accounted for and the scores balancing, the state through the database, and Medium against Easy.

The course (Learn, in the header, as River's; October 2026): Landlord taught from nothing, for a newcomer who means to sit down at a real table in China, in 18 lessons in three chapters on a sheet over the game. The game: one against two, the deal and the kitty, the cards' order, the simple plays, passing (不要, 要不起) and the lead, the bids and the stake. The plays: threes with kickers, straights, pairs in a row and airplanes, four with two, bombs and the rocket. Playing well: counting a hand's plays (手数) and taking it apart into them (拆牌), following without breaking it up, leading (the weakest first, the long ones first, the sure ones at the end), a player down to one card (报单), playing as peasants (letting a partner's play stand, feeding a partner who is nearly out, topping a partner's low lead ahead of the landlord), counting the cards (记牌), and when to bid; then a first game against Easy. A step is words over cards (examples with their names, a play to beat, your hand), a play to make from your hand (tap the cards, then Play: a wrong one is answered with why, "That's a single. To beat a pair, play a higher pair.", "That breaks your pair of queens: the other queen would need a lead of its own."; after two misses the answer is picked for you), or a choice of answers; Hint picks the answer. Chinese terms come with their pinyin the first time. Where you are is kept (`landlord:course`), so the course opens where you left it and its map ticks what's done. The lessons are data (`landlord-course.js`), on the course sheet shared by the games that aren't played on a board (`course.js`, `course.css`: River's course's look; Big Two's and Boast's too) and its card stage (`course-cards.js`: cards written as text, "7 7 7 4", "SJ BJ", drawn as the table draws them and as large as the stage allows, a tablet's larger). A choice whose options are all short (a number, a word or two) sets them side by side, so the words above keep their room on a short phone. `tests/landlord-course.mjs` works every exercise through the rules: every card real, every answer in the hand and judged right, every wrong pick it has words for judged wrong with them, and of every pick the hand allows only those the lesson means judged right; where a lesson teaches what the computer players do (follow with the card that costs least, lead the weakest, lead a pair against a last card, top a partner's weak lead, feed a partner, let a partner's play stand), Medium's rules choose it from the same seat; the bidding questions' answers are the computer's bids by the strength the lesson describes, and the hand counted in plays goes in five and no fewer. The Rules sheet, every rule on one page, opens from the course's map and the menu.

## Banquet
The hand games of a Chinese banquet table, for two (October 2026), against the computer or at a table with a friend each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, as it runs Boast). Two games, chosen in the menu, played for cups: whoever drinks three (or five, or seven) first loses; here a cup is a point, at a table it's a drink, or tea.

划拳 (huáquán, "drawing fists"): both throw a hand at once, none to five fingers, and call the total of both hands. Fingers go up from the thumb, as the game is played (one is the thumb, two the thumb and the forefinger: pointing a finger at someone is rude). Whoever calls it right while the other doesn't makes the other drink; both right, or neither, and you go again. Each total has its verse, said with the throw, and every region has its own; Banquet uses among the commonest: 宝拳 (bǎo quán) for none, 一心敬, 哥俩好 (gē liǎ hǎo, "two good brothers"), 三星照, 四喜财, 五魁首 (wǔ kuí shǒu, "five top scholars"), 六六顺, 七个巧, 八匹马, 九连环, 十全美, each shown with its pinyin and the number, and read out in Mandarin when the hands are shown. A call your own hand rules out, below your own fingers or past them and five more, is a 臭拳 (chòu quán, "stinking fist"), and the buttons don't offer it.

十五二十 (sap6 ng5 ji6 sap6, "fifteen, twenty"), Hong Kong's: each shows both hands, open (five) or a fist (nothing), so the four hands make 0, 5, 10, 15 or 20. One player calls, the other just shows; call it right twice in a row and the other drinks (and calls next); call it wrong and the call passes. The calls are Cantonese, with their jyutping, and read out in Cantonese where the phone has the voice: 冇 (mou5), 五, 十, 十五, 二十.

A tap on a hand and a tap on a call throws. The runner takes moves one seat at a time, so the throws come in turn (the first thrower alternates in 划拳; the caller throws first in 十五二十), but neither sees the other's until both are in, and you can throw before your turn: your throw waits and goes in the moment it's yours, so both throw at once as at the table. Then both hands are shown, drawn in ink on the page's paper (no skin), the calls in large characters, struck through when wrong, the total, and who drinks, for two and a half seconds. The computer (Lao Wang) on Easy throws and calls at random; on Sharp it watches what you've thrown before, never this round's, and calls on your habits, the last few throws counting more, with a surprise one time in four. The game's tile counts a run of wins.

`tests/banquet.mjs` checks the calls and what a hand may call, plays rounds of both games on seats.js (the hidden throws, who drinks, both right or neither, the call passing and two in a row), the game's end, the narrator's lines in Mandarin and Cantonese, the state through the database, the computer's throws always legal and its games ending, and Sharp reading a player who always throws five.

The course (Learn, in the header, where the Rules button was: the rules on one page now open from the course's map and the menu; October 2026): both games taught from nothing, on the course sheet the games off a board share (`course.js`; README, Landlord) with a stage of Banquet's own (`banquet-course.js`): the hands drawn as the table draws them (the drawing moved to `banquet-draw.js`, so the page and the course share it), the calls in their characters with their sound and number. The games, in eight lessons: a hand (fingers from the thumb), the total of both, the verses (酒令: the eleven, and three to name), your call (tap every call three fingers may make: below your own or past it and five more is a 臭拳, refused with why), who drinks (one right, both, neither), 十五二十 (both hands, open or a fist; its Cantonese calls), the caller (twice running and the other drinks); then a first game against Easy. Playing well, in six: the odds (against a hand thrown at random each reachable call is one in six, so the only thing to read is the thrower), reading Lao Wang's last throws (call your hand and his favourite; the last few count most, as Sharp counts them), not being read (seven twos in ten is what Sharp feeds on), 十五二十's odds against hands opened at random (call five more than you show: half the time; the others a quarter), against a mixer (a third each, whatever you call); then a game against Sharp. A step is words over hands, a hand to tap the calls of, a call to make (tap it, then Call), or a choice. Where you are is kept (`banquet:course`). `tests/banquet-course.mjs` checks every hand and call shown is one the game has, that every hand to tap through has as many calls as its words say and every other call is refused as a 臭拳, that every call asked for can be made and of all the calls only the meant ones are right; works out again every figure the lessons quote (the totals, the verses' numbers, who drinks and the caller's two in a row by the rules themselves, the odds by counting the hands, the favourite from the last throws by Sharp's own weighting, the sequence Sharp reads best); and taps every exercise through the stage.

Puzzle rush (the menu; October 2026, called Call it until every game's rush took one name): Banquet's rush, on the sheet Bones', Schafkopf's and Gammon's puzzle rushes use (`rush-sheet.js`), with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush): the calls a banquet table wants at once. Both hands shown, call the total in its verse, first with each call's number and pinyin on its button, then the characters alone; then 十五二十's Cantonese, with the numbers and without; then Lao Wang's last seven to ten throws and your own hand, and the call his habits make likeliest: your hand and his favourite, which leads by Sharp's own weighting (the last four throws counting more) and is thrown more often than any other, so counting alone finds it too; at the top ratings his habit is weaker and 十五二十's reads come in. The bank, 300 calls, 30 at each rating, is dealt in `banquet-rush.js` from a fixed seed, the same on every phone, and drawn on the course's stage (tap a call, then Call); the verdict gives the right call with its number and why ("Your 1 and his favourite 0: 8 of his last 9, 4 of the last four"). `tests/banquet-rush.mjs` checks every puzzle is what its rating deals, hands the game has, the right call the total or your hand and a clear favourite and never a 臭拳, that a tap on it on the stage makes it with the numbers on the calls only where the rating shows them, a run's order, and the verdicts' words.

## Gammon
Backgammon (October 2026), against the computer or at a table with a friend each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it). A match to 1, 3, 5 or 7 points, with the doubling cube if you like; a gammon counts two, a backgammon three, times the cube.

The board is drawn as the one playing it sees it: your home board at the bottom right, your checkers ivory, the other side's in ink, the points in terracotta and navy on parchment in a walnut frame, the borne-off checkers as slabs in the tray on the right, the dice on your side of the bar on your turn and theirs on theirs. A tap on one of your checkers moves it if it can go only one way; otherwise the points it may go to are ringed, and a tap on one moves it there (the tray, to bear off). Only moves that are part of a legal play are offered: the most dice that can be used must be, and when only one of two can be, the larger where it can (gammon-board.js works out every play of a roll, each order of the same moves kept, so a play can be made a move at a time in any order). Undo takes moves back; Done sends the play. Hint lays out the computer's play on Hard, for Done or Undo. A turn with no move is shown and passed. The other side's play is shown a move at a time, its points faded where they moved from. Before rolling, a side that may double gets Double or Roll; a double is taken (the cube to the taker, at twice) or dropped (the game to the doubler at the cube's value). The game after someone first comes within a point of the match, the Crawford game, has no cube; nor does a double that couldn't matter (the doubler already winning the match on the cube as it is).

The computer (Dimitri) judges a position by hand-made rules, not a trained network: in a race, the pip counts, the side to roll worth about four pips; in contact, the pips and what a player weighs: points made (the home board's 4 and 5 most, the bar point, an anchor on the other side's 5 or 4 point), the longest prime and the checkers trapped behind it, every blot with the shots at it counted over all 36 rolls and what a hit costs there (more far from home and against a strong board), checkers on the bar, stacks. Easy picks with a wobble; Medium plays the best by that judgement; Hard looks a roll further for its four best plays, the other side's best answer to each of the 21 rolls, in about a tenth of a second. It doubles at a seventy per cent chance (not past ninety, where it plays on for a gammon) and takes at a quarter.

The course (Learn, in the header; October 2026): backgammon taught from nothing, on the course sheet the games share (`course.js`; README, Landlord) with a board of its own: the table's, drawn by `gammon-draw.js` (moved out of the page so both draw it) with each point numbered by its tip, as large as the stage allows. A play is made as at the table: a tap on a checker moves it if it can go only one way, else the points it may go to are ringed; Undo takes a move back; Play sends it. Ten lessons: board and checkers (fifteen each, the pip count, 167 at the start), a move (3-1 as 8/5 6/5, 6-6 as 24/18(2) 13/7(2)), hitting a blot and where it goes, entering from the bar (and a closed board), bearing off (a bigger die from the highest point), using both dice (the larger if only one), gammon and backgammon, the cube (take, drop), a match and the Crawford game; then a first match against Easy. Positions are written side by side, each in its own numbering ("6:5 8:3 13:5 24:2 bar:1 off:3"), plays as players write them ("24/18(2)", "bar/22", "13/11*", "6/off"). Every play a lesson asks for is GNU Backgammon's own first choice (asked offline, its answer kept: gnubg is GPL and isn't shipped), the hit lesson's 13/11* 13/9 among them, every hit accepted there. `tests/gammon-course.mjs` checks every position (fifteen a side, no point held by both), that every play asked for or accepted can be made with its roll and is judged right, every wrong play with words judged wrong with them, and of every play the roll allows only the meant ones right; the figures quoted (the pips, 167, the gammon, the closed board); and taps every play through the stage on a page of plain objects. The Rules sheet opens from the course's map and the menu.

Playing well, the course's second chapter: the opening rolls (four that make a point at once, 3-1 the 5 point, 4-2 the 4, 6-1 the bar, 5-3 the 3, best first, and 6-5's run to safety; 3-1, 6-1 and 6-5 to play), pointing on a blot (13/7* 8/7 on a checker left on your bar point, 8/5* 6/5 on one split to your 5 point), an anchor (4-4 after they ran one back checker: the golden point, 24/20(2) 13/9(2)), a prime (8/4 6/4 for five in a row in front of two trapped checkers), the race and the cube (the 8-9-12 rule: three races on roll, 4, 8 and 19 pips ahead of 87, no double, double and take, double and pass; the pips shown), bearing off safely against an anchor (5/off 6/2, not 5/off 4/off and a blot in front of it); then a match against Hard. Every answer is GNU Backgammon's, money play at its 2-ply: a play its first choice, and clear of the rest, and a cube question its verdict. Its equities are kept with each step, best first, and quoted in the words ("+0.22 of a game; the next best, 13/10 24/23, −0.01"). `tools/gnubg.mjs` asks gnubg (the @nodots-llc/gnubg-hints add-on: now money play, where before it asked as if at 0-0 in a match to 7, which changed none of the first lesson's answers), and its cube verdicts read the package's take hint the way that agrees with the 8-9-12 rule (asked the other way it called takes 37% behind). `tools/gammon-check.mjs` asks again about every play and cube question the course has, and fails where gnubg's choice, its verdict or an equity has moved. `tests/gammon-course.mjs` checks, without gnubg, that the plays kept for each step can be made, each to its own position, best first; that the first is the answer, every play accepted within 0.02 of it and the rest 0.03 below; that each cube answer is the verdict kept, and that the verdict follows from its equities; and the race's leads as the words give them.

Puzzle rush (the menu; October 2026): as Bones' and Schafkopf's, on the same sheet (`rush-sheet.js`), with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush), with 400 positions (`gammon-puzzles.js`, 40 at each rating) from the computer players' own games. `tools/gammon-puzzles.mjs` plays Medium against Medium and now and then, on a roll with four plays or more, asks GNU Backgammon about every play the roll allows (money play, 2-ply): a position is kept when its best play is ahead of the next by 0.06 of a game or more, pure races more rarely than positions with contact. Every play is kept with gnubg's equity (only its numbers: gnubg is GPL), so any play you make has its value. A puzzle is drawn on the course's board, the play made as at the table (tap a checker, its point if it may go more than one way, Undo, Play); the verdict gives gnubg's equity for the best and for yours (or the next best), and how much of a game yours gave away, and what the best play does: comes in from the bar, hits (or hits two), makes a point in your board or your bar point, an anchor in theirs, a longer prime, brings a back checker out, bears off, or leaves nothing to hit where the next best leaves a blot. Ratings are by rank: Medium and Hard (a roll further) missing it, a closer call, more plays to choose from make one harder. Plays are written as players write them, the course's too: a checker moved on is written from where it started to where it ended ("24/13", "bar/15"), with a star at a point it hit on the way. `tests/gammon-rush.mjs` checks every puzzle: a board the game allows and a roll, every play the roll allows listed once (by the board it leaves) and best first, the best clear of the next, those as good within half the margin, what the best does found again, the best tapped on the board named back as the bank names it; and every rating stocked, every kind of play among the bests, a run's order and the verdicts' words.

`tests/gammon.mjs` checks the board (the start, pips, a made point blocking, a blot hit, entering from the bar first, bearing off exactly and from the highest point with a bigger die, never with a checker outside), the plays of a roll (both dice, the larger when only one, four of a double, each position once), single, gammon and backgammon, a match on seats.js (the opening roll, an illegal play refused, a play made, the cube offered, taken and dropped, the Crawford game, a turn with no move passed), the state through the database, and the computer playing whole games legally, Hard beating Easy.

## Trumps
The trick-taking games of a Swiss, a French and a Bavarian table (October 2026), four players, against the computer or with friends each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, the computer taking the seats nobody has). The Menu chooses the game: Jass (the Schieber, called Chibre in Romandy), Belote, Coinche or Schafkopf. The table is set out the game's way round: Jass and Belote pass to the right, Schafkopf to the left; your partner (in the partnership games) sits across, named in the game's colour, and held upright the other two sit at the top corners beside them. Your hand runs along the foot, sorted by suit with the trumps on the right; on your turn the cards you may play are lifted and the rest dimmed, and a tap on one plays it (a tap on a dimmed one says why it can't be played: follow suit, the undertrump, the called ace). The trick lies in the middle, each card on its player's side, the card taking it ringed; the bidding is said in bubbles by each player's place, and your bidding sits under your hand. The line across the top has the game, the contract (the trumps and who chose them, the value of a Coinche bid, the called ace of a Sauspiel) and the score.

The cards (October 2026) are as large as the screen allows, your hand the size of the trick's cards: one size for both, the largest at which the trick's cross fits the table's middle and your hand fits along its box with each card showing at least its corner (a little over a third of its width), worked out for as many cards as the deal gives, so the hand keeps its size as it's played out (Jass's nine make its cards a little smaller than the others' eight). They had been sized apart, the hand by its box alone and the trick by the middle, and the hand came out the smaller: 46 pixels wide against the trick's 54 on a phone, with most of the table empty. Upright, the other three sit along the top so the trick has the table's whole width; held sideways, they sit round the trick and your hand has the side column (25rem wide, 18rem on a phone on its side). The others' backs are as large as their places let them be, up to half your cards, and keep their row when a hand is played out. The rest of the page keeps its size and the cards take what's left: sized for the play (your status, or the coach's line, measured as the play draws it), the bidding's taller buttons take their room from the table rather than from the cards, and what a player says and a Jass player's Weis lie over the table under their place (the Weis on their backs), so the table never changes size. A card is now 101 pixels wide on a 390×844 phone (the others' backs 38, from 17), 83 on a 390×664, 157 on an iPad upright and 111 held sideways, your hand and the trick alike. `fitTable` in trumps.js does it, on every draw and when the screen turns.

Full screen (Menu, October 2026) puts the line across the top (the contract and the score) into the header's row, between the game's mark and the menu, so the cards have the other row's room (the game's name and the target go; Learn is in the menu too): 91 pixels on a 390×664 phone. Where the browser can, the page goes full screen too (an app on the home screen already is); the setting stays when the browser leaves it.

The packs are drawn for the game: Jass in the Swiss pack (bells, shields, roses, acorns; Under, Ober, King, Ace, the ten the Banner) or, by choice, the French; Schafkopf in the German (acorns, leaves, hearts, bells; Unter, Ober, King, the ace the Sau); Belote in the French (cards.js's cards). The suits' marks are drawn for the game, not copied from any maker's cards. One numbering serves every pack (trumps-cards.js: a card is its suit times nine plus its rank).

**Jass** (pagat.com's Schieber): 36 cards, nine each. The player after the dealer chooses trumps (a suit, Obenabe, high cards winning, or Undenufe, low cards winning) or pushes (schiebt) the choice to the partner. In trumps the Under (the Bauer, 20) is highest, then the nine (the Nell, 14), the ace, king, Ober, Banner; the pack is 157 with the last trick's 5, and a match (all nine tricks) 100 more. You must follow suit or trump, may always trump, but never under a trump already in the trick unless you've nothing else; the Bauer never has to be played. Weis (runs of three or more, four alike) are counted in the first trick and shown by each player's place; only the side with the best single Weis scores, all of its own. Stöck (the king and Ober of trumps in one hand) is 20. The count: all single, or the Swiss multipliers (acorns and roses ×1, shields and bells ×2, Obenabe ×3, Undenufe ×4; in the French pack black single, red double), to 1000, 1500, 2500 or 3000.

**Belote**: 32 cards, five each and one turned up; in turn take its suit as trumps (the taker gets the turned card) or pass, then a second round for any other suit, then a new deal. Trumps: jack 20, nine 14; 162 with the dix de der; belote and rebelote (king and queen of trumps) 20. Follow suit; if you can't, trump, over any trump already in, unless your partner holds the trick. The takers must beat the others or the others score 162; capot 250. To 501 or 1000. **Coinche**: Belote with an auction instead of the turned card: 80 to 160 or capot in a suit, coinche (doubled) and surcoinche (redoubled); made, the bidders score the contract (multiplied) and, uncoinched, their points rounded to ten; down, the others score 160 and the contract (multiplied). To 1000 or 2000.

**Schafkopf**: 32 German cards, eight each. Each player in turn says whether they'd play and what, a Solo over a Wenz over a Sauspiel; all passing throws the hand in. Sauspiel: hearts and every Ober and Unter are trumps, and you call an ace (a Sau) you don't hold of a suit you do: its holder is your partner, unknown until it's played. The called ace must be played when its suit is first led, may not be thrown away before the last trick, and its holder can't lead another card of its suit (unless holding four). Wenz: the four Unters alone are trumps; Solo: the Obers, Unters and a suit of your choice, you against three. 61 of the 120 wins; the tariff is 10 for a Sauspiel and 50 for a Solo or Wenz, 10 more for Schneider (the players under 31 when they lose, the others under 30 when the players win: 91 makes it, 90 doesn't, as pagat has it; it once counted 90), 10 more for schwarz and 10 for each Laufende (the top trumps held unbroken by one side, three or more, two in a Wenz); in a Sauspiel the losers pay the winners one each, a soloist is paid by or pays all three. 8, 16 or 24 deals; the running score is each player's own.

The computer players bid by counting each hand's strength for each way it could be played (Jass pushes a weak hand to its partner; Coinche bids what its hand looks worth with its partner's help guessed and coinches a high bid it holds trumps against; Schafkopf, at Easy and Medium, plays a Solo with six trumps and three high Obers and Unters, a Wenz with Unters and aces, a Sauspiel with four trumps and an ace to call, calling the suit it holds least of; Hard's is below). They play by sampling: Medium and Hard deal the cards they can't see among the others as they could lie (each hand's size; the suits a player has shown to be out of, a Jass player's trumping in revealing nothing and one not following trumps perhaps still holding the Bauer; Belote's turned card with its taker), play each card they may to the end of the hand in each deal by a rule of thumb (take a trick worth taking as cheaply as possible, fatten a partner's sure trick, else throw the least), and play the card that did best for their side on average, crossing the contract's line counting for more than a point either side of it (Medium 14 deals, Hard 40, under a quarter of a second; Schafkopf's Hard looks further, below). Easy plays the rule of thumb alone, with a wobble, and doesn't know a Schafkopf partner until the ace is seen.

Hard, in Schafkopf (October 2026), plays as the strongest programs for Skat and bridge play: perfect-information Monte Carlo. Before each card it deals the cards it can't see as they could lie (`trumps-deals.js`: each hand's size, the Farben a player has shown out of, what the call says, every card played so far legal from the hands so dealt) and as the bidding makes likely (`trumps-think.js`: cards are then swapped between the hands it can't see, eighty times, each swap kept by the Metropolis rule on how likely what was said is from the hands as dealt, as Hard itself would have bid them, so a Solo's defenders deal its player a Solo's trumps (five on average where plain deals give three), a Sauspiel's caller a game worth calling, and whoever passed none worth more). Each deal is then played to the end with every card seen, both sides at their best (`trumps-dd.js`, double dummy: alpha-beta over the cards as bits for the players' points, positions at a trick's start remembered with what's known of their worth, runs of cards that can't differ tried once, and a budget of positions past which a deal is left out), and it plays the card that does best on average in what the hand pays: a Sauspiel's tariff, a Solo's or Wenz's five, Schneider and schwarz one more each, the Laufende (which go to whoever wins, and so make winning dearer), and a hair for each card point. In the first trick a deal is searched only for whether the game is won, in the second for which of the lines that pay (31, 61, 91) the points fall between, from the third for every point, and schwarz (no trick at all) is looked for once a side is about to take nothing. Hard weighs a card over 8 deals when it leads the first trick, 12 when it follows in it, 16 in the second and 24 after; a card takes 42 ms at the median and 0.4 s at the 95th percentile on a laptop. It thinks in a worker (`trumps-worker.js`) while the table waits its pace, so the page never stands still, and past a second and a half (a slow phone's first trick) plays on what it has weighed; where no worker runs it thinks briefly on the page's own thread. Against Medium (two of each at a table, every deal played twice with the seats swapped) it came out ahead by 2.1 a seat a hand, a fifth of a Sauspiel, with a standard error of 0.9, over 780 hands: its Sauspiele with a Hard partner were won 79% of the time against Medium's 57% with a Medium one, its Solos 70% against 55%.

It bids by what a game is worth on average: its chance of being won times what a win pays, less the chance of losing times what a loss costs (a Solo's or Wenz's three times over, since all three pay), against nothing for passing; it says the best. The chance and what a game won or lost comes to are `trumps-bids.js`'s, fitted by `tools/trumps-bids.mjs`: a logistic regression of winning on the hand's features as its player sees them (the eight Obers and Unters one by one, the trumps, the Laufende, the trump Farbe's Sau and Zehner, the Säue, Zehner with and without their Sau, Könige, blank Farben, singletons, the longest Farbe and the Farben's points, the called Farbe's length and Zehner, and whether it leads), and least squares for what each comes to, on 29,006 games played out with every card seen in random deals (15,866 Sauspiele, 9,103 Solos, 4,037 Wenz). Every card seen favours whoever would otherwise have to guess, so the chances are then moved to Hard's own play (the log-odds a plus b times the fit's, and what a game comes to likewise): in 238 Sauspiele, 429 Solos and 182 Wenz said near the line and played with Hard on every seat (the Solos and Wenz on hands dealt strong, since random deals rarely give one), the Sauspiel came out as every card seen said (won 55% where it said 55%; 0.08 plus 0.71 times), but a Solo was won 24% of the time where it said 14%, and a Wenz 45% where it said 27% (0.44 plus 0.44 times, 0.68 plus 0.46): a lone player's three opponents can't play as one when they can't see each other's cards. The Hint and the Why sheet show the same numbers for your hand.

The coach (the menu's Coach, Schafkopf; October 2026): Off, Hints, Check or Always, as the other games' coaches. Hints: a Hint under your cards on your turn rings the card Hard would play in your seat, or the game it would say, and says why in a line: what the card does (a trump led, to draw theirs; the called Farbe searched while its Sau is out; a Sau cashed; schmieren on your side's trick; stechen; a trick taken, or let go to keep what takes one later, the course's motifs) and how often it wins the game over the deals of the cards you can't see against the next best, or, winning as often, what more it comes to on average; for a game, its chance and what it comes to on your score, with what in the hand speaks for it (its trumps and how many are Obers and Unters, its Säue besides, the called Farbe). Check: a Hint when asked, and a word after a card of yours that was behind the coach's by an eighth of a game's base and by two standard errors of the gap over the same deals (each deal's worth is kept, so a real gap is told from the deals' luck), or after a bid behind its own by a tenth of a Sauspiel. Always: its card ringed and its line at each of your turns, unasked. Why? opens its reasons: every card (or game) it weighed, how often each wins, what each comes to on your score (the table's own units: a Sauspiel 10, a Solo's player three times what each of the others pays) and what each does. The coach weighs in a second worker, up to 32 deals in under three seconds, so it never holds up the table; its line keeps its room under your cards while it's on, so the table doesn't change size as it speaks.

At a table (October 2026) the coach is the table's: its host (a party's host) sets it from the menu, it's kept on the table (`house`), and every phone coaches as it says, so the host's Off takes everyone's Hint away; the others' menus say what the host chose. Solo, a phone's own setting governs. The table's code is version 2 for it, so a phone on older code updates before it sits down.

The Bavarian pack (the menu's Cards: Bavarian, Schafkopf; October 2026): the whole pack drawn as vectors (`trumps-deck.js`) after the Bavarian pattern as it is played: the Munich pattern of the nineteenth century, double-ended as today's packs print it, with the rank (U, O, K, A or the number) over the Farbe's small mark in every corner, where a fanned hand shows them. The figures keep the pattern's conventions, which are free to draw; the drawings are our own, not traced from any maker's pack. The Obers: der Alte, the Eichel-Ober, long-haired in a green tunic, with an oval red shield bearing a cross of fleurs and a sabre; the Gras-Ober, the drummer, in a bicorne, a red cross-belt over a yellow front; the Herz-Ober in a blue beret with a red plume, one hand on his hip and one on his sword; the Schellen-Ober in a lace collar and a red doublet buttoned in gold, his sword across him. The Unters: the Eichel-Unter in a green coat faced red, pointing, his hand on a cup hilt; the Gras-Unter, the fifer, moustached, a cockade on his bicorne; the Herz-Unter with a spear held high; the Schellen-Unter swinging a sabre over his head, a round shield on his back. The Könige sit enthroned and crowned, the Gras-König's crown set on a turban as the pattern has it. The Säue: Bacchus as a child astride a barrel among hops, his Maß raised (Eichel); a great leaf, a stalk of roses under a red lily and Bavaria's arms in a cartouche (Gras); a great heart over an urn of roses between acanthus scrolls (Herz); and the wild boar with a hound at his neck (Schellen). The marks are drawn as printed: the acorn's nut yellow and striped red over a blue band and a green cup, the bell's red band with its three holes over a crown of green leaves, the right half of every mark hatched. The small cards repeat all their marks in each half in overlapping columns, the acorns and the leaves lying about a stalk, the Zehner with its X. Shading is hatched, as on an engraved card. A court's or a Sau's upper half is drawn once and shown again turned about the centre. The back is Bavaria's white and blue Rauten. Each card is an SVG of the table's cards' shape, made on the page once as a blob's address for an `<img>` (a hand's markup stays small, a card is drawn once): 26 KB at most, 550 KB in all, drawn by code, so there is nothing to download and nothing to keep for offline play. The table, the course and the puzzle rush draw it; Simple (each Farbe's mark, the rank in the corner) stays the default. It replaces a first drawing whose Unters were stockmen in Lederhosen, which had replaced the scans of J. N. Valter's Bayerisches Bild (Regensburg, 1826) the menu first offered; the side branch `fetch/bavarian-cards` that fetched those scans, and the pictures of the pattern these drawings were studied from, can be deleted.

The course (Learn, in the header; October 2026): Schafkopf taught from nothing, whatever game the menu has, on the course sheet the card games share (`course.js`; README, Landlord) with a stage of Trumps' own (`trumps-course.js`): the German cards drawn as the table draws them (the drawing moved to `trumps-cards.js`, so the page and the course share it) and sized as the card games' course sizes its own (`course-cards.js`'s `fitCardStage`), the game and who plays it, the trick with who played each card, your hand held as the table holds it (the Farben left to right, the trumps on the right, high first). Cards are written as Bavarians say them, Farbe then rank ("EO" der Alte, "GA" die Gras-Sau, "H10"), and named in German ("the Herz-Siebener"). The game, in eleven lessons: the short pack and what it counts (120), the fourteen trumps of a Sauspiel, the Farben (the Zehner above the König), following (an Ober or Unter led is a trump led; with nothing of it, anything goes), who takes a trick, the bidding (weiter, i spui; a Solo over a Wenz over a Sauspiel), calling a Sau and which you may, the called Sau's three rules (gesucht, never thrown away, davonlaufen only with four), 61 and what a hand pays (Schneider's two lines, schwarz, Laufende), the Wenz and the Solo; then a first game against Easy. A step is words over cards, a hand to tap through (every trump, or every card you may play to the trick: a wrong one is dimmed with why, "The called Sau can't be thrown away before the last trick"; Hint marks the rest), a card to play (tap it, then Play), or a choice. Where you are is kept (`trumps:course`). `tests/trumps-course.mjs` checks every card written and that the trick and the hand never share one, that every hand to tap through has as many cards to find as its words say, that every card to play is legal and judged right and of the cards the hand may play only the meant ones are, that every whole trick a question shows is taken by the card its answer names, the points, the Sau the hand may call (the rules' `callable`) and what the Sauspiel won 92 to 28 with three Laufende pays (50, by the rules' own `scoreHand`), and taps every exercise through the stage on a page of plain objects. The Rules sheet, Schafkopf's, opens from the course's map, and each game's from the menu.

Playing well, the course's second chapter: counting the trumps (fourteen in a Sauspiel or a Solo, four in a Wenz: how many are still out, and with five out against your Solo, an Ober led), drawing their trumps (the players lead trumps, the others lead Farben, the called one first while its Sau is out; with one trump out, an Ober to draw it before the Gras-Sau; with none out, the Säue stand), schmieren (a Zehner on a trick your partner holds when you play last; a small card when the two still to play could take it), stechen (over the Herz-Zehner that took your partner's Sau; with the Herz-Sau, not the Unter, when its 11 make 61), your partner's trick (the Herz-Unter under his Schellen-Ober, der Alte kept for the last trick, and 60 enough against a game); then a game against Hard. Its positions are from the computer players' own games, shown as they were reached: the tricks played so far (each taker's card ringed, every card's corner showing) and what each side has taken, as far as you'd know the sides (`takenOf`: the players once the called Sau is out, and always your own side). Each card a lesson asks for is the best when the cards you can't see are dealt every way they could lie and each deal is played to the end of the hand with every card seen (`trumps-solve.js`: double dummy, alpha-beta over the players' side against the others, worth counted in games won, Schneider and schwarz, and a hair for each card point), by at least an eighth of a game over every other card. The deals honour each hand's size, the Farben a player has shown out of, the call (the caller holds no called Sau, and still a card of its Farbe until one is played), and now every card played: a deal under which some card played would have been illegal is dealt again, which keeps the called Sau with whoever ran away with it (davonlaufen: its Farbe led and the Sau not come). On a stage with this much on it the hand takes less of the height and the trick doesn't shrink below a readable size, the hand giving way instead (`fitCardStage`'s share and floor, which the other card games' courses leave at their old values). `tests/trumps-course.mjs` checks that each position is one pack with each trick led by whoever took the last and some deal of the unseen cards under which every card played was legal, that a question counting the trumps has the position's count, and weighs every card to play again, 300 deals each: the lesson's card best by an eighth of a game and three standard errors of the difference clear of every card not accepted, every card accepted within that eighth.

Puzzle rush (the menu; October 2026, called Schafkopf puzzle rush until every game's rush took one name; its positions are all Schafkopf's, as its words before a run say): as Bones' (README, Bones) and on the same sheet (`rush-sheet.js`), with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush), with Schafkopf's 400 positions (`trumps-puzzles.js`: 40 at each rating; Sauspiel and Solo alike, and every Wenz found). `tools/trumps-puzzles.mjs` finds them in the computer players' own games (Medium against Medium), late in a hand (four cards a hand or fewer, sometimes five) where one card is best by a quarter of a game or more on average, four standard errors clear, when the cards that player couldn't see are dealt every way they could lie and each deal is played to the end with every card seen (`trumps-solve.js`, as Playing well is checked). A puzzle is drawn on the course's stage (the game and who plays it, the tricks played, what each side has taken, the trick, your hand): tap a card, then Play. The verdict says how often the best won the game over its deals and how often yours did (or, when you found it, the next best); where both win alike, what each came to on average in games (a game won counts 1, Schneider and schwarz one more each); and what the best card does (a trump led, the called Farbe searched while its Sau is out, a Sau cashed, schmieren, stechen, the trick taken or let go). Ratings are by rank: the computer's rule of thumb missing a puzzle, a closer call, more cards to choose from and to hold make it harder, and the bank is cut into tenths. The sheet writes its words before it draws the stage, which sizes itself to the room they leave (a long verdict had pushed the stage past its box). `tests/trumps-rush.mjs` checks every puzzle could have been reached (one pack, each trick led by the last one's taker, some deal under which every card played was legal), that the cards weighed are exactly those the hand may play, the best the one worth most and clear of the rest, its motifs found again, every rating stocked and every game in the bank; weighs sixteen again in 160 deals of their own, the best still best; and checks a run's order and the verdicts' words.

`tests/trumps-deck.mjs` checks the drawn pack: every card and the back an SVG of the cards' shape, well formed, every number a number, each card its own and small, every id in a card defined once (two parts under one id would clip each other), both ends alike, the rank and the Farbe's mark in the corners, a court's great mark high beside an Ober's or a König's head and lower on an Unter, and the addresses the page uses. `tests/trumps-hard.mjs` checks the search with every card seen against a plain minimax (every card's points, in Sauspiel, Wenz and Solo, some mid-trick; the lines at least so many points; schwarz's search; a whole hand's 61, 91 and 31), the deals of the unseen cards (sizes, a Farbe not followed, the called Sau's player on the caller's side), that the weighing finds the puzzle rush's best in ten of its positions, the bidding's fit on hands whose game is plain (four Obers a Sauspiel all but won, sevens and eights lost, the top six trumps an Eichel-Solo, four Unters and four Säue a Wenz), Hard's bids, moves worked out ahead taken only when legal, Hard through a whole hand, and the coach's words. `tests/trumps.mjs` checks Jass's cards and play (the 157, the Bauer and the Nell, Obenabe and Undenufe, following or trumping, no undertrump, the Bauer's freedom), its Weis and multipliers; Belote's 162, its play and its bidding (taken, the second round, a new deal); Coinche's auction and a redoubled contract made; Schafkopf's trumps, the called ace's duties, the Laufende, a hand thrown in and a Solo's payment; and a whole game of each played by the computer, every move legal, the state coming back whole from the database.

## Quest
Hidden loyalties round a table (October 2026): the mechanics of The Resistance and Avalon, with roles, names and drawings of its own, for five to ten, against the computer or with friends each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, the computer taking the seats nobody has). Most at the table are loyal and a few are traitors (two at five and six, three from seven to nine, four at ten), and only the traitors know who the traitors are. Five quests set out one after another: three succeeding win it for the loyal, three failing for the traitors.

For each quest the leader (the lead passing clockwise, a quest at a time and after every refusal) names a party of the size the quest asks, and may go too: two or three at five players, up to five at eight and more (`SIZES` in quest-rules.js, the published table). Everyone votes on it, openly; more approvals than refusals and it sets out, otherwise the lead passes on, and five parties refused in a row hand the game to the traitors at once. On the quest each member plays success or fail, unseen: the loyal can only succeed, a traitor may do either, and one fail fails the quest, except the fourth from seven players up, which needs two. Only the number of fails is told, never whose. If the loyal win three, the traitors confer and name the one they think is the Seer, and naming the Seer wins it for them (The strike, below).

The roles, chosen in the menu: the Seer (loyal) knows the traitors, and the Assassin (a traitor, the traitors' last word on the Seer) comes with it; the Guardian (loyal) knows the Seer and the Impostor (a traitor) but not which is which; the Veiled (a traitor) is hidden from the Seer; the Hermit (a traitor) neither knows the other traitors nor is known by them. The rest are loyal knights, who know only themselves, and traitors, who know each other. The Guardian and the Veiled come only with the Seer, and a table has room for as many traitors' roles as it has traitors: at five, the Seer and the Guardian take both, and the Veiled and the Hermit are left out. The narrator never says a role; at the end every role is shown.

The page seats everyone round the table's edge, you at the foot and the lead passing clockwise, evenly along a rounded oblong rather than an oval, since the players' chips are wider than they're tall and round an oval at ten the ones beside you touched. A chip shows the player's name, a crown on the leader, a pennant on the party, the last vote (approve or refuse, from its reveal until the next vote; a dot while one is going on means they've voted, not how), what your role tells you of them (traitor, or the Guardian's "Seer?") and, at the end, their role, in verdigris or oxblood. The middle has the five quests (the party's size, a red 2 on the quest that needs two fails, then each result and its count of fails), the refusals so far, and the step; tap the quests for every party so far, who named it and who voted how, which is what a deduction game is played on. Your role card sits under the table (beside it, held sideways), with what the role means; a tap covers it, for a table where neighbours can see your phone. To name a party, tap its members round the table and Send; vote Approve or Refuse, and on a quest Succeed or Fail (Fail only a traitor can press). A vote or a card can be given before your turn: it's kept until your turn and goes in then, so nobody waits on you, and the computer players vote and play their cards at once, since they're secret till all are in, while a party named waits a second so you can see it, and the computer's strike two, for the traitors' names.

Talk (October 2026). A deduction game is played out loud, and alone with the computer it wasn't: you could only vote. Now a tap on a player opens what's been said of them (who accuses them, who trusts them) and your word: Accuse, or Trust, pressed again to take it back. Nobody may accuse more players than there are traitors, nor trust more than the others who could be loyal, so a word means something and nobody can accuse the whole table. A word isn't a move: the game never waits for one, so it goes on the table as a change of its own (`talk()` in quest-rules.js, the line kept with how many parties had been voted on), not through the runner, whose moves are turn by turn. What's been said shows at each player's foot (thumbs down accused, thumbs up trusted, counted, yours ringed in brass), the newest line, the narrator's or a player's, under the table (held sideways, the last several), the one who spoke marked at the table for a moment, and all of it between the parties in the parties so far. Leading, a tap chooses a member, so Talk turns it to talk for a word before you choose. The computer players talk too (a switch in the menu stills them): from the phone that runs the table, in the lulls, a beat after the table last changed, so they speak while someone thinks or a result is shown rather than over the moves; one at a time, at most one word of their own each party and three a step, a word back to an accusation sooner and always.

The strike. When the loyal have won three, the traitors confer: they know each other now, the table sees who they are, each may name whom they think is the Seer (shown as a dagger with a count at the foot of the named), and then one strikes. Which one: the Assassin when a person plays it, else the first traitor round the table from the Assassin that a person plays, else the computer's Assassin, going with the name most of the traitors gave. So a person among the traitors always has the strike, even when the computer plays the Assassin: whom to strike is the traitors' decision together, and leaving it to the computer took the end of the game out of the hands of the person who'd played it. Which seats people play is read from the moves (the computer's carry bot, `s.people` the last word on each seat), so a friend who takes over a computer's seat has the strike once they've voted, and the computer has it back for a seat whose person has been away long enough for the computer to play it. The striker sees in short who names whom; the others among the traitors tap to name; the loyal wait.

The computer players (`quest-bot.js`) reason from what their role lets them know and from the play, never from roles they can't see. Each weighs every way the traitors could be seated (at most 210, at ten) by how well it explains what has happened: a quest's fails need that many traitors in its party, and a traitor fails more often than not; every vote, by how often a loyal player and a traitor, on the party or off it, approve a party clean or foul, as measured from their own games at each level (a traitor refuses a clean party far more often than the loyal do; on Sharp it votes on a foul one much as they do, on Easy it approves one with its own on it nearly every time); a traitor leading takes no other traitor along, except for a quest that needs two fails; and what's been said, each standing word by who says it of whom: a loyal player's accusation doubles the odds against its target (the Seer's is always true), a traitor's says nothing, since traitors accuse the loyal and their own alike; a loyal player's trust counts a little for its target, a traitor's covers its own a little. With no Seer in the game only the first few to accuse or to trust each player count (two at five or six, three at seven, six from eight), since those who join in mostly repeat what the first saw; with the Seer, all of them, since any of them may be the one who knows. From that, each player's chance of being a traitor and each party's chance of being clean. The loyal name the cleanest party with themselves on it, approve their own, one nearly as clean as the best they could name, and always the fifth, which the loyal can't afford to refuse; at five, also one with nobody on it they suspect more than most (so the first quest's goes, with nothing yet against anyone), and from six one as clean as the next leader is likely to name if it's refused (each rule plays best at its sizes). The Seer votes and leads as a plain knight would on what everyone can see, steering by what it knows only some of the time, so its votes don't hand it to the traitors' strike. The Guardian hears its two: in each seating the loyal one of them is the Seer, whose word is true and whose votes follow what it knows, and once they've spoken it votes for a party only when it's more likely clean than not. Traitors on Sharp vote as a knight in their seat would, no more readily for a party with one of their own (which would tie them together), against a clean one now and then once the loyal are two quests up, and against the fourth clean party in a row when the next leader is one of them, since the loyal must send the fifth; they lead with themselves and the players the table trusts most (one more of their own when two fails are needed); on a quest the one of them the table suspects most fails it, having least to lose, and the others keep clean; they let the first quest pass now and then to be trusted, and fail every quest once two have failed. Easy players know only their own side, choose and vote loosely, fail every quest they can (two fails show two traitors), vote for a party with one of their own more readily, and half guess at the Seer.

They talk as they think, each with a temper of its own for the game, some quicker to speak than others. Nobody says anything of their own before the first vote. A knight accuses the players it's fairly sure of (two at most), trusts the ones it's surest are loyal once a quest has been played, and takes back what it no longer thinks, giving its reason: on the failed quest, chose the party that failed, approved it, agreeing with others, or nothing it can name. The Seer accuses only traitors (or, with the Veiled in the game, players it can't see), never trusts one, and waits for the play to give it some cover; on Sharp, with a Guardian to follow its lead, it speaks up sooner and more often. A traitor blames the loyal player who was on a failed quest with it, joins in against the loyal the table doubts more than their share, and trusts a loyal player the table trusts; on Sharp it also accuses one of its own once the table suspects them as much as most (a word that costs them little and keeps it clear of them), on Easy it now and then defends one under fire instead. Accused, a player answers: back at the accuser when it doubts them (a traitor, mostly anyway), else, half the time, "not me"; the rest it lets pass, decided once so asking again changes nothing. Conferring, each traitor names the loyal player whose votes, parties and words best fit someone who knew the traitors (a vote against a party with a traitor on it, or for a clean one; leading a clean party; accusing a traitor, all the more before a failed quest showed them; not accusing a loyal player or trusting a traitor), and agrees, once, with another's pick it nearly made. The page words each line from its reason, with the player named after the verb, so "you" fits.

Between Sharp players alone, talking, over 400 games at each size from five to ten, the loyal win 44%, 35%, 26%, 28%, 30% and 28% with no Seer, and 54%, 45%, 49%, 56%, 37% and 47% with the Seer and the Guardian (the traitors finding the Seer 42%, 50%, 41%, 33%, 39% and 30% of the time they get the chance); with the Seer and no Guardian, 42%, 34% and 46% at five, seven and ten, the Seer having nobody to hide behind. Easy against Easy at seven, 34% with the Seer and the Guardian and 50% with no Seer: on Easy the traitors fail every quest they can, and the Seer blurts what it knows and is found. Until October 2026 the loyal won more with no Seer than with one (79%, 74% and 61% at five, seven and ten): the computer traitors gave themselves away, voting for every party with one of their own on it, failing quests by their seats rather than by who could best take the blame, and leaving a partner the table had caught to hang alone, so the loyal won on the quests, and with no Seer there was nothing to win back. Now the traitors play as above, the game with no Seer is a hard one for the loyal, the Seer, who knows the traitors, is what evens it, and the strike keeps it from being more than even. Getting there showed how a table of computer knights can be led. Counting every knight's word, a whole table agreeing with a traitor's first accusation buried a loyal player (at nine, with no Seer, the loyal won 14% and named worse parties than chance would), so with no Seer only the first few voices count; and with one threshold for every player, a little either way swung whole games (the same table of six won 41% or 58%), hence each player's temper, so a table doesn't move as one. Nine with the Seer is still the hardest table for the loyal. Before the talk the same players won 49%, 40% and 52% with the Seer: what the loyal say pools what each knows (a knight on a failed quest of two knows the other was the traitor), and the first weights, which read a traitor never accusing another as near proof, made the loyal win nearly every quest at ten (62%); the weights were lightened, and traitors given the habit of blaming the loyal player beside them on a failed quest and answering back, until the table was even again. An earlier version that told the traitors to refuse every clean party gave them away at once and lost nearly every quest; one that read a leader's own place on the party as a sign of guilt let them win nearly all. The tile counts a run of wins.

`tests/quest.mjs` checks the roles dealt at every size (the traitors' count, the Seer and the Assassin, the specials with no room left out, none needing the Seer without it) and what each role knows; the parties' sizes; a quest step by step (a party of the wrong size or named out of turn refused, the votes round from the leader and kept unseen, approved and shown, the loyal unable to fail, one fail failing it and only the count told, the lead passing); five refusals; three won and the strike, right and wrong, only at one of the loyal and only by the one who strikes; the fourth quest at seven needing two fails; a game with no Seer won on quests; a vote or a quest half done coming back from the database; the talk (accused by anyone at any time and the line kept, not twice, not of yourself, a word turned and taken back, no more accused than there are traitors nor trusted than the others who could be loyal, a word back not twice running, a reason kept only when the page can word it, no naming before the traitors confer, whose turn it is untouched, all of it back whole from the database); the strike (a person playing a traitor strikes when the computer plays the Assassin, a person playing the Assassin first, the computer's Assassin with no person among them or once the computer has played that person's seat; only traitors name, only the loyal, a mind changed; the computer's strike at the one most named); the computer players (a traitor leading takes no other traitor but brings one when two fails are needed, two traitors on a quest fail it only once, the first round the table or the one the table suspects more, the Seer doesn't wave a traitor's party through, every leader approving its own party, the fifth approved by the loyal, the fourth refused by the traitors when the next leader is theirs, a Sharp traitor voting on a party with its partner exactly as a knight in its seat would, the Assassin never naming a traitor, a knight suspecting the one two others accuse and leaving them out of its party, an accusation from a caught traitor telling next to nothing, a fourth accuser at seven counting only with the Seer in the game; nothing said before the first vote, a word back to an accusation the same however it's asked, a knight on a failed quest of two accusing the traitor beside it and then holding its tongue till the next party, a Sharp traitor joining in against one of its own the table suspects and an Easy one not, a Seer who accused every traitor before a quest failed struck every time, each traitor naming one of the loyal once); whole games between them at every size, with every role and at both levels, every move legal; and whole games with the table talking between the moves, every word fit to be said, the Seer's never a lie.

## Bones
Dominoes (October 2026), for two to four, against the computer or with friends each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, the computer taking the seats nobody has). Three games of the double-six set (28 tiles, every pair of numbers from blank to six once), as pagat.com gives them, chosen in the menu. A tile is laid against an end of the line with the same number, a double across it; whoever can't lay one knocks.

**Partnership**, the block game of Latin America and the Caribbean: four, partners across the table, seven tiles each, play passing to the right. The first hand is led with the double six by whoever holds it; after that the lead passes on and any tile may lead. A hand ends when someone lays their last tile, and their side scores the pips left in the other side's hands, or when nobody can lay one (all four knock in a row), and the side holding fewer pips scores the other side's; level, no score. To 100, 150 or 200; the menu can count every hand's pips instead (as in Puerto Rico).

**Gaple**, Indonesia's: four, each for themselves, seven each. The first hand opens with the double blank; after it the last hand's winner leads a double, or the next player round who has one. Whoever goes out, or holds the fewest pips when nobody can lay a tile, wins the hand (a tie to the holder of the lowest tile), and every player pays every other the difference between their counts, as chips: at 12, 5, 11 and 7, the 5 collects 15. After 4, 8 or 12 deals the most chips win. In the menu: lay a tile face down when stuck, dead and counting against you, instead of passing; and a lone double blank counting 25.

**Fives and Threes**, the English pub game: two to four, four as two partnerships; five tiles each, seven for two (or the championship's nine for two and six for four), the rest out of play, never drawn. The first lead goes by lot, then passes to the left; any tile leads and play goes to the left. Every tile scores what the two ends then add up to, a double at an end counting both halves and the first tile all its pips: a point for every five and every three in it (5 or 3 make 1, 6 and 10 make 2, 9 makes 3, 12 makes 4, 15 makes 8, 18 makes 6, 20 makes 4). Going out scores 1; in partnerships play goes on until both of a side are out, and that side gets the point. Points that would take a side past the target don't count, and making it exactly (31, 61 or 121) wins at once.

The line is laid out as dominoes snake across a table (`bones-layout.js`): the first tile in the middle, each arm along the middle row toward its edge, then turning under its last tile's end (the right arm down, the left up) and running back along the next row; a double lies across the line, except in a turn. The row's length is the shortest that leaves each arm room for a long hand on the board's shape, so the tiles are as large as they can be from the first tile (never more than 2.4rem across) and don't shrink as the line grows; an arm that runs past its room widens the view. The others sit along the top in their places round the table (the next to play on your right in Partnership and Gaple, on your left in Fives and Threes), each with their tiles face down, their score in the individual games, and a bubble for what they just did (a knock, the points they made, out). Your tiles are along the foot, those that fit lifted on your turn: a tap lays one. Where a tile would go on either end, both places show on the line as dashed outlines, to tap (or the two buttons, by the number each end shows); where both ends are alike it goes on the shorter arm. The last tile laid is ringed, and the line under your tiles says what was last done. A hand's end shows over the line, with every hand's tiles left and their pips (in Fives and Threes, what each made that hand).

The computer players (`bones-bot.js`) all play a rule of thumb: in the block games shed the heaviest tiles and the doubles (which fit only their own number) early and keep tiles that fit the ends left; in Fives and Threes score what the ends allow, the most first. Medium and Hard look further: they deal the tiles they can't see among the others as they could lie (each hand's size, and nothing a player has knocked on: a knock on a five and a two means no five or two in that hand, nor in what it laid face down; the tiles out of play anywhere), play every move they may to the end of the hand in each deal by the rule of thumb, and take the move that did best for their side on average: the hand's points won or lost, the chips, or the points scored either way (nothing past the target, a point for going out). Medium deals 12 times, Hard 24, a few milliseconds a move. Between the computer's own players, Hard partners beat Easy ones about three games in four, Medium about two in three.

The course (Learn, in the header, as Landlord's and Boast's; October 2026): dominoes taught from nothing in 18 lessons in two chapters, on the course sheet the games off a board share (`course.js`; README, Landlord) with a stage of its own (`bones-course.js`): examples in rows of tiles, the others round the table (their tiles face down, what they've knocked on, your partner marked; in Fives and Threes the score), the line on its felt as the table lays it (a long one laid out again to the board's shape), your tiles along the foot, larger where the stage has room. The game: the 28 tiles (every number on seven), laying a tile against an end, doubles across the line, knocking when nothing fits and what a knock tells the table, then the three games: Partnership (going out, the blocked hand or tranque, the side with fewer pips scoring the other side's), Gaple (each paying every other the difference) and Fives and Threes (the ends counted, a double at an end both halves, 61 exactly); then a first game against Easy. Playing well: counting the numbers (how many of a number are out, and who can't hold them), making the next player knock (pasar: both ends on numbers they've knocked on), doubles that hang (ahorcado: a double whose number is gone can never be laid), owning a number (la data: both ends on the number you hold most of), closing the hand when your side holds fewer (el tranque, with the pips counted), keeping a tile that fits, keeping it light in Gaple (a pip left in your hand costs three chips), looking a play ahead in Fives and Threes (a point now that hands the next player four), and 61 exactly; then a game against Hard. A step is words over tiles, a hand to find the fits in (tap every tile that fits an end: a wrong one is dimmed, with why; Hint marks the rest), a tile to lay (tap it, and where it would go on either end tap the place on the line, then Lay; once right, the line shows it laid and the hand loses it, and a wrong one is answered with what it leaves: "That leaves a 4 and a 5: Rosa may hold fives"), a tile that scores most in Fives and Threes (the ends' total shown; what each play would make isn't, until it's laid), or a choice. Lines are written left to right as they lie, the first tile laid in brackets ("1-6 [6-3] 3-5"), and checked as they're read: a tile that doesn't touch its neighbour is refused. Every position in Playing well comes from a game the computer players played, and its tile to lay is the one that does best when the tiles you can't see are dealt every way they could lie and each deal is played to the end of the hand exactly (`bones-solve.js`, below), by a clear margin over every other. Where you are is kept (`bones:course`). `tests/bones-course.mjs` checks every line, that no step shows a tile twice, that every hand to search holds as many fits as its words say, that of every play a hand allows only the meant ones are judged right (in Fives and Threes, exactly those that score most, and as much as the words say), every total the words quote, the scores the lessons work out against the rules' own scoring (a side going out, the blocked hand with all seven fours down, Gaple's chips); weighs every Playing well position again, 300 deals each, the lesson's tile best by at least half the rush's margin and three standard errors of the difference clear of every other play, every position a whole deal (the line, the hands and the tiles out of play make 28) whose knocks some deal honours; checks the figures the lessons quote (the fours out, the pips at the block, Gaple's 22 and 27, what Alf could make after each play, 61 exactly); and taps every exercise through the stage to its end on a page of plain objects. The Rules sheet opens from the course's map and the menu.

The solver (`bones-solve.js`) plays a hand's rest with every tile seen (double dummy), each player doing what's best for their side: Partnership and two-sided Fives and Threes as one number, searched with alpha-beta (a search that skips the moves that can't change the result) and a table of positions already solved; three or four each for themselves (Gaple, Fives and Threes for three) by each maximising their own, every position solved once. Worth is what the computer players count (the hand's points either way, the chips, the points scored to the end of the hand, the target made exactly counted as 100). A position is weighed as its player sees it: the tiles they can't see dealt as the computer players deal them (`bones-bot.js`: each hand's size, nothing a player has knocked on), the same deals for every play, each solved. Hands of four tiles solve in a few milliseconds a deal; it's for the course's checks and the rush's puzzles, not a turn at the table.

Puzzle rush (Menu, October 2026): the best tile in positions from the computer players' own games, as many as you can find, with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush: Random or Today's; 3 min, 5 min or Survival; three misses end the run; until the settings were made one, five minutes and no choice). It has a sheet of its own over the game, laid out as the course's, the table drawn by the course's stage: the others with their tiles and their knocks (and the score, in Fives and Threes), the line, your tiles; tap a tile, its place if it could go on either end, and Lay. The verdict comes at once: Best (or As good), or Not the best, with what the best and yours were worth on average over the puzzle's deals (points a hand in Partnership, chips in Gaple, points either way in Fives and Threes, a game won counted as 100), the best laid on the line, and what it does in a sentence: the knock it forces ("It leaves the ends 1 and 5, numbers Rosa and Marisol have knocked on: both must knock again"), the hand it closes, the end only you can play on, the number you hold most of, the double while its number is about, the heavy tile, the most points now or fewer now and less for the next player, 61 exactly. The clock stands while you read it, while the sheet is closed and while the app is in the background; a run starts easy and rises, four puzzles at each rating, those seen lately last. The bank (`bones-puzzles.js`, 380 puzzles: 45 at each rating from 1 to 8 and 20 at 9; Partnership 123, Gaple 115, Fives and Threes for two, three and four 142) is found by `tools/bones-puzzles.mjs <first deal> <deals> <out.jsonl>`, then `merge`: hands the computer players (Medium) play among themselves, every decision late in a hand with two plays or more (every hand at five tiles or fewer, four in Gaple) weighed by the solver in batches of 40 deals until one play beats every other by a clear margin (4 points in Partnership, 6 chips in Gaple, 1.5 points in Fives and Threes), four standard errors clear, at most 400 deals; 3,118 puzzles from 2,400 deals in seven minutes on two cores, the bank taking the games in turn at each rating and lines of 18 tiles at most. A puzzle is a position written as the course writes its steps (`bones-course.js`: `lineText`, `playName`, `motifsOf`), so the stage draws it and the rules judge it; it's rated by whether the computer's rule of thumb and Medium would have found it, how close the call, how many plays and tiles. The sheet is `rush-sheet.js` (shared with Schafkopf's rush: the run, its order, the clock, the intro and the score, any game's course stage drawing the position), `bones-rush-ui.js` Bones' part of it (its stage, its bank, its verdict), `bones-rush.js` the verdict and the words without a page. `tests/bones-rush.mjs` checks that every puzzle is a position a deal could reach (the line, the hands and the tiles out of play make 28, some deal honours every knock) with a choice, that the plays weighed are exactly the hand's, the best the one worth most and clear of the rest by the margin, its motifs found again, the ratings and games stocked, a sample weighed again in deals of its own with the same best, a run's rising order, and the verdicts and their words. The run is kept as it goes (`bones:rush`, so it waits where it was), your bests by clock in `bones:rushBest` and today's runs in `bones:rushDaily`, as the sheet keeps them for every game on it (README, Puzzle rush). It's solo only.

`tests/bones.mjs` checks the set and the count of the ends (a double led, a double left at an end, the first tile); Partnership (the double six leading and nothing else, a tile that doesn't fit refused, a pass only when nothing fits and what it was on remembered, going out and a block scored both ways, level with no score, the next hand led by the next player, the target); Gaple (the double blank opening, pagat's example of the chips, the winner leading a double next, the lone double blank, a tile face down counting); Fives and Threes (the deals, the tiles out of play, each play scored, nothing past the target, exactly it winning at once, the point for going out, partners playing on until both are out); the state through the database at every move; the layout of sixty random lines at five row lengths (no tile on another, each touching the one before, each with its own numbers); and whole games of every game at every level, every move legal, with Hard partners beating Easy ones.

## Dandan
Forgetful Fish (October 2026, from Korbi's list, https://tappedout.net/mtg-decks/daves-dandan/, read on 19 October 2026): a way of playing *Magic: The Gathering* with no deck-building, against a computer player or at a table with a friend on their own phone (README, Seats). It is a communal format: there are not two decks but **one library of eighty cards** that both players draw from and **one graveyard** that every used card goes to, so whatever you counter, discard or mill is the other player's to draw, flash back or fetch. The list has exactly eighty cards in twenty-five kinds (it names a sideboard of Fire // Ice, Mission Briefing and More or Less, which the format doesn't play and the game leaves out).

The cards are in `dandan-cards.js`, ten Dandâns, sixteen Islands and a deck of blue instants and sorceries around them. Their *cost and type* were read from the magicthegathering.io card database (Deem Inferior, which it lacks, from Scryfall) and their *rules text* from each card's Tappedout page, the same site as the list; nothing was written from memory, and the source of each is in the file. The text on screen is our own wording of it, as Cartel's and Hong's rules are; the game uses no Wizards of the Coast artwork, mana symbols or logos (a cost is drawn as small plain pips, blue and red in the suite's enamels, the generic cost grey), and the card names and the format belong to their makers.

The rules were checked card by card and against the format's own (October 2026): every card's Oracle text and rulings on Scryfall, and the format as Wizards of the Coast set it out with its Secret Lair Dandân deck (March 2026, "From the Chaos Vault"): one library and one graveyard, "your" library and graveyard being the shared ones; the owner of a spell or a permanent is the player who cast it (so Repulse returns a Dandân to its caster's hand, and Narset's Reversal a spell to its caster's); when both players draw at once the cards are dealt one at a time from the active player (Memory's seven); and a hand with fewer than two lands or fewer than two spells may be shown and thrown back free, as often as it comes, until the player takes an ordinary mulligan. The rules in short: 20 life each, the first to play skips their first draw, a land a turn, seven cards in hand at the end of yours. The Dandân (4/1, two blue) is the only card that does damage and is the whole game: it **can't attack unless the defending player controls an Island, and is sacrificed the moment its own controller controls none**. So the game is about Islands: your own keep your Dandâns alive, theirs are what let you attack, and the deck is full of ways to bounce, fetch, bend or lose one. Memory Lapse counters a spell by putting it on top of the *shared* library, so the next player to draw gets it (the one who countered it on the opponent's turn, usually); Mind Bend swaps a basic land type in a permanent's text for good (a Dandân told to look for a Swamp is sacrificed at once, an Island told to be a Mountain taps for red); Commit and Deem Inferior put a permanent second from the top; Memory, from the graveyard, shuffles both hands and the graveyard into the library and deals each seven. Mana is spent from lands that untap only on their owner's turn, so mana left open on your own turn is the counterspell on theirs.

What the game does for you: priority and the stack are the real ones (both pass in turn, the last spell cast resolves first), but you are asked only where you could act: your own main phases, the attack and the blocks, the end step when you hold something castable, whenever there is a spell on the stack you could answer, and at an upkeep when the card about to be drawn is worth acting on (you know what it is and could draw it first, bury it or shuffle it away, or it is the other player's draw and you hold a Brainstorm, to put two cards of your choosing on top for them, or a Predict, to mill it). Tap a card to see what it does and what it costs, and a spell that needs a target lights the legal ones on the table; mana is paid from the lands that fit, choosing for you the ones you will need least (a dual land last), what a land makes and a cost doesn't use (Izzet Boilerworks' red, paying for a blue spell) floats in your pool, shown beside your lands, until the step ends, and Menu, Hold priority when I cast, lets you cast twice before the other player may answer. The shared library shows how many cards it holds and, when a card on top is known to both (a countered spell, the top three Halimar Depths showed), which; a card you saw go into the other player's hand (drawn from a top you both knew, bounced, found by a search that reveals it) is counted beside their hand size and named in the library's sheet, as a player at a real table would remember it. While a question is up (the mulligan, the cards to put on the bottom, discard or put back, the order of the top three, a surveil) a card tapped opens in a sheet of its own, its full text large, with the question's choice for it ("Put on the bottom", "Put back on top") as the sheet's button; a card in a question's list (a land to fetch, a card to name) has a ? beside it that does the same.
