A question with a list (which land to bounce, which card to name for Predict) is a sheet that can be put away to look at the table and brought back with the Answer button.

What the rules review of October 2026 put right: the upkeep had no stop at all (so a card countered onto the top could never be drawn first in its owner's upkeep, and a Brainstorm could never hand the other player two chosen cards); mana a land made and a cost didn't use was lost instead of floating to the end of the step; Mystic Sanctuary's card was chosen as the trigger resolved instead of as it went on the stack, where the other player can answer it knowing the target; a permanent that left the battlefield and came back was still the old object to a spell aimed at it and to its Dandân's sacrifice trigger; a copy from Narset's Reversal whose target had gone wasn't offered the one other target there was; a bounced card, a card drawn from a top both players had seen and a card found by a search that reveals it were forgotten at once, where a player at a table knows where they went; and (found on 20 October) a Mystic Retrieval flashed back could choose itself as its target, though it is on the stack by the time its target is chosen (`targetLists`' `self`; another Retrieval in the graveyard it may choose).

Where it still plays differently from the paper game, and why (also on the Rules sheet, in the menu):
- There is no stop at the beginning or end of combat or in the draw step, and an upkeep is a stop only when the coming draw is worth acting on (above): what could be done in those steps is done as well at the declarations either side of them or at the end step, and each stop is a tap for nothing. The one play lost: at the beginning of combat, Mind Bending your own last Island so that a Dandân can't attack you (yours dying with it).
- Combat damage among several blockers is assigned for you (enough to kill each in turn); you choose blocks, not the split.
- Predict names no player: it mills the one library whichever it names. Mind Bend changes basic land types; no permanent in this deck has a colour word for it to change.
- Mystic Sanctuary's "may" is answered when its card is chosen, as it triggers: choosing none is declining.
- Holding priority is the cast flag in the menu above and not a separate step.
- Flashback (Mystic Retrieval) and the aftermath half of Commit // Memory are cast from the shared graveyard, so either player may; they are exiled afterwards as printed.
- The opening hands are dealt alternately from the first player, and the first player settles their mulligans before the second decides (paper has both declare in turn each round; with one shuffled library it comes to the same). The loser of the last game goes first, a lot decides the first.
- State-based checks (a Dandân with no toughness left or no Island, a player at 0 life or drawing from an empty library) run once a resolving spell has finished asking its questions, not between them, so a half-resolved spell is never judged.

The engine (`dandan-engine.js`, `dandan-core.js`, `dandan-spells.js`) is pure functions on a plain JSON state, as Seats asks: `deal`, `toMove`, `act`, `moves`, `over`, `restore` (the database drops empty lists, so a state read back is made whole), with `dandan-rules.js` the module `seats.js` runs. The state keeps what each player knows (`kn`, the library cards each has seen, by position; `kh`, the cards each knows to be in the other's hand), each player's floating mana (`pool`, emptied at every step), and an object number on each permanent (`oid`) that a target and a trigger carry, so a card that left and came back is not the one they meant; `act(…, { quiet })` keeps no log, for the games the computer plays out in its head. Every random draw is from the game's seed and the move's number. The cards' effects are one table (`RES`), the questions a seat can be asked (mulligan, bottom, attack, block, discard, put back, order, surveil, search, pick, owner, bend, name, retarget) are `pending` in the state, and `moves()` lists a seat's legal moves one to a card name, which is what the page's buttons and the computer player's choices both come from, so neither can offer a move the rules refuse.

The computer players (Menu, Computer player: Easy, Medium, Hard, Hard by default; rebuilt in October 2026, when the first ones were found Mind Bending a lone Island on the first turn, and reworked again on 20 October, when Hard cast Mystic Retrieval from its hand with Korbi's red on the table and a Piracy Charm in the graveyard, and lost its Dandân to it). All three play from what their seat may know: their hand, the table, the graveyard and exile, the library cards they have seen go there, and the cards they have seen go into the other hand; the rest are the cards they haven't seen, in the other hand or the library at random.

Medium (`dandan-policy.js`) plays by the habits of a good player of the format, worked out afresh at each decision, with no look ahead. It weighs everything in cards (an average card in hand is 1): what a card held is worth by itself and to the hand it's in (a land by how many lands its holder has), a Dandân on the battlefield by whether it can attack (the other player controls its land type) and how safe it is (its last Island), a spell on the stack by what it does for whoever cast it, and the chance the other hand holds a card, counted from the copies not yet seen and the size of that hand. The format's turns of the screw are in it. The library is shared, so a countered spell goes on top for whoever draws next, which is you on their turn and them on yours: it counters their spells on their turn, and holds its own threats back from open mana on its own. Cards put back on top are chosen for who will draw them (Brainstorm's two, Halimar Depths' three, a surveil, Mystic Sanctuary's card), and a known card on top is drawn first in the other player's upkeep. Lands untap only on their owner's turn, so on its own turn it keeps two open for a Memory Lapse it holds (one more for a Piracy Charm or Mind Bend while their Dandân could attack), and spends what's left at the end of theirs. Removal waits for a Dandân to attack, or for the end of their turn, unless they're tapped out on its own turn (nothing can counter it then, which their turn won't be), and the more their next attack would hurt, the sooner it answers. On its own turn Gone Missing or Metamorphose has them draw their own card back instead of a new one, and Deem Inferior is a kill (its owner bottoms it rather than hand it over as the second card). Mind Bend is for a Dandân or for the last Island under one, never for an Island alone. Islands cut both ways: its own keep its Dandâns, and are what let theirs attack it, so with no Dandân of its own it prefers a land that isn't one. It attacks and blocks by the race: what they could swing back with, and a block not made when taking the hit and swinging back wins the game. Easy is the same player made careless: it taps out on its own turn, casts what it can when it can, counters whatever it can, aims removal at anything, attacks with everything, blocks only a lethal attack, and plays a random move one time in eight.

The shared graveyard. A spell cast from the hand ends in the graveyard both players use, and a Mystic Retrieval there is anyone's with a red source and three mana (Thundering Falls, Izzet Boilerworks, an Island Mind Bent into a Mountain). So Medium weighs every cast with what it leaves the other player there (`gift` in `dandan-policy.js`): the best card their Retrieval would find on their next turn, flashed back with their red or cast from their hand (at the chance they hold one), a removal spell they could cast the same turn counted at what it would do to our best Dandân, against what they could find already. Our own Memory Lapse with two left open through their turn halves it (the Retrieval may be countered), and a cast on their turn counts it at six tenths (our own turn comes first). The same arithmetic runs the other way: taking away the card they'd find, or flashing the Retrieval back ourselves so it's exiled, is worth what it denies them; and a Retrieval for a removal spell with the mana left to cast it at once (an instant any time, a sorcery in our main phase) is worth what that spell does now, not what a card in hand is worth. So Medium no longer casts the Retrieval into their red, flashes theirs back for the Charm and kills the Dandân with it the same turn, and takes the Charm out of their reach when it has red of its own. Two smaller habits changed with it: a sorcery-speed removal spell (Gone Missing, Deem Inferior) is no longer held back for a better moment, which for a sorcery only comes after their attack; and a land is weighed by the mana it adds when that mana is wanted, to cast now or to keep open through their turn (a tapped land, or an Izzet Boilerworks that has to return an untapped land, counts against it then). These positions are rare, and Medium against the Medium before them won 1,015 of 2,000 games; its other weights (a Dandân's worth, a land's taken away), tried higher and lower over 2,000 games each, moved nothing beyond chance either: Medium's results hang on its habits more than on its numbers.

Hard (`dandan-think.js`) looks ahead. At each decision it takes the moves worth weighing (Medium's own choice, passing, and the other moves Medium scores within a card of it, seven at most; for a question, its reasonable answers: every set of attackers up to three, each block, every order of three cards, the pairs a Brainstorm could put back), and plays each out to the start of the turn after next in up to 32 deals of the cards it can't see, Medium moving both seats there but for one moment: at the other player's first main phase of their own turn, their four likeliest moves (Medium's own, passing, and the two it scores best after them) are each played out, and the one worst for Hard counts. Medium's habit alone may not find the answer that hurts, and a move that leaves one open then looks as good as a move that doesn't; with their answers tried it can't. (The dealt game knows Hard's hand and the library's order, so the other player chooses knowing them, which makes every move look a little worse, all alike.) A deal keeps every card the seat knows where it is and deals the rest at random; what the other player knew of the library stays with the places they knew. Where each move lands is judged by the chance of winning from there (the judge, below), and the move with the best average is played. The deals and the rest of the game's luck are the same for every move, so the moves are compared on the same footing, and a move that falls clearly behind stops being played out: by twice the standard error of its gap, deal by deal, plus a hundredth, the spread taken as at least 0.06 (over the first few deals two moves can come out alike by chance, and in one position the best move was dropped that way after six). The best is chosen among the moves still in the race, never one dropped on a lucky few deals. Medium's own move stands unless another beats it by more than the deals can explain: two turns ahead is not far, and Medium's habits about what to keep for later are worth more than a gap that is mostly chance. Its mulligans, its cards to the bottom and its names for Predict are Medium's. The seed comes from what the seat sees, so the same position gets the same move on any phone. On the page it thinks in a worker (`dandan-worker.js`), with the runner held until the move is in (seats-ui.js's `hold`), so the table never stops; at a table, the phone that runs it thinks on its own thread. On this machine a decision takes 64 ms at the median, 285 ms one time in ten and 0.8 s at worst, about what it took before it tried the other player's answers, which doubled the work: the rest was found in the code (the unseen cards counted in a typed array, a card's entry looked up once, the creature checks skipped with no creature, the judge's other side swapped rather than worked out again). A phone is perhaps four times slower, and 2.5 seconds is the backstop.

How they measure, over seeded games with each side taking each seat in turn (scratch runs, not kept in the repository; since 20 October each deal is played twice, the seats swapped, so its luck falls to both alike): the Hard of 20 October (the other player's answers tried, the network judge, Medium's graveyard) beat the Hard before it in 70 of 100 games and Medium in 36 of 40; trying the answers alone was worth 47 of 80 games, the network judge alone 31 of 60. Before that, Hard beat Medium in 99 of 120 games, and the computer player that came before (the old Hard) in 91 of 100. Medium beat the old Hard in 234 of 300 and Easy in 237 of 300; Easy beat a player of random legal moves 195 times in 200, Medium all 200. A game between Medium players runs about 25 turns. Some things that were tried and didn't pay: looking three turns ahead instead of two (55 of 100 against two, at half as much time again), twice the deals (48 of 100), and eleven more things for the judge to read (the log loss moved in the fourth place). How well any of them does against a person is not measured.

What a pass shows. A player who lets an opposing Dandân resolve with the mana for a Memory Lapse open very likely holds none (Medium never does, holding one). The engine notes it, in public (`ev`: the count of cards the seat had drawn when it passed, `draws` counting them), and until that player draws again the computer players read their hand as holding no Lapse but seldom (Medium's odds take a seventh of the chance; Hard's deals deal such a hand again, mostly), so the next Dandân goes in without fear of a counter that probably isn't there; the coach says why ("They let your last Dandân resolve with a Lapse's mana open, and haven't drawn since").

The judge (`dandan-eval.js`): the chance a seat wins from the start of a turn, as Medium against Medium would play it on: a score of the situation learned from results, by a small neural network (a value network). It reads 100 numbers off the position. For each side: the hits it can take (life in fours, and how close to the last), its Dandâns on the table, those that can attack and those on their last Island, its lands, Islands and having none, red, the mana its lands make and what of it is open, Clues, and its hand, by size and card by card (each of seventeen spells and seven kinds of land). And what both share: whose turn it is, the library's size, the graveyard card by card, the turn. The network scores a position from one side (the 100 numbers, two hidden layers of 32 and 16, rectified); a seat's chance is the logistic of its score less the other's, so the two always add up to one. It was trained on 1.3 million positions, the start of every turn of 45,000 games of Medium against Medium (one move in twelve and a half one of its three best at random, for variety), each with its game's result, by Adam on the log loss, with a tenth of the games kept out to check it: there its log loss is 0.542, against 0.582 for the logistic model of thirty-five features it replaced (fitted the same way earlier in October) and 0.693 for a coin. Bigger networks did no better (0.543 with layers of 64 and 32, 0.544 with 128 and 64): the results are as noisy as the game. Its weights are rounded to signed bytes with a scale for each output (6 KB of base64 in the file), which moves a chance by a third of a point on average and leaves the log loss as it was. Hard plays about as well with it as with the old model (a first network of the kind beat it in 31 of 60 games of Hard against Hard); what it changes is the figures, a Dandân, a land or a card now worth what it is worth in that hand at that point of the game, and so the chances the coach shows. A game played out in the head has dealt the other hand, so the judge reads both.

The coach (Menu, Coach: Off, Hints, Check, Always; Hints by default), as Hold'em's. Hints: nothing until you ask; Hint, in the coach's line above the buttons, gives its move for the decision you're at with the one thing that decides it and its chance of winning ("Cast Memory Lapse on their Dandân · Countered now, their Dandân goes on top, and you draw it · 73% to win"), and Why? after it: every move it weighed, its own first, each with its chance of winning, ± (the standard error: how far an average over that many deals may stray by chance), the deals it was played in, and in how many of them the coach's move did better and worse. Check: Hint too, and after a move of yours it would not have made, its word in oxblood, so it isn't taken for advice: how much worse it reckons yours, and why ("Casting Mind Bend on their Island: about 10 points worse than playing Island (63% to win against 72%). An Island turned into a Swamp still makes mana for them: keep Mind Bend for a Dandân, or for their last Island under one."). Always: the same, and at each of your decisions its move without asking, ringed on the table: the card in your hand, its target, the button, the row of a question's sheet. Off: nothing, and no Hint. A move counts as agreeing when it is the coach's or as good as near as the deals can tell (no more than 3 points worse, and not two standard errors clear of it), so a close call is never called a mistake; a word stays through the moves that follow it (a spell and the questions it asks) until another replaces it or the turn after next, and a game ends with how often it agreed with your decisions, Where it differed listing the three that cost most. Its reasons are worked out from the table, the move and the format's rules of thumb (`dandan-coach.js`, each about one thing, two about the same thing being one): who draws a countered card, the free mana at the end of their turn, the two kept for Memory Lapse, their open mana against a spell cast on your turn (with the chance they hold a Lapse), the last Island under a Dandân, a Mind Bend on an Island that kills nothing, a Dandân attacking you, the swing back, the race, a known top card drawn first, and the shared graveyard ("The Mystic Retrieval stays in the graveyard: their red flashes it back for the Piracy Charm, and your Dandân dies to it", "It brings back the Piracy Charm with the mana left to cast it: their Dandân is answered this turn"). Then what the deals showed (`evidence`): each deal remembers which of the other player's answers hurt most and where it left the game two turns on (a Dandân of yours on the table, one of theirs, both lives and hands), and the word cites the answer when it was the same in many of the deals and seldom after the better move ("Their best answer to yours, in 20 of the 64 deals: they cast Mystic Retrieval for Piracy Charm with flashback"), and the biggest difference in the better move's favour when it is big enough to say ("Two turns on, you have a Dandân on the table in 23% of the deals after yours; after playing Island, in 63%"), three reasons at most; the Why sheet the same for the coach's move against yours or the next best. Where neither applies, the word says how often each came out ahead ("Played out, passing came out ahead in 41 of the 64 deals and behind in 9"). It weighs a decision as Hard does, wider (nine moves) and in up to 64 deals, in its own worker (210 ms on average here, 1.7 s at worst); the figures are Medium's chances, a model of these computer players and not of people, which is the point at this table and nothing to rely on at another. A partner's frame, watched from the games screen, coaches nobody; at a table with a friend each phone's coach is its own, and it sees only what its seat does.

The page (`dandan.html`, `dandan.js`, `dandan.css`): the other player's side across the top (their life, hand size, lands and creatures), the stack and the two piles (library and graveyard, tap one to look through it) in the middle, your lands and creatures below it, your hand, the coach's line (two lines high whatever it says, hidden when the coach is off) and the buttons (pass, attack, answer). It never scrolls: the log runs in its own box and the sheets scroll inside themselves, from 390×600 up. Sideways, on a tablet or a screen held across, the table is on the left and the log, the stack and the buttons in a column beside it (`beside`). It follows all three themes in day and night; the card faces use the suite's tokens (the blue and red enamel for mana, verdigris and oxblood for what is good and bad), with no card art, and the tile's logo is a card face with a fish drawn on it in the game's cobalt (Kontor's icon the same card as a line drawing). The page stands still while the menu, the rules or the games screen is open (`onPause`, though nothing here runs by itself except the computer player, which waits).

Saved: `dandan:game` (the game, as every seat game keeps it, so another of your devices or a partner watching sees it as it stands), `dandan:opts` (the computer player's level, the coach, hold priority), `dandan:record` (games played and won) and `dandan:streak` (the run of wins, which the games screen shows, `noteStreak`). A watching frame (`IN_FRAME`) reads the partner's game and its writes go nowhere. Playing together: Invite players, or a party (README, Party), seats two at a table (`rooms.js`, and Party's `ELSEWHERE` in `party-core.js`), the computer in the seat nobody has.

`tests/dandan.mjs` checks the list (eighty cards in the list's counts, and the costs), each card's effect on its own and the engine's rules, with scenes set up by hand (`tests/dandan-scene.js`: the Island rule on attack and on the Dandân's own sacrifice, Mind Bend on each of Dandân, Island and the duals, Memory Lapse's top card going to the next drawer, Commit second from the top, Narset's Reversal copying and returning and a copy's new target, Deem Inferior's discount, the fetch, island-cycling and cycling lands, Mystic Sanctuary's target chosen as it triggers and fizzling when the card has gone, the upkeep's stops and a countered card drawn first in its owner's upkeep, floating mana, what each player knows of the other's hand, a pass on a Dandân with a Lapse's mana open noted until the passer draws, a returning permanent being a new object, the mulligans, the stack's order and priority, the questions), then the computer players: Medium and Hard each on positions with one right answer (no Mind Bend on a lone Island on turn 2 or at the end of the opponent's turn, and Mind Bend on their last Island under two Dandâns or on a Dandân with three Islands under it; Memory Lapse on a Dandân cast on its owner's turn; the +2/−1 on a Dandân attacking; a Dandân cast only with an Island to keep it; a Dandân kept home when the swing back would be lethal, a lethal attacker blocked, and no block when taking the hit and swinging back wins; a known Memory Lapse on top drawn first in the other player's upkeep; Narset's Reversal on a Lapse aimed at its Dandân, and the copy aimed at the Reversal itself; two kept open for Memory Lapse over a Take Inventory; Izzet Boilerworks returning a tapped Island, not itself; no Mystic Retrieval cast from the hand while the other player has red and a Piracy Charm lies in the graveyard; and, playing a whole turn, the other side flashing the Retrieval back for the Charm and killing the Dandân with it, and the Charm taken back with our own red to kill theirs), a flashed-back Retrieval never its own target, Hard's move the same twice from the same position and always legal, every kind of question answered legally at each level, then whole games by seed: a hundred games of Medium against Medium with no move refused, no card lost or made (all eighty accounted for every 25 moves), every game finished and neither seat winning fewer than a quarter, a seeded game played the same way twice, Easy against random play (34 of 40 at least), Medium against Easy (62 of 100) and Hard against Medium (8 of 12). `tests/dandan-coach.mjs` checks the coach: its words for every kind of move, its advice legal and the same each time, that what the seat can't see (the other hand, the library's order) changes nothing (with the other player's answers tried too), that a deal keeps what the seat knows where it is and makes or loses no card, that a player who passed on a Dandân is mostly dealt no Lapse, the judge (the two seats' chances adding up to one at every move of two games, a Dandân on the table counting for and against, a finished game certain), the verdict's margin, the reasons on positions with one right answer, what the deals showed (where each move left the game, and the words for the biggest difference), and the Retrieval cast into their red called out: the graveyard's reason, their answer (the flashback for the Charm) and where it left your Dandân. A state read back from the database is made whole by `restore` and moves on as the original does.

## Factor
An island-trading game (October 2026), three or four at a table, against the computer or with friends each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, the computer taking the seats nobody has). It plays by the rules of the genre that Catan made well known and that Colonist plays in a browser; the names, the pictures and the code are its own (`factor.html`, `factor.js` and `factor.css`; the island's geometry in `factor-board.js`, the rules in `factor-rules.js`, the computer players in `factor-bot.js`, the island's picture in `factor-draw.js`, the rules on one page in `factor-sheet.js`). The name is a trader's: a factor was a merchant's agent on a far coast, which is what you are. Its tile and logos are in `apps.js`, its entry in `rooms.js` (`GAMES`) and its place at a party's table in `party-core.js` (`ELSEWHERE`).

The island is nineteen hexes in rows of 3, 4, 5, 4 and 3: four of timber and of wool, three of clay and of iron, four of grain, and one of dunes where the blockade starts, each with a number token from 2 to 12 (not 7), the odds shown as pips. Where 6 and 8 would touch, the numbers are dealt again, so the two likeliest are never neighbours. Nine harbours ring the coast: four that trade any resource 3 for 1 and five that trade their own 2 for 1. A game is dealt from a seed, so the same seed is the same island on every phone.

Each seat in turn places a post (on a corner, never beside another building) and a road beside it, then each again in the opposite order; the second post pays one card for each land hex it touches, so a seat puts it beside what it lacks. A turn: roll two dice, and every post at a hex with that number is paid one of its resource, every factory two (a factory is a post made larger: 2 grain and 3 iron, for 2 points); a post is 1 point. Then, in any order: build, trade, buy a card, play a card; then End. A road costs timber and clay, a post timber, clay, wool and grain, a card wool, grain and iron. A road joins your own roads or buildings and another player's building at its end stops it. There are 15 roads, 5 posts and 4 factories to a seat. The bank holds 19 of each resource; if a roll owes more than it has, a lone player gets what is left and several get nothing, as the genre has it.

A seven pays nobody. Anyone holding eight cards or more gives up half (rounded down; you tap the cards to give up), then the roller moves the blockade to another hex, which pays nothing while it is there, and takes one card, at random, from a player with a building on it. Trading is the bank's 4 for 1, 3 for 1 at a 3:1 harbour and 2 for 1 at that resource's own; or an offer to the table, on your own turn only: what you give and what you want, and each of the others in turn says yes or no. One yes settles it; several and you choose among them. This differs from the genre's free haggling on purpose: each seat has to answer on its own phone, so a counter-offer would have the whole table waiting on two people, and it is limited to four offers a turn (`MAX_OFFERS`) so a table of friends can't be held up by a bargain that never comes.

The 25 cards (bought blind from one deck, never played the turn they were bought, one a turn, a Privateer allowed before you roll): fourteen Privateers (move the blockade and take a card; three played make the **largest fleet**, 2 points, which passes to anyone who plays more), five Patents (a point each, kept hidden until they win), two Causeways (two roads for nothing), two Windfalls (any two resources from the bank) and two Corners (name a resource: every other player gives you all of it). The **longest road**, five roads in a line at the least, is 2 points and passes to anyone who gets a longer one; a rival's building on the road cuts it, and a road that loops counts each road once. First to 8, 10 or 12 points (the menu; 10 by default) wins, on their own turn: a point that comes to a seat on someone else's turn (the longest road passing to it when a rival's road is cut, say) is counted when its turn begins. The say-lines are in the past tense and agree with "You" and with names, since the narrator speaks them on whichever phone runs the table.

The computer players (`factor-bot.js`) are Medium and Hard (19 October 2026, Korbi: "ensure bots are medium and hard, and hard ones are extremely strong"; Easy went, and a menu still set to it plays Medium). They see what their seat may: its own hand and cards, the board, everyone's points without hidden patents, and what a player counting cards would know of the others' hands. Medium is the earlier Hard: it plans by the numbers (each corner by its pips, a resource it lacks and a harbour it can use; roads toward the best corner still open; the longest road when it can take it), buys what its hand can pay for or reach with the bank, makes one offer to the table a turn, plays its cards for what they win, refuses trades to a player within three points of winning, and puts the blockade where it costs the leader most.

Hard weighs every legal move of its turn (each road, post and factory, each bank trade, each card it could play, buying a card) by the position it leaves, and takes the best, ending the turn when nothing beats standing still. A position is worth its points first and far above all else; then what its land makes a turn (each resource's pips, the scarcer on this island the dearer, more for a new kind than another of the same, the robbed hex left out), less what the leaders make; the best corners it could build on now, one road away and two; how far its hand is from its nearest post, factory, card or road, counted in the rounds its land takes to make what's missing (the strongest single term: it keeps its hand pointed at its next build); its harbours, its road and fleet and the races for the two-point prizes; its cards; and the risk of a 7 (more than seven cards loses half, about two times in five before its next turn). Buying a card is valued without looking at the deck: its cost, a card in hand, and a patent's point at the odds of the cards printed. It trades with the table as a strong player does: it tries each offer of one or two of a kind for one of another on a copy of the others' minds (how Medium answers), offers the one worth most to it that someone would take, up to four a turn, and takes an offer only when it gains and the one offering isn't near winning. For its setup posts and the blockade it looks ahead: each of its best few choices is played on (the setup and fourteen turns, or eight after the blockade) twenty-four times, every seat by Medium's rules and its own by its own, with fresh dice and the cards nobody at the table can see (the deck, the others' unplayed cards) dealt anew, the same twenty-four futures for every choice (common random numbers, so the difference is the choice's and not the luck's), and takes the one whose futures end best. Playing whole turns out that way chose worse than the ranking (the futures are too noisy to tell building now from building next turn), so its turns don't. Its weights were tuned by playing thousands of games against Medium.

How strong: in four-player games of Hard against three Medium players, rotating the seats, Hard won 46.5% of 400 (a fair share is 25%; the earlier Hard won 33.5% against the same players, and Hard without its look-ahead 39%). It is quick for a turn and slower for a setup post (a second or two). Medium is no pushover either: it is what Hard was until this.

The page: a plate for each seat across the top (its colour, name, points, cards in hand, cards bought, the longest road and the fleet, with a ring on whoever's turn it is and a dashed one on whoever must answer), a line saying what is asked or what just happened (with the dice), the island, your five resources and your cards, and your buttons. Tapping Road, Post or Factory rings the places it may go on the island; tap one. The buttons that you can't press yet are dimmed and say, when tapped, what is missing. Your hand shows each change for a moment (+2 timber, −1 grain). Card opens your cards (the Windfall and the Corner ask which resources), Trade the bank's rates or an offer to the table. Held sideways, the island is on the left and the players, what things cost, your hand and your buttons beside it. The island is one picture sized to its box, whichever way the box is short; a tap on a corner is the fill of its circle alone, since a stroke would reach the neighbouring corners (they are one unit apart).

The island in 3D (19 October 2026, Korbi: "make Factor 3D, subtle table, real dice, the roads and towns proper there, tiles; a 2D mode in settings for battery saving"): `factor-3d.js`, on the vendored three.js the other 3D games use, with no model or texture files, so it's there offline. A quiet table (walnut in Almanac, pale oak in Modern, baize in Kontor, grey linen in Office, its grain or weave worked out in the shader and kept low in contrast), a sea in a wooden frame with a brass line, and nineteen bevelled tiles standing a little apart, each with its land on it: pines on the forest, stacks of bricks on the clay pits, sheep on the pasture, sheaves on the fields, rock on the hills and mounds on the dunes, all kept clear of the token in the middle. Tokens are discs; harbours are jetties out to a round landing, cream for a 3:1 and in the resource's colour for a 2:1. The pieces stand up and throw shadows: a road is a beam along its edge, a post a house, a factory a long shed with a saw-tooth roof and a chimney, all in the seats' colours, and a piece just built drops on with a little bounce. The blockade is a dark ship with oxblood sails, turned broadside to you, and sails in an arc to the hex it's moved to. Two ivory dice lie on the table in the frame's free corner, bottom right; every roll throws them in tumbling, bouncing and settling on the numbers rolled (a check reads which face is up), so the line above the island no longer shows the dice in 3D. The camera is fitted to the land and its harbours, not the frame or the dice: the island fills the whole width of the box (the frame and the table run off its edges), or its height where the box is too short for that, the camera's height (46 to 72 degrees) and whether the island sits flat side or point toward you chosen to make it largest; the dice lie in whichever corner of the view is furthest from the land. Two fingers pinch to zoom (to 3.2 times) and move the island, the mouse wheel zooms where it points, one finger moves it once zoomed, and a double tap puts it back; a tap that didn't move is still a tap on a ringed place, and a drag or a pinch never is. What must read sharply and every place a tap may go are an SVG over the canvas, placed through the camera's own projection: the tokens' numbers and pips (6 and 8 in oxblood, dimmed under the blockade), the harbours' rates, the last move's ring and the ringed corners, edges and hexes with the same data-v, data-e and data-h as the flat island, so the page takes a tap the same way in either. Colours come from the page (the land from factor.css, the seats from style.css) with each theme's light by day and by night, and it follows the theme as it changes. It draws only while something moves (a roll, a piece dropping, the ship sailing), and asks the device for its low-power graphics. The menu's "Flat island, to save battery" (`flat` in `factor:opts`) puts back the island as it was, the SVG of `factor-draw.js`, with the dice in the line; a device that can't draw in 3D gets the flat island on its own. The 3D drawing isn't covered by tests; it was looked at in software WebGL at 390 by 600, 664 and 844, at 820 by 1180 and at 1180 by 820, in all four themes by day and by night, the dice checked to settle on the roll over several rolls, and the switch to flat and back clicked through. Not yet seen on a phone's own GPU.

Saved state: the game as `factor:game` (seats.js), the menu's choices as `factor:opts` (points to win, players, computer level, the narrator's voice, flat or 3D) and your record as `factor:record`.

`tests/factor.mjs` checks the island over 300 seeds (nineteen hexes, 54 corners, 72 edges, the harbours, 6 and 8 never touching), the setup (the snake, the second post's payout), the roll (payout, a factory's double, the blocked hex, the bank's shortage), the seven (who discards, the blockade, the steal), building (costs, the distance rule, the piece limits), the longest road (cut by a rival, a tie, passing on), the cards (not the turn bought, one a turn, the largest fleet), trading (every rate, offers answered seat by seat), the win on your own turn, the state through the database at every move, and whole games of every level at three and four seats, every move legal, resources conserved and the same seed the same game. Not yet: a course (Learn), and a result on the games screen.

## Contract
Contract bridge (October 2026), four at a table in two partnerships, North–South against East–West, against the computer or with friends each on their own phone (Invite players: seats.js runs it, the computer taking the seats nobody has). You sit South with your partner North across the table. Two people brought by a party sit across from each other as partners (Party's `across`); anyone else sits down in the lowest seat free.

A match is Chicago, the four-deal match clubs play when four sit down for an evening: the dealer moves clockwise, and the vulnerability (which raises both what a side's games and slams score and what its contracts cost going down) follows the deals, nobody on the first, the dealer's side on the second and third, both on the fourth. Each deal is scored as duplicate bridge scores it, its game or part-score bonus on the deal itself, and the running score adds them; the side ahead after four deals wins the match, and a run of matches won shows on the games screen. A deal all four pass is dealt again by the same dealer. The rules, the whole scoring table among them, are on the Rules sheet (the menu).

The auction: from the dealer, each player in turn passes, bids (one to seven tricks over six, in clubs, diamonds, hearts, spades or notrump, each bid higher than the last), doubles the other side's last bid or redoubles their double of their own; three passes after a bid end it. Of the side that won the contract, whoever first named its strain is declarer; the player on declarer's left leads, then dummy (declarer's partner) lays down their cards and declarer plays both hands. Thirteen tricks: follow suit if you can, the highest card of the suit led takes the trick unless a trump is played. Made, the contract scores its tricks (20 a trick in the minors, 30 in the majors, 40 then 30 in notrump; doubled twice that, redoubled four times), 300 more for a game (100 or more in tricks; 500 vulnerable), 50 for a part-score, 500 or 1000 for a small or a grand slam (750, 1500 vulnerable), 50 or 100 for making it doubled or redoubled, and its overtricks; down, the defenders score 50 a trick (100 vulnerable), doubled 100, 200, 200, then 300 (vulnerable 200, then 300), redoubled twice that (`dealScore` in `contract-core.js`).

The page: your partner across the top, the opponents left and right on the felt, your hand along the foot. While it's bid, the auction is drawn in the middle as bridge writes it, a column a seat from your left, and every call in it says what it showed when tapped; under your hand, the bidding box: a level, then a strain (a second tap on it bids it), or Pass, Double or Redouble. Once the lead is made dummy's cards lie face up: as a bridge column (a suit a line, high to low) by its seat, or across the top as cards to play when it's yours to play, your side declaring. The trick lies in the middle, each card on its player's side, the card taking it ringed. The line across the top has the deal and who's vulnerable, the contract (tap it for the auction and what every call meant), the tricks each side has taken, and the match. After a deal its result shows on the felt with where its points came from; The four hands (and Review) shows the deal, the auction, the opening lead marked, and the tricks the contract makes with every card seen. Held sideways the auction stays beside the table through the play, and the trick, the auction and the seats grow with the table. The narrator announces each deal, the contract and the result, if you like (the menu). The game lives in `contract.html`, `contract.js` and `contract.css`, its rules in `contract-core.js` (cards, calls, the play, the score) and `contract-rules.js` (the match on seats.js).

The bidding (`contract-sayc.js`) is SAYC, the Standard American Yellow Card, the system American clubs and most online play teach first, written as tables of rules. Each rule is a call and what it shows: a range of high-card points (an ace 4, a king 3, a queen 2, a jack 1, 40 in the pack), lengths in each suit, a balanced hand (no short suit), a stopper in the other side's suit (a card that stops them running it), or a test of its own. A computer player calls the first rule that fits its hand and is legal; the same tables read every call, so what a call shows is what the rules that make it at that point have in common, and what a player has shown is every one of their calls read together (a seat that opened 1♥ and then passed partner's invitation has shown 11 to 14). The openings: 1NT 15–17 balanced, 2NT 20–21, 2♣ strong and artificial (22 or more, or nine tricks in hand), five-card majors and the longer minor, light in third seat, the rule of 20 in first and second (points plus the two longest suits' lengths), the rule of 15 in fourth (points plus spades), weak twos in diamonds, hearts and spades, preempts at the three and four level. The answers: raises (single 6–10, limit 10–12, preemptive to four, Jacoby 2NT with four trumps and an opening, game forcing), a new suit (forcing; at the two level 10 or more), 1NT 6–10, 2NT and 3NT by points, Stayman (2♣ over 1NT: a four-card major?) and Jacoby transfers (2♦ and 2♥ for hearts and spades, 2♠ for a minor), the feature ask over a weak two, opener's rebids by strength, reverses, jump shifts. Competing: overcalls (a good five-card suit, 8–16), the 1NT overcall (15–18 with their suit stopped), takeout doubles (short in their suit, support for the others, or any strong hand), the negative double (responder's takeout double after their overcall), cue-bid raises, the reopening double, the penalty double of 1NT, and leaving partner's penalty double in. Slams: Blackwood (4NT asks for aces, 5NT then for kings), the quantitative 4NT over notrump, slam tries over transfers. A call no rule makes still reads as natural. Over 2000 random deals bid by four computer players, every call was legal and 1% were passed out; partnerships with 26–32 points between them bid game or slam 93% of the time with eight cards in a major and 90% without, with 23–25 60% and 40%. A call takes a quarter of a millisecond.

The play by the computer players (`contract-play.js`): Easy plays by the rules of thumb (the opening lead's, second hand low and an honour covered, third hand high, the cheapest winner fourth, declarer drawing trumps, winners cashed, partner's suit returned, discards from the weakest suit). Medium and Hard play by perfect-information Monte Carlo, the way the strongest bridge programs play: before each card they deal the cards they can't see as they could lie (each hand's size, the suits a player has shown out of, and what the auction showed: each hand's points and suit lengths, relaxed a step at a time if no deal fits), solve every deal double dummy (with every card seen, both sides at their best: how many tricks each card they may play takes), and play the card that reaches their side's goal (the contract made, or beaten) in the most deals, then the one with the most tricks; among cards as good, the one convention plays (the top of a sequence, the lowest that does the job, a high spot card to encourage partner's suit when holding an honour in it). Medium weighs up to 12 deals in under half a second a card, Hard up to 48 in a second. Every level leads by the rules (`openingLead`: partner's suit, the top of a sequence, fourth best from length, never away from an ace against a suit, the longest suit against notrump): over 150 deals a lead weighed by sampling (Hard's, 48 deals) left declarer as many tricks double dummy as the rules' lead (9.29 against 9.28, the rules' lead the best there is on 110 of them), so it isn't worth a second's wait, and the rules' lead is the one partner can read and the course teaches.

The solver is DDS, Bo Haglund's and Soren Hein's double-dummy solver (Apache 2.0), compiled to WebAssembly by the npm package @bridge-tools/dd (MIT) and shipped unchanged in `vendor/dds/` with its licences and a note of where it came from (`NOTE.txt`): a single-threaded build, so it needs nothing GitHub Pages can't send, started by `contract-dds.js` with the few imports it asks for (the package's own JavaScript isn't used). A deal of thirteen cards a hand takes it about a tenth of a second on a laptop, a late trick a few milliseconds. It runs in a worker (`contract-worker.js`), started once, so the page never stands still: the computer players think while the table waits its pace (solo the table holds their card until it's in; at a table, the phone that runs it waits as long as the level may think and a little more, and only a card still not in by then is played by the rules of thumb), and the hint and the review's every-card-seen line ask the same worker. Offline, the worker and the solver are kept as every page's files are (`tools/offline.mjs` follows the worker's and the solver's addresses). Against each other (`tools/contract-arena.mjs`, every deal bid by SAYC and played twice with the sides swapped, the difference counted in IMPs, international match points, the scale team games score by, which keeps one big swing from drowning the rest): Medium gained 4.5 IMPs a deal on Easy over 30 deals (better on 26, worse on 1), Hard 5.3 (better on 29, worse on none); Hard against Medium came out level, 0.2 IMPs a deal over 40 deals (better on 8, worse on 5, the same on 27): past a dozen deals, more deals weighed tell less than the deals' own luck. Declaring against Easy's defence they took 0.9 and 0.8 tricks a deal more than double dummy (the defence's slips taken); against each other, 0.1.

Hints (the menu's Hints, on at first): in the auction, Hint lights the call the system makes with your hand in the box and says what it shows partner ("1♠: new suit (6+ points, 4+ spades; forcing)"; a pass by its reading, "up to 12 points", or "no bid fits this hand"); in the play, the card Hard would play in your seat (48 deals, a second and a half), ringed, with how often it makes or beats the contract over the deals weighed and the next best card's figure; on the opening lead, the rules' lead. Tap any call in the auction for what it showed, as the system reads it.

The course (Learn, in the header; October 2026): bridge from nothing, on the course sheet the card games share (`course.js`; README, Landlord) with a stage of its own (`contract-course.js`): examples of cards and calls with their names, the auction as a bridge column with your call to make, a bidding box, dummy and your hand, the trick. 28 lessons in nine chapters: the game (thirteen tricks, trumps, the auction, dummy and the opening lead), valuing a hand (high-card points, balanced hands), opening the bidding (one of a suit, notrump, strong and weak openings), answering partner (a major opened, a new suit, a minor opened, opener's second bid), notrump bidding (Stayman, Jacoby transfers), competing (overcalls and doubles, the negative double, slams and Blackwood), declarer play (counting winners, drawing trumps, the finesse, ruffing in dummy, setting up a long suit), defence (opening leads, second hand low and third hand high, signals), the score (what a contract scores, Chicago); then a first match. A step is words over cards, a hand to tap through (every honour, every card that may follow), a call to make in the box (judged against the system's own call for that hand and auction), a card to play from your hand or dummy (Hint marks it), or a choice. Where you are is kept (`contract:course`).

Puzzle rush (the menu; October 2026): as the other games' rushes, on the same sheet (`rush-sheet.js`) with every game's rush settings (README, Puzzle rush), with 399 positions (`contract-puzzles.js`: 283 declaring, 116 defending) from 400 of the computer players' own deals. `tools/contract-puzzles.mjs` plays deals the computer players bid by SAYC and play at Medium; from the seventh trick on, each decision with a choice is weighed over 16 deals of the cards its player can't see, dealt as that player could know them (each hand's size, the suits shown out of, what the auction showed) and each solved double dummy; one whose best card makes or beats the contract in 25 points more of the deals than any other is weighed again over 160 fresh deals and kept if it still is, four standard errors clear, deal by deal against the next best. Cards that did as well in every deal (touching cards, small cards alike) are all as good. At most two are kept from a deal, a trick or more apart, a defender's beside a declarer's where there's one, each with three cards or more to choose from. A puzzle is its deal, its auction and the cards played so far, the seats turned so its player is South (declaring, from your hand or dummy's, or defending), put back together by the rules (`contract-rush.js`) and drawn on the course's stage: the contract with its auction in a line, dummy, the trick, your hand; tap a card, then Play. The words say what your side has and needs, and the cards still out (what a player keeping count would know). The verdict: how often the best and yours (or, when you found it, the next best) made or beat the contract over its deals, and what the best does (takes the trick, lets it go, trumps it, draws trumps, cashes a winner, throws the card that isn't needed). Ratings are by rank: Easy's rule of thumb and Medium missing it, a closer call, more cards to choose from and to hold make one harder, the bank cut into ten. The sheet's page part is `contract-rush-ui.js`.

`tests/contract.mjs` checks the duplicate score of part-scores, games, slams, doubled and redoubled contracts, overtricks and undertricks by vulnerability, and where a result's points came from; the calls (what may be called, a redouble only by the doubled side, the end of the auction, a passed-out deal) and the contract and its declarer; following suit and who takes a trick; SAYC's call in sample positions and what calls show (1NT, Stayman, Jacoby 2NT, the negative and the takeout double, a pass in first seat, an unknown call read as natural); 60 deals bid and played by four computer players, every call and card legal, every card once, each scored as the table says; the solver against a plain search of small endings in every strain, mid-trick too, and on deals whose answer is known; the deals of the unseen hands (each hand's size, the known cards kept, a void known); whole deals played by sampling and a cold grand slam taking all thirteen; the opening leads; the Chicago match (four deals, the dealer clockwise, the vulnerability by deal, the running score, a passed-out deal dealt again); a match through the database's round trip; a whole deal at a table of two people and two computer players on the stand-in database, both phones seeing it the same; and the course: every call it asks for is the system's own and no other is accepted, every card to play is in the hand that plays it and judged right, every card judged right takes as many tricks as the best with every card seen where the lesson gives all four hands, every quiz's answer an option and every score it quotes the scoring's own; and the puzzle rush: every puzzle a position its deal reaches (one pack, the auction and every card legal), South's card (or dummy's, South declaring), the cards weighed exactly those it may play, the best ahead of every card not as good by 25 points of the deals, what the best does found again, the stage and the verdict judging alike, every rating stocked, and a puzzle of each rating weighed again over 60 deals of its own, the best still ahead.


## Classics
The canonical puzzle genres (October 2026), in a section of their own on the games screen: games you know before you open them, played by their usual rules. Korbi weighed a roster of newspaper and app puzzles and kept every one, with the crossword moved to Learning because it is built on the knowledge base (README, Crossword). What Almanac adds is the making and the company. Every puzzle is generated, not dealt from a fixed pack, and proven to have exactly one answer; difficulty is measured by the reasoning a solver needs to finish it, not by how many clues it has; each has a daily, the same for everyone (`today()`), and endless play besides. Each can be played together, mostly by splitting what each player sees (one has the row clues, the other the column clues) so that neither can finish alone, raced on the same puzzle (README, Head to head), and many at a party; the computer players are the games' own solvers, paced like a person. Where a genre has content (a picture, a theme, a final answer) it comes from the knowledge base. Everything works offline.

The fifteen, with an Almanac name wherever the genre's best-known name is someone's trademark (Wordle, Spelling Bee, Strands and Connections are the New York Times's, Jumble is Tribune's, Mastermind a board game's):
- Number grids: Sudoku, Calcudoku (the genre KenKen made famous) and Kakuro.
- Logic grids: Hitori, Nonogram and Mines (Minesweeper).
- Code-breaking: Bulls (Mastermind, named after Bulls and Cows, the pencil-and-paper game it came from).
- Words: Assay (Wordle's genre: an assay tests what a sample is made of), Yield (Spelling Bee's: how many words seven letters give), Codeword, Ladder (Lewis Carroll's word ladders, his Doublets), Isomer (Jumble's: the same letters rearranged, as an isomer is the same atoms rearranged), Lode (Strands': the theme's words run through the board like ore, the word that spans it the mother lode), Lots (Connections': sixteen words sorted into lots of four by wordplay, where Crates sorts them by what they point to) and Acrostic.

Fifteen games were built at once, each by its own Claude, so the pieces several of them share are each kept by one game and only used by the others: `wordlist.js`, the English word list every word game draws on (below); the clue bank for ordinary words, which Crossword and Acrostic use; the digit-grid board Sudoku made, which Calcudoku and Kakuro use; the grid filler Codeword made, which Crossword uses.

`wordlist.js` holds every English word of 3 to 15 letters, lower-case a to z only (no names, abbreviations, hyphens or apostrophes), each with how common it is: the SCOWL size it first appears at (10, the commonest few thousand, then 20, 35, 40, 50, 55, 60 and 70, the widest list kept), and whether it is one spelling's only (colour, color). A game picks the commonest tiers for its answers and a wider one for what it accepts. The words themselves are in `wordlist-data.js`, written by `tools/build-wordlist.mjs` from SCOWL (Kevin Atkinson's Spell Checker Oriented Word Lists, used under its permissive licence, whose notice travels in the file); words on a list of offensive terms are left out. `tests/wordlist.mjs` checks the lists are clean, sorted and tiered, and that Slate's own common words are all among the commonest here.

### Sudoku
Fill the grid so that every row, every column and every region holds each digit once (October 2026). Four rules, chosen in the menu (Rules, one of): **Classic**, the regions the boxes; **Diagonals** (Sudoku X), the two long diagonals units too, their cells shaded; **Jigsaw**, the regions pieces of any shape; **Killer**, no digits to start with, only cages, each with the sum of its cells and no digit twice in it. Each at two sizes (Size): 9×9, and 6×6 for a few minutes (boxes two rows by three columns, digits 1 to 6). The grid is drawn on the digit grid (below), which Calcudoku and Kakuro share. There is nothing to choose from the knowledge base: the genre's content is its digits. Letters in place of digits (Wordoku) were weighed and left out: they slow the scanning the puzzle is made of, and a hidden word turns a logic puzzle into an anagram.

**How a grid is made, with one solution.** `sudoku-solve.js` is an exact solver: candidates as bit masks, it settles what's forced (a cell with one candidate, a digit with one place in a unit, a cage's sums) and tries the cell with fewest candidates, counting solutions up to two; a pared-down 9×9 takes it well under a millisecond. Every grid starts as a random solution (the same solver filling an empty grid in a random order). For the digit grids its digits are then taken away in pairs, each the mirror of the other through the centre (a printed grid's convention; a jigsaw's pieces aren't symmetric, so its digits go singly), for as long as the solver still finds exactly one solution. Jigsaw's pieces are made from the boxes by swapping a cell on the edge of one piece for a cell on the same edge of its neighbour, each piece kept in one piece, 81 swaps; a cut with fewer than a third of its cells moved out of their boxes isn't a jigsaw, and not every cut can be filled at all (about one in four can't), so a solution is searched for before it's used. Killer cuts its solution into cages grown from random cells to sizes drawn from the level's odds (two to four cells, mostly two and three for an Easy grid), never a digit twice in a cage; a cell left on its own pairs up with a neighbour or joins a cage; then, wherever the solver finds two solutions, the cage of a cell where they differ is split in two (the longest first, so it doesn't leave single cells), until one is left. A single-cell cage is a digit given in all but name: an Easy Killer keeps up to four, a Medium two, a Hard one. The same seed always makes the same grid (`sudoku-grid.js`'s random numbers are integer arithmetic), which is how today's grid and a match's are the same on every phone, and how a link (`#s=SEED&r=RULES&z=SIZE&l=LEVEL`, or `#d=DAY`) brings one back.

**How hard a grid is, and the levels.** Difficulty is the hardest technique a person needs, never the number of givens. `sudoku-logic.js` is a human-style solver that always takes the easiest step it can find, each technique a function that finds one step and says why, in the tiers solvers commonly agree on (HoDoKu's, for one): rank 1, singles (the last cell of a unit, a hidden single, a naked single); 2, locked candidates (pointing and claiming, and the same between any two units that cross, a jigsaw piece and a row, a diagonal and a box), naked and hidden pairs and triples; 3, naked and hidden quads, the X-wing, swordfish and jellyfish, the XY-wing, XYZ-wing and W-wing, the short single-digit chains (the skyscraper, the kite, the turbot fish) and simple colouring; 4, X-chains of three to six strong links and XY-chains. Killer adds its own: a cage's sum read exactly (the ways it can add up, given what's left in each cell), a digit a cage must hold lying in one unit (rank 1 and 2), and the rule of 45 (a unit adds up to 45, so the cells its cages leave over add up to the rest: a single unit at rank 2, two or three rows or columns side by side at rank 3). A grid that needs more than all of these (a forcing chain, a guess) is never dealt. On a 9×9 the four levels are the four ranks: Easy (singles, and it keeps 36 digits, so it's gentle as well), Medium, Hard and Expert. A grid is pared to its last digit, then graded; one harder than its level gets a digit back where the solver first needed more than the level allows (or where it got stuck), until it's down to it; one easier is thrown away and the next seed in the stream tried. Of 200 pared classic grids, 62% needed singles only, 12% Medium's techniques, 13.5% Hard's, 6.5% a chain, and 5.5% more than any technique here; so a Medium takes about ten tries and an Expert about twelve, Hard about nine. A 6×6 almost never needs anything between singles and chains (97% of pared ones need singles only, and nearly all the rest jump straight to fish and short chains), so its levels are Easy (singles, 16 digits kept), Medium (singles only, but pared until no digit is to spare, so there are single places to find rather than several) and Hard (anything beyond singles). Killer's cages settle so much by their sums that its grids come out Medium whatever the cages' sizes and hardly ever need a chain: Easy (sums and singles), Medium (the rule of 45, locked candidates, pairs) and Hard (two or three rows at once, fish, wings), a Hard one made from a Medium cut by joining neighbouring cages (up to six cells), while it keeps one solution, until it needs a Hard technique. So Expert is offered on a 9×9 without cages, and the level menu has no Expert for a 6×6 or Killer. A grid that can't be found at its level in the tries allowed (it hasn't happened in testing) is dealt as the level it is, never as a harder one. Every grid is made in a worker (`sudoku-worker.js`), so a hard one never holds the screen; on the machine here, on average, Classic and Diagonals take tens of milliseconds to a fifth of a second, Jigsaw up to half a second but its Expert one to three seconds, Killer a fifth of a second to a second; a phone is slower. One spare grid of each kind is kept ready on the device, made in the background after each deal, so New grid is at once; the rete shows only when there's none.

**Today's grid** is a classic 9×9 at Medium, the same for everyone (`today()`'s date its seed), counted the moment its last digit goes in: the games screen shows today's time (`noteDayTime`, kept in `sudoku:today`), and opens on it while it's still to play. It's always played alone, with the computer off, so the times compare.

**Hints teach.** Hint costs 30 seconds. A wrong digit comes first (every step after a wrong one is built on sand), struck through; then pencil marks that leave out the digit their cell needs; then the easiest step from the grid as it stands, counting your pencil marks where you've made them, so it never tells you what you've already worked out: named and explained in the line under the grid ("In box 2, the 8 fits only one cell: every other is ruled out by its row, column or box"; "X-wing on 4: …"), its cells lit, and its target picked out. A digit's cell is selected for you to write it yourself; candidates a step strikes out go from your pencil marks there. Anything the hint says comes from the same solver that grades the grids, and the tests check every one of its steps against the solution.

**The computer** is the same solver at a person's pace (`sudoku-bot.js`): each step takes the time a person needs for its technique (a hidden single eight seconds or so, an X-wing a minute, an XY-chain nearly three), shorter when there are many singles on offer and longer with one left, pencilling in costs a second a cell at the first step beyond singles, with a little luck either way; at three paces, Steady (a newspaper solver), Keen (a regular) and Sharp (a competition solver). On a classic Medium they take a median of about 10, 7 and 4 minutes; Keen takes 3 minutes over an Easy, 9 over a Hard and 21 over an Expert, and 16 to 35 over a Killer. In the menu (Computer: Off, Race it, With it): **Race it** has it solve the same grid on its own, its digits to go in the line over the grid, and the result says who was quicker (its race is worked out once from the grid, so it's the same on every look); **With it** gives you and it a share of the givens each, as a pair at a table gets (below): its share hatched on your grid, its digits arriving in copper as it finds them, working from what it can see (your digits included), and waiting for you when that isn't enough. A grid solved with it isn't your best: it's a team's time.

**Playing together.** A duo match (asked for from the partner sheet) or a Party's table of up to six (a race first; Together is in the menu), on the head-to-head table (`duel.js`: the lobby, Ready, the countdown to a start everyone shares), in either of two ways, switched from the menu (Race instead, Together instead), which deals a new match for everyone at the rules, size and level of whoever asks. **A race**: the same grid for everyone, each on their own phone, the others' digits to go in the line over the grid; the lowest time with hints wins; the pair's record counts the wins. **Together** (a pair's match starts this way): one grid for the table, and each player sees only their share of the givens; the others' are hatched cells (in Killer, every cage is outlined on every screen but a sum only someone else holds shows as "?"). What anyone writes is everyone's at once, in that player's colour, and anyone may write anywhere; the cell each of the others has selected is framed in their colour on your grid, with their initial, so two don't set off for the same one (written to their seat as they move, at most every 150 ms); pencil marks stay your own; hints are the team's (30 seconds each, the hint shown to whoever asked); the team's time runs from the start to the last right digit, recorded as the pair's best. The less able player can't just watch: they hold givens the other needs. How much each holds was measured, not guessed. Half the givens each is too little: a pair split half and half got stuck after one round of placing what each could, in every Medium grid tried. So every given is dealt to one player, round each region in turn, and then, a few at a time and at random, givens are shown to everyone, until the relay finishes: each player works out all they can from what they see, at the grid's own level, what they find goes to the others, and round again. As few are shared as that takes: about a third of the givens on an Easy grid and two thirds to three quarters on the harder ones, so each player still holds givens only they can see, a dozen on an Easy grid and three to five on the harder ones, and each finds a good share of the digits (in the relay, the one who finds fewer still finds a quarter to a third of them). The tests check that neither share alone has one solution (each has many), that the two together see every given, and that the relay finishes, so the two can finish without a word (saying "what's your middle cell in box 4?" is quicker). A table of three to six gets its shares the same way, round the table. The players who began at the start hold the shares; one who sits down later shares one of theirs; a share whose player has been away for 20 seconds is lent to everyone else (shown in grey, or its sums shown) until they're back, so a phone that sleeps never leaves the table stuck. The split is worked out from the grid and the table's size on each phone, in the worker, the same everywhere. Computer players don't sit at a table: a seat nobody has is no share at all, and a share that goes unheld is lent, which serves the table better than a computer filling it in.

**Twists**, weighed. Kept, each because it changes the reasoning: the 6×6 for a few minutes; Diagonals, a mild change of scanning that costs the engine two more units; Jigsaw, where a piece's shape is half the puzzle; and Killer, the variant newspapers print most, with a reasoning of its own (sums, the rule of 45). Left out: Windoku and other extra regions (Diagonals' change again, with more clutter), anti-knight and anti-king (modern, and a generated one is rarely more than a duller classic), Kropki dots, thermometers, arrows and sandwiches (hand-set genres whose generated grids are rarely elegant), 16×16 and Samurai (they don't fit a phone), and Wordoku (above).

**The digit grid** (`digits.js`, `digits.css`, `digits-edit.js`; `tests/digits.mjs`) is the board, its pad and its keys, designed for all three number grids from the start: any grid from 4 to 12 a side, rectangles too (Kakuro's); a value per cell shown as one of the game's digits (so a Calcudoku of 0 to 5 can have a 0); regions with heavy rules between them; cages, outlined in dashes just inside their cells, each sum in its first cell's corner over the dashes, as printed Killers have them, or drawn as heavy rules (Calcudoku's, `cageRules`); blacked-out cells, and Kakuro's clue cells split by a diagonal with an across and a down sum; diagonals shaded. It draws and it listens: the game keeps every bit of state (what's in the cells, the selection, Notes), so a saved run is the whole picture and a partner watching sees it, and the board says what was tapped (`onInput`: select, digit, erase, undo, redo, notes, or a game's own tool). Its API is at the top of `digits.js`. A selected cell lights its row, column and region (or what the game says its peers are: a Kakuro run), every cell with its digit, and that digit in every cell's pencil marks; repeats, wrong digits, a hint's cells and its target, a partner's hatched givens and lent ones are marks a game sets, and where the others at a table are (`cursors`) is framed in each one's colour. `digits-edit.js` keeps the entries: writing a digit (again on the same digit rubs it out; its pencil mark goes from the cells that see it, a setting), pencil marks, rubbing out, every pencil mark at once, and undo and redo of the last 300 moves, each move undone whole, all as plain JSON.

**Controls.** On a phone: tap a cell, then a digit; Notes writes in pencil (the keys show their digit small in the corner, as a pencil mark is); a digit tapped with no cell selected lights where it stands. Each key shows how many of its digit are still to place (and dims when none are). Undo, Redo, Erase, Notes and Hint are under the keys; Fill in every pencil mark and Show the solution are in the menu. The keys are a row of nine on a phone, taller on a tall phone (the grid can't use the height: it's as wide as the screen already), lower on a short one so the grid keeps its room; at 390 by 600 nothing scrolls. On a keyboard: the arrows move, a digit's key writes it (with Shift, in pencil), Backspace or Delete erases, N switches Notes, Ctrl+Z undoes and Ctrl+Y redoes. On an upright tablet the phone's layout is drawn larger. Held sideways, the grid takes the left with all the height and the clock, the line and a three-by-three keypad stand beside it, the keys at the bottom. The menu has the rules, size and level (picking a level deals a grid of the rules and size chosen), the computer and its pace, Mark repeats (on by default: a digit twice in a unit marked in oxblood), and Clear pencil marks as you write.

**Scoring and what's kept.** A grid's time is the time it was on screen: the clock stops while the menu or the games screen is open, and while the phone is elsewhere, and the grid hides behind a plate meanwhile, so the stopped clock buys no thinking. A hint adds 30 seconds. Bests are by rules, size and level, lowest first, in `sudoku:times` (not `sudoku:best`, which the games screen reads as highest-wins); the games screen's figure is today's time. The run in play is `sudoku:run`: the grid itself (its givens, solution, regions or cages, as strings, so it opens at once and a partner watching sees it without making it), your entries with their undo, the selection, the Notes switch, the time and hints, and with the computer its share and its next digit; the settings `sudoku:settings`, the spares `sudoku:spares`, the way a table plays `sudoku:together`, and a match's own run `sudoku:duel` (duel.js keeps it), so a reload anywhere comes back as it was.

`tests/sudoku.mjs` checks the rules as units, the exact solver on known grids (one solution, none, more), about fifty grids of every rules, size and level (one solution each, at their level, mirrored where they should be, the cages' sums and pieces right, every step of the human-style solver agreeing with the solution, the solver finishing each), the jigsaw and Killer pieces, grading and hints on known grids, the computer (every digit right, in order, the paces in order, the same race every time, a partner's next digit right), grids and entries through JSON and the database, the splits (each player holds givens the other lacks, neither can finish alone, the relay finishes, for a pair and a table of three), the table through the database's round trip (a digit in for this match only and never on a given, who placed it, the last right digit solving it with its time, the team's hints, a share nobody holds, a latecomer's share), and today's grid the same each time it's made, with nothing in the engine but its seed (no `Math.random`, no clock).

Not done: there's no mistake limit or check-as-you-go mode (a hint finds a wrong digit, at 30 seconds); a Killer is drawn from generated cuts, whose sums can look less shapely than a setter's; computer players don't take seats at a table (above); an Expert Jigsaw can take a phone several seconds to make the first time (the next is already made). Whether Medium is the right daily, and whether the pace of the computer's Keen matches a regular, is for Korbi's thumb to say.

### Calcudoku
(Being built, October 2026.)

### Kakuro
(Being built, October 2026.)

### Hitori
Shade cells so that no number is left twice in a row or a column, no two shaded cells touch side by side, and the cells left unshaded stay joined in one piece: Nikoli's rules, unchanged (October 2026). Every puzzle has exactly one answer, reached by reasoning, never by a guess. Tap a cell to shade it, again to keep it (a ring in the game's plum, the circle a solver pencils round a number that stays), again to clear it; hold a cell, or right-click it, to keep it at once. Only the shading counts: the puzzle is solved the moment exactly its answer is shaded, so keeping is for your own notes. Undo and Redo step through your marks. A rule you break shows at once, with a line under the board saying which: two shaded cells side by side in oxblood, a number kept twice in a line, the cells your shading has cut off from the rest tinted; all from the rules alone, never from the answer. A hint (30 seconds) first puts a wrong mark right, and otherwise makes the next step a person would take and says why ("two 4s side by side: one of them stays, so the other 4s in that line are shaded"), the cells it settled in gilt and the cells that say so dashed.

The board is the twist, chosen in the menu: Square, 5×5 to 12×12, numbers 1 to the side; or Hex, a hexagon of hexagons 5, 7, 9 or 11 across (19 to 91 cells), numbers 1 to its width, every cell in three lines (a row and two diagonals) with six neighbours. Hex earns its place as hex Minesweeper does: the same rules on a lattice that changes how they play, three directions to scan for a number's rivals and a connection rule that hardly ever bites. Left out: letters for numbers (the same puzzle in other symbols, nothing new to reason about), and boards past 12 (a cell of a 12×12 is already 26 pixels on a phone). Nothing comes from the knowledge base: Hitori is numbers, and has no content to choose.

Making a puzzle (`hitori-engine.js`). Shade a board at random as far as it will go (every cell, in random order, shaded if it touches no shaded cell and leaves the rest joined, so no cell could be shaded on top: a maximal shading); number the kept cells with no number twice in a line (the cell with fewest choices first); give each shaded cell the number of a kept cell in one of its lines, since a shaded number with no rival left unshaded needn't be shaded; then mend it to one answer: the counting solver finds a second, and a cell shaded in ours and kept in that one takes the number of a cell kept in both, so the other answer has a number twice; again until one is left. The counting solver searches with every forced consequence followed (a shaded cell's neighbours kept, a kept number's rivals shaded, a cell that would cut the board kept) and counts the answers in which every shaded cell has a rival left unshaded; that and the maximal shading together prove the answer unique among every shading there is, since any other would either have a needless shade or a cell more shaded than ours. `tests/hitori.mjs` checks it against a plain search of every shading on small boards. Counting takes a fraction of a millisecond.

Difficulty is the hardest step a solver needs that reasons as a person does, the simplest step first, never how many numbers there are. Easy: what a mark beside a cell forces, a kept number's rivals, a pair side by side (one stays, so the line's other copies go), a sandwich (a cell between two of the same number is kept), a number with no rival left. Medium: the connection rule too (a cell that joins two parts of the board is kept; one that, kept, would wall itself in with its own rivals is shaded). Hard: a trial, a cell supposed one way and its consequences followed a round at a time until a rule breaks, within two rounds. Expert: a trial three rounds deep or more, how deep the search must go. The generator steers towards the level asked: a shaded cell's number changed to another rival's, kept when the puzzle stays unique and grades nearer, the first to reach the level taken, else the nearest, and the line says so ("No Expert puzzle came of this size this time: this one is Hard"). Measured: every square size reaches every level; about eight in ten 12×12 and hex 9 Experts come out Expert. On a hex board the connection rule was never once needed in 120 puzzles steered towards it (a kept cell has six neighbours, so the kept cells are hardly ever at risk of being cut apart), so hex has no Medium; on the widest hex a supposition always broke within two rounds, so it has no Expert either. The menu offers only the levels a board has. Puzzles are made in a worker (`hitori-worker.js`), with a spare of each kind lately played kept, so New puzzle is instant; in Chromium with the processor slowed four times, as a phone's stand-in, today's 8×8 Medium takes 20 ms, a 10×10 or 12×12 Hard a quarter to a third of a second, an 8×8 Expert a fifth, a 12×12 or hex 9 Expert a second and a half (seven at worst, when Expert doesn't come and every try is used), and the rete shows only for a kind with no spare yet. The same seed makes the same puzzle on every device: only the seed's randomness and a fixed number of tries, never a clock.

Together, one table on `duel.js`, two ways. The split board, a pair's default: one board for the two of you, each seeing the numbers on your own half only (the first to sit down the top half and the left of a middle row, the other the rest), the other half blank paper. Shading and keeping are shared and show on both phones at once, each ring in its keeper's colour; Point lights a cell on both phones in your colour for a few seconds, so "what's this one?" needs no coordinates.
