Your rows are whole, so you can work your half, but every column takes both of you: alone, a half settles between 6% and 53% of a board, never all of it (in 80 halves measured, not even with trials), and the deal checks each board's halves with the plain rules (a few milliseconds; with trials it took a tenth of a second and more, and never found one the plain rules missed). A chessboard split was tried first and dropped: alone, a player could settle almost nothing, so one of you only read numbers out to the other. One time for the team, from the start to the solve (plus hints), the same on both phones (the first phone to see it solved writes the time, and both take it from the table), recorded as the pair's team result. The race: everyone the same puzzle on their own phone from the same countdown, the others' progress showing as how much of the answer's shading they have right, the lowest time plus hints winning; a party plays it, up to six. The menu's Together part chooses the next match's way and its board, size and level; the level in the menu deals a new match at that level.

The computer rival, solo: Off, Steady, Quick or Sharp, in Settings. It solves the same puzzle beside you with the engine's solver, a step at a time, each taking the time a person's would: a glance for what a mark next to it forces, longer for a pattern, longer still for the connection rule, and for a trial a time growing with its depth and with how few of the suppositions there were to try break, which is how hard the step is to find. Its progress shows under your time and its finish in your result. On 8×8 Medium, Steady takes about 3½ to 4 minutes, Quick 2¼, Sharp 1¼. It plays on your clock, stopping when it stops, and isn't at a table (`duel.js` seats people only).

Score: your time plus 30 seconds a hint, lower better. Bests by board, size and the level a puzzle came to, in `hitori:times` (not `hitori:best`, which the games screen reads as highest-is-best). Today's puzzle is an 8×8 Medium made from the date (`today()`), the same for everyone; its first solve is today's time on the games screen (`noteDayTime`, `hitori:today`), counted the moment the shading is the answer, and the tile opens today's while it's still to play, asking before leaving a puzzle you've started. A link (Copy a link to this puzzle) carries the seed, board, size and level. The run in play is `hitori:run` (its numbers and answer, your marks, undo and redo, the clock, hints, the hint showing, the rival), saved at every mark and every five seconds, so a reload or a partner watching sees exactly what's on the screen (the rival's progress follows from the clock); `hitori:prefs` is the board, size, level, rival and how a match for two is played, `hitori:spares` the spare puzzles, `hitori:duel` a race's own run (`duel.js`). The clock and the rival stand while the menu or the games screen is open, and while the page is away.

Held sideways, the board takes the left with all the height, the time and the rival beside it at the top, the line and the tools low. By night the board's tiles are lifted towards the lettering and the shading is near black, so a shaded cell reads as a hole in the board (on the dial's own tiles the two were too close to tell at a glance).

`tests/hitori.mjs` checks the boards and their lines, the rules, the counting solver against a plain search of every shading (56 small puzzles, and a needless shade the maximal check catches), 99 puzzles across every board, size and level offered (one answer each, solved by reasoning to it at its label), a seed's puzzle the same every time and today's the same for a day, each level needing its hardest step and no harder, every step of the solver true of the answer, marks, rule breaks, hints that alone solve a puzzle, the split (halves within a cell of each other, neither finishing alone, both together settling all of it), the rival's timetable, and a run and the split board's table through JSON and the database's round trip. Two phones in one browser on the stand-in database played a split board through (the lobby, the countdown, a mark and a pointed cell crossing over, the solve with one time on both, the pair's team record) and a race (the result on both, the head-to-head record).

Not done: the computer at a table, a split board for more than two, hex boards past 11 across.

### Nonogram
A picture hidden in a grid (October 2026): each row and column carries its clue, the runs of filled cells along it in order ("3 1 2" is three filled, a gap, one, a gap, two), and the clues together allow exactly one picture. The pictures are the knowledge base's, so finishing one is also a question: whose outline, whose flag, which lake. Three kinds, chosen in the menu (Pictures: Countries, Flags, Lakes or All, one of): a country's outline (Chart's own shapes, `chart-countries.js`, Natural Earth at 1:110m, 168 countries), its flag in colour (195 flags), and a lake (Chart's 34, from Lake Geneva to the Aral Sea). In a flag every run has its colour too, shown on the clue as a chip of that colour, and the usual colour rules hold: two runs of different colours may touch, two of the same colour need a gap. A region narrows any of them (Worldwide or Chart's seven regions, one of), and the size is the menu's Size: Small, Medium or Large, about 100, 225 or 400 cells, the grid taking the picture's own shape (a 15 × 15 for a square country, 20 × 10 for a long flag), never longer than 14, 20 or 26 cells on a side nor shorter than 5, 7 or 9. A country too long and thin for any size (Chile, the Gambia, Indonesia's archipelago) or too scattered to be a picture (the Solomon Islands) isn't dealt.

How a picture is made (`nonogram-pictures.js`). A country is drawn in Mercator, as every map draws it, and only its main land: its largest piece and whatever lies near it (a piece joins when its centre is within a quarter of the picture's diagonal of what has joined), so France comes without French Guiana and the United States without Alaska and Hawaii, while Indonesia's or Japan's islands, near one another, stay; pieces cut at the antimeridian (Russia's Chukotka) are moved round to their side. Each cell is sampled at 36 points, scanline by scanline, inside by the even–odd rule (Lesotho is a hole in South Africa), and filled when the land covers half of it; rows and columns left empty at the edges (a small island stretching the frame) are cut off. The knowledge base has flags only as emoji, which every phone draws its own way, so each flag is described in `nonogram-flags.js` as the shapes it's made of (stripes and their proportions, crosses, discs, stars, crescents, triangles at the hoist, diagonal bands, the Union Jack in a canton, an emblem as the blot of colour it makes at this size) and painted cell by cell, each cell the colour most of its 25 samples have; of the grids near the size's, the one whose edges fall most cleanly between cells is taken (thirteen American stripes in thirteen rows). A flag's commonest colour is its blank, so what's left to draw is its pattern in the other colours, and the finished flag shows the blank's colour again; Nepal's pennants stand on nothing at all. Every flag was checked against its design at Medium and Large; Afghanistan's and Syria's are left out, since they changed lately and phones still disagree about them, and Chad's and Romania's (Monaco's and Indonesia's) are the same picture, so either name is right for both.

Exactly one answer, reached by reasoning (`nonogram-engine.js`). A search counts the answers the clues allow, up to two: the complete line logic, then a guess on an open cell and both ways followed. A picture that has one is then solved the way a person would (below), and is dealt only if that reasoning finishes it. When the even split of the cells doesn't, the generator tries a little more and a little less of each cell (a cut of 45%, 55%, 40%, 60%, 35%, 65% instead of a half), then changes cells one at a time, each time one where two answers differ, the cells the shape half covers first, so the picture moves as little as it can (up to ten). Of the outlines about one in five needed a cut other than the half and about one in twenty a changed cell or two; a few lakes need more. A picture makes the same puzzle at a size on every device, so today's is the same for everyone and a match the same for everyone at the table (`tests/nonogram.mjs` makes the days' puzzles again in another process and compares them). Making one takes a few milliseconds, a sparse lake at Large a few hundred: the rete says so ("Drawing the picture"), and the next random picture is made in a worker while you play this one (`nonogram-worker.js`), so Next is instant.

Difficulty is how far the reasoning has to reach, measured, not the number of clues. The solver climbs a ladder the way a person does: the simple rules first (push every run as far left as it will go and as far right; a cell inside the same run both ways is filled, a cell no run can reach is blank, a line whose runs are placed is done); when they say no more, the complete line logic (every arrangement of the line's runs that fits what's known: a cell they all agree on is settled), on one line, then back to the simple rules; and only when no line says anything, a contradiction (suppose a cell is filled, follow the lines, and if they clash it's blank). It also counts the rounds of simple reasoning a puzzle needs, each worked from what the round before settled. Easy is the simple rules alone in six rounds or fewer; Medium seven to ten, or the complete line logic somewhere; Hard eleven rounds or more, or a contradiction. A puzzle that needs more than thirty contradictions is a slog and isn't dealt. Measured on every picture: countries at Medium are 49 Easy, 86 Medium and 29 Hard (Small leans easy, Large hard); flags are nearly all Easy, as colour gives so much away; lakes are mostly Hard, being thin. The menu's Difficulty is Any, Easy, Medium or Hard; where a region has nothing of a band at a size, the nearest is dealt and the line says so. The bands' cut points come from these measurements, not from play: whether Medium feels medium is for Korbi's thumb to say.

Playing. Fill (or a flag's colours, as swatches) and Cross are the tools; a tap fills or crosses one cell, a drag runs along its row or column, and the first cell decides what a stroke does: on an empty cell it fills, on one of its colour it clears, and a fill never covers a cell crossed out. Undo and Redo take whole strokes. Mistakes (Settings) are Shown, the default: a wrong fill or cross is refused, the cell shows what it really is and it costs 20 seconds; or Hidden, as on paper: nothing says you're wrong, and the picture is done when every row and column agrees with its clue. A hint (+30 s) fills in the next cell the clues force, the simplest way a person would see it, and names the line that says so and why ("from row 4: its runs pushed both ways overlap there"); with mistakes hidden and one standing, it rings the wrong cell instead. A finished line's clue greys; the row and column under your finger light up with their clues; the corner holds a thumbnail of what you've drawn so far. On a phone a Large picture's cells would be too small to hit, so the board zooms (pinch, or the zoom button, or the wheel on a computer) and pans (two fingers, or one along a clue strip), the clue strips staying put while the grid moves under them, so a cell's clues are always in sight. On a computer, X picks the cross, 1 to 7 a colour, Ctrl+Z undoes. The board is drawn on a canvas (`nonogram-board.js`) in the theme's colours by day and night; the finished picture sweeps in in its own colours, land green, water blue, a flag as it flies. Held sideways the board takes the left with all the height and the strip, the line and the tools stand at the right, the tools low.

Name it (Settings, on by default). Before the picture is finished you may name it once: right takes off up to 30, 60 or 120 seconds (Small, Medium, Large) in proportion to how much was still to draw; wrong adds 30. Not named by the end, it asks then, for nothing off. Naming is typed, recall rather than recognition, with the names that fit what's typed offered under the box (`typing.js` forgives case, accents, a leading "the" and a slip of a letter; a place answers to what else it goes by: Czechia, Burma, Ivory Coast, the UK, Léman). One you couldn't name goes to the pile, carrying its knowledge-base entity, and comes back in Deck as a card: the picture and four names from its region; and in this game's learning mode (on, as everywhere) as a picture again, before any new one, followed by pictures of what other games found you weak on. A missed Chile in Chart can come back here as Chile's flag.

The computer (Settings): Off, Partner or Rival, at a pace (Easy, Medium, Hard). It solves the way the hints do, the simplest rule first, and is paced like a person: a while to see each step, longer for a harder rule (about 2.5, 6 and 15 seconds at Medium), then a moment for each cell it fills or crosses out; at Medium it draws Italy at Medium size in about three minutes, at Hard in about half that. As a partner it has the columns' clues and you the rows' (theirs show as shaded marks in its colour): it works from the grid you share, and when its columns say no more it waits on you, as a partner would; a picture drawn with it doesn't count for your best. As a rival it solves its own copy alongside you, the strip saying how far it has got, and the result says who was quicker (`nonogram-bot.js`).

Together, on `duel.js` (README, Head to head), from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six, two ways (the menu's Next match). Split, the default for a pair: one grid between you and the clues dealt out, the rows to one and the columns to the other (at a table of more, the rows dealt round and the columns round one seat on, so everyone reads some of each). Neither can finish alone (the tests check that the rows' clues alone never finish a picture, nor the columns'): each must say what their lines need ("my row 3 is 2 5"), and tapping a clue lights that line on the others' screens for a few seconds, to point at it. Each sees the other's cells as they're drawn (the room keeps the cells one by one, so two people drawing at once never collide); a partner away gives their lines to those still here, so nobody's sleeping phone holds the picture up; mistakes, hints and the one go at naming it are the team's, and the time runs from when the table started. The finished picture counts for the pair as a team result. Race, a party's first match: the same picture for everyone, each on their own grid, the others' progress in the line under the strip, the lowest score winning; duel.js does the lobby, the countdown and the result. Your own picture, bests and today's stay as they were.

Scoring, bests and today's. The score is your seconds on the clock, plus 20 a mistake and 30 a hint, less a right early name's credit or plus 30 for a wrong one; lower is better. Bests are kept for each kind of picture and size (`nonogram:times`, not `nonogram:best`, which the games screen would read as highest-wins). Today's picture (Picture: Today's in the menu, and the games screen's tile while it's still to play) is a country's outline, Medium, of Medium difficulty, with mistakes shown and name-it on, the same for everyone on the day (`today()`); its score counts the moment the last cell is right (`noteDayTime`), and the tile shows it as today's time.

Saved state. The picture in play is `nonogram:run`: its id and size, the grid (a character a cell), the strokes to undo and redo, the clock, mistakes, hints, the name, the rules it was dealt with and the rival's copy; it's saved at every stroke and every five seconds, so a reload or a partner watching sees it as it stands (the puzzle itself is made again from the picture's id and size). The menu's choices are `nonogram:settings`, the pictures dealt lately `nonogram:seen` (so they don't come round again soon), the counts `nonogram:stats`; a match's run is `nonogram:duel`. The clock and the computer stand still while the menu, the rules or the games screen is open, and while the page is away.

`tests/nonogram.mjs` checks the clues; the complete line logic against every arrangement of 2,500 random lines in one to three colours, and that the simple rules never claim more than it; the ladder's grades against what each rule alone can finish, on 400 random grids; that every picture at Small, half of them at Medium and a quarter at Large make puzzles with exactly one answer, the one drawn, reached within thirty contradictions; that a picture always makes the same puzzle, today's included in another process, and every day of a month has its medium country; the bands; dealing (not lately, the region, learning mode's picks first, the nearest band); the split (each half alone never finishes, both together always do, the lines dealt to every seat); the computer (it finishes, never a cell wrong, paced like a person, Hard twice as quick as Easy, a partner that waits); hints, checking and the score; names; and a run saved as JSON and opened again. Two phones in one browser on the stand-in database played a split match through (each saw the other's cells, the finish counted once for the team and went to the pair's record) and a race (the winner by score, Rematch); the computer played as partner and as rival; a missed flag came back in Deck with its picture. Its logo is a nonogram's own shape: the grid with its clue strips along the top and the left, the corner between them empty, a heart drawn on it in oxblood.

Not done: hexagonal nonograms, the genre's other real variant (three directions of clues round a hexagon of cells), would be this game's Hex Mines, and the engine's line logic would carry it, but the board and the rasterising onto hex cells are still to build; the flags' drawings and the other names places go by live in this game's own files and could move into the knowledge base as facts, for other games to draw on; Deck labels the card "nonogram" until the pile's list of games names it.

### Mines
Minesweeper (October 2026), by its usual rules, on fields that never need a guess. A field of cells hides mines; dig a cell and it shows how many of the cells touching it are mines, and a cell with none round it opens its neighbours by itself, and theirs, so one dig can open a patch. A flag marks a cell you're sure of, and keeps it from your own dig. Tapping an open number is chording (Minesweeper's word for working a number at once): if as many flags stand round it as its number, the rest of its neighbours are dug; if its hidden neighbours are exactly the mines it still lacks, they're flagged. The field is cleared when every cell without a mine is open; flags are never needed. What Almanac adds: every field is proven to clear by reasoning alone from a marked start (no guess, ever), its difficulty is the reasoning it takes rather than only its size, it comes in three tilings, there's a field a day, and it's played together, as one team that has to talk or as a race.

**The start and a mine.** Every field has its start ringed in brass: a cell with no mine on it or round it, so digging it opens a patch, and the solver's proof begins there. Until it's open no other cell can be dug (a tap elsewhere says so), which is also the guard against a first tap that lands a thumb's width off. Since a field never needs a guess, a mine dug is always a slip, of reasoning or of the thumb, and on a phone it's often the thumb: so by default a mine is a strike, as in Survey (it stays on the field, shown, counts as found, and costs 60 seconds) and the field goes on. The menu's A mine: Ends the field is classic Minesweeper's rule for those who want it, the field lost at the first mine, with The same field to try it again. A hint costs 20 seconds: it rings the cheapest thing what's open proves now (green a safe cell, red a mine) and rings in blue the numbers that prove it; it never trusts your flags, so it will also say a flag is wrong. A strike costs three hints on purpose: if a mine cost less than a hint, a stuck player's cheapest way on would be a guess, and the game is about never having to. Your score is your time plus those costs, lower is better; the clock starts with your first dig and stands while the menu or the games screen is open or the app is away.

**Three tilings.** Squares are Minesweeper's own, each cell touching the eight round it. Hexes (Korbi's example) touch six: pointy-topped hexagons in rows, every other row shifted half a cell. Triangles, pointing up and down in turn along a row, touch twelve: three along their edges and nine more at their corners, as triangle Minesweeper counts them, so a number can be as high as 12. Each comes in three sizes, Small, Medium and Large: for squares Minesweeper's 9×9, 16×16 and its Expert's 30×16 (drawn 16 across and 30 down, to stand up on a phone), and hexes and triangles about the same number of cells (8×10, 14×18 and 16×30 hexes; 11×10, 17×15 and 23×21 triangles, a little fewer cells, since a triangle's number has less room round it). Who touches whom is `mines-grid.js`, checked against the geometry itself: a cell's neighbours are exactly the cells its outline touches. Mines has no content of its own, so nothing comes from the knowledge base: what you choose in the menu's Content is the field, Random or Today's, and its shape, size and level, each one of three.

**How a field is made and proven.** `mines-engine.js` lays the level's mines at random, none on the start or round it, and hands the field to the solver (`mines-solve.js`), which plays it from the start the way a person does, cheapest reasoning first, in four levels: one number (a number whose mines are all found frees the rest of its neighbours; one with as many hidden neighbours as mines still missing has them all mines); two numbers together (the 1-1 and 1-2 patterns: what two overlapping numbers prove between them); a group (the numbers round one number, every way of placing their mines tried, keeping what all of them agree on, over a patch of at most 22 hidden cells: a person's "if this were a mine, then…"); and the count (near the end, with 40 hidden cells or fewer, the mines left over every cell left). A field is kept only if the solver clears it, which is the proof that it needs no guess: each step it takes is forced, true in every way the mines could lie given what's open, so at every moment there is a certain move, and the field has exactly one answer. Nothing deeper is used (a search of the whole field mid-game would find more, but no person would), so a field the solver clears is one a person can. The search inside the group and the count has a budget counted in steps, never in time, so every device deals the same field from the same seed. The first field that clears and meets its level is taken, else after 400 tries the one closest to the level among those that cleared (a field that clears is never given up for one that fits better). Random fields at Expert's density clear from their start about one time in six, hexes at their hardest one in ten, triangles two in three; the solver takes a few milliseconds a field, so a deal is quick: in Node the slowest, a large hard hex field, took 0.2 seconds at most and 80 ms on average, and while a large field is dealt the rete turns.

**Difficulty.** Measured by what the reasoning takes, never only by the mines: the hardest level a field needed, and its hard moments, the rounds where one number wasn't enough. Each level asks for a band of hard moments on each shape and size, set from samples of each (squares, Medium: none on Easy, one to five on Medium, five or more on Hard; Large Hard ten or more), with more mines as it rises: squares keep Minesweeper's counts where they meet them (10 on Small Easy, 40 on Medium Medium, 99 on Large Hard, Expert's own). The samples said something about the twists. On hexes two numbers almost never prove more than one does (six neighbours overlap too little: of 230 random medium hex fields at their Medium density that cleared, three needed it, where more than half of the same size of squares do), so a field of hexes either clears one number at a time or sticks on a true fifty-fifty; hexes therefore take more mines than squares for the same level (fewer would leave them all easy), and a Hard one is the densest that still clears, ending on the count. On triangles nearly every field needs two numbers together, and often (the middle Large Hard one 37 times): twelve neighbours overlap a lot, so triangles take fewer mines, and even Easy allows a few such moments. An Easy field of squares or hexes never needs more than one number at a time. `tests/mines.mjs` deals three fields of every shape, size and level and checks each clears from its start, has its level's mines, meets its band (or is the closest of its 400), and that Hard needs more than Medium and Medium more than Easy everywhere.

**Today's field.** One a day, the same for everyone (the suite's day, UTC), growing through the week as a newspaper's puzzles do: Monday small squares, easy; Tuesday small hexes; Wednesday medium squares; Thursday small triangles; Friday medium squares, hard; Saturday Expert (large squares, hard); Sunday large hexes, hard. The menu says which it is. Its score is counted the moment the last safe cell opens and is the games screen's figure for Mines (`mines:today`, the first clear of the day); the tile opens today's field while it's still to play. Today's is played alone (the computer partner sits it out; a rival may race it). Any field also has a link (Copy a link to this field: `#s=SEED&f=shape.size.level`, or `#d=DAY` for a day's).

**Playing together, as one team.** The pair's match from the partner sheet (and a Party's, up to six) is one field for the table whose numbers are shared out: each player reads the numbers on their own share of the cells, and sees the others' dug cells open but with a dot in their colour where the number is. The shares are as fine as the tiling allows, so every number has the others' all round it: a chessboard on squares (every cell's four edge neighbours are the partner's), alternate rows on hexes (four of the six), up and down triangles on triangles; for three or more a lattice of the same kind. Flags (in their placer's colour), digs, mines, hints and the clock are the team's, and anyone may dig or flag anywhere. Neither of you can clear it alone: a team's field is dealt only if the solver, reading just one share of the numbers (and the empty cells, which open for everyone), gets stuck before the end for each of you, while both shares together clear it, so the less able player holds numbers the other can't do without, from the first patch to the last. The way to talk is to talk; for a pair apart, Point (a third tool, while together) rings a cell on everyone's screen for four seconds, and pointing at one of your own numbers shows it to everyone from then on, underlined in your colour: "what's this one?" and its answer. A player away for half a minute (a phone asleep, Back to solo) hands their share to the others, so a table never waits on a phone that slept. A pair's cleared field goes to the pair's record as the team's time (a field lost to a mine counts as played, not as a time). Measured with two computer players each holding half the numbers, a split cell by cell needs a lot of talk: about 35 numbers shown on a medium field, 135 on Expert. Splitting into blocks of two to four cells was tried and barely changed it (most cells of a small block sit on its edge); only quarters of the field did, at the cost of each working alone, which is what the split is meant to prevent.

**As a race.** The menu's together part chooses the next match: One team or Race. A race is a head-to-head on one deal (README, Head to head): everyone gets the same field, digs it on their own phone from the same countdown, and sees the others' progress under the score (the share of safe cells open, sent every second and a half); the lowest score at a cleared field wins, and a field lost to a mine (when the table plays that rule) comes last. Your own field, bests and today's stay as they were. The table is `duel.js`'s in both modes, its lobby, countdown, Rematch and Back to solo; the team's field lives beside the table in the same branch (`field`, in `mines-team.js`), every move a transaction on it, shown on your screen at once and set aside if the table has moved on (a cell someone else dug a moment before). Choosing a shape, size or level in the menu deals a new match at it for everyone (asking first if one is under way); the rule for a mine, and One team or Race, apply from the next. A race's winner goes to the pair's record as a win.

**The computer.** Settings › Computer: Off, Partner or Rival, at a pace of Steady, Quick or Sharp, for your own fields. Both think with the solver and are paced like a person: a moment to see what a number proves (longer for two numbers than for one, longer still for a group or the count) and a moment for each tap. The partner is a one-person team: it holds the other half of the numbers (its cells carry its copper dot), digs and flags what its half proves, trusts its own flags and never yours (it points at a flag of yours it knows is wrong), and when the field has stood still for four seconds it shows you one of its numbers that would let you on, or points at one of yours it needs (Point at it to show it); after nine it asks for the next of yours on the edge all the same, since sometimes it takes two at once. Point at one of its numbers and it shows you that too. A steady partner reasons with no more than two numbers at a time and leaves the rest to you; Sharp uses all four levels. A partnered field keeps bests of its own. The rival plays its own copy of your field: its whole game is worked out when the field is dealt, from the solver's steps and its pace (with now and then a slip of the thumb onto a mine), so it's shown against your clock, pausing when you do, as a share in the strip and its score once it's through; your result says who won, and the menu keeps the count against each pace. On a medium field of squares, Steady takes about four minutes, Quick two and a half, Sharp one.

**Twists.** Minesweeper's real variants, and what came of each: hexes and triangles are in (the logic changes with the neighbours, as above, and each looks its own); classic sudden death is the menu's rule for a mine; a field that wraps round at its edges (no corners to start from, but on a phone the wrap can't be seen), several mines to a cell, and the puzzle variants where numbers lie or count a knight's move away were left out, as changing what a number means rather than where it looks. Minesweeper Flags (MSN Messenger's two-player game: turns, each mine found scoring, a find giving another turn) would make a third way to play together, against each other on one field; it isn't built (below).

**Fingers.** A tap digs (or flags, with Flag chosen); holding a finger down for a moment does the other, with a buzz; a right click flags with a mouse. Holding a number rings its neighbours (on hexes and triangles, where the eye doesn't know them yet), and a number tapped that can't chord rings them briefly. A field that fits the box at a thumb's size (34 px a square cell on a phone, 36 a hexagon's width, 40 a triangle's side; more on a tablet) or nearly (80% of it) is drawn whole; a larger one opens close up round the start, pans under one finger and zooms between the whole field and well past a thumb's size with two (a mouse: the wheel pans, with Ctrl it zooms), and the magnifier at its corner goes between the whole field and close up. A drag never digs: a finger that moves more than nine pixels is panning. There's no undo, on purpose: a dug cell has shown its number, so taking the dig back would be a free look under the paper; a flag comes off with the same tap or hold that put it on, and a mine dug by a slip costs its minute and the field goes on (with sudden death, The same field starts it over). The page never scrolls: the field takes all the room under the score, the line and the tools under it; held sideways the field takes the left with all the height and the score, the line and the tools stand at the right, the tools low. The field is one canvas (`mines-draw.js`), drawn from the theme's own tokens (paper tiles with a brass edge standing up from the page, dug cells sunk into it; at night the tiles lit a little and the dug cells the dial itself), with Minesweeper's colours for the numbers in enamels (1 cobalt, 2 verdigris, 3 oxblood, 4 navy, and on), every theme by day and by night. A flood opens as a wave from the cell dug.

**Kept.** The field in play is `mines:run` (its seed and settings, its cells as one character each, strikes, hints, the time, what the partner has shown and asked), saved at every move and every five seconds while the clock runs, so a reload, another of your devices or a partner watching sees it as it stands, the computer partner's moves and its rings included; the view, how close and where, is `mines:view`. Bests, the lowest score by shape, size and level (and with the computer partner apart), are `mines:times`; your record against the rival's paces `mines:rivals`; the menu's choices `mines:prefs`; a race's own run `mines:duel`.

**Tests.** `tests/mines.mjs` checks the tilings (neighbours both ways, exactly the cells that touch, a finger finding its cell, the shares covering the field about evenly for two to six), the rules (the start first, the flood, flags, both chords, a wrong flag under a chord, strikes, sudden death, 3BV: Bechtel's Board Benchmark Value, the fewest clicks that clear a field without flags, which speed players divide a time by), every deduction the solver makes on small random fields against every way the mines could lie, a one-number and a two-number deduction each following from those numbers alone, the patterns at their levels, the dealt fields as above, the same field from the same seed, hints that alone clear a field and name a flagged cell safe, the rival (it clears its copy, the same way each time; Sharp quicker than Quick, Quick than Steady), the split (both shares clear every team field, neither alone does) and two computer partners clearing fields together with no mine dug, talking, the team's field through the database's own round trip (the start first, moves on an older copy set aside, flags by seat, showing and pointing, two players clearing it, a rematch leaving it behind, sudden death), a run kept as JSON at every move, and today's field the same for everyone. In a browser: fields of every shape played to the end with mouse clicks; touch through Chromium's own touch events (a tap digs, a held finger flags, a drag pans and digs nothing, two fingers zoom); a team match and a race in two tabs on the stand-in database (the same field on both, complementary numbers, the clear on both, the race's result), on a phone and on an iPad held sideways; today's field from the games screen's tile, a reload mid-field, the menu stopping the clock, a field lost to a mine and dealt again, the rival, and a field cleared with the computer partner, its requests answered by pointing; every theme by day and night at 390×664, and some of them at 390×600 and 844 and on an iPad both ways.

**Not done.** Minesweeper Flags, the turn-taking head-to-head on one field. A computer player at a table (duel.js has no computer seats, so the partner and rival are for your own fields). A table of three or more as one team is tested in its parts (the shares for up to six, who reads when someone's away), not played through. A dealing worker: deals run on the page, fast in Node, not yet timed on an iPhone. Untried on a real phone: the hold and the pinch on iOS Safari, and the buzz (iPhones have none from a page). For Korbi to say: whether a mine should end the field by default (classic) rather than cost a minute; whether the week's dailies are the right ones; and whether the team's split should be coarser (halves of the field, each player solving their own half and talking at the seam) for a pair who'd rather work side by side than in constant conversation.

### Bulls
Mastermind and Bulls and Cows (October 2026): break a hidden code from the score each guess earns. A bull is a peg of the right colour in the right place, a cow a colour the code holds but elsewhere, counted as often as both hold it (a guess of two reds against a code of one red earns one red, not two), the way Mastermind (1970) scores it and the pencil-and-paper Bulls and Cows it came from. Three ways to play alone, chosen under Play in the menu: Break (the computer hides a code, you break it), Duel (you and the computer each break the other's) and Lock (a code to reason out from clues, guessing nothing). The rules, the codemakers and the computer players are `bulls-engine.js`'s, the locks `bulls-lock.js`'s, the table for playing together `bulls-table.js`'s, all pure and tested in Node; `bulls.js` draws the board.

The boards are the genre's own: Classic, four pegs of six colours, repeats allowed, ten rows (Mastermind as sold); Super, five pegs of eight colours in twelve rows (Super Mastermind); Bulls and Cows, four different digits in ten rows, every guess four different digits too (the pencil game); and Small, three pegs of six colours in eight, a short game to learn on. Every colour carries a mark of its own (red a triangle, yellow a dot, green a square, blue a diamond, white a cross, black a star, purple a ring, orange a bar), so a colour reads by its shape too: for a colour-blind player, and in every theme. The key pegs are a peg of ink for a bull and a ring for a cow; an empty hole is a small dimple, so it never reads as a ring. Bulls draws nothing from the knowledge base: its content is colours and digits, and the menu's choices are all one-of (Play; Code, random or today's; Board; Codemaker; Computer; Level).

There is no puzzle to make in Break and none to prove: the code is drawn from the board's codes, each as likely, by a seeded generator, so a seed (a link) or a day is the same code on every device, and the code is its one answer. What makes a board hard is how many codes it has against how much a guess can tell, and that is measured by the guesses a perfect breaker needs: Knuth's minimax (1977) breaks every Classic code in five at most, 4.476 on average (5,801 guesses over the 1,296 codes, checked here code by code); Small in five, 4.05 on average; Bulls and Cows (5,040 codes) in about 5.4 on average and seven at worst, and Super (32,768 codes) in about 5.6, in samples of games (on those boards the search weighs a sample, below). The rows give room above that: ten where five will do. A single code isn't graded: picking hard codes for a hard level (AABB, say, which catch people out) would tell you something about the code before you guess. The luck in Break is in your first guesses; the Devious codemaker (Codemaker in the menu) takes it out: it hides no code at all and answers every guess with the score that keeps the most codes possible, never the win while another code still fits, so only a strategy that wins in the worst case wins (Knuth's still takes five on Classic). It never contradicts itself: the code it shows at the end fits every answer it gave.

Lock is where the making and the proving are. A lock is a code of three or four different digits and a few clue rows, each a guess and its score ("one right and in its place, one right but misplaced"), the "crack the code" puzzle of the newspapers. The generator hides a code, adds random clues while each still rules codes out, until one code is left, then drops every clue the others don't need, so each one matters; a clue that is the code itself is never dealt. Uniqueness is counted, not assumed: every code of the size (720 of three digits, 5,040 of four) is tried against the clues. Difficulty is the reasoning a person needs, measured by a solver that knows what a person writes down: which digits are in, which are out, and which digits each place can still hold. Each round it reads every clue alone against what the round before settled; when no clue alone says anything more, it reads two together, then three. The most clues read at once and the rounds it took are the grade, and the levels are made of it: Easy, three digits, each clue saying something by itself, two rounds at most; Medium, three digits, three rounds or more, or two clues read together; Hard, four digits, two clues read together; Expert, four digits, three together. Of random locks, about three in four of three digits yield to one clue at a time and one in four needs two together; of four digits, about half need two together and one in twenty three. A lock of three digits takes under a millisecond to make, of four a few; an Expert is about one lock in twenty tried (nineteen tries on average, 120 at the most before the nearest is taken), a fraction of a second on an idle computer and longer on a phone, with the rete turning meanwhile. The famous lock (682, 614, 206, 738, 780: 042) grades Medium, one clue at a time over three rounds. Hint gives the solver's next step in a person's order, the clue (or clues) that says the most first, "Clue 4: 3, 7 and 8 aren't in it.", lights it and marks its digits in the notes; a lock's score is its time and 30 seconds for each hint and each wrong code tried. Lock has no daily: today's code is the daily.

The computer plays Duel at three levels, each a breaker the engine also tests: Easy loses track of older rows (each forgotten with a chance of two in five, the latest never), as a person misreads a row, about 5.7 guesses a Classic code; Medium guesses any code that fits every row, about 4.6; Hard is Knuth's minimax, 4.476 and never more than five. Hard sometimes guesses a code that can't be the answer, because it splits the codes left better (Knuth's insight). On the big boards it weighs a sample, half codes that fit and half any, within a million scores a move: under a tenth of a second here, so a phone keeps up. In a duel you set a code for the computer (or Random), it hides one for you, and you take turns, your guess and then its, a second or so later; the fewer guesses wins and the same number is a draw (breaking its code on your fifth, it still gets its fifth). On a phone its board is the second tab over yours, your code under the shutter and its guesses at it; held sideways the two stand side by side, its guesses at your code over the controls. It stands still while the menu is open.

Today's code is Classic with the fair codemaker, the same for everyone (the suite's day), and the first visit of the day opens on it (a game under way is never cut short: the menu's Code then offers Today's first). It counts the moment it's broken or its rows run out, the first finish of the day only, in `bulls:count` (the day, and the guesses, 0 for a code not broken): the games screen's figure, "Today 4". Each board and codemaker keeps how many were played and broken and the guesses they took (an average, not a best: a best of fewest guesses is luck); each lock level its best time; each duel board and level the wins, losses and draws. The menu's About says them.

Help (Settings) is off by default. Count shows how many codes still fit what you've seen, under the shutter and after each row; Marks also marks the colours no fitting code holds as ruled out, and those every fitting code holds as in, and says when the row you've entered can't be the code. Notes are your own marks, Help or not: tap Notes, then a colour (or a digit) to rule it out, again to mark it in, again to clear it; in a lock, tap a digit where it stands in a clue. At the end of a game the codes left after each row always show, so you can see what each guess told you.

The controls are a phone's: the board of rows above (as many as the board has, so nothing moves as you play), the row being entered in large pegs under it, the colours to pick, then Notes, the back key and Guess, the plate, where a thumb is. A colour tapped fills the next empty hole; tap a peg in the row to change it, twice to take it out; tap a played row to have it back in the entry; on the digit boards a digit already in the row moves rather than repeats. On a computer, 1 to 8 pick the colours, the digits the digits, Enter guesses and Backspace takes a peg out. Nothing scrolls: the rows share the board's height, down to 22 px a row, and only Super and Bulls and Cows on the shortest phone (600 tall) scroll their rows, by a row or so, in their own box, the row being played kept in sight. The line of what to do keeps two lines of room (three in a lock), so a message never moves the board. On a tablet held upright it is all larger; held sideways the board takes the left with the height and the rows grow into it, the strip, the line, the entry, the colours and the buttons standing at the right, low. Wide or not, the rows keep a phone's width, centred, so each row's keys stay beside its pegs. The look is the instruments': the code under a brass shutter that turns its pegs over at the end, the board framed in a double rule, the pegs enamel domes; Modern, Kontor and Office have them flat and plain.

Together (October 2026), from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six, each on their own phone (`together.js`), three ways, chosen for the next game under Together in the menu (whoever deals it, with their own board or level):

- Team, the pair's first game: one board and one code for the table, anyone guessing, and the key pegs of your guess show on everyone's screen but yours ("ask Sarah", sealed on yours), so whoever guesses is always told by another what it scored. It is Crates' rule (you never see the result of your own action; your partner does): neither player can finish alone, and the one who reasons less is the one who holds the answers. A guess made on a board someone else has just guessed on is turned down, and stays in the entry to be made again on what theirs showed. Every key shows to everyone once the code is broken or the rows run out. In a lock, the clues are shared out instead: each player holds some (a table of more than four, one each, some held twice), nobody holds enough to open it alone (the clues are the fewest that open it, so any part of them leaves two codes or more, which the tests check for two to six players), the others' are sealed rows with their holder's name, and a wrong code tried costs the team 30 seconds and is told to everyone.
- Race: the same code (or lock) for everyone, each on their own board; the others' progress shows in a line under the strip (their guesses, never what they guessed). The fewest guesses wins, then the quicker, each timed from their own phone's first look at the game; a lock's race goes to the best time, hints and wrong codes at 30 seconds each.
- Versus, Break only: each sets a code for the next player round the table (two: each other's) and breaks the one set for them; the breaking starts for everyone when the last code is set, the fewest guesses wins, then the quicker. The second tab (held sideways, the side column) shows the other player breaking your code as they go.

In Team each guess carries its player's badge (their initial on their seat's colour), and at every table the strip shows who's there, yours ringed, those away greyed. Every move is a transaction made only if the table is still as the phone showed it. The end, on every phone, is the standings (`standings.js`) or the team's result, with Another game and Back to solo. The pair's record gets head-to-heads as wins each way and team games as games won of those played (a team's guesses and a lock's seconds don't compare, so no team best). The table is one `together.js` table holding all three ways rather than a race on `duel.js`: a new game for the table changes the way of playing for every phone at once, as Manifest's does, and a race of guesses needs no lobby or countdown, since guesses rank first and each player's clock runs from their own first look. The computer doesn't sit at tables: two people can always play, and it plays in Duel.

Kept on the device and synced with your solo code, all under `bulls:`: `bulls:run` (the game in play, the row being entered, the notes and the board on show, so a partner watching sees exactly your screen; a watching frame runs no clock and no computer), `bulls:prefs` (the choices), `bulls:stats`, `bulls:locks`, `bulls:duels`, `bulls:count` and `bulls:table` (the row being entered at a table, so a reload keeps it). A lock's clock runs while the page is on screen and stops with the menu, the rules and the games screen; so does the computer in a duel; a table's clock doesn't stop. Links: `#b=SEED&v=BOARD&m=MAKER` a code, `#d=DAY` a day's, `#u=SEED&v=BOARD&c=LEVEL` a duel, `#l=SEED&lv=LEVEL` a lock (Copy a link, About).

`tests/bulls.mjs` checks the scoring (repeats, symmetry, every board's count of codes), the hidden code (the same from a seed on every device, every colour as likely), today's code (the same all day, a new one each day), Knuth's tree over every Classic code (five at most, 5,801 in all) and Small's, the three levels in order and Medium's guesses always fitting, Hard on the big boards breaking every code with three rows to spare (its speed is held by the engine's budget of scores a move, so it isn't timed), the devious codemaker (consistent, as hard as the worst case, never handing over the win early), a game and a duel through to their ends and back through JSON, 115 locks across the levels (one answer counted over every code, every clue needed, none giving the code away, the grade the level asks, the hints followed settling every digit, and the work each level takes to find, in locks tried rather than seconds, which a loaded machine would stretch), the clues shared among two to six (each holds one, together all, nobody's alone enough), and tables played through the database's own round trip: Team's sealed keys and a guess turned down when another got there first, Team Lock's shared clues and strikes, a Race's standings and who it waits for, Versus's codes round the table, a new game, and a move on an old game doing nothing. In a browser, a phone played Break, Lock and a Duel through with mouse clicks and touch taps, and two phones on the stand-in database played Team, Race, Versus and Team Lock end to end.

Not done: Bulls and Cows with words (the variant guessed with four-letter words, from the suite's word list) is left out, a different game from Assay's; the computer doesn't fill a seat at a table; the games screen shows today's guesses as a plain figure, and a code not broken as 0. Not yet tried on an iPhone.

### Assay
Wordle's genre, by its rules (October 2026). A hidden word; every guess must itself be a word, and each of its letters is marked: verdigris in place, ochre elsewhere in the word, the ink's warm grey not in it. A repeated letter is marked exactly as Wordle marks it: the letters in place first, then elsewhere for as many as the word has left, the rest grey (KEBAB against ABBEY: the middle B in place, the last elsewhere; GEESE against THOSE: one E, in place, the other two grey). Six guesses for five letters. A word not in the list, or of the wrong length, shakes and costs nothing. The keyboard under the board shows what each letter has shown, Enter at its left and Delete at its right as on a phone's own; a key answers as the finger goes down, and a hardware keyboard works too. The rules, the words, the levels and the daily are `assay-engine.js`'s, the computer players `assay-solve.js`'s, the team's table `assay-table.js`'s, all pure; the page is `assay.js`.

The words (`assay-words.js`, English: the engine asks only for a lexicon of that shape, its keyboard, what it accepts and what it keeps as answers, so French or German can follow with a list and a keyboard of its own). Answers are everyday words: the three commonest tiers of `wordlist.js` (SCOWL sizes 10 to 35), a spelling both sides of the Atlantic share, and a word in its own right, never a form of another. Regular forms go by rule: plurals and third persons in -s, -es and -ies, pasts in -ed and -ied (a doubled consonant included), -ing forms, a comparative or superlative with its partner beside it (nicer and nicest), and -ves, -men and Latin plurals; a short list keeps what the rules mistake (seed is not see+d, thing not th+ing, a genus no plural) and nouns that are only ever plural (jeans, pliers). Irregular forms (came, taught, geese) and words that make poor answers (crude ones and neighbours of slurs the word list's own filter missed, jargon, archaic and informal spellings, names in lower case) are on a list. That leaves 1,386 answers of four letters, 1,873 of five, 2,402 of six and 2,294 of seven. Any word of the length in the widest list is a guess. Slate's dictionary, which marks every form of another word, checks the rules: no four- or five-letter answer is only a form there.

How hard a word is. The measure is how many guesses a human-style player needs, not how rare the word is: one who opens with a word people open with (SLATE, CRANE, RAISE…), then guesses only words that could still be the answer, the commoner ones first and those of different letters rather than repeats, played 32 times from a seed made from the word itself (integers only, so it comes out the same on every phone). A word in a large family that differs in one letter takes this player many guesses, a rare word does too (it comes to mind late), and so does a repeated letter; a word that splits the field cleanly takes few. It finds the genre's known traps: HATCH, LATCH, MOUND, JOKER and SHAVE grade among the hardest, RAISE, EARTH, HEART and COURT among the easiest. Grading every word takes a minute, so it's done ahead: `node assay-grades.mjs` writes `assay-grades.js`, and the test fails, saying so, once the word list or the answer rules have changed since (meanwhile the levels go by how common a word is, at once, never a phone grading thousands of words). The levels, quantiles of each length's own grades: Easy, a word of the two commonest tiers no harder than the middle; Medium, neither among the easiest fifth nor the hardest tenth; Hard, the hardest quarter. For five letters that's 634, 1,359 and 475 words, which the human-style player solves in 3.3, 3.8 and 4.4 guesses on average.

Lengths, four to seven letters. The same measure says four is the hard length (whole families differ in one letter: BAKE, CAKE, FAKE, LAKE, MAKE; the human-style player needs 4.8 guesses and runs out of six one game in seven), so it gets a seventh guess; six and seven letters give more away with every guess (3.4 and 3.0 guesses on average) and keep six.

The rules (Settings): Free; Hard, Wordle's hard mode (a letter found in place stays there, and every letter shown to be in the word is used, as often as shown); Strict (every guess could be the word, so a letter shown elsewhere moves and a letter shown out stays out). A guess they forbid is turned away with the reason ("The 2nd letter must be R", "Use the E", "There's no C"). A change of rules takes effect at once on a word not yet guessed at, else on the next.

Today's word: five letters, Medium, one board, six guesses, the same for everyone (the suite's day, the UTC date). It's the next in one fixed shuffle of Medium's words, counted in days from 20 October 2026, so no word comes back for three and a half years. It counts the moment its last guess lands: `assay:daily` keeps each day's guesses (7 for a word not found), `assay:best` the fewest, `assay:streak` the days solved in a row (a day missed or lost ends it). The tile opens on it while it's still to play (`#today`), asking before it leaves a word you've started. Hard or Strict may be on for it; Copy the grid marks that with an asterisk, as Wordle does.

Modes (the menu's Content), each with the length and level beside it: One word; Boards, two, four or eight words at once, every guess played on each, with Dordle's, Quordle's and Octordle's 7, 9 and 13 guesses (several boards always play free, since each board's hints would contradict the others'); Dodge, Absurdle's rule, where no word is chosen: each guess is marked to keep as many words in play as it can (ties to the marks that show least), the strip says how many are left, and it's beaten only by leaving it one word and guessing that, the fewest guesses best; Sprint, as many words as you can in three minutes, six guesses each, a word solved or spent moving on at once. Weighed and left out: lengths past seven (they test recall more than deduction), Fibble's lies and Xordle's twin words (frustration more than reasoning), and sixteen or more boards (no phone holds them).

The rival (Settings): the computer plays the same game beside you, its latest marks showing in the strip as it goes (its words are shown at the end), at four levels that solve differently rather than roll dice. Novice forgets what's elsewhere (it plays on what's in place and what's out); Steady is the human-style player that grades the words; Sharp guesses the possible word that splits the rest best (the most information); Master may guess any word that splits them best, one that can't be the answer included, and closes on one when it can. On Medium five-letter words they take 4.8 guesses (Novice loses about one in seven), 3.9, 3.4 and 3.4; on four boards Novice solves about one game in four and the others every one; in a sprint they manage 1.6, 3.4, 5.6 and 8.2 words. Each guess comes after a person's thinking time (a word takes Novice about a minute and a half, Master twenty seconds), so fewer guesses win and equal guesses go to the quicker. The record against each level is kept (`assay:rivals`). A rival's whole game is worked out when yours starts (it doesn't depend on yours), so a reload and a partner watching see the same; its clock is yours, standing still while the menu, the rules or the games screen is open.

Together, two ways. The pair, from the partner sheet, plays as a team: one word for the table, the guesses taken in turns (whoever's turn it is, or anyone while that player's phone is away, so nobody's sleeping phone holds the table up), and the colours split: one of you sees what's in place, the other what's elsewhere, and neither sees what's out, which is only what neither of you has lit, so you have to say what you see. A letter that's your partner's to see is a dashed tile, never a grey one; the roles swap with each word; while someone types, the others see the letters come, with the typist's initial; and each of you can strike letters out on your own keyboard (Notes). Measured: after the same three guesses the pair has 2.5 words left, the one seeing what's in place alone 110, the other 22, and a solver given one colour alone needs about seven guesses where the two together need four, running out of six about half the time. Three or more share the three kinds round the table, the third seeing what's out. Once a word is over everyone sees everything; the pair's record keeps the team's best, the fewest guesses. Or a race (`duel.js`): the same game for everyone at the table, in the dealer's mode, length and level, each on their own phone; the fewest guesses win, then the quicker (a sprint, the most words). A Party plays the race, up to six. Either kind can be swapped for the other from the menu (Race instead, Play as a team instead): the swapping phone deals the other kind into the room's branch and the others' phones follow it there. Your own game, its stats and today's stay as they were. The computer doesn't sit at a table; the rival is for playing alone.

On the screen: the board's tiles size themselves to their box (container units), so nothing changes size while a game is played, and they grow on an upright tablet and held sideways, where the boards take the left column and the strip, the line and the keyboard stand at the right, the keys low. Several boards stand side by side or in rows, whichever gives the larger tile among those whose tiles still fit across at a legible size; where they don't fit, the box scrolls down, never across (four boards on the smallest phone, eight on any phone; held sideways, eight fit). Checked at 390×600, 390×664 and 390×844, on an iPad both ways, in Almanac, Modern, Kontor and Office by day and night, played with taps (touch events), a mouse and a keyboard, and a pair and a table of three played through on the stand-in database.

What's kept, under `assay:`: `run` (your game as it stands: the guesses, the draft being typed, the rival's game, the time played), `prefs`, `stats` (by mode, length and level: played, solved, the streak, how many guesses each took; a sprint's and the dodging word's best), `daily`, `best`, `streak`, `rivals`, `seen` (answers dealt lately, left for later), `notes` (yours, at a team's table) and `duel` (a race's run, kept apart from yours). A partner watching sees your game as it stands; a watching frame runs no clock. Where answers are kept (a game, a table, a race's match) they're shifted letter by letter, so a glance gives nothing away.

`tests/assay.mjs` (under two seconds) checks the marks, repeated letters included, against worked examples and the solvers' quick marking against 3,000 pairs; the hard and strict rules and their words; the answers (sizes, no forms of others, Slate's dictionary agreeing, nothing crude, every answer a guess); the kept grades against grades worked out now, the levels' order, the traps and the clean splitters, and levels by commonness when the grades are stale; the daily (the same word for a day, worked out again by a fresh process as another phone would, none twice in 400 days, always Medium); every mode played through, a game read back from storage, deals the same from a seed; the openers and the four rivals' order and that the stronger three solve every word; and the team's split (the parts make the whole, neither part alone narrows it as far) at a table through the database's own round trip.

Not done: the team game is one board of one word (its colours split across several boards would be more to hold than to play); a rival can't sit at a table; the share grid is Wordle's squares, plain text.

### Yield
Spelling Bee's genre, by its rules (October 2026). Seven letters in a hive; make words of four letters or more from them, every word with the centre letter in it, any letter as often as you like. Four letters score a point, a longer word a point a letter, and a pangram (a word using all seven) seven more; every puzzle has at least one. The name is the refinery's: yield is how much of each product one crude gives, here how many words seven letters give. The ranks are a refinery's run, each a share of the puzzle's points as Spelling Bee's are: Crude, Warming up (2%), On stream (5%), On spec (8%), Steady (15%), Full rate (25%), Cracking (40%), Record run (50%), Master refiner (70%, the one to aim for, Spelling Bee's Genius) and Full yield (every word, its Queen Bee). They're a gauge across the top, an instrument's scale with a tick for each rank and the index at your points; tap it for the points each rank needs. The rules, the puzzles, the levels, the daily and the computer player are `yield-engine.js`'s, pure and tested in Node; the page is `yield.js`.

The words. Spelling Bee's commonest complaints are obscure words counted and ordinary ones refused, so what counts is chosen with care and kept in `yield-words.js`: 16,791 words. SCOWL (the word list behind `wordlist.js`) files every form of a word under its base word, so its common lists hold "gonging", "tenanting" and "queening" because gong, tenant and queen are common. A word counts when its own form is used, by its Zipf frequency (the base-10 logarithm of its uses in a billion words of film subtitles, books, Wikipedia, the web and social media, from wordfreq, CC BY-SA 4.0, credited in the file): SCOWL's sizes 10 to 40 at a Zipf of 2 or more (once in ten million words; 1.5 for the commonest sizes), and sizes 50 and 55 at 3 or more, for the ordinary modern words SCOWL files as rare (online, timeline, texting, pandemic), less the names, brands and slang whose frequency flatters them (tony, japan, google). Never: an S (Yield's letters never include one, as Spelling Bee's don't, or every plural would count), a spelling only one side of the Atlantic uses (colour and color both stay out, so neither side misses the other's), and a list of slurs, vulgarities, crude words and words that sound like slurs. A word you type that doesn't count is still told apart: "a word, but rarer than Yield counts", "one side of the Atlantic's spelling only", or "not in Yield's list" (a slur is never called a word), and the first two are kept beside your words, without points. `yield-words.js` is written by a builder from `wordlist.js` and wordfreq's English list (with the coordinator to file in `tools/`), which also keeps the frequency of each word to a tenth: the levels and the computer player are made of it.

The yield is exact: every word the letters make, found by trying every subset of the seven letters against the words that count (128 lookups), so nothing is missed and nothing invented. Spelling Bee has no single solution to prove; what every puzzle must have is a pangram, and a deal without one is never made (2,526 letter sets have one, each with seven centres). A puzzle is never mostly one ending (-ing words 30% of it at most, -ed words 45%), since a yield of one suffix is one trick, not a puzzle.

The Wheel (the menu's Board) is the genre's other half, the newspapers' Target and Polygon: nine tiles from one nine-letter word, the centre tile in every word, each tile used once (a letter on two tiles can be used twice), and the nine-letter word scores as a pangram. It asks something else of you, finding words inside a word rather than building them up, and a tile in use greys out as you type. 2,107 nine-letter words make wheels.

Levels are crude grades, Light, Medium and Heavy, and they measure depth, how far down the vocabulary the coveted rank needs you to reach: finding the words in order of ease (the commoner and the shorter first, as people tend to, each letter past four counting as a fifth of a Zipf), depth is the ease of the word that takes your points past Master refiner's 70%. Light reaches it on everyday words (a depth of 2.75 or more), Medium needs a few less common ones (2.35 to 2.75), Heavy words people rarely use (under 2.35); the size is only a range for each (a hive's Light 20 to 42 words, Medium 26 to 55, Heavy 30 to 75; a wheel's 16 to 36, 20 to 45 and 22 to 55), and Light's and Medium's pangrams are common ones. Measured on generated puzzles (`tests/yield.mjs`): median depths 2.9, 2.6 and 2.2; median sizes 30, 40 and 49 words in a hive, 24, 34 and 27 in a wheel; and the computer player at Steady takes a median 17 minutes to Master refiner on Light, 35 on Medium, and on Heavy mostly never gets there, which is the grading checked against how a person plays. Whether those are the right bands is for Korbi's thumb. A deal takes a millisecond or two.

Today's puzzle (the menu's Puzzle, which also picks a random puzzle's level) is a Medium hive, the same for everyone (`today()`). Deciding which letter sets can make a Medium hive takes seconds, too long for a phone to work out as it opens, so `yield-words.js` keeps the walk: every one of the 1,039 sets that can, in a fixed shuffled order from 20 October 2026, a set a day, so none comes back for nearly three years; each day takes the first of its set's centres that makes a Medium puzzle, in an order its date sets. `tests/yield.mjs` fails if the walk no longer matches what the engine decides (rebuild it then). Today's points go to the games screen as each word is found (`yield:daily`, by the day: the figure is today's points), so leaving costs nothing, and the tile opens today's puzzle while you have none. Today's and a random puzzle are kept apart, so switching between them loses neither; the daily before today's is kept too, and the menu shows its words, what you missed faint (Yesterday's words). Show the words (the menu) ends a puzzle there and lists them all.

Playing: tap the letters (a letter goes in as the finger lands, so quick tapping keeps up), Enter, Delete (hold it to clear the word), and Shuffle, which turns the outer letters round; on a keyboard, type, Enter, Backspace, Space to shuffle, Escape to clear. The word shows over the hive, its centre letters in the game's honey ochre and any letter not on the board faint; what a word got is said under it ("Fine +6", "Pangram! +14", the new rank when it changes one, or why it doesn't count, the word shaking). Under the gauge, your words on one line, newest first (tap for them all, in columns), and the Chart: Spelling Bee's hints grid, what's left by first letter and length, and by first two letters. It's free, as Spelling Bee's is. A word tapped in any list says what it means, the clue bank's plain clue (`clues.js`, Crossword's and Acrostic's), so the words you missed teach you something; the bank covers the commonest words so far, and says when it has none.

Sprints and the rival (Settings). Clock: a random puzzle untimed, or a sprint of three or five minutes, the most points before time's up (best kept for each clock, board and level); a sprint stands still while the menu or the games screen is open, and its clock runs only while the page is on screen. Rival: the computer plays the same puzzle beside you, at Novice, Steady, Sharp or Master, its index under the gauge and its words and rank in a line, its words shown at the end. It plays as a person does: it knows a word with a chance that falls as the word's ease goes below its vocabulary, finds what it knows commoner and shorter words first with some luck in the order, takes longer over rarer words and much longer as the easy ones run out, and a moment to tap each in. Its whole game is planned from the puzzle's seed when the puzzle starts, so a reload or a partner watching sees the same, and it runs on your clock. Fitted to how people play, on a Medium hive: in a three-minute sprint a median 34, 45, 61 and 69 points; Master refiner in about 35 minutes for Steady, 15 for Sharp and 10 for Master, never for Novice, who finds about half the points in half an hour. A rival joins the puzzle in front of you, as far on as it would have got by now.

Together (duel.js, README Head to head: from the partner sheet, or a party of up to six), in the dealer's way (Together in the menu, switched at the table for the next match): a race or a team; until you choose, a pair plays as a team and a party races. A race: the same letters for everyone and a clock of three or five minutes, the others' points as small indexes under your gauge and in a line; the most points wins, equal points going to whoever reached them first, and the result stars the words nobody else found and lists the best you missed. A team: the same letters, and a centre letter each, so every player's words are their own: the deal picks the two letters whose words only one of you can enter are the most even (each about a third of the team's yield), so the less practised player has words that only they can play, and the other can only tell them; a word typed with only your partner's letter says so ("That one's Sarah's: it has their C, not your B"). Your hive has your letter in the middle and your partner's ringed in their colour with their initial; each word shows in the colour of whoever found it; the ranks are the team's, over every word any of you can make. The chart is split: your phone holds your partner's (what's left that they can enter, by first letter and length) and theirs holds yours, so each of you guides the other. At a party, every player gets a letter of their own, each holding the next player's chart. A team's puzzle ends when every word is found or someone calls it a day (the menu), and the pair's record keeps it as a team's: the share of the yield found, won at Master refiner. Each phone writes only its own words to the table, so two finds at once never collide; the team's yield is everyone's together (`teamFound`). The computer doesn't sit at a table: the rival is for playing alone.

What's kept, under `yield:`: `day` (today's puzzle as you've played it: the words, the word being typed, the order of the outer letters, the time), `run` (the random one), `on` (which of them is on screen), `last` (the daily before today's), `prefs`, `stats` (random puzzles started, Master refiner and Full yield reached, sprint bests), `daily` (today's points by the day, for the games screen) and `duel` (your run of a match). Everything on the screen is in them, so a partner watching sees the same board, letters in the same places.

The screen never scrolls: the gauge, your words, the word being typed and the board share the height, the hive taking what's left (390×600 and up). On an upright tablet it's drawn larger; held sideways, the board, the word and the keys stand on the left with all the height, and the gauge and every word found, alphabetically, in a box beside them with the Chart under it. Every theme by day and night: the hive's centre is honey ochre in all of them, Almanac's ornaments (the hairline inside each hex, the plate's inner line) left out of the others, which `yield.css` undoes itself.

`tests/yield.mjs` checks the words (every one a word, none with an S or one side's spelling, the blocked ones never, ordinary ones counted and SCOWL's odd forms not), every kind of word typed, points and ranks, that a puzzle's yield is exactly what a brute-force pass over every word finds and has a pangram (72 puzzles of both boards and every level), that a seed always deals the same puzzle and the levels go deeper and slower in order, today's puzzle the same everywhere and 120 days of different sets, the walk against the engine, the rival (the same game from a seed, only the puzzle's words, its levels in order), the chart's counts, a team's split (the two players' words make the team's yield and neither alone does, each with a fair share only they can enter, the same deal on every phone, through the database's round trip) and a run through JSON. Two pages on the stand-in database played a team match (a word with the partner's letter turned away and found by the partner, both phones showing it, the chart split, called a day on both) and a race to its result, with real taps; a sprint ran out with a rival; every size in every theme by day and night was looked at.

Not done: meanings for the rarer words (the clue bank has the commonest; Slate's dictionary has more, but it's Slate's to share); bonus points for the rarer words typed (they're acknowledged, not scored, so a race can't be won by trying letter strings); Boggle's rule for a race, where a word anyone else found scores nothing (the stars show those words, without the scoring); a wheel daily. For Korbi to decide: the ranks' names, the levels' bands, and whether today's figure on the games screen should be the rank rather than the points.

### Codeword
(The game is being built, October 2026; its grid filler is in.)

The grid filler, `gridfill.js`, fills a crossword grid with real words; Codeword made it and Crossword uses it, without changing it (its use is documented at the top of the file). It makes grids too, symmetric under a half turn as printed ones are: dense ones for a codeword, where most letters cross (about a quarter black, never four blacks in a square or three in a row, words of three to eight letters, nine from 13 across), and British blocked ones for a crossword, the lattice of black squares every blocked grid grows from, with more blacks added to cut its long runs, every word at least half crossed and room asked for answers of given lengths. It fills depth first, the open slot with fewest words left first; each slot's words are a bitset over its length's list, so a crossing letter narrows them with one AND per 32 words, and after each word every slot it crosses must still have a word and a letter it can share with each of its own crossings. The budget is counted in search steps, never in time, so the same seed fills the same way on every phone (a daily grid depends on it), and split over many fresh starts, since a search that runs long was usually lost in its first few words. Words to place first (Crossword's knowledge-base answers) each go where they leave the slots they cross the most words; words for given slots and letters already in the grid are kept. A cover (Codeword's: every letter of the alphabet in the grid) is met in two goes: the fill tries the rare letters it lacks on cells no other word crosses, where they cost nothing, then each letter still missing is brought in by emptying a six-by-six window of the grid and filling it again with that letter required and none lost, window after window. Measured here: a dense 13×13 grid with all 26 letters about three times in four, a third of a second each, and British 13×13 grids with four or five answers placed nine times in ten. `tests/gridfill.mjs` checks slots and clue numbers, the grids it refuses, both styles at three sizes (symmetric, valid, the same for the same seed, the dense rules and the lattice), fills of distinct words from the source, fixed and placed words, letters already in, the cover, and the same seed filling the same way.

### Ladder
Lewis Carroll's Doublets (October 2026), the word ladder he invented on Christmas Day 1877, called Word-links in his diary and published as Doublets in Vanity Fair from 29 March 1879: from a first word to a last of the same length, one letter changed at a time, each letter keeping its place and every rung a word. Par is the fewest steps it can be done in, found by searching every word there is, so it's proven, not estimated; and every ladder dealt can be done at par in words a person knows. Korbi once turned down a word-ladder mode as "just finding words with letters again". What makes this one more is par (you always play against the best there is, and learn how many routes reach it), the route (the end shows the commonest ladder at par, how many there were, and whether yours was it), and pairs with a link between them: Carroll's own, the word golf Nabokov gave Kinbote in Pale Fire, and pairs chosen for what joins them (COLD to WARM, LEAD to GOLD, BULL to BEAR, SINK or SWIM).

Playing. The ladder is drawn as one: brass rails, the first word pinned at the top of the card and the last at its foot, a rung for every word, and between them the rungs still to go at par as short rungs with a brass dot, so you see how far par is. Tap a letter of the working rung, then its new letter on the keys: if that makes a word it is the next rung; if not, the next rung shows the word in red for a moment, the line says why (not a word here, more than one letter, already on your ladder), and the letter stays chosen, so another key can be tried at once. A whole word can be typed instead, Enter placing it, which suits a keyboard (the arrow keys move along the letters, up and down switch ends). The letter each rung changed is underlined in cobalt. You work from either end, as on paper: Work up (or a tap on the last word) builds from the bottom, Work down from the top, and the ladder is whole the moment a word made at one end is a word of the other; any rungs past the join, a way that end needn't have gone, are dropped. Undo takes off the last rung made, at whichever end, and costs nothing, since a step is counted in the finished ladder, not in the tries; a word already on your ladder can't come back (Undo back to it instead).
