Hint, 30 seconds, puts on the next rung of the commonest shortest route from where you are, and says how many steps are left. A real word is never refused: a rung may be any word of three to six letters up to SCOWL 55 (tier 5 of `wordlist.js`), British and American spellings both.

The words. The two ends are the commonest words (SCOWL 10), never a plural, a past tense or an -ing form of another word, a word like ALSO or FROM, an irregular past (CAME, KNEW), a comparative, an -ly adverb, computing's jargon (BYTE, CURSOR), or one spelling's alone (COLOUR). Par counts any word a rung may be; the generator keeps only ladders that can be done at par in words no rarer than SCOWL 35, so par never hangs on a word nobody knows. The word list leaves out offensive words but kept some of their plurals and past tenses (CUMS, CUNTS, FUCKS, KIKES and about forty more): Ladder treats those as no words at all, and a few crude ones a player may use (BUM, PEE) the game never shows itself, as an end, a hint, a route or the computer's rung. That belongs in `wordlist.js` for every word game, and is for its owner.

Par and its routes. The words of a length are a graph, two words a step apart when they differ in one letter (found through patterns with one letter blanked, kept as numbers, which is quicker on a phone than strings: a length's graph takes about 25 ms in Node). A breadth-first search from each end gives par and every word on a shortest route; the routes at par are then counted exactly, layer by layer, up to a million, and the test checks the count against a walk of every shortest route by hand. The commonest route at par is the one whose rarest word is commonest, plurals and past tenses counting a shade rarer: it's what the result shows and what a hint follows. Searching takes under a millisecond.

Difficulty. What makes a ladder hard is the search it takes, not its length alone. A ladder whose letters can each be changed straight to the last word's, in some order, has no detours: trying the obvious change at each rung finds it. One that needs a letter changed to a go-between and changed again later (HEAD to TAIL changes its third letter twice: HEAD, HEAL, TEAL, TELL, TALL, TAIL) has a detour, which hides par from the obvious; and one with a single route at par hides it best. The measure is `effort`: how many words a careful person's search tries before it holds a ladder at par (best first by the steps taken plus the letters still wrong, in common words). On 1,800 ladders, a ladder with no detours took par plus one words (straight there); one detour at par 5, about 9; two at par 6, 23; three at par 7 or 8, 56 to 113. The levels, at four letters (three, five and six letters have ranges of their own): Easy is par 3 or 4, at most one detour, at most 12 words tried and two routes at par or more; Medium par 5 or 6, one or two detours, 8 to 60 words; Hard par 6 to 8, two detours or more, 25 words at least, and exactly one route at par, so on Hard there is one way to do it in par and the count proves it. A test run of 360 ladders climbs as it should: par 4.1, 5.6 and 7.2 on average, 6, 16 and 73 words tried. The generator draws a first word, takes every end a par's distance away that can be reached at par in common words, and grades them until one fits: 1 to 15 ms at four letters, 70 at six. These figures come from the search, not from people: whether Easy is easy is for Korbi's thumb.

Ladders, in the menu: Today's, Random, Linked or The book. Today's is Medium, four or five letters, Classic, made from today's date (`today()`), the same on every phone, and counts for the games screen the moment it's whole, as its score in seconds (`noteDayTime`; the tile shows it as a time and opens today's while it's still to play). Random deals at the menu's level from the word lengths ticked (3, 4, 5, 6; four and five to start). Linked deals 160 pairs in six themes, ticked in the menu (Opposites, Trade, Food & farm, Nature, Creatures, Life & play), each with its line in Carroll's manner ("Turn a BULL into a BEAR", "Render FAT into OIL", "Roll SPOT into TERM"), at the level its par puts it, none twice until every pair at that level has come up (`ladder:seen`). The book is Carroll's own, his doublets in his book's order (Macmillan, 1879), his example HEAD to TAIL first, each a chapter that opens once the one before is climbed, with his line ("Drive PIG into STY", "Evolve MAN from APE"); then Kinbote's three. Seven of Carroll's thirty-eight are left out: ROGUE to BEAST, ELM to OAK, HARE to SOUP, PITCH to TENTS, PITY to GOOD and EEL to PIE need words nobody uses now (BEAUT, OIK, SOUK, TENCH, POOS, PEE), and BUY an ASS a word the list leaves out. The rest may use the words of his day, up to SCOWL 50 (GIRD, GIRT, RICK, as in his own solutions). CAIN and ABEL are names: they join the graph as guests, the ends of their own ladder and rungs of no other. Kinbote's records are "hate—love in three, lass—male in four, and live—dead in five (with lend in the middle)": the first two are par, and the third can't be done in five through any word of SCOWL's widest list (it takes six, through LEND as he says), which the result points out. The knowledge base supplies none of the pairs: its things are names and phrases the word list doesn't hold, and the few plain words among them (gold, coal, corn, cotton) are in Trade by hand.

Score, bests and the games screen. A finished ladder's score is its seconds, a minute for every step over par and half a minute a hint; lower is better. A step costs more than speed does, since par is the point. Bests are kept by kind, level and rules (`ladder:bests`, lowest), and clean pars in a row (at par without a hint, `ladder:pars`). The book keeps each chapter's steps (`ladder:book`).

Rules: Classic, or Grow and shrink. The genre's own variant, word ladders with additions, lets a step also add or take away a letter: it earns its place by opening pairs of different lengths, which Classic can't play at all (HOT to COLD in three, SUN to MOON, WAR to PEACE, MAN to WOMAN, ORE to METAL, IRON to STEEL, CRUDE to FUEL, GRAPE to WINE, SHEEP to WOOL), and by routes through shorter and longer words. 35 of the 160 linked pairs need it, and every pair plays by it. Tap a gap between two letters (or before or after them) to add one there, ⌫ on a chosen letter takes it away, or type the whole word. Its graph is every length at once, 15,378 words and 48,570 steps, built in about half a second in Node (the rete says Setting out the words), and its search's lower bound is the edit distance. Weighed and left out: an anagram as a step (rearranging every letter at once makes most ladders a step or two and the puzzle another game), a word the ladder must pass through (two ladders end to end: more length, nothing new), and a timed run of ladders (the race below is three against the clock already).

The computer, in Settings, is off, a rival or a partner, at a level: Novice, Club or Master. As a rival it climbs the same ladder as a person does, with `search()`: a novice knows only the commonest words and rushes at the end word, so it often takes a step or two more than par; a club player rushes less; a master searches exactly and finds par. Each thinks before every rung, longer for each word it tried, with a person's unevenness, and its climb comes from a seed, so it's the same on every device and through a reload; it stands still with your clock while the menu is open. On ten Medium and Hard ladders a novice took 1.8 steps over par in about 98 seconds, a club player 0.1 in 50, a master par in 32. Its progress shows under the figures, and the result says who won, by score. As a partner it takes the seat nobody has: it holds every other letter of a random or linked Classic ladder, dealt as a team's are (the letters that must change first, so both of you are needed), and the two of you work down from the first word. Whenever the ladder changes it looks for a step it can make with its own letters (`partnerMove`): a master takes one on a shortest route whenever there is one, a club player or a novice follows the route its own search finds, which may be longer; it makes the step after a person's thinking, or waits for yours, and the line under the figures says which. You can change only your letters (ringed in your colour; its rungs are marked in copper), and you see the whole of the last word, since the computer can't talk. Undo and Hint work as alone; the result counts each one's steps; a partner's ladders keep bests of their own. With a master and a careful player, sixteen ladders in the test all came out at par.

Together: the pair from the partner sheet, or a party of up to six. One table plays either game, chosen in the menu's together part for the next game and in the lobby for this one until it starts; a pair plays as a team by default and a party races. Everyone says Ready, and a countdown starts the game for everyone at the same moment by the database's clock, as the head-to-heads on `duel.js` do; anyone ready may start without waiting for someone who never comes (in a team, that player's letters are let go to the rest until they arrive). Ladder runs its own table rather than `duel.js` because the table also holds a team's ladder and a request carries no mode, so the page decides, as Manifest's does: it is `duel-core.js`'s table (the same Ready, countdown, runs and standings) with the team's ladder beside the runs, every change a transaction on it (`ladder-table.js`, pure; `ladder-room.js`, the page round it).

The team: one Classic ladder, long enough for everyone to hold a letter (four letters for a pair or three, five for five, six for six). The letters are dealt round the table, the letters the first and last words differ in first, then the rest, starting a step further each game; and the dealer makes sure there are as many letters to change as players. So everyone holds a letter that every ladder between the two words must change, and nobody can climb it alone. Only you may change your letters, which are ringed in your colour (another's are faded, and a tap on one says whose it is); and each of you sees only your own letters of the last word, the others' sealed, so you know where your letters must end and your partner knows where theirs must, and the team has to talk. A rung made on a ladder that has moved on is refused, and the page shows it as it is. Undo and Hint are the team's (a hint's rung may be anyone's letter). A player who is away, asleep or gone, lets go of their letters, and anyone may change them until they're back, so a sleeping phone never holds the team up. Each rung's changed letter carries its maker's colour, and the end counts each player's rungs. The team's score is its time from the start, a minute a step over par and half a minute a hint, kept for the pair as a team's best.

The race: the same three ladders for everyone, dealt by whoever deals at their level, letters and rules, each climbed on their own phone from the shared start, Next ladder after each, the clock running through. The others' progress shows under the figures (Sarah 2 of 3, then her score). The lowest score wins (seconds from the start, a minute a step over par, half a minute a hint), recorded for the pair as a head-to-head; Rematch deals three more, the one who asked ready. A reload carries on where it was (`ladder:match`). In a party it's listed as a race for up to six.

Saved state. The ladder on screen is `ladder:run`: its pair and par, the rungs at each end and the order they came in, the hints, the clock, and the computer's climb or a partner's letters and who made each rung, saved at every rung and every five seconds, so a partner watching sees it as it stands. Choices are `ladder:prefs`. Links: `#d=DAY` today's, `#s=SEED&l=LEVEL&n=LENGTHS&r=RULES` a random ladder, `#p=FIRST-LAST&r=RULES` a pair (`&c=` a chapter of the book).

The screen. On a phone: the figures (par, steps, the clock), the pair's line, the ladder's card taking what's left, one line of what to do or what happened (one line always, so nothing moves), the tools, the keys. A tile is as large as lets the whole ladder at par fit the card, and never smaller than a thumb's target (2.2rem); a longer ladder scrolls inside the card with both ends pinned. Held sideways, the ladder takes the left with all the height and the figures, the line, the tools and the keys stand beside it, the keys at the bottom; an upright tablet draws it all larger. Checked at 390×600, 390×664, 390×844, 820×1180 and 1180×820, in the four themes by day and night, played through with taps and touches.

`tests/ladder.mjs` checks Carroll's rules, par for known pairs, the route counts against a hand walk, the generator at every level and length across 360 seeds (each fits its level; Hard's single route counted by hand up to two; the same seed, the same ladder), the grading's climb, a run through JSON, hints and scores, the computer players at their three levels, the computer partner (it changes only its own letters, waits when the next step is yours, and finds par with a careful player), the daily, every pair of the book and the linked pairs, and the table through the database's round trip: the letters dealt round two, three and four players with everyone holding one that must change, the last word's letters each sees making the whole between them and neither alone, one player stopped at the other's letter, the pair climbing at par together, refused rungs, undo and hints, an away player's letters let go, a race's standings and its end. Two phones in one browser on the stand-in database played a team game (each seeing only its own letters of HOST: H _ _ T and _ O S _) and a race of three.

Not done: the computer in a seat at a table (an away player's letters go to the others instead, which keeps people central), Grow and shrink for a team or the computer partner (a letter keeps its owner only while letters keep their places), a daily drawn from the linked pairs (today's is made, so it never runs out or repeats), and the words' meanings (Slate's dictionary could give them, a tap on a rung).

### Isomer
Jumble's genre, by its rules (October 2026; Jumble is Tribune's name, and an isomer is the same atoms arranged differently, as a jumble is the same letters). Four jumbled words to put right, one letter to each square; some of the squares are circled, and the circled letters, put in order, are the final answer. The newspaper's final is the punchline to a cartoon; ours is a thing from the knowledge base clued by its hand-written hint, asked either way round, the two Korbi likes: the thing from its hint and its country or commodity ("Peru · Food & drink: a grape brandy it claims as its own" → PISCO), or the country or commodity from one of its clues ("A country · History: Pharaohs: ancient god-kings" → EGYPT). The answer's squares show its words' lengths. The English is `isomer-words.js`'s, the finals and themes `isomer-kb.js`'s, the puzzle, the run and the solver `isomer-engine.js`'s, the pair's table `isomer-table.js`'s, all pure; the page is `isomer.js`. The logo is a molecule of four lettered atoms in a zigzag, the third enamelled and ringed in brass, as a circled square is.

Playing. The jumbles stand in rows, each its jumbled letters beside its squares, the answer's squares under a rule. Tap a row and its letters stand in the rack at the bottom, large, under the thumb; tap them in order and each goes to the next empty square. A tap on a placed letter, ⌫ or Clear takes letters back, and Shuffle puts the rack in another order (a solver's own trick: a new arrangement to look at). A full row that's the word turns verdigris, its jumble struck through and its circled letters in cobalt, and the next row comes up; one that isn't shakes and says so, its letters left where they are to be moved. A wrong try costs nothing: every jumble has one word, so any other full row is no word at all. The final's rack is the tray: the circled letters in a jumbled order, each blank until its word is solved, as the newspaper's circled letters are only yours once their word is; it's put together from them the same way. Hint puts the right letter in the first square that lacks it, locked there, for 15 seconds; on the final it can lend a letter whose word isn't solved yet. A hardware keyboard works too: letters, Backspace, Enter (the next row to do) and the arrows.

The words. A jumble's answer is an everyday word from `wordlist.js`, the commonest tiers by level (SCOWL 10, 20 or 35), never a spelling only one side of the Atlantic has, never an inflection of another word (a plural, a past in -ed, an -ing, an -est with its stem a word: a jumble that ends -ING gives itself away), and its letters spell exactly one word in the widest list (SCOWL 70, both spellings), so it has one answer: FLOAT is never dealt (ALOFT), BOTTLE is. That leaves 234, 507 and 1,250 words of five letters by tier, 267, 663 and 1,879 of six, 255, 631 and 2,079 of seven. A jumble keeps no letter in its own square and no pair of the answer's neighbours, and isn't a word itself, so FLOAT would never show as TLOAF (LO and OA show through); a word whose letters can't be jumbled that way (BANANA, KNOCK) isn't dealt.

Making a puzzle, and proving it has one answer. The final is drawn first: by your topics and the level's clue difficulty, the thing or its country or commodity (each answer as likely as the next, however many clues it has). Its letters are shared out among the words, a circle at least to each and at most three to a word of five or six letters (four to one of seven), and a word is found for each share, the shares with the rarest letters first, while the most words are left; its circles go on letters it has. A word two edits from a final, or with four letters in a row in common (FRENCH beside FRANCE), would hint at it and is passed over. The tray's order is jumbled by the same rules, so it never spells a final. Then the solver counts the answers, up to two each: the words each jumble's letters spell; the things in the knowledge base each final's clue could be, spelled with the tray's letters; and the tray holding exactly the finals' letters. One each, or the deal goes again. A hint that says its own thing (SAUNA in "saunas", COPPER in "Copperbelt") isn't a clue, nor one that names its answer. A puzzle is dealt in 5 to 30 ms on a laptop once the solver's model is made; the first deal on a page makes it and lists the words (a fifth of a second on a laptop, perhaps a second on a phone), which the page does in a quiet moment after it opens, and a puzzle kept from before draws without it.

How hard a jumble is: how deep a person's search goes. People unjumble by trying the arrangements that look most like English first (a common start and ending, familiar pairs and triples of letters) and stopping at the first word they know, so the measure is the answer's place in that order: a letter trigram model (each letter's chance after the two before it, the word's start and end counting as letters) trained on the word list's SCOWL 40 and commoner words, every arrangement scored as it's built. BLACK and BEFORE fall into place at once (depth 1); ISLAND waits behind 61 likelier-looking strings, WISDOM behind 70, ETHNIC behind 165; listing every arrangement agrees. Commonness counts too (a rare word is passed by), and so does the jumble's own look: a jumble that looks like a word fixes the reader on a wrong reading, as anagram studies found. The levels give each length a warm word and a deep one. Easy: depths 1 to 2 and 1 to 3, SCOWL 10 only, jumbles that look like nothing, the final's clue of the easiest kind. Medium: warm 1 to 4, deep 4 to 30, SCOWL 20 and commoner, any jumble, clues mostly of the first two kinds. Hard: warm 2 to 12, deep 14 and beyond, SCOWL 35 and commoner, lures (the most word-like jumble that still hides its word: ETHNIC as CHIENT), clues mostly of the harder two. Graded against 40 puzzles a level, the median depth is 1, 4 and 14, and the standard solver (Sharp, below) takes at the median: Classic 0:32, 1:03 and 2:14; Theme 0:58, 1:17 and 1:39; Double 0:36, 1:04 and 2:24; Jumbo 0:50, 1:47 and 4:40. That's the puzzle's par, shown at the end. Whether Medium's deep band is right is for Korbi's thumb.

What the finals are about. The finals and the themes are Crates' clue links (`bank.js`, through `core.js`): 10,027 finals, 5,052 things and 4,975 of a country or commodity from a clue (over 131 answers: UK, UAE, HVO and the other acronyms are never a final themselves), and 116 countries and commodities a theme can be. The menu's Content: Answers (one of: Countries, Commodities or Mixed, each puzzle one or the other), and Topics, Crates' mix, each topic out, in or more, in a country grid and a commodity grid, with the presets (Balanced, Trader, Culture night); a final is as likely as its link's likeliest topic. A mix too narrow for a level deals from every topic and says so. Your own mix doesn't count for your best; the presets and today's do.

What you learn. A final you needed a hint for (and a theme's clue) goes to the review pile as a name-it card: the hint with its answer and topic, the name to give, and four options, which is how Deck asks a card it has no typed form for (Deck asks Punt's name-it cards typed, and could ask Isomer's the same way). One found without a hint is marked recognised in `known.js`, so Punt's name-it questions may ask it the other way round, and a card of it that was due moves up a pile.

The modes, the genre's real variants that earn their place. Classic, the newspaper's: two words of five letters and two of six, one final. Theme, Jumble's Brainbusters: the four jumbles are a country's or a commodity's own clues, each with its hint in the clue box while you're on it, and the circled letters name what all four point to; the four are chosen, where they can be, so that no other answer in the bank carries all four (Crates' rule for a crate), and a clue whose letters spell another word can't be one (CHOPIN would be PHONIC, KURTA KRAUT). Double: two finals from one tray, the circled letters unmarked, a thing clued by its hint and topic alone and its country or commodity clued by its kind and region, so splitting the tray is a step of its own and each answer helps with the other; a hint shared word for word by other clues isn't used for it ("The second-biggest producer", under India for Sugar and under Sugar for India, would let the two trade places). Jumbo, the Sunday size: six words of five to seven letters and a longer final, names of several words welcome (Rudolf Diesel, The Fuggers). Weighed and left out: a countdown (the race and the rival give the pressure, and the score is a time already); four-letter children's jumbles (Easy is that); Jumble's crossword (another genre, Codeword's and Crossword's); hiding the circles (the final would have nothing to stand on).

Playing together. The pair's match, from the partner sheet, splits the puzzle: each holds two of the jumbles (three in Jumbo), the other's sealed on their screen (hatched squares, the circles showing, the holder's initial), dealt so that each has a word of each length and one of the two easier ones; and each holds half of the final's clue (one the hint, the other what it's under and its topic; for a country or commodity from its clue, one the clue and the other its hint), with a line saying the partner has the other half. Nobody can see the other's letters, so they have to be read out; a jumble solved shows on both phones and drops its circled letters into both trays, and the final needs every circled letter, so neither can finish alone; either can put it in once the two halves of its clue have been said aloud, and a hinted letter of the final shows on both. The team's clock starts when both are there, hints cost the team, and the team's time (with its hints) is the pair's record, lower the better. A race (`duel.js`): the same puzzle for everyone at the table, each on their own phone, the quickest (time and hints) winning; a Party plays it, up to six. The pair can go from one to the other in the menu (Race instead; Two jumbles each instead): the other phone follows, and a reload sits back down at whichever of the two that phone was at.

The computer. A rival (Settings) plays the same puzzle beside you, as the solver: down the arrangements by how English they look, a try at a time, recognising the word with a chance that falls with its rarity (a word missed costs a pass of 40 more tries), the final known from its clue with a chance by its difficulty and otherwise worked out from its letters, the jumbles quickest first and the final once its letters are in. Three paces: Steady, Sharp (the standard solver, par) and Ace; on Classic Medium they take 2:49, 1:03 and 0:26 at the median. Its progress is in the strip, its time at the end, and the record against each pace is kept. The dealer's rival comes along to a race as well: every phone plays the same one from the match's seed, and its time is in the result.

On the screen. A phone held upright is the design: from 390×600 up nothing scrolls but the clue box when it's long; the squares and the rack's tiles size themselves to their boxes (container units), so nothing changes size while a puzzle is played, and a long final's letters stand in two lines of the rack from the start. On an upright tablet everything grows; held sideways the stage takes the left, and the clock and the clue stand at the top right, where they're read, the rack and the tools at the bottom, where a thumb is. Modern, Kontor and Office drop the stage's inset hairline and round the corners (in `isomer.css`; `themes.css` is shared). Checked at 390×600, 390×664 and 390×844, 820×1180 and 1180×820, in Almanac, Modern, Kontor and Office by day and night; puzzles of every mode played through with taps; a pair on two phones (the stand-in database) through to the record, to a race and back, with a reload mid-race; and a party's race of three.

Scoring and today's. The score is the seconds on the clock and 15 for each hinted letter, lower the better; bests by mode and level in `isomer:times` (not `isomer:best`, which the games screen reads as highest-is-best). Today's puzzle is a Classic at Medium, every topic in, Mixed, dealt from the suite's day, the same for everyone; it counts the moment its last answer goes in, its score the games screen's figure (a time: `noteDayTime`), and the tile opens it while it's still to play, asking before it leaves a puzzle you've started.

What's kept, under `isomer:`: `run` (your puzzle and how it stands: every square, the hints, the rack's order, the clock; saved at every move and every five seconds, so a partner watching sees it), `opts` (mode, level, answers, rival), `mix`, `times`, `today`, `rivals`, `pair` (your own squares at the pair's table), `where` (which of the pair's two tables you were at) and `race:duel` (a race's run, apart from yours). A watching frame runs no clock and writes nothing. The clock stands still while the menu or the games screen is open and while the page is out of sight; at a table it doesn't.

`tests/isomer.mjs` (about two seconds) checks the depth against listing every arrangement, the solver's counts, inflections, the word pools, that jumbles hide their words and lures look more like English than plain ones; names as letters and the finals and themes; 168 puzzles over every mode and level (each with exactly one answer, the tray exactly the finals' letters, the circles within bounds, every jumble hiding its word, none echoing a final, every word in its level's band, the same seed the same puzzle, a theme a crate by Crates' rule, Double's clues apart, Jumbo's size); the levels apart in what they take the solver, and the depths; the computer the same for a seed, every item solved in order, its paces apart; the rules (placing, a wrong row, taking back, a solve, the tray opening, hints locked, over a wrong letter and lent, the final, the score, Double's shared tray); a run kept through JSON; the pair's split through the database's round trip (each holds two jumbles of each length and they hold all four, the clue's halves make the whole and neither alone does, neither can solve the other's jumbles or open the whole tray alone, the hints, the end, the record, a stale tap, a partner's solve shown); and today's puzzle the same for a day and another the next.

Not done: a pair's empty seat isn't the computer's (the rival is for playing alone and alongside a race); the finals come from Crates' bank only (countries and commodities, not the paintings, cities and the rest of the knowledge base); and the pile's cards are asked with options in Deck until Deck asks Isomer's typed.

### Lode
Strands' genre on the knowledge base (October 2026). A board of 48 letters, eight rows of six, hides a theme's words: each is a path of touching letters (sideways, up and down, or diagonally), no letter used twice, and between them the words use every letter on the board exactly once. One of them, the spanning word or mother lode, names the theme and touches two opposite sides of the board (left and right, or top and bottom). A title, a clue to the theme, heads the board. Trace a word by dragging a finger through its letters, or tap them one at a time and tap the last again; slide back to take the last letter off, and tap off the letters to clear. A theme word found locks in lapis, the spanning word in gold, and the line under the board says what links it to the theme. Any other English word of four letters or more (`wordlist.js`, every tier) counts towards a hint, three to a hint, as in Strands. A hint rings the letters of the hardest word still hidden; a second while it's ringed numbers them in order. The score is the seconds on the clock plus 30 a hint, lower being better.

The themes are the knowledge base's countries and commodities: 148 of Crates' 159 answers can make a board (122 at Easy). A theme's words are its clue links in Crates' bank (`bank.js`, built from `kb/`), so they follow the knowledge base as it grows: every clue whose name is one word of 4 to 10 letters once accents are dropped (Björk as BJORK, Weißwurst as WEISSWURST, Ørsted as ORSTED), never one holding the theme's own name (Copperbelt never comes under Copper, though Copper comes under Zambia), each with its link's difficulty, topics and reason. The spanning word is the one thing no source gives, so `lode-themes.js` writes it by hand: the theme's name where it's long enough to cross the board (six letters side to side, eight top to bottom), else its people's name or a longer form (PERUVIAN, JAPANESE, MESOPOTAMIA for Iraq, CUBALIBRE; GOLDRUSH, CORNBELT, CARBAMIDE for urea, GREENDIESEL for HVO, CASHEWSHELLOIL for CNSL), and two where both read well (NORWAY or NORWEGIAN), Easy taking the plainer. Nine themes have too few one-word clues to fill a board (RINs, the THG quota, LCFS credits, EREs, RTFCs, recycled carbon fuels, tyre pyrolysis oil, ETBE, MTBE), and Lumber's and Black mass's word lengths can't add up round any spanning word; they come in by themselves as `kb/` gains clues. The title is another of the theme's clues, one not on the board, as a riddle ("Awarded in its capital, unlike the other Nobels"; "The pots of ash that named it"), never one that says the theme, its people or a word on the board: a well-known link at Easy, a specialist's at Hard. Easy and Medium say whether the theme is a country or a commodity; Hard doesn't.

What a board deals from is the menu's Content: the themes (Mixed, Countries or Commodities: the header's choice, as in Crates, mirrored there), the topics (the knowledge base's country and commodity topics, each out, in or more, with Crates' presets; a word only of topics left out comes only when a theme can't fill a board without it), and the regions and sectors (Crates' groups, ticked in or out, All or None; with nothing left to deal the board comes from every theme, and says so). A random board passes over the 30 themes dealt last.

The generator (`lode-engine.js`, pure) makes a board from its deal alone, the same on every device. It picks the words (the spanning word, then words drawn by the level's and the topics' weights whose lengths add up to the rest of the 48, five to seven of them), then packs them: the spanning word as a walk from one side to the side across, then the rest cut into paths of the other words' lengths, always from the free cell with the fewest free neighbours (it has to be the end of whatever path takes it), every patch of free cells checked to be fillable by the words still to place, and no two diagonals crossing, so every word draws as a line. It writes the words along their paths and climbs towards a lettering where each word traces only along its own path: a word that traces a second way almost always does so at an end (BALI's I with another I beside its L), which turning that word or a neighbour round, or swapping two words of a length, puts right. Last, a solver counts the ways the words cover the board, up to two, and the board is kept only if there's exactly one: the board's one solution. A word may still trace a second way through another word's letters (at most two a board; on about three hex boards in ten and two square ones in three, where eight neighbours give letters more twins): traced there it's found all the same, and shows where it really runs, the only cells the one cover leaves it. That rule is Lode's own, since knowing the word should never be what fails you. On this machine (two cores shared with several others) a hex board takes 2 ms to deal and a square one 9 to 13 (the medians), nine in ten within 8 and 40 ms; a phone is a few times slower, and the next random board is dealt in the background as you play, so New board is at once.

Difficulty. What makes a board hard is knowing its words and finding them. Knowing: each link carries the knowledge base's difficulty, 1 (well known) to 3 (specialist). Finding: a word is as hard to find as its path turns, runs against the reading direction (leftwards or up) and offers false starts, the traces of two or more of its letters that a search for it would follow but aren't the word (every A beside an F, looking for FJORDS). A board's grade is its words' mean link difficulty (×100) plus a quarter of their mean of turns, backward steps and false starts (×100), the quarter putting the two on one scale. The levels set their boards apart by how they're made: Easy deals no specialist link and no acronym, its paths run straight on and read rightwards or down, its title a well-known clue; Medium mixes; Hard leans on specialist links, turns its paths and runs them backwards. Each level keeps a board only inside its band of grades (Easy up to 390, Medium 360 to 480, Hard from 450), making it again if not, and a theme that can't make a board near its band (Freight's CLARKSONS, DEMURRAGE and WORLDSCALE make no Easy board) gives way to the next the seed picks. Over boards as dealt (`tests/lode.mjs` prints the spread), Easy's 10th, 50th and 90th percentiles are 279, 332 and 367, Medium's 374, 419 and 465, Hard's 480, 533 and 589: no Easy board is as hard as any Hard one. Whether the bands feel right is for Korbi's thumb.

Twists, both in Settings and from the next board: Hex (Board), the same 48 letters as a honeycomb, rows of six with every other one set half a cell over, each letter touching six: the paths bend differently and there's no diagonal to read along, and the spanning word still has to reach two opposite sides. Every word (Clues), every word's clue with its length in place of the title, one at a time with arrows, the spanning word's last: the board becomes a clued word search, the words worked out from what you know and then found, which makes the knowledge base the game rather than its backdrop. Considered and left out: the classic straight-line word search with a message in its leftover letters (another, easier genre), a bigger board (a phone has no width for another column of letters a thumb can hit), and two themes on one board (a word fitting both would have no right answer).

Together, from the partner sheet or a Party of up to six: one table (`lode-table.js`, on `together.js`), the board itself kept in the room so every phone has the same one, in one of two modes chosen in the menu's Together part for the next board; a pair's first board is Team, a party's Race.
- Team: one board for the table, its letters shared out. The t-th letter of the j-th word goes to seat (t + j) mod n, so every word runs through every player's hands, each sees one letter in n of every word, and no word of four letters or more can be read alone; each holds a fair share (24 each of two, 16 of three). The others' letters show as sealed tiles marked with where they are (C4), to ask for. Anyone traces through any letters, sealed or not, and what you trace shows on the others' boards as it grows, ringed in your colour with your initial at its head (written to your seat at most every 90 ms, as in Delta), so whoever holds a letter can say what it is. A word found opens its letters to everyone, in its finder's colour; the hints and the meter are the table's. A seat nobody has, or whose player is away, has its letters open to all, so a sleeping phone never holds the others up. The pair's record gets the team's score.
- Race: the same board on every phone from the same moment (after a three-second count, its letters sealed till then), a run each, kept on the phone (`lode:table`) and reported as it goes; the strip shows how far each of the others has got, and once every run that began is done, the standings (`standings.js`), the lowest score first. One who comes late runs on a clock of their own. The dealer may add a computer rival, whose run every phone works out the same from the board, so it costs the database nothing, and it never holds the race open. For the pair's record, the winner is first alone.

Why not `duel.js` for the race: a table holds one kind of state and a pair's request always opens the game one way, so a race on `duel.js` and a team on `together.js` couldn't share a request, nor let a pair switch between them for the next board as Manifest's two modes do; the race keeps `duel.js`'s shape (one start, late runs on their own clock, a finished run standing, the suite's standings).

The computer: a rival paced like a person. It takes the words in the order a person would, the best known and easiest to find first, the spanning word once a few are in, the last two quicker as the board left narrows them down; each takes the level's pace (Steady 48 s, Sharp 31, Ace 18), longer for a specialist link or a path hard to follow, give or take a fifth, so a board takes Steady about five minutes and Ace under two. Solo, Rival (Settings) races you on your own board: the strip shows how far it has got, never its words, it stops when your clock does, and the end says who won and by how much. At a race table it races everyone.

Scoring, bests and the daily. Giving up (Show the words still hidden, in the menu) adds 120 seconds a word shown: more than earning and using a hint ever costs, so it's never the quick way out. Bests are kept for each level and board shape (`lode:times`, lowest wins), not for a board given up. Today's board is the same for everyone (the suite's day): Medium, square, its title, from every theme and topic. It counts the moment its last word is found (`noteDayTime`), and the games screen's tile shows today's time.

Learning: a word you needed a hint for, or were shown, goes to the pile with its theme and its entity, so Deck asks it back (which country or commodity: WORD, its link), with fresh wrong answers each time, and the other games' learning modes deal it too; one found unaided that was due counts as known.

Saved state: `lode:run` holds the board itself and your run (the words found, the other words, the hints, the clock), so a reload, another device or a partner watching sees it as it stands, and a newer generator never re-deals a board under way; `lode:settings` what the next board deals and how it shows; `lode:times` the bests; `lode:seen` the themes dealt lately; `lode:table` this phone's run in a race. A partner's watching frame draws the board as you left it, runs no clock and writes nothing. The clock stops with the menu and the games screen (the pause), and while the page is hidden.

The page fits the screen from 390 by 600: the theme, the count and the clock, the board as large as the room left (drawn in an SVG to its own units, it never changes size as you play), then the line and the tools, the result taking their room at the end. Held sideways the board takes the left with all the height, and the theme, the words found so far with their links, the line and the tools stand beside it, the tools low.

`tests/lode.mjs` checks the themes and their words; a board for every theme tried at every level and shape (every letter used once, each word on its own path, the spanning word across, no crossing diagonals, one cover by the solver, which counts two on a hand-made board that has two); the grading and its bands; the same board from the same deal, and today's on every device; a run's rules (theme words, other words towards hints, both hint steps, giving up, the score, a word traced through its twin letters); the rival's pace; the shares, every word through every seat; and a table of three through the database's own round trip (Team: who reads which letters, traces, one player's find for all, a stale move refused, the team's result; Race: starts, reports, a finished run standing, the end, the standings with the rival, the pair's record). In Chromium: three phone sizes and an iPad both ways, in Almanac, Modern and Kontor by day and night; played by mouse and by touch (drags, taps, sliding back, other words, both hints, giving up, today's board from the tile and again on a second phone, a watching frame); two phones through Team and then Race on the stand-in database, the pair's record written; and a party of three through a race, a team of three and a race with Ace.

Not done: the knowledge base's collections as themes (rivers, gods, painters of a movement, wines), which need spanning words of their own; a computer team mate in Team, which would have to hold a share of the letters and answer for them; a hint that gives only a word's first letter. For Korbi to decide: the bands and the rival's paces come from the generator's numbers, not from play; and whether a given-up daily should count on the tile at all.

### Lots
Connections' genre, by its rules (October 2026): sixteen words, four lots of four, each lot held together by a link you have to see. Pick four and Submit: four of one lot and it's found, a bar in its colour with the link named; three of one lot is "One away"; anything else "Not a lot". Four mistakes end the board; the same four can't be tried twice (it costs nothing); with three found the fourth falls into place. The lots are ranked plain to devious and coloured as the newspaper's are, in the suite's enamels: ochre, verdigris, cobalt, oxblood. Once the board is over every lot shows, and a tap on a lot's bar says why its words belong (FOOT·BALL, CARP inside CARPENTER, PERU rearranged, S·PAIN) and which were red herrings. Shuffle reorders the words left (a reload keeps the order), Deselect drops the picks, a tap on a pick takes it back, Enter submits from a keyboard. The rules, the bank and the generator are `lots-engine.js` and `lots-bank.js`, a board as it's played `lots-run.js`, the computer player `lots-bot.js`, the pair's table `lots-room.js`, all pure; the page is `lots.js`.

The bank (`lots-bank.js`), written for Lots: the knowledge base knows countries, commodities and paintings, and Lots' links are about words, so nothing comes from it. 354 categories in ten topics (Wordplay, Phrases, Meanings, Things with, Markets, Science & nature, Places, Culture, Sport & games, Food & drink), each with a difficulty: 1, a plain set anyone sees at once (Trees, Board games), 73 of them; 2, a set or meaning that takes a moment, or a common phrase, 147; 3, a specialist's set, a harder phrase, "things with", a homophone, 105; 4, wordplay you have to spot and the most devious, 29 (Hidden animals, Anagrams of countries, Bonds sold abroad by their nicknames). Each has more than four members (4,298 in all, 3,460 different words), so a lot is never the same four twice, and a list of words that fit it too but are never dealt in it (a drake is a bird, a mako a fish, a viola has a bridge): that list is how the generator sees a red herring, so it errs wide. The kinds: sets and meanings (Things that bark; Steal); things with (Things with keys: PIANO, MAP); 67 phrases before or after a word (___ JACK: FLAP, CRACKER, LUMBER, APPLE); homophones (They sound like letters: SEA, GEE, AYE, CUE); and wordplay the page checks by the letters, so any word on a board that fits counts, listed or not: a word hidden inside, a word it starts with, an anagram, a letter before a word (G + LOBE) or after one (ARM + Y), a word without its first letter (S·PAIN), palindromes, no vowel, letters in alphabetical order, another word backwards. Markets is Korbi's own topic, 32 categories: the Greeks, crude grades, Incoterms, ship sizes, biofuels, bonds' nicknames, rogue traders, curve shapes. The test holds the phrases to the suite's word list (`wordlist.js`): any word of the bank that makes a phrase's compound there (FOOT and BALL make FOOTBALL) must be in that phrase, and likewise any word that reads as another backwards; the few compounds the list has and nobody says are set aside by name.

Making a board (`deal`, from a seed). The level picks four difficulties (Easy 1-1-2-2 or near it, Medium 1-2-3-4 or near, Hard 2-3-4-4 or near); categories are drawn for them weighted by your topics (one dealt lately at a fifth), at most two of a topic, never two of one trick (two anagram lots are the same idea twice) or on one key word, and four members each, no tile holding another lot's key (FOOTBALL beside ___ BALL). Red herrings are planned, not stumbled on: a word of one lot that fits another lot on the board, drawn from an index of such bridges made once (by lot, then word, or the few categories half the bank fits would deal every herring). Crates' rules hold: a word fits at most one other lot, and no two herrings join the same two lots (they could swap). Easy has none; Medium sometimes one (planned on two boards in five, about half have one); Hard always one, two on about one board in five, and one must bite: its false home is the easier lot, which then shows five words, so a harder lot must be found first. Then the proof: the solver counts the ways the sixteen sort into the four links, every word in a lot whose link it fits, four to a lot, stopping at two, and only a board with exactly one is dealt. It counts beyond the board: any other category four of the words fit across lots (a decoy: four metals, one of them a golf club) is tried in place of each link, and the board must still sort one way; Easy and Medium allow no decoy, Hard one. In Chromium with the processor slowed four times, a phone's stand-in, a board takes well under a millisecond typically and a tenth of a second at worst; the first on a fresh page a third of a second, while the index of which words fit what is made.

Difficulty is what a solver has to do, not how many words there are. The grade is the links' difficulties, plus twice the times a person must look past (the solver takes the easiest link whose words in sight are exactly four; each time the easiest left shows five, a harder must come first), plus the herrings, plus twice the decoys. Measured: Easy grades 5 to 7, Medium 9 to 18 (median 10), Hard 15 to 24 (median 18). The computer players agree: on 150 boards a level, Club solves every Easy one without a mistake in 54 seconds on average, 96% of Medium (0.22 mistakes, 89 s), 87% of Hard (1.27, 161 s).

Today's board: Medium, classic, every topic, the same for everyone (the suite's day is the seed), one go. It counts the moment it's over: points (a point a lot, and once all four are found a point for each mistake to spare: 8 is perfect), then time, in Crates' form, so the tile reads "Today 7/8 in 1:23"; `lots:daily` keeps each day's figure by the day, `lots:best` the best. The tile opens on it while it's still to play, asking before it leaves a board you've started. Share copies the guesses as the newspaper's coloured squares.

Play (Settings): Classic, as above, or The wall, Only Connect's connecting wall: two and a half minutes, no Submit (the fourth pick is the guess), a wrong guess says only Wrong, guesses free until two lots are found and three lives after; when the clock or the lives run out, or the wall is solved, it freezes and you name each lot's link from four (its own and three that fit some of its words, or play the same trick), those you didn't find too: a point a lot, a point a link, two more for all eight, ten in all. A classic board solved oxblood first, ochre last, is called a reverse rainbow, as players boast of it. Weighed and left out: five lots of five (25 words don't fit a phone's width legibly) and hints (they'd give away the genre's one decision, what the link is).

Content (the menu): Random or Today's; the level (Easy, Medium, Hard, also in the header); the topics as a mix, each out, in or more, with presets Balanced, Trader (markets and places more), Culture night (culture, food and drink) and Wordsmith (wordplay, phrases, meanings). A mix too narrow for a board deals from every topic and says so; today's always does.

The rival (Settings, Against the computer: Off, Novice, Club, Expert) plays your board beside you as a person would. Each link comes to it after a while, or never, sooner the plainer the link and the better the player (a devious link: Novice two times in five, Expert seventeen in twenty); a link it sees shows it every word that fits, herrings included; it puts in the lot it's surest of first (one whose words in sight are exactly four), waits while its links show five, and when it can wait no longer guesses the likeliest four, keeping three after a one away. On Medium Novice solves 90% (0.47 mistakes, two and a half minutes), Club 96%, Expert 99% (0.07, 53 s); on Hard 69%, 87% and 92%. Its whole game is worked out at the deal, a timeline the board's clock plays, so a pause, a reload or a partner watching sees the same; four squares under the header fill in as it finds lots. The more points win, then the quicker; your record against each level is kept.

Together. The pair, from the partner sheet, plays one board (together.js) three ways, chosen in the menu, none of them leaving the less able player to watch. Open: both see all sixteen and each other's picks (ringed, with an initial), four mistakes between them. Crossed: both see all sixteen, but what a guess came to shows only on the other's phone (the guesser's says Wrong, the other's one away or not), so you have to say; and a lot one of you finds is the other's to name, from four links, a point more for each named (12 in all). Hidden, the default: each sees eight of the sixteen and the other's eight sealed, every lot holding one to three words from each side, so neither can make a lot alone; you pick among your own, a guess is both players' picks, either submits, and a one away tells the one who didn't guess whose pick was off. A board counts in the pair's record as a team board, solved or not ("Duo 5/7"). Or a race (duel.js): the same board for everyone, each on their own phone, the more points then the quicker winning; Race instead in the menu takes the pair from their board to one, and a Party plays the race, up to six. The computer doesn't sit at a table; the rival is for playing alone.

On the screen: the tiles size themselves to the board's box (container units: four across, as tall as the box allows), a long word shrinks to its tile rather than wrap, and nothing changes size during a board. Held sideways the board takes the left; the strip, the rival, your wrong guesses so far (a list a phone has no room for), the line and the buttons stand at the right, the buttons low. A wrong guess shakes its picks once. Checked at 390×600, 390×664, 390×844, 820×1180 and 1180×820 in Almanac, Modern and Kontor (and Office) by day and night, played with clicks, and the three ways together and the race played through on two pages over the stand-in database.

What's kept, under `lots:`: `run` (the board as it stands: the words' order, picks, guesses, lots found, mistakes, the time, the rival's timeline, the wall's names), `prefs` (level, play, topics, rival, the pair's way), `stats` (played, solved, perfect, streaks, by level, against each rival, the wall's best), `seen` (the last 80 categories dealt, dealt less often), `daily` and `best`. A partner watching sees the board as it stands; a watching frame runs no clock. The clock stands while the menu or the games screen is open, or the page is away.

`tests/lots.mjs` checks the bank (sizes, no word twice, every rule's members fit it, the phrases and reversals against the word list); the rules; the solver on boards made to sort one way and two; 270 boards across the levels (one way to sort, by the solver and by brute force; the level's herrings and decoys; no tile holding a key; easiest first; each under 400 ms); the grades' separation, the herrings' shares, topics left out and lots seen lately; the rival (the stronger fewer mistakes and quicker, Hard harder for every level, its game fixed by its seed); a classic run and a wall through JSON, and the naming; the hidden split (the two halves make the board, neither holds a lot); the pair's table through the database's round trip (Crossed's split of what a guess came to, its naming, Hidden's odd one out); and today's board, the same everywhere for a day.

Not done: five lots of five; hints; the computer as a partner (a pair's table seats two people); the knowledge base's countries and commodities as sets (Lots' bank is its own); the pair's races are recorded under `lotsrace`, which no tile reads yet.

### Acrostic
(Being built, October 2026.)

## Idle
Games to play without much thought (October 2026): no puzzle to work out, but as much thinking as Block Blast, a bubble shooter or match-3 ask for, which is more than it looks. What those have in common, and what every idle game here keeps: a real choice every move among a dozen or so, a board that remembers a careless one, a reward for setting up combinations, and pressure that ends the game when you're careless. A game with less in it (a tune-the-plate idea, one slider and a pattern of sand) was turned down as too little. Each idle game changes one rule those games share for one nobody has used, and each is prototyped and tried in hand before the next.

Four were bookmarked, to be prototyped one by one, and all four now have a first draft to try in hand:
- **Heliograph** (aim by building the path): first a flat field of gems under a beam fired from the foot through `/` and `\` mirrors laid on a grid; rebuilt in 3D (October 2026) as a glass tube with a chandelier of crystals hanging in its middle and white light falling from the top, which you carry with mirrors set anywhere in the tube through colour crystals and into the chandelier. A bubble shooter aims the shot; this builds the shot's path, and every mirror changes the paths after it. The first prototyped (below).
- **Shunt** (push in, line up): a rail yard of tracks holding wagons of grain, timber, steel and oil; shove the incoming wagon onto any track from either end and the train rolls along to close its first gap; three or more alike across or down couple up and depart; a full track pushes its end wagon off the rails, a life lost. Match-3's swap replaced by the push of the board game Labyrinth, which moves a whole row and with it the lines in every column it crosses. The second prototyped (below).
- **Polder** (enclose to clear): an 8×8 sea with the coast along the foot; three dyke pieces at a time, placed anywhere on open water; when dykes and coast cut a pocket of sea off from the open sea, it drains into farmland and clears with its ring of dykes, more for a bigger polder but more of the board held up meanwhile; a piece that fits nowhere ends it. Block Blast completes lines; this completes areas. The third prototyped (below).
- **Topple** (set up the chain, then push it): three pieces of standing dominoes at a time (chains of one to five) on a table of sixty-one hexagons; when the table's knock comes you push one domino the way you choose, as hard as the table says (weak, medium or strong), and it falls on the dominoes ahead of it, and so on down the chain, everything that falls clearing, the score the dominoes felled times the steps they took; waiting for a bigger chain fills the table. Played in 3D on a mahogany and birch table you can tilt, with the clack of each domino. The fourth prototyped (below).

Five more were bookmarked after those (October 2026), to be drafted in this order:
- **Orrery** (real-time, in 3D: set five hoops going or tipped until planets of a colour line up with the sun): five brass hoops of planets on a pivot axle round a sun, a speed of its own each; tap a hoop and press a button to send it left or right, slowly, strongly or very strongly, or to hold it still, tap it again and the same buttons tip its orbit; three to five planets of one colour, one to a hoop, in one direction from the sun clear and score; a new planet lands every few seconds, quicker as the game goes on, and no room for one ends it. It began as a turn-based match-3 and was rebuilt twice (below). The fifth prototyped (below).
- **Avalanche** (you set off the chain reaction, and where it runs matters): a slope of snow on the sandpile model (each square holds three layers, a fourth slides one onto each neighbour, which can slide in turn); snow falls at random each night, and each day you set one charge; a slide you trigger scores the square of its size, a slide the night sets off that reaches the road at the foot costs a life. The sixth prototyped (below).
- **Netting** (lines replaced by loops): six counterparties; each obligation arrives from one of them and you choose who it's owed to; a cycle (A owes B owes C owes A) nets out by its smallest leg and scores; a party past its credit limit defaults. The seventh prototyped (below).
- **Settle** (you pick the tank, density picks the level): six tanks, parcels of four grades from water to naphtha; a parcel sinks below everything lighter and lifts it a level; three of a grade level with each other in neighbouring tanks ship out, and what floated above drops a level, which can line up more; an overflow spills. The eighth prototyped (below).
- **Drift** (2048 turned round: the forecast picks the direction, you pick the square): lay the next tile on any empty square, then the forecast wind blows and everything slides downwind to the wall or the next tile; three alike in a line clear, and the wind keeps pushing. The ninth prototyped (below).

A tenth followed (October 2026), asked for as a game where you make something, in 3D, with guilloché as its base:
- **Rosette** (Block Blast where the lathe repeats every piece round the dial): a watch's dial of five rings and twelve sectors on a rose engine; each cut is repeated once for every lobe of its rosette, evenly round, and a repeat that would cross metal already cut is skipped; a ring cut all round or a spoke cut from the centre to the rim is faced off and scores. The tenth prototyped (below).

An eleventh followed (October 2026), asked for as something more casual and braindead, still about putting something together in 3D:
- **Slipway** (carving: a ship's parts worked by hand from blocks of timber): each part's ghost lies in its block; saw, plane, chisel, drill and sandpaper take the wood away, and the part is graded on the waste left, any cut into it and any knot in it. The cog's eleven parts, from a mast wedge to the masthead. The eleventh prototyped (below), rebuilt as a carving game on 19 October 2026.

A twelfth followed (19 October 2026), asked for as a sewing game: "Fix buttons, disattached belt loops, holes, whatever."
- **Thimble** (mending: a piece of cloth with one thing wrong in it): sew a button back on, hem a leg, close a tear, set a belt loop back on its band, lay a patch, darn a hole; the needle goes in where the marks are ringed, and the grade is how near it went. Eight jobs, each different again every time it is played. Prototyped (below).


### Heliograph
The first of the idle games (October 2026; README, Idle), rebuilt in 3D the same month: `heliograph.html`, `heliograph.js` and `heliograph.css`, its rules in `heliograph-engine.js`, the camera in `heliograph-view.js`, the tube drawn by `heliograph-3d.js` (three.js) or, without WebGL, flat by `heliograph-flat.js` through the same camera. It began on a flat field of gems with `/` and `\` mirrors on a grid; Korbi asked for it in 3D, the grid gone, the light coming from above and the time spent setting mirrors to do something plain to see.

A glass tube on a brass-rimmed plate, open at the top under a frame of spokes. In the middle of the frame a crown on a spindle, skirted in smoked glass, hangs a chandelier: strings of crystals under the places of a hexagon of nineteen, in a shape of its own (a column, a cone longest in the middle, a bowl longest round the edge, a hollow tube, a star, a slant, a spiral; each new chandelier another), so which string reaches the line first and where to cut differ from one to the next; the crystals wear the four enamels, each colour with a cut of its own (round, lozenge, square, hexagonal prism) so they're told apart without their colour. White light falls from a port in the frame beside the crown. Two colour crystals stand low in the tube. Light that passes through a colour crystal takes its colour and the crystal is spent (another appears somewhere, its colour shown coming); light through both carries both colours. In the chandelier, light shatters every crystal of a colour it carries and stops at the first of another; white light stops at the first it meets and does nothing. Everything on a string below a crystal shattered falls, so a cut high up brings a long string down. The skirt hides the top two layers from the side: light reaches them only from below, up through the chandelier along a run of its colour. (Korbi found that aiming at the top layer from the side, which brought every string down at once, left no reason to aim anywhere else.) A shot scores ten times the square of the run the light shatters (one 10, five 250) and ten for each crystal that falls: cutting high buys time, but the points are in long runs of a colour, low or across; a chandelier cleared is 1,000 more and a new one hangs.

You build the light's way with up to six mirrors, discs silvered both sides on brass stems from the floor, set anywhere in the tube: under the port, beside a colour crystal, right up against the chandelier to send light up into it or across a layer. Drag one from the tray into the tube: near the light it settles onto it (the light as it would be without that mirror), turned to send the light level toward the middle of the tube; elsewhere it moves on the upright plane facing you. A tap on a mirror chooses it and shows its handle; drag the mirror to move it, drag the handle to turn it (across the screen turns it about the screen's upright, up and down about its level, slowly, so light can be laid on a crystal by hand). There is no aiming by pointing: the light's whole way is drawn as you move and turn, white then in its colours, and every crystal it would shatter is ringed, with the line under the tube saying what Fire would bring down ("Fire brings down 12 (7 falling): +1,440"). Mirrors stay where they're set from shot to shot. Turning one is free, as often as you like; setting, moving or taking one back is a move, and there are two a shot (the squares in the strip, before the drop's pips), which is what makes it a puzzle: the light's way has to be rebuilt for the new colour crystals with two changes and as much turning as you need. A mirror set or moved this shot moves again free, and taking back one set this shot gives its move back. Take back returns the chosen mirror to the tray. Only Fire takes a turn. A drag on empty space turns the camera round the tube and tips it a little up or down.

Every six shots the chandelier comes down a layer, a crystal more at the top of every string of its shape (every five after 30 shots, four after 70, three after 120, two after 180; the strip's pips count down); a mirror it comes down on goes back to hand, and a colour crystal in its way moves. A crystal reaching the line low on the glass ends the game. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `heliograph:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`heliograph:run2`) and opens again where it was. The flat game's run and best (a different scale of score) were cleared once when the 3D one first opened (`heliograph:v2`). A first game's first shots say what to do next in the line under the tube. Held sideways the tube is on the left with all the height, the score, the line and the tray beside it.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`plan` in the engine sets a mirror under the port turned to a colour crystal and one beyond it turned into the chandelier), keeping to two moves a shot, one setting no mirror is overrun in 30 to 40 shots (sooner under a deep shape), and so is one picking a crystal of the colour at random, since every string must be kept short and a random cut seldom shortens the longest; one taking the best of four lasts about 55 to 80 shots, and one taking the best-scoring shot each turn about 140 and scores about 10,000. Before the skirt and the new scoring that best player cut at the top layer in more than a third of its shots; now its cuts spread down the chandelier, and the colours of a string are less often the same from one crystal to the next (a fifth of the time, from two fifths), so a run straight up to the top is rarer. Strings rather than a lattice joined every way: joined every way almost nothing fell, a shot brought down two or three crystals, and the best player was overrun as fast as one doing nothing. The colour crystals stand clear of the glass so light through one has room to run on to a mirror beyond it. The engine is pure (the light is a ray reflected off mirror discs, R − 2(R·n)n, tested against spheres for the crystals, the crown's disc, the glass, the floor), and every random thing (the chandelier, each new layer, the colour crystals) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have; the drawing is on demand, still when nothing moves.

`tests/heliograph.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (a chandelier in a shape, each string as deep as the shape has it, games of different shapes, two colour crystals of colours the chandelier has, clear of the glass and the chandelier), the light (from the port to the floor; reflection; off a mirror at 45° level to the glass; turned or moved out of the light; taking a crystal's colour; across a layer through five crystals of its colour and out; stopped by another colour; white light doing nothing; through both colour crystals carrying both colours and shattering either), a shot (everything below on the strings falling, the points for the run and for each fall, the crystals spent and replaced), the skirt (level light at the top layers stopped by it, lower down running through; no mirror against it), where a mirror may stand, two moves a shot (a third mirror refused, one set this shot moving again free, turning always free, a mirror set this shot taken back giving its move back, two again after a shot), the chandelier coming down (a crystal more on every string of the shape and none elsewhere, a mirror under it back in hand, the end, the pace), a cleared chandelier, a game saved and opened again, firing leaving the game passed in as it was, and whole games: no mirrors overrun in under 50 shots, a glance lasting longer, the best shot longer still and every game ending.

### Shunt
The second idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `shunt.html`, `shunt.js` and `shunt.css`, its rules in `shunt-engine.js`. A rail yard six tracks across and six down, crossing on every square; wagons stand on it in the four enamels, each cargo with a mark of its own (grain's ears, timber's log ends, a steel beam, oil's drop) so they're told apart without their colour. Round the yard is an arrow at both ends of every track: twenty-four ways to shove the incoming wagon in, the one after it shown beside it. Shoved in, it pushes the wagons ahead of it along the track until the first gap closes; wagons beyond the gap stand where they are. On a full track the wagon at the far end goes off the rails, and a derailment costs one of three lives (the strip's pips). Three or more alike in a line, across or down, couple up and depart, a wagon in two lines at once sending both; a shove scores ten times the square of the wagons departing (three 90, five 250), so one shove that lines up two runs beats two shoves that make one each.

Press an arrow and the shove is shown before it's made: the yard as it would stand, the wagons that would depart ringed, a wagon that would go off the rails drawn beyond the end and crossed, and a line under the yard saying what it would do. Slide along the arrows to see each in turn, let go on one to shove, let go anywhere else and nothing happens (a mouse shows each shove as it passes). An arrow is red where its track is full. Why show it: a shove moves up to six wagons and with them the lines in the six tracks they cross, too much to see in the head at a glance, and the thinking is meant to be in choosing among the shoves, not in imagining them (as a bubble shooter draws its aim).

Every few shoves a goods train leaves a wagon of its own on an empty square, one that makes no run where it stands: every three shoves at first, every two after 25, every shove after 60, two at a time after 120 and three after 180 (the line under the yard counts down to it).

Scrap flats (October 2026): about one wagon in ten the goods train leaves is a scrap flat, a rusty grey with twisted ends on it, never one of the wagons coming. It matches nothing, so it never departs and breaks any line it stands in; the one way out is off the end of a full track, and a scrap flat shoved off costs no life. So a scrap flat is a small job of its own: shove it along to a track's end, fill that track, and send it off from the other end. An arrow whose full track would send scrap off is drawn grey rather than red, the shove shown says the scrap goes for nothing, and until you've shoved one off the line under the yard says what one is for while one stands there. The yard fills, the gaps close, full tracks multiply, and the game ends at the third derailment. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `shunt:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`shunt:run`) and opens again where it was. Held sideways the yard is on the left with all the height, the score, the wagons coming and the line beside it.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`shoves` in the engine lists all twenty-four with what each would do), one shoving anywhere goes off the rails three times in about 25 shoves; one shoving at random among the shoves that don't derail (or only scrap), and among those that score or scrap when there are any, lasts about 140; one taking the shove that scores most and leaves the yard emptiest and clearest of scrap lasts about 167 (126 to 193), scrapping about 11 flats, and scores about 11,000. Before scrap flats those two lasted about 175 and 182 (scoring about 16,000) with the goods train a little quicker (every two shoves after 20, every shove after 50, two after 100, three after 160); scrap at one in ten on that pace cut the careful player to about 140, at one in eight 138, one in twelve 139, so the pace was eased to give the scrap room. With the goods train bringing no more than a wagon a shove, a careful player outlasted 2,000 shoves, so later it brings two and then three, and a game ends soon after. Every random thing (the yard dealt, the wagons coming, where the goods train leaves its wagons and what they carry) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/shunt.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (no run standing), a track's squares from each end, a shove rolling the wagons up to the first gap and leaving the rest, one from the far end, a full track's derailment and its life, a shove shown without being made, runs across, down and both at once, the points, the goods train's wagons (on empty squares, more and more often, never in a run over thirty games played at random), scrap flats (never in a run and breaking one; shoved off a full track for no life, a cargo wagon at the far end still costing one; from either end; about one of the goods train's wagons in ten, never one coming), the end at the third derailment, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: shoving anywhere off the rails three times in under 60, shoving with thought lasting past 100.

### Polder
The third idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `polder.html`, `polder.js` and `polder.css`, its rules in `polder-engine.js`. A sea eight squares by eight, the coast along its foot, open sea beyond the top and both sides. Three dyke pieces wait under it: lines, corners, cups, Ts and steps of one to five squares, none of them solid, each laid as it's dealt and never turned. Drag one onto the water and, while it's held where it fits, the water it would cut off from the open sea is drawn as farmland and the dykes that would go with it are ringed; let go and it's laid, let go anywhere else and it goes back. When all three are down, three more come. Water flows across a square's sides, not its corners, so dykes touching only at a corner hold it back (a knot of earth is drawn there to show it). Water the open sea can't reach is a polder: it drains, and the dykes touching it, corners included, go with it. A polder scores ten times the square of what drains in one go (one square 10, four 160, twelve 1,440), so one big polder is worth far more than several small ones, but a big one holds much of the sea up while it's built. The coast is the cheap side to build against, where a cup of three dykes closes a square; the sides and the top are open sea, so a polder there needs dykes all round.

Why silt: with dykes alone the sea never fills (a player taking the piece that drains most and leaves least built laid well over a thousand pieces, most often 3,000, without running out of room), so every few pieces the sea leaves silt on a square of water. Nothing can be built on silt and it holds no water back; only a polder takes it away, counting its squares like water. It settles along the coast first, then beside silt already there, a sandbank growing up from the shore, so it can be planned for: wall the bank in and drain it (silt settling anywhere would only be in the way). It comes every five pieces at first, every four after 60, every three after 150 and every two after 250 (the strip's pips count down to it). A piece that fits nowhere is dimmed, and when none of those left in hand fits, the game is over. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `polder:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`polder:run`) and opens again where it was. A first game's first pieces say what to do, written on the empty sea. Held sideways the sea is on the left with all the height, the score and the three pieces beside it, low where a thumb is.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`moves` in the engine lists every place each piece in hand fits), one laying anywhere has no room left after about 25 pieces; one taking the piece that drains most and leaves least built and silted lasts about 100; one looking two pieces ahead in its hand lasts about 155 and scores about 9,000, its biggest polder about 13 squares. Silt every three pieces from the start ended even the look-ahead player in under 80. Every random thing (the pieces dealt, where the silt settles) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/polder.mjs` checks the deal from a seed, the pieces (each joined along its sides, none solid, measured from its top left), where a piece fits (on water, not on a dyke or silt or off the sea), polders (four dykes touching only at their corners holding a square; the coast holding one, the sides and the top not; silt holding nothing; a polder from side to side taking its silt; the dykes at its corners going with it), the points, a piece laid (the polder drained and cleared, the hand, the game it was laid from left as it was, three more after the third, a piece where it doesn't fit refused), the silt (along the coast, beside silt, more often as the game goes on), the end when no piece fits, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: laying anywhere out of room in under 60 pieces, laying with thought lasting half as long again or more.

### Topple
The fourth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `topple.html`, `topple.js` and `topple.css`, its rules in `topple-engine.js`. A table of sixty-one hexagons, four out from the middle every way, in birch inlaid in a mahogany frame ("The table in 3D", below). Three pieces of standing dominoes wait under it, each piece in one of three enamels, each colour with pips of its own (one, two, three a half) so they're told apart without their colour. The pieces are chains of one to five dominoes: lines, bends of sixty degrees, arcs, waves and forks of three (no three of a piece all touching, none touching more than three others, so never a solid blob), each in every way it lies, laid as dealt and never turned, a straight line twice as often as another shape of its size. When all three are down three more come. Drag one onto the table and, while it's held where it fits, the dominoes of its colour it would join are ringed, with what their best push would bring down ("9 together: they all fall in 7", or "9 together: 7 fall at most").

A knock is a push: press a domino and slide the way it should go, and what a push that way would fell is ringed, what of its colour would stay standing is marked, and the steps and the points are written above it; let go to push. Pressed, a domino shows a compass of the six ways, each with what it would score (not towards the table's edge, where only the domino itself would go), and a press that lets go without sliding leaves the compass up to read. Dominoes fall as dominoes do, as hard as the push is ("The strength of a push", below): a falling domino knocks, of its own colour, the dominoes on the three hexagons ahead of it (straight on, and sixty degrees either side), as many of the three as the strength allows; each after that falls the way it was knocked and does the same. A domino at more than sixty degrees to the way a fall is going, to the side or behind, is never taken.
