So a line pushed from its end falls end to end and bends sixty degrees where it must, at any strength; pushed in its middle only what's ahead of it goes; pushed a sixty degrees off its line from an end, it still goes whole, since the line is one of the two at sixty degrees; and a flower of seven pushed from a petal takes its row of three and leaves four, unless the push is strong, which takes all seven. A push scores ten times the dominoes felled times the steps they take to fall (the serpent rule): a straight run of ten pushed from its end falls in ten steps, 1,000; the same run pushed from its fifth domino felled six, 360.

The knock is the table's to time, not yours: when a hand's last piece is down and a knock has come due, the next hand, dealt, waits dimmed until it's made (the strip says Knock now, and before then when the next is due). A knock can't be kept for later and none comes in between, so a chain has to be worth pushing when the knock comes, and a hand that fits nowhere ends the game, since the knock only comes once a hand is finished. The knocks come every hand for the first sixteen hands, then fewer: each finished hand adds the knock rate to a credit and a knock falls due when the credit reaches one, the rate falling in a straight line from one a hand at the sixteenth hand to seven in ten at the fifty-sixth and staying there. The strip counts it, with how hard it will be ("Medium push now", "Strong push after this hand", "Weak push in 2 hands"), a glyph of one, two or three bars beside the words. And a bell hangs on one hexagon of the table: a push whose fall passes it (the domino standing on its hexagon among those that fall) scores double, and the bell moves to an empty hexagon at random. The bell is what takes play round the table: a corner is a good place to start a line, and the bell is rarely there. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `topple:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`topple:run`) and opens again where it was. A first game's first pieces say what to do, written on a plaque on the empty table. Held sideways the table is on the left with all the height, the score, the knock and the three pieces beside it, low where a thumb is.

The strength of a push (October 2026): a knock is weak, medium or strong, one, two or three of the three hexagons ahead of a falling domino. Weak takes the domino of its colour straight ahead and nothing else. Medium is what a push always was: that one, or, where none of its colour stands straight ahead, the two at sixty degrees either side. Strong takes all three of its colour. Each is a part of the next (a weak fall is always inside a medium one on the same table, and a medium one inside a strong one; `tests/topple.mjs` tries four hundred random tables), and all of them stay inside the pushed domino's colour. A line falls whole from its end at any strength; what tells them apart is what lies beside a line, so a weak knock wants a line and a strong one is the chance to bring down a blob.

The table deals the strength, from the game's seed like everything else, and shows it a knock ahead in the strip, so you lay with it in mind. It isn't yours to choose because a free choice would always be strong: nothing would be decided. The first knock of a game is always medium, the rule you learn on; after that weak, medium and strong come in the odds 1.5, 4.5 and 4 (`FORCE_ODDS`: about one in seven, nearly one in two, two in five). Measured in the harness (40 games, a careful player that looks at the strength the next knock will have), a weak knock felled 4.5 dominoes and scored 229 on average, medium 10.4 and 1,100, strong 11.5 and 1,202: weak is the one that costs you, and strong is better than medium mainly where a blob has formed, which a player who keeps lines doesn't build much of. With the strengths the same players lasted a little less long than on the plain rule (the corner habit about 37 pieces, by colour 54, the careful player about 100, the bell player 96, a random one 26, against 35, 52, 117 and 106), because strong knocks fell more; the knock pace was retuned from twelve hands and a half (every hand to the twelfth, then fewer to one in two at the forty-eighth) to sixteen and seven tenths (`PACE`) to bring a careful game back to about a hundred pieces and make the table fill enough to matter. Those are a simulation, not a play-test.

The table in 3D (October 2026): the dominoes are lacquered slabs with a bevel, a bar across the middle and their pips (`topple-3d.js`, on the vendored three.js that Rosette also uses), standing on a hexagonal table of birch in a mahogany frame with a brass stud at each corner. There are no image files: the wood is drawn by a shader (birch in three tones, each hexagon its own piece with its grain turned a third of a turn from its neighbours', the mahogany's grain running along each side and meeting at the corners in a mitre), so it's there offline and the same at any size. Each theme has its light by day and by night (a cool light with a warm fill at night), and the table follows the theme as it changes. A phone upright is taller than wide, so the table sits with a corner at the top and the bottom (a quarter turn about the vertical) when that fills the room better, and the camera height, between 50 and 66 degrees, is picked the same way, so the table is as large as the screen allows at any size; a piece in the tray is drawn turned the same way, so it looks as it will on the table. Press and drag on the bare table to tilt it a little (about seven degrees, as Rosette's dial does); let go and it eases back in a third of a second; a tap leaves the table level and takes any compass away. The compass, the rings, the written scores and the hint are an SVG drawn over the canvas through the table's own projection, so they follow the tilt. Where there's no WebGL the page draws the same game flat (`topple-flat.js`, the same calls, no tilt and a simpler fall), so it still plays.

The fall (`fall` in `topple-3d.js`): each domino of a push turns to face the one that knocked it, hinges over about its lower edge and falls under gravity, faster to start when the push is harder, bounces a little where it lands, lies for a moment and sinks into the table; a few specks fly where it lands, the table shakes a hair at each landing and the bell, if it was rung, drops, rings and hops to its next hexagon. A chain keeps its rhythm: a domino falls when the one before it has reached it. The sounds (`topple-sound.js`) are made as they play, in Web Audio, with no files: a short bright clack, wood on wood, where one domino meets the next (slightly different in pitch for each, panned across the table with it), a duller thud where one lands flat, a tap when a piece is laid, a heavier knock for the push itself and the bell's inharmonic ring. A fall hands the whole run over with each sound's time, and they're scheduled on the audio clock, so a long chain clacks evenly whatever the frames do; sounds within 25 milliseconds of each other become one louder sound, so a fork or a cone of dominoes isn't a racket. The menu's Sound switch (`topple:sound`) turns them off, and the table, its sound and the clock of its fall all stand still while the menu is open. Run by hand in a headless browser, a fall of 13 dominoes scheduled 22 sounds (the tests don't cover the page); how they sound has not been heard.

Why it's a hexagonal table (October 2026): the square version let the pieces and the fall turn any way, and the strategy was thin: laying every piece in the corner, with a habit of putting each beside its own colour, was most of the game. Tried in a harness (`moves` lists every place each piece in hand fits, `chains` what's standing and its best push), the same simple players on the old table lasted: the corner habit about 43 pieces, the corner habit by colour 62, a careful player (keeping what each piece joins in lines that fall whole, with room for the rest of the hand) 87, and one that also brings its lines to the bell 85, the careful player's move being the corner-by-colour habit's 62 times in a hundred and the bell player's 54. A straight port to hexagons was worse. What changed it was three rules together: a fall that goes only forward, straight on or at sixty degrees, so that what stands beside a line, at a hundred and twenty degrees or more, is never taken and the shape of a group matters as much as its size; a push whose direction is yours to choose, so the same chain is worth different amounts from each of its ends; and pieces that are chains, so that what's laid is always something that can fall. Then the same players on this table: the corner habit over after about 35 pieces, by colour 52, the careful player about 117 (41 to 176) and the bell player about 106, ringing the bell about eighteen times a game, and the careful player's move the corner-by-colour habit's only 45 times in a hundred (the bell player's 38): there is something to decide at nearly every piece. The board's shape made no difference to length (an eight by eight grid with every other row shifted half a hexagon played the same), so it's the hexagon, which has no ragged edge. A fall that fans, straight on and both sixty degrees at once, was easier and so more trivial (the careful player's move was the corner habit's about three in four).

Why the knocks taper (October 2026): a knock every hand until the twelfth and then every second was a cliff: careful play was over in about 58 pieces, since the table filled all at once, where the straight-line taper lasts 117. A knock every hand throughout never ended a careful game (26 of 30 were still going at 1,500 pieces); every second from the first hand ended it in about 26. Bests from the square table keep their scores; a game saved on the square table, or before the strengths and the facing of each domino (version 3), can't be played on this one and is replaced by a new game. Every random thing (the pieces dealt and their colours, where the bell goes) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have. A piece is named by its hexagons from its first ("0,0;1,0;2,0", a line of three going east), so a saved hand doesn't depend on the order the pieces are made in.

`tests/topple.mjs` checks the table (sixty-one hexagons, six neighbours in the middle, three in a corner, each its neighbour's neighbour back), the deal from a seed (the bell on the table), the pieces (141 ways: one of one, three of two, nine of three, 29 of four, 99 of five, each a chain, a straight line of three dealt twice as often as the bend, the sizes dealt as they weigh), where a piece fits (on empty hexagons, not off the table), how dominoes fall (a line end to end; pushed in the middle only what's ahead; against its line only itself; sixty degrees off its line the whole line; bending where its line ends; straight on before sixty degrees; both at sixty degrees where none is ahead; never a hundred and twenty degrees or behind; a flower of seven leaving four; stopped by another colour, the edge or an empty hexagon; each from a neighbour a step before it), a push (cleared, the points, a line of three 90 from its end and 40 from its middle, a line of five 250, the best push on what's joined round a bend in seven, a lone domino 10), the bell (a fall past it doubled and the bell moved to an empty hexagon, one that misses it leaving it), the strength of a push (named; weak, medium and strong on a line, with nothing ahead, with one ahead, a fork, a flower and a colour that stops it; each fall inside the next on 400 random tables, always in one colour; a knock as hard as the game says; the strength dealt from the seed in the odds, medium first), how each domino faces (a line along itself, at a bend, a lone domino by where it stands, kept with the game and cleared when it falls), the pace (a knock after each of the first sixteen hands, about seven in ten from the fifty-seventh, `untilKnock` naming the hand whose end brings it), laying and the knock on the table's time (the third piece dealing three more, nothing laid until the knock is made), the end (no room and no knock due; a finished hand with the knock due not), a game saved and opened again, and whole games: laying every piece in the corner over in under 60 pieces, keeping lines that fall whole lasting half as long again or more, and still ending, the facing and the score kept right through whole games, and a weak knock felling well under what a medium one does.

### Orrery

The fifth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle), built first as a turn-based match-3, rebuilt the same month as a real-time game in 3D, and rebuilt again the same week with set speeds and tipping hoops: `orrery.html`, `orrery.js` and `orrery.css`, its rules in `orrery-engine.js`, its drawing in `orrery-3d.js` (flat, in `orrery-flat.js`, where there's no WebGL) and its sounds in `orrery-sound.js`. Five brass hoops hang on a pivot axle round a sun, like an armillary sphere: a straight rod from twelve o'clock through the sun to six, on which each hoop turns and tips. Twelve places on each hoop hold a planet, strung on its wire like a bead, in four enamels, each colour with a mark of its own (Saturn's ring, bands, a crescent, a polar cap) so they're told apart without their colour. Three to five planets of one colour, one to a hoop, in one direction from the sun (within about four degrees of their middle, `TOL`, 0.07 radians) and held there for a tenth of a second (`DWELL`) are an alignment, and they clear and score: 100 for three, 300 for four, 700 for five, times the combo (a quarter more for each alignment made within eight seconds of the last, to three times). Every few seconds a new planet lands in a free place, every 6.5 seconds at first and quicker as the game goes on, to every 2.4 seconds from the fifth and a half minute (`gap`); it is foretold 1.5 seconds ahead (a faint planet and a ring of its colour where it will land, on the hoop, so it travels with it). A planet that has nowhere to land ends the game.

What "in one direction from the sun" means, since the hoops tip: each planet has a direction from the sun in three dimensions, and an alignment is planets whose directions are within `TOL` of their middle. Two hoops only share a direction in two ways. Every hoop crosses the axle at twelve o'clock and at six, however it is tipped, so planets at twelve (or at six) on hoops tipped in any way are in line: those are the gates, and the easy alignment. And hoops tipped alike lie in one plane (are coplanar), so planets anywhere round them can be in line, as on a flat orrery. Hoops tipped differently are apart everywhere else, and to bring three of them together away from the axle you'd have to tip them alike first. A hoop tipping through a gate carries a planet standing at it with no movement at all, so tipping never breaks an alignment on the axle.

Controls. Tap a hoop (its wire, or a planet on it: the nearest within about 20 pixels) to choose it; it glows, and the seven buttons under the orrery are for it: very strong left, strong left, slow left, hold, slow right, strong right, very strong right. Slow, strong and very strong are 0.05, 0.14 and 0.3 radians a second (`SPEED`): a turn in two minutes, in 45 seconds and in 21. A hoop keeps the speed it is set to until it is set to another, and a hold stops it and keeps it stopped however long; it eases to a new speed over about 0.3 seconds (`ACCEL`), so a hold coasts a little first: about one degree from slow, two and a half from strong, five from very strong, which is the player's to allow for. Tap the same hoop again and the buttons tip it instead (a glow in a cooler colour, and a faint level ring showing where it would lie flat): 45, 30 and 15 degrees to the left or the right, and the middle button makes it level; the Spin and Tilt switch beside the buttons does the same as tapping again, for a hand that hasn't found that yet. Left is the three o'clock side down, right up. A swipe is the hand's way of the same, and different from the buttons: along a hoop (from the finger's first moments to its last) it sends the hoop the way the finger went, at a speed that goes with the finger's, from the slowest to very strong (0.07 times the finger's speed round the sun, the most it goes at 4.3 radians a second); it never stops a hoop (that's the middle button); and on a hoop that's being tipped a swipe across it tips it left or right by 15, 30 or 45 degrees by its speed. A drag on the sky away from the hoops turns the camera round the orrery and from low to nearly overhead (kept as `orrery:view`; the menu's Reset the view lets the room choose the height again, which is as high as fills it). The clock starts at the first hoop chosen or button pressed, and stops with the menu, the tab and the end of the game; a game opened again waits for a touch, under a plaque that gives the rules for the first games, then only how to go on. The strip has the score, the combo while it lasts, the room left (free places) and your best, kept as `orrery:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the hoop chosen is kept as `orrery:sel`, so a partner watching sees it. The game in play is kept (`orrery:run`) every five seconds while it runs and whenever an alignment clears, and opens again where it was. Sideways, on a tablet, the buttons, the Spin and Tilt switch and what the hoop is doing ("Hoop 3 · ▶ 8°/s", "Hoop 4 · tipped +30°") are in the side column, low; on a phone they're under the orrery.

The speeds are far slower than the first real-time version's, where the inner ring went a tenth of a turn a second by itself and a flick sent one a quarter of a turn a second: the fastest a hoop goes now is a twentieth of a turn a second, and the hoops start at 0.03 to 0.11 radians a second, all different, neither too still nor too quick. It is fast enough, though, for the last wish of the request: a hoop going very fast one way and two going very fast the other, all of one colour and tipped alike or at the gates, close on each other at 0.6 radians a second and stay in line (all within four degrees of their middle) for about a third of a second, which is longer than the tenth of a second an alignment is held for, so they clear as they pass, with no hand to stop them. (`tests/orrery.mjs` plays exactly that.)

The orrery in 3D (`orrery-3d.js`, on the vendored three.js that Rosette and Topple also use; it has no perspective camera, so one is made from the orthographic one): brass hoops (thin lathed tubes, the inner one close to the sun and the outer five wide of it) with a small brass bead at each of their twelve places, on an axle with a knuckle where each hoop crosses it, the planets on the wires and a little larger on each hoop outward, so the inner hoops don't crowd; the sun, a glowing core in a halo, on a column from a foot of brass inlaid with navy at the bottom; a horizon ring, a band of navy under brass with a scale of degrees, ticks every five degrees and a heavier one every thirty, round it all, with a soft shadow beneath. The hoops are drawn tipped and turning in real three dimensions, the camera is fitted to the outline of the hoops at their furthest tips, the horizon and the foot (a hoop tipped 45 degrees either way must not leave the screen), and a drag orbits it. There are no model or texture files, so it's there offline. The light is the sun at the middle (planets are bright on the side that faces it and dark outward, whatever the camera does) with a soft key light and the sky and ground; there are no lights in the scene but those the shader works out, since the bundle has no point light. An alignment that is charging has a beam from the sun to each of its planets and a glow round each (it lasts a tenth of a second now, so it is a flash more than a charge); one that clears swells, rises and goes in a flare with sparks, the sun kicks, and the score rises from the place with the combo beside it. A newly landed planet slides out onto its hoop while it swells. Each theme has its brass, its enamel and its light by day and by night, and the orrery follows the theme as it changes. Where there's no WebGL the page draws the same game flat (`orrery-flat.js`, the same calls, from straight above: a hoop is an ellipse, as narrow as it is tipped, and the axle a line). What it says over the orrery (the plaque, the scores) is an SVG in the screen's pixels, through the same projection.

Sounds are made as they play, in Web Audio (`orrery-sound.js`), with no files: a tink where a planet lands (higher on the outer hoops, on a pentatonic scale), a click when a hoop is chosen, a button's tick that climbs with its strength (a dull knock for a hold), a soft two-note sweep for a tip, a bell struck up a chord when an alignment clears (a note for each planet, so five is a longer chord than three, and a combo starts it higher), and two falling notes at the end. The menu's Sound switch (`orrery:sound`) turns them off; they stand still with the menu. Not heard by anyone yet.

Why it was rebuilt (Sunday 18 October 2026, Korbi): "make everything much slower, top speeds included; the player should be able to hold things in place; tap to select a ring, then the buttons at the bottom of the screen should be left, right, strong left, strong right and very strong both directions; the player can also swipe, but that's different; if the player taps the same ring twice, instead of orbit speed they set the angle of the orbit around the center point; make the whole thing more disconnected, the orbs on wires, very orrery-like; it should be faster to make orbs disappear: an orb going very fast the opposite way of two orbs going also very fast should be enough to trigger it." Readings chosen where that left room, to be checked by playing: a hoop's speed is set by the buttons and stays (nothing relaxes it back), and the hold, the middle button, is mine, from "hold things in place"; the angle of the orbit is the tip of the hoop's plane about the axle (the reading the first rebuild left unbuilt), which is why alignment is now in three dimensions, along the axle or on hoops tipped alike, since on hoops that are all level and flat the buttons would have been all there was; the swipe is a flick, not a drag that follows the finger (a hoop has no place to be dragged to now, since its speed is what's set); and "disconnected" is hoops and a sun with space between them, beads on wires and a horizon ring, instead of a solid dial. The old game's best was in another scale, so `orrery:best` is kept as it is, though a score here is not worth what it was there; a game saved by the previous version (version 2) is replaced by a new one (version 3). The first version, a turn-based game (a ring turned one place a move, three planets of a colour on one spoke in neighbouring rings were a conjunction, a comet swept a spoke), is in the history (commit 57730ad), and its best moved to `orrery:turns`.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players on the engine (a harness that picks the colour and gate it can bring together most cheaply, sets the hoops going at the strength that suits the distance, holds each as it nears the gate, leading it by what the button press and the coast will take, with a time to choose a hoop and to press a button and a hand that misses where it aims by a few degrees, and looks again if the line doesn't take hold; it doesn't tip anything), a player who touches nothing has the orrery full in about 316 seconds (243 to 345) with a median of 925 points, from chance (about seven alignments in a game, by hoops of different speeds drifting into line at the gates or on hoops tipped alike, which is why the tolerance isn't larger: 0.08 radians gave eleven a game). A careful player (0.8 seconds to choose a hoop, 0.6 to press, 2.5 to think between alignments) lasts about 475 seconds and scores about 3,350; one who is quicker (0.55 and 0.45 seconds, 1.2 thinking) was still going after twenty minutes in six games of ten, scoring about 16,000 by then; a quick one (0.4, 0.3, 0.6) was not over in any game. These bots are neither a person nor a phone, they make three-planet alignments nearly always (a four is about one a game and a five never came, so the awards for them are untested), and they don't use tilt, which a person will (the coplanar alignments are covered by the engine's tests, not by the bots), so the real range of play is somewhere between the first two and the rest, and all these numbers are first guesses for playing to tune: the arrival rate (`gap`), the hold (`DWELL`), the tolerance (`TOL`), the speeds (`SPEED`, `START_SPEEDS`), the easing (`ACCEL`, `TILT_EASE`) and the awards are in the first lines of the engine. The foretold planets land on the hoop with more room of two picked at random, in a place picked at random, with a colour that favours those that are scarce on the orrery, so every colour stays in play. Every random thing comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/orrery.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (five hoops of twelve places, tipped -30, -15, 0, 15 and 30 degrees in some order, fourteen planets, every colour at least twice, none in line, the first planet foretold on an empty place), the speeds and tips, the angles (round twelve o'clock, the short way between two) and directions (every hoop at one point at twelve and at six, however tipped; a tipped hoop lifting its three o'clock side), what an alignment is (three, four or five of a colour from different hoops at the gates, at twelve and at six, but not away from them on hoops tipped differently; on hoops tipped alike anywhere, and on hoops tipped nearly alike; two not, mixed colours not, just in and just out of the tolerance, across twelve o'clock, a planet too far off left out, two at once with the larger first), the hoops turning (each at its speed, a held hoop not turning, easing to a new speed, a hold standing still however long, the fastest a hoop can be sent), tipping (easing to the angle, no more than 45 degrees, a tip leaving an alignment on the axle standing), an alignment clearing (half the way after half the blink, its points, four 300 and five 700, one that breaks starting again, a fourth joining keeping what was charged), one hoop very fast one way and two the other clearing as they pass, the combo, the planets landing (the pace, not before they're due, in the place foretold on the hoop's own turn, the next foretold on an empty place, a place taken sending the newcomer to another, no place ending the game and everything stopping), a game saved and opened again going on as it would have, a game from the earlier orrery (version 2) not taken, and whole games: touching nothing full in about 300 seconds with a few points, a player setting hoops to a gate every few seconds lasting much longer and scoring a great deal. The page, the drawing and the sounds are not covered by tests; the page was clicked through in a headless browser with real pointer events (choosing a hoop, tapping it again, the Spin and Tilt switch, each button, a swipe each way along a hoop and across one, a drag on the sky, reloading with a hoop chosen), the swipe's speed checked with timed events, and the drawing looked at (software WebGL) at 390 by 600, 664 and 844, at 820 by 1180 and at 1180 by 820, in all three themes by day and by night, flat as well. It has not been played on a phone: the swipe's thresholds in particular (how fast is a flick, how far across tips a hoop) are guesses.

### Avalanche
The sixth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `avalanche.html`, `avalanche.js` and `avalanche.css`, its rules in `avalanche-engine.js`. A slope seven squares across and eight down, above a road. Each square holds up to three layers of snow (a tick for each, and a full square cracked, so depths read without their shade); a fourth sets it sliding: four layers go, one onto each neighbour, which can slide in turn, round and back, so a square can slide twice in one avalanche. This is the sandpile model (Bak, Tang and Wiesenfeld's, 1987), the standard example of self-organised criticality: a pile fed one grain at a time settles where the next grain can set off an avalanche of any size. Snow sliding off the top or the sides is gone; off the foot it lands on the road. Each day you place one charge, a layer of your own on any square: press a square and the avalanche it would set off is shown (every square that would slide ringed, the points above it, what it sends onto the road in the line under the slope), and let go on it to set it off. A slide you set off is controlled, the road closed for it, and scores ten times the square of its size, the number of slides in it (ten 1,000, twenty 4,000). A charge on a square that isn't full only adds a layer, which can be how a bigger avalanche is built. Then night falls and snow lands on squares at random, layer by layer: two a night at first, three after 20 days, four after 50 and five after 100. Whatever it sets off runs where it runs and scores nothing. The plough clears a little off the road each morning, but a night whose slides send three layers or more onto it buries it, a life lost, and the third ends the game. Two chalets stand on the slope (never on the foot): a slide through a chalet's square carries it away, to be built again elsewhere, and each chalet a slide of yours carries away halves what that slide scores (the press shows it ringed in red, and the halving), so where your own avalanche runs matters as well as how big it is: the biggest slide on the slope may be worth less than a smaller one that misses the chalets, and a loaded stretch round a chalet is one you'd rather the night didn't set off either. And each night's wind is forecast by day, streaks on the side it blows from: from the west it loads the east side of the slope (each column's share of the snow rising across it, seven to one), from the east the west side, and a still night spreads it evenly; two nights in five blow each way and one is still. The line under the slope says how much snow is coming tonight, on what wind, and how many squares above the road are full. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `avalanche:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`avalanche:run`) and opens again where it was. Held sideways the slope is on the left with all the height, the score, the lives and the line beside it.

Why three layers: with the road buried by any snow at all, careful play hardly mattered (about 40 days against 26 for charging anywhere), because every full square along the foot was a burial waiting for one flake and one charge a day can't empty seven. Needing three, a night has to send a real avalanche down, so the danger is a loaded stretch of slope that reaches the foot, and a charge can take that down first, on your terms and for points.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`charges` in the engine gives what a charge on each square would set off), one charging anywhere has the road buried three times in about 47 days; one setting off the biggest slide each day lasts about 80 and scores about 48,000; one weighing a slide's points against the full squares it leaves near the foot lasts about 99 (35 to 167) and scores about 68,000 (100 games each, chalets and wind and all). Every random thing (the snow dealt and where each night's falls) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

Why the foot is left as it is (reviewed October 2026): a player who fires a full square on the bottom row whenever there is one, and otherwise the biggest slide, outlasts that careful player (about 139 days against 99) but scores two-thirds as much (about 46,000), and one charging the fullest square nearest the road lasts 118 days for 25,000. Keeping the foot clear is the safe way to play and big slides the rewarding one; the best score comes from weighing the two, which is the game. Burying the road only under a night avalanche of ten slides or more was tried and left: the foot habit fell to about 74 days, but firing the biggest slide each day became about as good as careful play (85 days and 54,000 against 92 and 62,000), so every day's choice came down to the biggest slide. Needing five or six slides kept careful play ahead but shortened every game by a third.

`tests/avalanche.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (nothing full yet, two chalets off the foot, a wind forecast), a slide (four layers to the neighbours; at the foot onto the road, at the side gone; nothing set off below the limit), a row sliding one after another both ways, a square sliding twice in one avalanche, no snow made or lost, the day's charge and its points, the game charged from left as it was, the night's snow layer by layer, the day's slide onto the road not counting, a night sending three layers or more burying it, a bare slope where nothing slides, the points, the pace, chalets (a slide through one carrying it away for half the score, built again elsewhere, two quartering it), the wind (from the west loading the east side, from the east the west, still spreading it; the next night's forecast), the end at the third burial, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: charging anywhere buried three times in under 60 days, careful charging lasting half as long again or more.

### Netting
The seventh idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `netting.html`, `netting.js` and `netting.css`, its rules in `netting-engine.js`. Six trading houses round a baize table (GVA, LON, SIN, HOU, RTM, SHA), an arrow for each obligation between two of them, from the debtor, its amount on it. Three new obligations wait under the table, each from a given debtor for a given amount; you choose who each is owed to, in the order you like, and three more come once all three are booked. Drag one onto the house it's to be owed to (an arrow follows the finger from its debtor), or tap it and then the house; while it's held over a house the loops it would net are lit, with what they'd score or the default it would cause in the line above the obligations, and letting go there books it. A loop of obligations (A owes B owes C owes A) is netted out by its smallest leg: every leg pays down by that much, and a leg paid off goes, as a clearing house's multilateral netting does; owing each other is the smallest loop, two legs. Netting scores ten times the square of the gross it cancels, the amount times the legs, so a long loop scores most (a loop of three paying down 2 cancels 6, 360; six houses paying down 3 cancel 18, 3,240). If a booking closes more than one loop, the one cancelling most goes first. A house may owe up to its limit, ten to begin with (the gauge round it, red from two short); past that it defaults, its debts are written off and a life is lost, and the third default ends the game. A booking only ever adds to its debtor, so the way to keep a house within its limit is to book its obligations where they close a loop through it. Interest: an obligation that stands for four hands without being added to or netted grows by one as the next hand comes. A ring round its amount fills a quarter a hand and is red when the next hand will charge it, and the line under the table says how many are due. Contagion: a default cuts each of the defaulter's creditors' limits by what it owed them, to no less than five (the cut limit red after the slash), and a creditor now owing more than its cut limit defaults too, a life each, which can run on round the table. Limits recover by one with each new hand, and a defaulter's is whole again. While an obligation is held over a house the houses that booking would bring down are ringed in dashes and the line names them, including any the new hand's interest would tip over when it's the last of a hand; a cascade plays out house by house, each flashing as its debts go and its creditors' limits fall. Obligations come for one or two at first, up to three after 8 hands, four after 20, five after 40 and six after 70. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `netting:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`netting:run`) and opens again where it was. Held sideways the table is on the left with all the height, the score, the line and the three obligations beside it, low where a thumb is.

Why it plays as it does: no loop is ever left standing (each booking nets out everything it closes), so every loop is made by a booking, and a loop can only be closed by an obligation from a house that something leads back to. A player who books only for what nets now does worse than one booking at random (about 48 bookings against 74 when it was tuned), because with nothing to net it piles everything onto one house and no ways back form; one that also weighs how many future bookings would close a loop lasts about 200. That's the game: lay the ways back, then close them. Interest and contagion (October 2026) make the ways back cost something to keep: a way back left standing too long grows, so a debt parked where no loop will come for it isn't free, and a default is no longer one house's affair, so it matters who a strained house owes as well as how much (a house near its limit owed by another near its own is a fuse). The floor of five keeps a cascade from cutting a limit to nothing, and the recovery of one a hand lets a cut limit mend if it's given room.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`moves` lists the fifteen bookings a hand offers), one booking anywhere has three houses default in about 54 bookings (75 to 80 before interest and contagion); one weighing what nets now, how near the houses are to their own limits and how many loops are waiting to be closed lasts about 183 (93 to 426) and scores about 26,000 (about 200 and 28,000 before). Over 30 games the random player set off 16 cascades and the careful one 6: the threat is there, and care keeps it rare. Interest every three hands with a floor of four took the random player down to 43 and the careful one to 165, too harsh; every five hands played much like four. With obligations never larger than four the careful player once lasted up to 825, so they keep growing. Every random thing (each obligation's debtor and amount) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/netting.mjs` checks the deal from a seed, a booking (the debtor owing it, the game booked from left as it was, never to its own debtor, five houses for each of three obligations, more to the same house adding up), loops (owing each other paying down by the smaller; a loop of three and its 360; the loop from the debtor round to it again; of two loops the one cancelling most first; no loop left standing after any of 300 random bookings), the points, a default (debts written off, a life; netting first keeping a house within its limit; owing exactly ten within it), contagion (a default cutting its creditors' limits, the one just booked to as well, and tipping one over; never below five; a defaulter's limit whole again; a creditor owing exactly its cut limit holding; limits recovering one a hand), interest (an obligation ageing a hand at a time, growing by one after four, its clock starting again; what was added to this hand not charged; netting starting the clock again; interest tipping a house over as the new hand comes; a game saved before all this given ages and limits), a new hand after three, the pace, the end at the third default with no new hand, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: booking anywhere defaulting three times in under 90 bookings, booking to build loops lasting two and a half times as long or more.

### Settle
The eighth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `settle.html`, `settle.js` and `settle.css`, its rules in `settle-engine.js`. A tank farm of six tanks, eight parcels high, under a header pipe, and four grades from the heaviest to the lightest: water (999 kg/m³ at 15 °C), FAME (fatty acid methyl ester, biodiesel, 883), diesel (835) and naphtha (700), each in an enamel and lettered W, F, D, N so it's read without its colour. Two parcels are offered at a time under the farm, each with its density. Drag one onto a tank (or tap it, then the tank): a parcel sinks below everything lighter and settles on what's as heavy or heavier, lifting the lighter ones a level, so a tank always stands in order, heaviest at the foot. While it's held over a tank the farm is shown as the pump would leave it (where it settles, what it lifts, the lines that would ship out ringed, the line above the parcels saying what it would score or the spill it would cause), and letting go pumps it. Parcels of a grade at one level, in neighbouring tanks, ship out once there are enough of them for the tanker at the jetty (three of its grade, four of any other; below); what floated above them drops a level, which can line up more, a second wave. A pump scores ten times the square of all it ships, its waves together (three 90, six 360). You choose only the tank: density chooses the level, so the thinking is in what a parcel lifts and into line with what. Every few pumps a delivery comes in of its own (it never makes a line, and never fills a tank past eight): one every three pumps at first, every two after 25, every pump after 60, two at a time after 120 and three after 200; the line above the parcels counts down to the next. A ninth parcel in a tank spills its lightest over the top, a life lost, unless the pump ships some of that tank out first; the third spill ends the game.

Slops and the draw-off (October 2026). FAME standing directly on water sours, as biodiesel on a wet tank bottom does: after five pumps with the two touching (every pump counts, whichever tank it went into), the FAME parcel touching the water turns to slops (S, a speckled mud of FAME's colour and water's), an emulsion of 950 kg/m³ that settles between them and never lines up. A mark where water and FAME meet counts down the pumps left, red when the next pump sours it, and the line under the farm names the tank nearest souring; once slops stand between the water and the FAME they no longer touch, so a tank sours once until it's drained. The clock stops when they part (the water or the FAME shipped out). Every ten pumps a draw-off is ready, the third card beside the parcels (it counts down until then, and waits once ready): dragged onto a tank like a parcel, it drains the water and slops from that tank's foot, the two heaviest, and what stood on them drops into their place, where it can line up with the neighbouring tanks and ship. Held over a tank it shows the farm drained and the lines that would ship, and the line says what it drains and scores; a tank with nothing at its foot can't take it. It counts as a pump (the deliveries and the souring move on), the parcels offered stay as they were, and the next draw-off is ten pumps away. A game saved before slops and draw-offs opens with both clocks at their start.

The tankers (October 2026). A tanker at the jetty, drawn over the header pipe with its grade's cargo on deck, takes one grade: three of it in a line ship, and any other grade needs four. Every ten pumps the next comes in, always for another grade, and waits beside it ("next", its grade, "in 7": the pumps until it comes); as it berths, the lines of three of its grade standing ready ship, scored as a pump's are, ten times the square of all it takes. A line of three that doesn't ship yet is dotted in brass, standing ready for its grade's tanker or a fourth, and a parcel held where it would make one says so. Water goes by barge (tank bottoms taken away to be treated), the rest by tanker. The line under the farm explains the tanker for three pumps the first time it's seen, in a first game after the first pump (`settle:jetty` counts them); a game saved before the tankers opens with one in and the next ten pumps away.

The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `settle:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`settle:run`) and opens again where it was. Held sideways the farm is on the left with all the height, the score, the line and the two parcels beside it; on a phone on its side the score runs along one line and the line under it takes two rows, as upright, since a taller column rode up over the score.

Why the deliveries grow to three at a time: tanks that always stand in order line up easily (the foot of most tanks is the same heavy grade), and a player taking the pump that ships most and keeps the tanks low lasted 3,000 pumps with a delivery every pump. Three at a time after 200 ends that player's game soon after.

Why the tankers (reviewed October 2026): with three of any grade shipping, lines formed by themselves, since every tank's foot is water and every tank stands in order. A habit of taking whatever ships, else filling the emptiest tank, lasted about 251 pumps against a careful player's 287 and scored four-fifths as much (24,000 against 31,000), and only one of its spills in ten came before pump 200: the deliveries' pace ended a game, not the play. With the tankers that habit lasts about 194 pumps, its first spill at about 169, and scores about 18,000; a player who lays pairs (two of a grade side by side at one level, a line one parcel short) lasts about 252 and scores about 30,000, and one who also readies lines of three for the next tanker lasts about 265 and scores about 35,000, nearly twice the habit (80 games each). The grade that ships by threes changes every ten pumps and the next is known, so the tanks worth building towards change too. Four in a line for every grade, tanks standing on footings of different heights and one grade held back from shipping were tried and did less: four in a line paid only a player planning two pumps ahead.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`moves` lists the twelve pumps, two parcels into six tanks, and `draws` the tanks a ready draw-off could drain), one pumping anywhere spills three times in about 27 pumps, and one always filling the emptiest tank in about 63; one taking the pump that ships most and keeps the tanks low and level lasts about 216 (135 to 321) and scores about 21,000. Before the tankers, that player lasted about 230 (138 to 295) and scored about 25,000; slops alone cut it to about 213; the draw-off as well brings it back to about 249 (135 to 415, 40 games), scoring about 23,000, with some 18 slops made and 21 draw-offs spent a game, so the two roughly balance and the game asks more: when to spend the draw-off, and where FAME may stand. A draw-off every eight pumps let it last about 295, every twelve about 252; souring after four pumps, 277 with a draw-off every ten (it drains more slops than it lets form), after three, 244. Every random thing (the parcels dealt and offered, each delivery's grade and tank) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/settle.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (every tank in order, no line standing), settling (below everything lighter, on what's as heavy or heavier), lines (three at a level in neighbouring tanks, two not enough, a tank without that level breaking it, any level counting), shipping (what floated above dropping into place, a second wave seen in play), the points, a pump (the game pumped from left as it was, the parcel used replaced, twelve pumps), a spill past eight and a pump that ships its own tank down first, the deliveries (never a line, never past eight, every tank in order; more often and more at once), slops (FAME on water a pump nearer souring each time, the fifth turning it; never in a line; settling under FAME and on water; keeping water and FAME apart; the clock stopping when water ships from under it), the draw-off (draining water and slops, what's above dropping and shipping; counting as a pump, the next ten away; only a tank with something at its foot, only when ready; waiting once ready; a game saved before it given its clocks), the tankers (one in from the start and the next for another grade; three of its grade in a line, four of any other, three of any with none given; a pump shipping with the tanker it finds, then the next coming in and shipping the three of its grade standing ready, scored; the clock between; one every ten pumps, never for the grade before, every grade in its turn; a game saved before them given its first), the end at the third spill, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: pumping anywhere spilling three times in under 60 pumps, pumping (and drawing off) with thought lasting half as long again or more, and laying pairs scoring a quarter more than the habit of shipping when it can, else filling the emptiest tank.

### Drift
The ninth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `drift.html`, `drift.js` and `drift.css`, its rules in `drift-engine.js`. A field seven squares by seven, tiles of the four enamels lying on it, each with a mark of its own (a dot, a ring, a bar, a chevron) so they're told apart without their colour, and the wind, forecast three turns ahead under the field beside the tile to lay and the one after. Each turn you lay the tile on any empty square; then the wind blows and every tile slides downwind until it meets the edge or another tile, keeping its order (the edge it piles against is drawn in brass). It's 2048 turned round: there you choose the way and the game lays the tile, here the forecast chooses the way and you lay it, so along the wind each row is a queue and where you lay a tile is where it slots in. Three or more alike in a line, across or down, clear, and the wind blows again, which can line up more; a turn scores ten times the square of all it clears (three 90, six 360). Press a square and the turn is shown before you let go (the field after the wind, the lines that would clear ringed, what the turn would score); slide to another square to see it, and let go to lay the tile (a mouse shows each square's turn as it passes). Every few turns a squall blows stray tiles in on empty squares before the wind takes them, none making a line where it lands: every two turns at first, every turn after 15, two at a time after 40, three after 80 and four after 150. A squall is forecast a turn ahead (October 2026): its strays are drawn faint on the squares they'll land on, no tile can be laid there (never on the last empty square), and the turn shown before you let go has them in it, so it's the turn you'll get; the line under the field says when the next squall comes, or that one is about to. Stakes (October 2026): every sixth tile to lay comes with a stake, a pin in its corner (on the tile to lay, and on the next when it's the one). Laid, it stays put whatever the wind, and the tiles the wind brings pile against it as against an edge, those beyond it sliding on to the field's edge as before; it clears in a line like any tile, and its stake goes with it. So a stake is a wall you place: something to build a line against in the middle of the field, or a way to hold a pair apart from the tile that would spoil it, and a nuisance where it blocks the line you wanted. The turn shown with a staked tile holds it in place, and the first time one comes the line under the field says what it does. A field with no empty square left after the wind ends the game; the strip counts the room left. The score and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `drift:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`drift:run`) and opens again where it was. A first game's first turns say what to do, written on the field. Held sideways the field is on the left with all the height, the score, what's coming and the line beside it.

Why the squall is forecast: blown in unseen, its strays undid whatever a turn set up, so a player laying where the turn shown clears most and otherwise dropping the tile in from the windward side lasted as long as one weighing the room left or looking at the next tile too (about 99, 97 and 98 turns, scoring 13,700, 12,900 and 15,000), and every way of playing that took the clears shown ended alike. (The tuning players below were first run on what a turn really did, squall and all, which the page never showed; judging only what it shows, the careful one lasted about 91.) Forecast, the habit lasts about 168 turns and scores about 34,500, the careful player about 184 (104 to 350) and 40,700, and the one looking at the next tile too about 189 and 52,100: reading the field pays again, and laying beside a stray to line it up is a move of its own. Players that never take a clear still fill the field in about 50 turns.

Why the squalls grow to four at a time: the wind packs every tile against one edge each turn, and packed tiles line up easily, so a player laying for the wind lasted 3,000 turns with a squall only every fourth turn, and one laying anywhere about 110. Four strays a turn late in the game is what ends the careful player's.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`moves` lists the squares a tile may go on), one laying anywhere fills the field in about 53 turns; one laying where the turn clears most and leaves the most room lasts about 143 (53 to 276) and scores about 26,000; one that also keeps pairs of a colour together lasts about 184, and looking at the next tile too about 189. (Before the forecast the second of these read 235 turns and 58,000 points: it judged each square by the turn it would really make, squall and all, and since where the strays landed hung on where the tile went, it could pick its luck, which no person could.) Stakes, when they came in, left the players about where they were (a stake every sixth tile, four or five standing at most; every fourth tile or every eighth, much the same), so they change what a turn can do rather than how long a game lasts. Every random thing (the tiles dealt and coming, the winds, the squalls' strays) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

`tests/drift.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (no line standing, two tiles and three winds forecast), the wind (to the edge or the next tile in order, east, west, south and north, and what moved told), lines (across, a gap breaking one, down, a square in two counted once), a turn (a line made by the wind and its 90, nothing made where the wind takes the tile elsewhere, a clear and a second gust lining up more, the game laid on left as it was, the next tile and the forecast moving on, empty squares only, the gale as the page shows it), the points, squalls (forecast a turn ahead, their strays on empty squares where no tile can be laid, landing there before the wind so the turn shown is the turn played, the next forecast once it's a turn away, never on the last empty square, a game saved before forecasts given one; the count starting again, more often and more of them), stakes (a staked tile staying put and the wind piling tiles against it either way, only what moved told, down a column the same, a stake keeping a line apart, a staked tile laid staying where it's laid, one in a line clearing with its stake, the count to the next, a game saved before stakes given none), the end with no room, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: laying anywhere full in under 100 turns, laying for the wind lasting half as long again or more.

### Rosette
The tenth idle game (October 2026; README, Idle): `rosette.html`, `rosette.js` and `rosette.css`, its rules in `rosette-engine.js`, the watch drawn by `rosette-3d.js`. Guilloché is engraving by machine: a rose engine, a lathe whose spindle rocks against a lobed cam (the rosette) as the work turns, so the cutter traces a wave round it, cuts rows of fine grooves into a watch dial, and translucent enamel fired over them (flinqué) glows where their facets catch the light. Here the dial is five rings round the centre and twelve sectors round, one to each hour. Three cuts wait under the watch, each a small shape of one to four cells (a dot, arcs and rays, corners, a square, Ts, steps), in one of four patterns, each in its enamel: grain d'orge (barleycorn, two families of waves crossing), clous de Paris ("Paris nails", a grid of small pyramids), soleil (a sunburst, rays from the centre) and vagues (waves); and each with a rosette of one, two, three, four or six lobes. A cut goes on as it's dealt, never turned, and the engine repeats it once for each lobe, evenly round the dial: four lobes, four copies a quarter turn apart. The first copy, the one you place, needs bare metal; a repeat that would cross metal already cut is skipped whole, the others cut. So a dial kept in a rosette's symmetry takes every repeat, and one cut out of it wastes them. Cut the three in any order; three more come when all are in. A ring cut all round, or a spoke cut from the centre to the rim, is finished: faced off (turned flat on the lathe, bare again) and scored, ten times the square of the cells one cut finishes, each cell once and a band all in one pattern (true: the guilloché runs unbroken round it or along it) counting its cells again. A ring in mixed patterns scores 1,440, a true ring 5,760, a ring and a spoke at once (16 cells) 2,560. Each cut in hand is drawn on a small dial of its own with all its repeats, the first outlined, and its rosette in the middle with the number of lobes; its pattern is drawn over its enamel, so cuts are told apart without their colour.

Drag a cut onto the dial: while it's held where its first copy fits, every copy is shown on the dial (a skipped one marked in red), the rings and spokes it would finish are lit, and the line under the watch says what it would do (all four copies cut, finishes a ring in one pattern, +5,760; or two of three copies cut, one would cross a cut). It's held above the finger so the place is in sight, and goes to the nearest place it fits within a ring or a sector of where it's held; let go to cut it, anywhere else and it goes back. A tap picks a cut up instead; a press on the dial then shows it there, and letting go cuts it. A cut plays out on the watch: each copy engraved in turn, then the bands it finished faced off, the cutter sweeping round a ring and out along a spoke, and the points rising off them. With no cut in hand, a drag across the dial turns the watch in the light (it tilts a little with the drag and settles back), and the menu's "The light follows the phone's tilt" has the light follow the phone instead (an iPhone asks permission). A cut with nowhere to go is dimmed; when none in hand has anywhere, the game is over.

The cuts grow: six cells at most, repeats and all, in the first four hands, eight to the tenth, ten to the twentieth, then twelve, a ring's worth (the line under the watch says when they grow). A shape comes only with rosettes whose copies don't overlap (a cut three sectors wide, four lobes at most). From the thirtieth hand the cutter is worn: as each new hand comes it nicks a bare cell, in a pattern at random (two cells from the sixtieth hand, three from the hundredth), never one that would finish a band. The nicks are scratched in on the watch as the hand comes, and the line under it says so.

The watch is drawn pixel by pixel by one shader in WebGL, nothing modelled and nothing a picture: each pixel's ray meets the dial, tilted a little in perspective, and a cut cell's grooves are the real thing, V-cuts (grooves of a V section) setting the surface's slope pixel by pixel, so the facets catch a studio's lights as engraved metal does and the enamel tints what they reflect; bare metal shows the lathe's turning, a satin finish whose highlight runs across the circles. Round the dial are the chapter ring with its applied batons (the hour marks) and minute track, the hub, the bezel, the case with its crown at three, and the lugs (the horns a strap fixes to) where there's height for them. Almanac's watch is brass with a silvered dial, Modern's and Office's steel, Kontor's drawn in ink, each by day and by night. It draws only when something changes. Where there's no WebGL the dial is drawn flat, in SVG, and plays the same.

The score, the bands finished and your best are in the strip, the best kept as `rosette:best` the moment it's beaten, so the games screen has it; the game in play is kept (`rosette:run`) and opens again where it was, and the light setting is `rosette:tilt`. A first game's first cuts say what to do, in the line under the watch (`rosette:seen` once three are in). Held sideways the watch is on the left with all the height, the score, the line and the three cuts beside it, low where a thumb is; on a phone on its side the cuts sit side by side, as upright, since three one above another rode up over the score.

Why a repeat is skipped rather than refused: with every copy having to fit, the dial jammed at once (a player keeping the most room was out of places in about 8 cuts, with cuts of up to twelve cells from the start); skipping a crossed copy whole, about 92; cutting whichever of a repeat's cells were bare, about 365, some games never ending (2,000 cuts), since a repeat then fills gaps by itself. Skipped whole, the repeats are the reason to keep the dial symmetric and the price of not.

Why these numbers: played out by simple players (`moves` in the engine lists every place each cut in hand fits), one cutting anywhere has nowhere to go after about 14 cuts (8 to 34); a habit (always the first place listed, always nearest twelve o'clock, always the rim) lasts about 26 to 29; one taking the cut that scores most, about 41; one judging at a glance (the points, how much is cut, the rings and spokes nearly finished, a cut in hand left with nowhere to go), about 59, and with an eye for holes only a dot can fill, about 77. A careful player, also weighing the room left for every shape there is, lasts about 206 cuts (95 to 368) and scores about 89,000, finishing some 150 bands, one in eight true; the dial its cut leaves matches the one a habit's would no more than about one time in three. When the game was made, with cuts of up to twelve cells from the start, the players used then had nowhere to go after 13, 50 and 122 cuts, against 16, 49 and 167 with the cuts growing: the growing cuts give a first game room to learn the repeats in, and a careful player longer. Every random thing (each cut's shape, pattern and rosette, and each nick) comes from the game's seed and the number of the draw, so a game opened again goes on as it would have.

Why the cutter wears: once the cuts are a ring's worth nothing more changed, and a careful player could cut on and on. Reviewed in October 2026, the careful player above lasted 451 cuts in a median game and 2,210 in the longest, an hour and more of the same few moves. The nicks start after the thirtieth hand, past where a glance or a habit has ended, so they touch only the long games: a careful game is now about 206 cuts where it was 451, and nothing shorter changed (the glance's 59, the habits' 26 to 29). A nick never finishes a band, so it can't score for you; it takes room, and can spoil a band you were keeping in one pattern.

`tests/rosette.mjs` checks the deal from a seed (a bare dial and three cuts, each with a rosette its shape allows, six cells at most in the first hands), the shapes (each joined along its sides, none twice; no rosette making a cut's repeats overlap or engrave more than a ring), a cut's repeats (four lobes a quarter turn apart, round past twelve o'clock, a repeat crossing metal already cut skipped whole and the others cut, the first copy having to fit, on bare metal and on the dial), where a cut fits, bands (a ring all round, a spoke from the centre to the rim, true in one pattern, a ring and a spoke crossing counted once and a true band's cells again, eleven of twelve nothing), the points, a cut (four dots finishing a true ring for 5,760, faced off and counted, the hand, the game cut from left as it was, a mixed ring's 1,440, three more after the third, a cut not in hand or not fitting refused, every cut listed), the cuts growing to a ring's worth, the worn cutter (no nick while a hand is in play or before the thirtieth hand, one with the thirtieth, two with the sixtieth, three with the hundredth; on bare metal, in a pattern, touching nothing else and never finishing a band, none at all when every bare cell would; falling all over the dial in every pattern, from the game's seed), the end with nowhere to go, a game saved and opened again, and whole games: cutting anywhere out of places in under 60 cuts, cutting to finish bands lasting half as long again or more.

### Slipway
A carving game (rebuilt 19 October 2026; README, Idle): `slipway.html`, `slipway.js` and `slipway.css`, the workshop in `slipway-carve.js` (no page: the tests run it in Node), the bench drawn by `slipway-3d.js`. Korbi: "a carving, sawing, wood-block modification job, so the user does most themselves; realistically there should be a ghost outline; focus on this as a pure carving game at first." So it is the parts of a ship made by hand, one block of timber at a time, and nothing else yet: no yard, no assembly, no voyage. Those can come once the carving is right.

Each part is a block of timber with the part's ghost seen through it. Before the first cut the ghost can be slid along the block (◀ ▶) or turned end for end, which matters because each block has one or two knots: a knot left in the finished part costs 12 points, and the plane and chisel cut a knot a third as fast. The tools, in a row under the bench:
- **Saw:** draw a line across the bench and the saw cuts straight through the block along it (the cut is the plane through your eye and the line). Of the two pieces, the one with less of the part in it drops away, so a cut is always "take this off"; a cut through the part is allowed, counted, and graded.
- **Plane:** a stroke shaves under a flat sole about seven cells across, facing the way the wood faced where the stroke began (held square to the block when it's within 8° of a face, so a face meant to be flat stays flat). High spots go, hollows are left.
- **Chisel:** a stroke takes a small, deeper bite all along it; tap for a single chip.
- **Drill:** tap and it bores a round hole straight in along your line of sight, right through; the view snaps square to the block's faces as you turn it, so a hole can be set straight.
- **Sand:** smooths where you stroke it, taking the bumps and never adding wood.
- **Turn:** a drag turns the block round; so do two fingers anywhere (a pinch zooms), or a mouse's right button and wheel. The view button steps through corner, side, top and end views.

Within a stroke the plane's blade stays a shaving (0.45 of a cell) under the highest wood its sole has passed over, and the chisel takes only the wood that was open to the air when the stroke began (`exposed` in the engine), so going over the same place again in one stroke takes nothing more and a deeper cut is another stroke, as at a real bench. Without that, overlapping touches dug pits in a single swipe, and a plane stroke begun on a low spot dived along its whole length. Undo takes back the last ten strokes. While you work, the line under the bench says the waste left (as a share of the block while there's lots, then as a share of the part, which is what's graded), how much of the part you've cut into (in oxblood, and the surface is tinted oxblood where you went past the line) and whether a knot is in it, with the stars it would get. Finish grades it: the score is 100 less 1 for each per cent of waste left (measured against the part's own volume, not the block's, so roughing out with the saw earns nothing by itself: a treenail sawn square is a star, planed to an octagon two, rounded three), 3 for each per cent of the part cut away and 12 for each knot; 55 is a star, 75 two, 90 three. A band about a cell wide either side of the line counts as neither, as a pencil line would.

The block is a grid of cells, each holding how much wood is left in it (so a tool can take part of a cell and the surface can lie between cells), drawn as a smooth surface remade after each stroke by surface nets (a vertex where the surface crosses each little cube of cells, joined across the cells' faces). The wood is worked out in the shader from where each point lies in the log: long grain along the block, rings on its ends and wherever a cut opens them, the rings swirling round a knot's dark heart. The ghost is the part's own surface drawn through the block. The bench takes the theme (walnut, oak, baize or linen, by day and night), and it draws only while something moves. A device without WebGL gets a message: carving needs the 3D.

The parts are the cog's, the easiest first: a mast wedge (two saw cuts), a treenail (a wooden peg), a belaying pin, a deck beam with its camber, a block shell with its slot and pin hole, a tiller square at one end and round at the other, a frame (a rib, a curve), a yard tapering both ways, the stem, the rudder with its stock and tiller hole, and the masthead with its halyard hole. Each part opens when the one before has a star. Kept: the block in hand as `slipway:carve` (its cells run-length coded as text, saved after each stroke, so a partner watching sees it), each part's best as `slipway:parts`, and the stars together as `slipway:best`, which the games screen shows. The merge game's best, a score in the tens of thousands, was moved to `slipway:yard-best` so the games screen doesn't show it as stars; the merge game itself is in the history (commit 5dbc6ba).

`tests/slipway.mjs` checks that every part lies inside its block with timber all round it and that a perfect carving of each is three stars; that a block from a seed is the same every time; sliding and turning the part; the saw (taking the side with less of the part, everything past the line and nothing before it, nothing when it misses, a cut through the part counted); the plane (a shaving from the surface only, one shaving a stroke however often it passes, its blade riding on the highest wood it has passed, twelve strokes going deeper, its facing held square); a treenail sawn square, planed to an octagon, planed again and planed past the line graded one, two, three and no stars; the chisel taking less from a knot; the drill boring straight through; sandpaper never adding wood; the ray finding the face it points at; the surface closed and every triangle facing out; the grade (waste, cuts into the part, a knot); and a block packed into text and back. The page and the drawing were clicked through in a headless browser with real pointer events (a saw cut along a drawn line, plane strokes, a drill, undo, finish, a reload) and looked at in software WebGL at phone and iPad sizes in the themes; whether the tools feel right in the hand has not been tried on a phone.

### Thimble
A mending game (19 October 2026; README, Idle): `thimble.html`, `thimble.js` and `thimble.css`, the jobs and their grading in `thimble-engine.js` (no page: the tests run it in Node), the cloth and everything on it drawn by `thimble-draw.js` on a 2D canvas. Korbi: "New idle game: sewing. Fix buttons, disattached belt loops, holes, whatever." Read as the casual hands-on kind the Idle games are (Slipway's neighbour), not a puzzle: the work is doing a job well with a needle, in 2D rather than 3D because mending is done flat on the cloth, and because a bench of a hundred stitches needs no camera.

A piece of cloth fills the bench, woven, knitted or flecked to suit the job (poplin, twill, denim, knit, tweed, each made once on an off-screen canvas and kept). Under it a line says what to do next, and two buttons: Unpick (take back the last stitch or darning thread; it costs a little) and Start over. Three kinds of step make every job:
- **Set the part:** drag the button onto its chalk cross, the loose end of a belt loop down onto the band, the patch onto the worn place. It must land within a radius of its mark (otherwise it springs back and counts as a miss); how near is graded.
- **Stitch:** tap the ringed marks in order. The first is where the needle comes up through the cloth (gold ring), the next where it goes down (teal), and so on; the thread lies on the cloth from each up to the next down and runs under it from there to the next up, so only the first is drawn solid and the under-cloth stretch is a dashed guide while you work. A tap counts within one and a half radii of the mark and is graded by its distance (full marks on the mark, a third of the marks at the radius, none at one and a half); a tap farther off sews nothing and is a miss. Holding the finger down shows the needle and the thread trailing from the last stitch, and the stitch is made where the finger lifts, so you can slide to aim. The belt loop shows two marks at a time, the patch three, the others all of them.
- **Darn:** pull a thread across the hole from sound cloth to sound cloth, lengthwise first (the warp: the way the cloth's own threads run), then across (the weft, over and under the warp as it goes, so the darn is a small woven square). A pull has to start and end past the hole by half the darn's margin, lands on the nearest lane (the lane ends are ticked), and is graded by how near the lane's centre and how straight it was; a lane already filled, a slanting pull, one that starts inside the hole or the wrong way for the phase is turned away with a word, and counts as a miss. A touch that is not a pull counts for nothing.

The eight jobs, in order, each opening when the one before has a star: a two-hole shirt button (four passes), a four-hole button (crossed, three rounds), a trouser hem (a running stitch of eight along the fold), a torn seam (ladder stitch back and forth across the tear, then the two halves are drawn shut), a belt loop (set the loose end down and bar-tack it: five rows of satin stitch across the loop), a darned sock (some eight threads lengthwise, then as many across), an elbow patch (set it on the worn place and stitch round it) and moth holes in a sweater (two holes, each darned). Every job is made from a seed (`makeJob(index, seed)`), so the same seed is the same cloth, hole, button and thread; a new run takes a new seed and moves the marks, the angle of the buttons, the colours and the shape of the holes.

The grade is a score of 100: the mean of how well each thing landed (placing a part counts four times a stitch, a darning thread once), less 0.8 for each miss (to 10) and 1.2 for each stitch unpicked (to 6). 50 is a star ("it'll hold"), 72 two ("neat work") and 88 three ("invisible mending"; a perfect hand is a hundred, which `tests/thimble.mjs` plays through on every job and seed). The finished dialog breaks it into setting, stitching and darning, tidiness and the score; the menu lists the jobs with their stars.

Saved state is under `thimble:`: `thimble:run` (the job in hand, its seed and every stitch made, as plain data so another device can watch it), `thimble:jobs` (the best score by job), `thimble:best` (the stars in all, for the games screen) and `thimble:opts` (sound). Sounds are made as they play, like Slipway's. Nothing here has a clock, so there is nothing to pause beyond the drawing, which stands still while the menu is open.

`tests/thimble.mjs` checks that every job builds the same from a seed and lies inside the cloth (darns apart, marks apart, parts starting well away from their marks); that tapping every mark exactly earns a hundred and three stars on every job and seed; the accuracy curve; the stars at 50, 72 and 88; placing within and past the radius; stitching (a tap past one and a half radii sews nothing, a near one is graded low, up follows down, unpicking takes back one and costs, a finished step stays finished); the darn (a touch is not a pull, a pull starting in the hole, the weft before the warp, a slanting pull, a lane twice, a pull between lanes taking the nearer, one past the last lane, the weft beginning when the warp is in, two holes in order); that a run keeps through JSON; and that every thread of a torn seam's cloth crosses the tear.

Not yet: an economy or a daily, the tablet's finer fingers (it draws everything larger but asks no more precision), hand-sewn effects such as puckering when stitches are too tight, and the later jobs a tailor would do (a zip, a buttonhole, a cuff). Seen at 390×600, 390×664, 390×844, 820×1180 and 1180×820, in all three themes, day and night; played by mouse and by touch in a browser, not yet on a phone.

## Casino
Games of chance (October 2026), in a section of their own on the games screen because nothing in them rewards getting better: no choice changes the odds. (Hold'em, the last of them, is the exception: it is played against other players, and what you do with your cards is the game.) They're here to learn a casino floor before sitting down at it, and to play its games for play money (nothing real is staked, and the bankroll tops up from the menu).

### Baccarat
Punto banco as Macau deals it, the Venetian's tables in mind (October 2026): `baccarat.html`, `baccarat.js` and `baccarat.css`, its rules in `baccarat-engine.js`, the scoreboard drawn by `baccarat-roads.js`, the squeeze by `baccarat-squeeze.js`, the course in `baccarat-course.js` on the stage `baccarat-stage.js` (which also draws the betting layout, the table's and the course's alike), and the rules on one page in `baccarat-rules.js`.

The game, by the gaming regulator's rules for baccarat (the DICJ's: Despacho 55/2004 and its amendments): eight decks in a shoe (the rules allow six to twelve), shuffled from a seed; the first card turned and as many burned face down as it counts, one of the three ways the rules let a casino burn (the others: as many cards as there are decks, or a set number), with a ten or a court card burning ten here; a cut card fourteen cards from the back (the rules say at least about twelve), after which one more hand is dealt and the shoe is changed (the rules allow that hand or one more). Each hand two cards to the Player (閒) and two to the Banker (莊), Player first, a third card by the tableau (the Player draws on 0–5; the Banker on 0–2 always, on 3–6 by the Player's third card, on 7 never; a natural, 例牌, 8 or 9 on either side, ends it), the total's last digit nearer nine winning, equal totals a tie. After a tie a bet on Player or Banker stays on the table for the next hand, to be left, taken back or moved (the rules' Article 12), and the page says so. New games sit at a no-commission table (免佣), the usual kind on Macau's main floors now, Cotai's above all (Ryan Ho in Inside Asian Gaming, March 2025; Maven of Macau, July 2026); the menu switches to commission (佣金), the high-limit rooms' kind.
