The bets: Player 1 to 1; Banker 0.95 to 1 at a commission table, or 1 to 1 at a no-commission one but half when it wins with six; Tie 8 to 1; and the side bets Sands China's tables carry: Player pair and Banker pair (a hand's first two cards the same rank) 11 to 1; Pairs+ (a pair in either hand 3 to 1, one in each 30 to 1, the same pair in each 300 to 1); Lucky 6 (幸運六點, the Banker winning with six: 12 to 1 on two cards, 20 to 1 on three), Small 6 (two cards only, 22 to 1) and Big 6 (three only, 50 to 1); Lucky 7 (the Player winning with seven: 6 to 1 on two cards, 15 to 1 on three), Small 7 (15 to 1) and Big 7 (30 to 1); Super Lucky 7 (the Player's seven beating the Banker's six: 30 to 1, 40 to 1 with one third card between them, 100 to 1 with two); and Monkey no Monkey (the first four cards: even money with no court card among them, 50 to 1 if all four are). What the house keeps of each, with eight decks: Banker 1.06% (1.46% without commission), Player 1.24%, Tie 14.36%, a pair 10.36%, Small 7 13.05%, Small 6 14.33%, Super Lucky 7 14.83%, Big 6 15.25%, Big 7 15.41%, Monkey 16.50%, Lucky 6 16.68%, Pairs+ 17.14%, Lucky 7 18.30% (`EDGES` in the engine; the figures Wizard of Odds and Ryan Ho publish, which the test works out again exactly). GGRAsia's site checks found Small and Big 6 on every Venetian table in October 2024, and Pairs+ (in place of the two pairs on many tables) and Monkey no Monkey at Sands China's casinos from April 2026. The first version had a Super 6 at 12 to 1, a bet found in no source on Macau's tables; a game saved by it loses any Super 6 bet on the table (`upgrade`). Bets go down in HK$100s, Player or Banker never both, from HK$500 to HK$100,000 on either, up to HK$10,000 on each side bet; you start with HK$50,000.

The page: the strip has what you hold, the table's kind and limits, and the shoe and hand. Under it the scoreboard, as the screen over a Macau table shows it: the big road across the top (a column for each run, a ring for each win, red for the Banker and blue for the Player, a tie a green slash across the ring before it with how many, a pair a dot, a run past six rows turning along the foot as the dragon's tail), the bead plate below on the left (every hand in order, 莊, 閒 or 和 in its colour), and beside it the three derived roads at half size: the big eye boy's rings, the small road's dots and the cockroach road's slashes. Under the screen the shoe's counts, and the two ask buttons (莊問, 閒問) with the mark each derived road would make if the next hand went that way; tap one and the next marks blink on the screen where they'd go, as the table's own buttons show them. The felt: the Player's hand and the Banker's, each with its total in its colour, the third card laid sideways as the dealer lays it; the dealer's call (莊贏, 閒贏, 和) with the pairs and any side bet of yours that came in, and what the hand paid you; and the layout, the side bets above (the Tie first, three to a row) and Player and Banker below, nearest you, as a seat's boxes lie on the felt. Which side bets it carries is the menu's Side bets, so it can match the table in front of you: up to five spots beside the Tie (the pairs and the small and big six or seven come two at a time), one chosen past that pushing out the earliest; by default the Venetian's main floor of 2026, Pairs+, Lucky 6, Small and Big 6 and Monkey no Monkey. Each spot is lettered in Chinese only where the rules or the floor letter it so (閒, 莊, 和, 閒對, 莊對, 幸運6): the Chinese the Venetian prints for the newer bets wasn't found, so they're in English rather than in a guess. The chips on a spot stay there while the hand is dealt (the first version cleared them as Deal was pressed), and after it each spot shows what it won or lost, on a side bet before what it pays. The rail: the chips (HK$100 to HK$10,000), Clear, Rebet (the last hand's bets) and Deal. Deal deals as the dealer does: Player, Banker, Player, Banker, the Player's cards turned then the Banker's, the third cards if the tableau calls for them. On a short screen (under 760 points high, as a phone in Safari) the side bets are tighter, Player and Banker each on one line, the chips and buttons a little smaller and the scoreboard's squares too, so that two rows of side bets leave the cards their room. With Squeeze on (Menu), the cards of the side you're on are yours to squeeze (咪牌), as the biggest bet on a side squeezes them on the floor: each comes up large over the felt, face down, drawn with its pips laid out as a real card's (`baccarat-squeeze.js`); drag in from any edge and the back peels away from that edge as far as your finger goes, the bent strip showing; the two corners with the index stay down, as a squeezer's thumbs keep them, or the first peel would give the card away; let go and it lies flat again, unless it's mostly peeled, when it turns over; Turn it turns it at once. A long side peeled first shows whether there's a column of pips there at all, and how many. Menu: a new shoe, Top up, the course, Baccarat on one page, the table's kind, the side bets, Squeeze. The game is kept as it stands (`baccarat:run`: the shoe, where it's got to, the roads, your bankroll and the bets on the table), the chip, the side bets and Squeeze in `baccarat:opts`, the course in `baccarat:course`. Held sideways the felt takes the left with all the height, and the strip, the scoreboard, the chips and the buttons stand beside it.

Baccarat on one page (the menu, and the course's map): the game, the chart, every bet with what it pays and keeps, the shoe and the squeeze, the screen, the house's rules, and the words of the floor with their Jyutping.

The roads, as `baccarat-engine.js` works them out: the bead plate in order, six to a column; the big road's columns (ties marked on the win before them, the shoe's first ties on its first win), laid out six high with a run that would leave the grid, or meet a mark already there, turning right along its row; each derived road (k = 1, 2, 3 columns back) marks each ring of the big road from where it starts (the second ring of column k, or the top of column k + 1): a ring starting a column red if the two columns before it (the last and the one k further back) are the same length, blue if not; a ring further down red if the column k back reaches its row, blue if it stopped just short of it, red if it stopped two or more short; then laid out in columns by colour, as the big road is. The ask road appends a Banker or a Player win and reports each road's new mark; a road's two answers are always opposite colours.

The course (Learn, in the header; the course sheet the card games share, `course.js`): everything the floor shows and says, from nothing, in four chapters. The game: two hands and nobody's choice, counting a hand (公, the court cards, nothing), naturals (例牌), the Player's third card (補牌), the Banker's (the chart, as a table of D and S, with questions on single cells of it). The bets: Player, Banker and the Tie (and what a tie does to your bet), commission or none, the pairs and Pairs+, the sixes, the sevens, Monkey no Monkey, the limits sign (限紅, with this year's minimums at Sands China's Cotai casinos from GGRAsia's site checks), and what the house keeps on each. The scoreboard: the bead plate, the big road, the dragon's tail, the big eye boy (worked through a big road mark by mark), the small road and the cockroach road, the ask buttons, a whole screen read, and the patterns the floor names (長龍, 單跳, 雙跳) and why they don't move the odds. On the floor: the shoe (牌靴; the cut card, 白咭; the burn, 銷牌, the burned cards drawn; the last hand; the smart tables Sands China has had on every mass-floor baccarat table since January 2025, whose shoe reads the cards and fills the screen); the squeeze (who holds the cards and what they may do, by the rules' Article 16; the pips down a long side, 二邊, 三邊, 四邊, and those in the middle, in stills drawn by the squeeze itself; what to hope for on a Player's third card; the table's shouts, 開, 吹, 頂); money and chips (Hong Kong dollars throughout; the cage); what you'll hear (買定離手, 例牌, 補牌, 莊贏, 閒贏, 和局, 開); and the house's rules (21 and over since 2012, no cameras on the floor and no phone at the table, smoking only in the lounges since 2019 and e-cigarettes not at all, who touches the cards and the chips, dress). Chinese terms come with Cantonese Jyutping the first time. The first version called a natural 天牌; the DICJ's rules say 例牌. Where the course speaks of Macau it rests on the DICJ's rules, GGRAsia's site checks (2023 to 2026), Inside Asian Gaming, Desmond Lam's 2007 study of Macau's baccarat players (the squeeze's words), Macau's Law 10/2012 and its smoking laws. Not found for the Venetian, and so not claimed: its table maximums, its chips' colours, whether its tables take cash and in which currency, its burn and last-hand rules, its screen's exact layout, whether its dealers call 買定離手, its tipping, and the Chinese on its layouts for the newer side bets.

`tests/baccarat.mjs` checks what cards count and a hand's total, the tableau (all eighty cases of the Banker's third card against the chart written out on its own), hands dealt (naturals, the Player's draw, the Banker's by the tableau, ties, pairs by rank), the odds and every bet's edge worked out exactly over every deal from a full eight-deck shoe (each card drawn without replacement, by rank; Banker 45.86%, Player 44.62%, Tie 9.52%) and held to `EDGES`, what each bet pays on hands that make each of its cases, which bets may go down, a tie leaving the main bet on the table, the shoe (every card eight times, the burn, a new game's table, the cut card, the last hand after it, a new shoe keeping the bankroll), the roads on two worked examples (the second, with a dragon of eight and a tie inside a run, worked out independently for the research behind the course), the counts, an old game brought up to date, a game saved and opened again, and twenty shoes of Banker bets losing about the edge. `tests/baccarat-course.mjs` deals every pair of hands a lesson shows through the rules (bar those marked as the first two cards only), works every payout and every road a lesson states, checks every layout's stakes are on spots it has, every count of pips the squeeze lesson gives against the card the squeeze draws (and what a peel from the foot shows), what a first card burns, which third cards win the hand it asks about, and that every choice has its answer among its options.

### Roulette
Roulette as Macau deals it, the Venetian's tables in mind (October 2026): `roulette.html`, `roulette.js` and `roulette.css`, its rules in `roulette-engine.js`, the layout drawn by `roulette-layout.js` and the wheel by `roulette-wheel.js`, the course in `roulette-course.js` on the stage `roulette-stage.js`, and the rules on one page in `roulette-rules.js`. What the casino games share (the strip, the rail of chips and its buttons, the toast, the rules sheet's tables, the course's words of the floor; money written as HK$) is in `casino.js` and `casino.css`, which Baccarat moved onto too.

The game, by the gaming regulator's rules for roulette (the DICJ's Despacho 60/2004, unamended): a wheel of 37 pockets, 1 to 36 red and black by turns and a green zero, the only wheel the rules allow (no 00 anywhere in Macau). The bets and what they pay: straight up (孤丁) 35 to 1, a split (兩門騎綫) 17, a street (三門一列) 11, a corner (四門騎綫) 8, a six line (六門) 5, a dozen (小, 中, 大) or a column (直綫十二門) 2, and the even-money bets (紅黑, 單雙, 小大 for 1–18 and 19–36) 1 to 1. Zero takes every bet but a straight one on itself, the even-money bets too (the rules' Article 8: no half back, no prison), and can only be bet straight up: the rules list no split, trio or first four with it, so the layout takes none. Every bet pays 36 over the numbers it covers less the stake, so every one keeps the house 1 in 37, 2.70%. (The rules also allow a nine-number bet at 3 to 1 and two set twelve-number bets at 2 to 1 that no layout seems to carry; they're named on the rules page, not played.) The table: at least HK$100 on the numbers a spin and HK$500 on each outside bet (a guide to the Venetian gives these; the Londoner next door had HK$200 and HK$500 tables in GGRAsia's February 2024 check), up to HK$5,000 a number (a bet on several takes as many times that), HK$50,000 on a dozen or column and HK$100,000 on an even-money bet (no table's maximums were found published), in HK$50s; you start with HK$20,000.

The page: the strip has what you hold, the table's minimums and the spin. Under it the screen over the table: the last twenty numbers as coloured balls, newest first and ringed, and what the last hundred came to (red, black, zero, odd, even, the three hottest numbers and the three longest away). The layout, as big as the room left allows: upright on a phone (zero at the head, the numbers three to a row, the dozens and the even-money bets down the side of the first column, the columns' 2 to 1 at the foot), lying as the table does when held sideways. A tap on the numbers bets as the mode under the layout says (Number, Split, Street, Corner, Line: the split with the neighbour on the side nearest the tap, the corner nearest, the line with the row on the side nearest); an outside bet or zero takes a tap in any mode. Each chip lands where the dealer would put it (on the line between two numbers, at a corner, at the end of a row) and a toast says what it bet and what it pays. The rail: the chips (HK$50 to HK$5,000), Clear, Undo (the last chip; Rebet, the last spin's bets, when the layout is empty) and Spin. Spin: the wheel comes over the layout (held sideways it stands beside it), the rotor turning one way and the ball the other, slowing until the ball drops into the pocket the engine spun and rides the last of the rotor's turn under the pointer; then the dolly stands on the number, the winning bets light up with their chips, and the call over the layout says the number, what it is and what the spin paid you. Menu: a new table (the screen starts again), Top up, the course, Roulette on one page, quick spins. The game is kept as it stands (`roulette:run`: your bankroll, the bets on the layout, the numbers so far), the chip, the mode and quick spins in `roulette:opts`, the course in `roulette:course`.

The course: the wheel (37 pockets and why the numbers are mixed round it; a spin, the croupier's call and the dolly; a void spin, spun again from the last winning pocket); the bets (on the numbers, outside, zero and what it takes, and why every bet keeps the house the same); at the table (how betting closes, by bell or timer before the spin or at the call 停止投注 during it, and 買定離手; the pay-out, losers swept first and straight up paid last, by the rules' order; the screen, and why a hot number is no likelier; the electronic seats and the automatic wheels). Roulette is a small game in Macau: under half a per cent of the casinos' 2023 gaming revenue, baccarat 85% (the Macau Yearbook). Where the course speaks of Macau it rests on the DICJ's rules, GGRAsia's site checks and the Yearbook; not found, and so not claimed: the Venetian's live roulette tables today and their limits, its screens' exact layout, whether it has call bets or colour chips, and what its croupiers say beyond the rules' call.

`tests/roulette.mjs` checks the wheel (every pocket once, red and black alternating round it), every bet the layout takes (37 straights, 57 splits, 12 streets, 22 corners, 11 six lines, the outside bets; zero only on its own), what each pays and that every one keeps 2.70%, which bets may go down, that a tap where each bet's chip sits places that bet (upright and sideways), a spin from its seed and 37,000 of them landing evenly, and the screen's counts. `tests/roulette-course.mjs` checks every answer and payout a lesson gives, every bet a picture puts on the layout, and the winners it lights.

### Blackjack
Blackjack as Sands China deals it in Macau, the Venetian's tables in mind (October 2026): `blackjack.html`, `blackjack.js` and `blackjack.css`, its rules in `blackjack-engine.js`, basic strategy worked out in `blackjack-strategy.js`, the course in `blackjack-course.js` on the stage `blackjack-stage.js`, and the rules on one page, with the felt's words and the chart, in `blackjack-rules.js`. The strip, the chips, the buttons and the toast are the casino games' (`casino.js`, `casino.css`).

Macau's rules for the game (the DICJ's Despacho 57/2009, which replaced 56/2004 and its 2007 amendment) are a menu each casino picks from with the regulator's approval: the dealer's second card dealt after the players (no hole card) or a hole card checked by hand or a viewer; against the dealer's blackjack only the original bet lost, splits (Article 16) and doubles on any two cards (Article 17) coming back; surrender for half against anything but an ace, before any card is drawn (Article 18, which a casino may drop); insurance against an ace from half the bet to all of it, paid 2 to 1; even money for a blackjack against an ace or a ten; half the bet paid for five cards under 22 against anything but an ace (Article 19); three times the bet for a suited 6, 7, 8 or three 7s (Article 15); and one side bet a box from a list (any pair 11 to 1, the 7s, Over/Under 13, Perfect Pairs, a streak). On the dealer's soft 17 the text and the tables disagree: since 2007 the rules list hitting it, but every account of play has the dealer standing, and the edge published for Sands China's tables only comes out with the dealer standing.

The game here is what the sources found at the Venetian and its Sands China neighbours: six decks through a continuous shuffler (each round's cards go back in, so every round is dealt from all six: Maven of Macau's five or six, the Wizard of Macau's six, and the six its published edge fits); the dealer standing on every 17; double any two cards and after a split; split to four hands, aces once with one card each, and a pair two cards of one rank, as the rules define one (a jack and a queen aren't a pair; tens are never split, so it changes nothing); surrender against a 2 to a 10; blackjack 3 to 2; and against the dealer's blackjack only the original bet lost but for a double on 11, which Sands China's tables, the Venetian, the Parisian, the Londoner and the Plaza, keep whole (Maven of Macau, July 2026: the city's 0.16% becomes 0.24% there). Over/Under 13, the Venetian's side bet: your first two cards with an ace counting one, over or under 13 even money, 13 losing either; one or the other, as the rules allow one side bet a box. Bets HK$300 (the Venetian's minimum in 2026, by Maven of Macau; HK$500 to HK$1,000 in Golden Week 2023 and at Chinese New Year 2024, by GGRAsia's checks) to HK$50,000 (no maximum was found published), in HK$100s, Over/Under 13 HK$100 to HK$5,000 (the game's own); you start with HK$50,000. Insurance is half the bet; even money is offered against an ace only; the five-card and 6-7-8 payouts aren't dealt, nothing showing that Sands China's tables pay them.

Basic strategy isn't copied: `blackjack-strategy.js` works out every play for every hand against every up card, the dealer's chances drawn from the shoe less your two cards and the up card, a dealer's blackjack still to come weighed in as the table's rules have it, and a chart cell the play best over the hands that make its total. For the Venetian's rules it is the six-deck chart that stands on soft 17 and doubles after a split, with 14, 15 and 16 surrendered against a ten, 16 against a 9, 7s against a ten and 8s against a ten (else split), and 11 against a ten hit, not doubled. Played by it the house keeps 0.244% (Maven of Macau: 0.24%), 0.163% where a double on 11 comes back (the Wizard of Macau and Maven of Macau: 0.16%), 0.38% with the dealer hitting soft 17; Over 13 6.55%, Under 13 10.07%, insurance 6.8% or more. An independent calculation for this game (three-card removal, the dealer's hands exact) came to the same figures, to two decimal places.

The page: the strip has what you hold, the table's limits and the shuffler. The felt has the dealer's cards at its head, the rules in two arcs as a felt prints them (squeezed where a theme's lettering runs longer than the arc), your hands below (a split's side by side, the one in play ringed), and while you bet the circle with Over 13 and Under 13 either side of it. A chip is picked on the rail and tapped onto the circle or a side bet; Clear, Rebet (the last round's bets) and Deal. The cards come out one by one as the dealer deals them; your plays replace the chips: Double, Split, Surrender, Hit and Stand, each with the signal the dealer reads, and an ace up asks insurance first (even money with a blackjack). Over/Under 13 is settled under your hands as soon as your second card shows. The round's end says what the dealer made and what you came away with, and the next round's bets wait under it, smaller, already placed, so Deal goes again. The cards are sized to what the felt holds, measured, so a split of four with a third card each still fits. With the coach on (Menu) the chart's play is ringed and a different one noted; held sideways the chart stands beside the felt with the hand's cell picked out, where there's room for it. Menu: Top up, the course, Blackjack on one page, the coach, quick dealing; what the table keeps playing the chart. The game is kept as it stands (`blackjack:run`), the chip and the settings in `blackjack:opts`, the course in `blackjack:course`.

The course, in four chapters: the game (counting a hand, soft and hard; blackjack and 倍半; the dealer's rule, and how often a 6 busts; no hole card, and what the dealer's blackjack takes here and elsewhere); your plays (hit and stand, and a bust lost at once; doubling; splitting; surrender, half back even against a blackjack to come, never against an ace; insurance and even money, and why never); the chart (what it is and what it keeps, hard totals, soft totals, pairs, with a question on a hand each time); and at the table (the shuffler, and why counting has nothing to count; the box, and others' bets on it, 同門搭注, the seated player deciding; the calls and the signals; Over/Under 13; money, Sands China's minimums; the house's rules; the words). Chinese terms are the rules' own, with Cantonese Jyutping. Blackjack is a small game in Macau, about 1% of what the casinos won in 2025 (Statista's figure for the game against the DICJ's total). Where the course speaks of Macau it rests on the DICJ's rules, Maven of Macau's casino pages (2026), the Wizard of Macau (2015), GGRAsia's site checks and the government's 2016 instruction on phones at the tables. Not found, and so not claimed: a rules card from the Venetian itself, its deck count and shuffler, whether its cards come face up, whether it pays the rules' five-card or 6-7-8 bonuses, even money against a ten or insurance beyond half the bet, its maximums, whether a back bettor may refuse to put up a double or a split, and the everyday words at its tables (the course keeps to the rules' words).

`tests/blackjack.mjs` checks what cards count, a hand's total and what's a pair; the dealer's chances from each up card; basic strategy, held to the chart published for a hole-card table (cell for cell, and its edge, 0.40% with six decks, 0.43% with eight), to a table without one where the dealer's blackjack takes everything, to one where it takes the original bet only, to Macau's rules (0.16%) and to the Venetian's (0.24%, 11 against a ten hit); rounds from shoes set up for each case (blackjacks, insurance, doubles, splits, split aces, soft 17, what a dealer's blackjack takes of a double on 10, a double on 11 and a split, surrender against a blackjack to come and none against an ace, Over/Under 13 on 13, on a blackjack and after a split); the edges of Over/Under 13 and insurance worked out over the cards; the advice where the chart's play can't be made (four hands out, surrender gone after a split, a soft 18 in three cards); the shuffler dealing every round from all six decks and a shoe changed only at its cut card; and twenty thousand rounds played by the advice, every one settled into the bankroll. `tests/blackjack-course.mjs` checks every answer, every hand, and every claim a lesson makes of the rules against the engine.

### Sic Bo
Sic Bo (骰寶) as Macau deals it (October 2026): `sicbo.html`, `sicbo.js` and `sicbo.css`, its rules in `sicbo-engine.js`, the layout drawn by `sicbo-layout.js` (the page bets on it and the course shows it), the course in `sicbo-course.js` on the stage `sicbo-stage.js`, and the rules on one page in `sicbo-rules.js`. The die (ivory, the one's pip large and red and the four's red too, as Boast draws it) is in `casino-dice.js` and `casino-dice.css`, new files the dice games after it (Craps) share, so that `casino.js` and `casino.css` stayed as they were; the roll's tumble (`tumble`) is there too, and the strip above the dice leaves room for the newest ball's ring. Sic Bo's tile and its Modern and Kontor logos are in `apps.js`.

The game, by the gaming regulator's rules for it (the DICJ's Despacho 57/2004): three dice under a cup, which a croupier (莊荷) covers, shakes and rings the bell on, and the bets the rules list. Small (小, the total 4 to 10) and Big (大, 11 to 17) pay 1 to 1 and a triple takes both (通吃, Article 7); a single number (三軍) pays 1, 2 or 3 to 1 for one, two or three dice showing it; a total (點數) pays 50 on 4 or 17, 18 on 5 or 16, 14 on 6 or 15, 12 on 7 or 14, 8 on 8 or 13 and 6 on 9 to 12 (no total of 3 or 18 but as a triple); a double (短牌) 8, two numbers (長牌) 5, a named triple (圍骰) 150, any triple (全骰) 24. Nothing is rounded: the house's edge on every bet is worked out over all 216 ways the dice fall, and every bet keeps its own: 2.78% on Small and Big (6 triples in 216), 7.87% on a single number, 9.72% on a 7 or 14, 12.5% on a 10 or 11, 16.67% on two numbers, 18.98% on a 9 or 12, 29.17% on a 4 or 17, 30.09% on a named triple, 30.56% on any triple, 33.33% on a double and 47.22% on a 5 or 16. That is the point of the game, against roulette's one 2.70% for every bet. For the totals on 5 or 16 and 6 or 15 the rules give a range (18 to 30 and 14 to 18 to 1); this table pays the least of each, and the rules page says what the most would keep (13.89% and 12.04%). The rules list three bets more that no layout seems to carry, a double with a different single (50 to 1), three numbers (30) and any three of four (7), and 單 and 雙, an odd or even total at 1 to 1; since Article 7 takes only Big and Small on a triple, 單 and 雙 as written would be a fair coin (108 throws in 216 are odd), so none of these is played, and the rules page names them and works out what each keeps. A round the croupier finds with a die resting on another is void (無效) and thrown again at once (Article 3). The table: at least HK$100 on each bet, in HK$50s; the most HK$100,000 on Small or Big, HK$50,000 on a single number, HK$20,000 on two numbers, HK$10,000 on a double or a total, HK$5,000 on any triple and HK$2,000 on a named triple. The rules set no limits and no table's were found, so these are the game's own (a stake whose win stays a good deal under the most a table could pay out). You start with HK$20,000.

The page: the strip has what you hold, the table's minimum and the round. Under it the screen over the table: the last twenty totals as coloured balls, newest first and the latest ringed (Big red, Small blue, a triple green), and what the last hundred came to (Big, Small, triples, the three hottest totals and the three coldest). Under that the tray with the three dice and the croupier's call: the total, Big, Small or the triple, and what the round paid you. The layout, as big as the room left allows, in the order a Macau table lays it out: Small, Any triple and Big across the head; the six triples and six doubles; the totals, 4 to 10 under Small and 11 to 17 under Big; the fifteen pairs of numbers; the six single numbers at the foot, nearest the player. Each cell shows its dice and what it pays; a tap puts the chip on it, with its amount at the cell's corner and a toast saying what it bet and what it pays. On a short phone the layout scrolls inside its own box rather than shrinking a cell below a thumb. Held sideways the layout takes the left of the screen and the screen, the tray, the chips and the buttons the right. The rail: the chips (HK$50 to HK$5,000), Clear, Undo (the last chip; Rebet, the last round's bets, when the layout is empty) and Roll. Roll: the dice tumble in the tray and land one after another, then the cells they win are ringed and every bet's corner says what it won or lost. Menu: a new table (the screen starts again), Top up, the course, Sic Bo on one page, quick rolls. The game is kept as it stands (`sicbo:run`: your bankroll, the bets on the layout, the rounds so far), the chip and quick rolls in `sicbo:opts`, the course in `sicbo:course`. The screen is dark by day and by night, as an electronic one is; the lettering of Big, Small and triples is lighter at night.

The course: the dice (three dice and the 216 ways they fall, with the totals as a chart; the cup, the bell, the call to open the dice and the void round); the bets (Big and Small and why a triple takes both; single numbers; totals; doubles and pairs; triples; the rules' others); what the house keeps (each bet its own edge from least to most, what 100 rounds of HK$100 on Big cost, what the same bet keeps at the top of the rules' range; the screen and why a run on Big is no likelier to go on); on the floor (screens and machines, and the words, Cantonese with Jyutping). Where it speaks of Macau it rests on the DICJ's rules and on Inside Asian Gaming's May 2011 report that Sands China's Rapid Sic Bo, live croupier and electronic bets, started a row because electronic sic bo could pay up to 190 to 1 on any triple while a live table's most is 150, and that the regulator moved every automated table onto live pay-outs. Not found, and so not claimed: any Macau table's limits, the layout's printed Chinese beyond the rules' own words, and what the Venetian's tables pay on the totals within the rules' ranges.

`tests/sicbo.mjs` checks the dice (216 ways, the totals' ways, a 3 and an 18 only as triples), every bet the layout carries and the rules' others, what each pays by the regulation's articles, that a triple takes Big and Small and nothing else, every bet's edge worked out by hand, the ranges, which bets may go down, a round from its seed and 21,600 of them landing as three fair dice do, and the screen's counts. `tests/sicbo-course.mjs` checks every answer, payout, edge and count a lesson states against the engine, every bet a picture puts on the layout, and every throw it shows.

### Craps

Craps (花旗骰) as Macau deals it: `craps.html`, `craps.js` and `craps.css`, its rules in `craps-engine.js`, the layout drawn by `craps-layout.js` (the page bets on it and the course shows it), the course in `craps-course.js` on the stage `craps-stage.js`, and the rules on one page in `craps-rules.js`. The two dice and the way they tumble (`dieSvg`, `tumble`) are Sic Bo's, in `casino-dice.js` and `casino-dice.css`; `tumble` moved there from `sicbo.js` so that both games roll the same way. Craps' tile and its Modern and Kontor logos are in `apps.js`.

The game, by the gaming regulator's rules for it (the DICJ's Despacho 53/2010): the shooter (主客) throws two dice to the far wall of the table and they must roll, or the throw is void (無效, Article 3) and thrown again; here you are always the shooter. A round starts with the come-out roll (出場擲骰): a 7 or 11 wins the pass line (派司拉) and a 2, 3 or 12 loses it; any other total is the point, and the shooter throws on until the point comes again (the pass line wins) or a 7 does (a seven out: it loses). Don't pass (東派司) is the other side, and a 12 on the come-out roll is a push (和局, "bar 12"). Come (襟) and don't come (東襟) are the same bets begun on any later roll: the bet goes to the number thrown and waits there. Behind a line or come bet there is the additional bet (附加投注), the odds, which pays what the number is truly worth against a 7: 2 to 1 on a 4 or 10, 3 to 2 on a 5 or 9, 6 to 5 on a 6 or 8, and behind don't pass the lay at 1 to 2, 2 to 3 and 5 to 6. The rules list the rest by Article 6: the field (1 to 1; the rules let a table pay 3 to 1 on a 12, and this one pays 2 to 1, as it does on a 2); Big (8 to 12) and Small (2 to 6) at 1 to 1; the hard ways (a pair before a 7 or the number made another way) at 9 to 1 on 6 and 8 and 7 to 1 on 4 and 10; the Big 6 and 8 at 7 to 6, 5 and 9 at 7 to 5, 4 and 10 at 9 to 5; and the one-roll bets: Any seven 4 to 1, Any craps 7 to 1, Eleven and Ace-deuce 15 to 1, Aces and Boxcars 30 to 1, the Horn (a 2, 3, 11 or 12 in one bet) 27 to 4 on a 2 or 12 and 3 to 1 on a 3 or 11. Nothing is rounded: the house's edge on every bet is worked out exactly, over the 36 ways the dice fall and, for a bet that waits, every way it can end, and a play-out through the rules agrees with each. Pass keeps 1.41% (7 in 495), don't pass 1.36% (3 in 220), the odds and lays nothing (the only bets that keep nothing: the number's ways against the 7's six are exactly what they pay), the Big 6 and 8 1.52%, the Big 5 and 9 4%, the field 5.56% (2.78% were a 12 to pay 3 to 1), the Big 4 and 10 6.67%, a hard 6 or 8 9.09%, a hard 4 or 10 11.11%, Any craps, Eleven and Ace-deuce 11.11%, the Horn 12.5%, Aces and Boxcars 13.89%, Any seven and Big or Small 16.67%. That is the lesson of the table: the pass line and the odds behind it are the cheap bets, and the bets that pay big and look generous are the expensive ones. The odds don't change what the line keeps, but they put money on a bet that keeps nothing, so what the house keeps of all you stake falls with them: 1.41% with none, 0.85% with 1 times the line behind it, 0.61% with 2, 0.47% with 3, 0.33% with 5 and 0.18% with 10 (behind don't pass, 0.68% and 0.23% with 1 and 5 times).

Where the rules are silent this table chooses, and the rules page says so. Every bet works on every roll, the come-out roll too. A bet that is decided comes down, win or lose, and Rebet puts back what came down; a bet that isn't (a line bet at its point, a come bet on its number, a hard way, a Big bet, the odds) stays up for the next roll, so a round of several rolls is played on the same table. The odds, the hard ways and the Big bets may be taken down at any time (Clear becomes Take down when nothing has been put down since the roll); a line or come bet may not once the dice are thrown. Pass and don't pass go down on the come-out roll, come and don't come and the odds once there is a point (Article 5). The odds go up to 5 times the bet behind them (a lay, to win 5 times it); the rules name no limit. Stakes go in HK$50s, at least HK$100, and in the steps that keep every payout in whole dollars: the Horn in HK$100s, a Big 6 or 8 in HK$300s, a lay in HK$100s, HK$150s or HK$300s on 4 or 10, 5 or 9, 6 or 8. The most is HK$100,000 on a line or come bet, HK$50,000 on the field, Small or Big, HK$30,000 on a Big number, HK$5,000 on a hard way, Any seven or Any craps, HK$4,000 on the Horn, HK$2,000 on Eleven or Ace-deuce and HK$1,000 on Aces or Boxcars. The rules set no limits and no table's were found, so these are the game's own. The rules list no place, buy or lay bets (apart from the lay behind don't pass) and no C and E, so none is played. You start with HK$20,000.

The page: the strip has what you hold, "Come-out roll" or "Point 6", and the roll. Under it the screen over the table: the last twenty totals as balls, newest first and the latest ringed (a 7 in red, which ends a point), and what the last hundred came to (sevens as a share, the hottest and the coldest totals). Under that the tray with the two dice and the stickman's call: the total with Natural, Craps, Point 6, Point made, Seven out or Hard way, and what the roll paid you. The layout, as big as the room left allows, in the order a table lays it out: the pass line and don't pass with the odds and the lay under them; come and don't come; the six numbers, each a box with the come and don't come halves where a come bet travels (a tap on a half puts odds behind it) and the puck, ON, on the point; the field; Small and Big; the Big 4 to 10; the hard ways; the one-roll bets at the foot. A cell the table won't take a chip on now (the pass line once there is a point, the odds before one) is dimmed, and a tap says why. A tap puts the chip on a cell, its amount at the cell's corner and a toast saying what it bet and what it pays. The layout is longer than a phone has room for, so it scrolls inside its own box (the page doesn't) on a short screen; on a 390×844 phone it fits. Held sideways the layout takes the left of the screen and the screen, the tray, the chips and the buttons the right. The rail: the chips (HK$50 to HK$5,000), Clear or Take down, Undo (the last chip; Rebet, the bets of the last roll that can go down again, when none is put down) and Roll. Roll: the dice tumble in the tray and land, the cells they win are ringed, and every bet decided shows what it won or lost at its corner; a come bet that travels says to which number. Menu: a new table (the screen starts again, the puck comes off), Top up, the course, Craps on one page, quick rolls. The game is kept as it stands (`craps:run`: your bankroll, the point, the bets on the table, what was put down since the last roll, the rolls so far), the chip and quick rolls in `craps:opts`, the course in `craps:course`. The screen is dark by day and by night, as an electronic one is.

The course: the dice (two dice and the 36 ways they fall, with the totals as a chart; the throw and the void throw); the line (the come-out roll and the point; don't pass and the bar 12; the odds, at what they pay, why they keep nothing, and what they do to what the house keeps of all you stake, with the 5 times this table takes; come and don't come); the traps (the one-roll bets and the Horn; the field and its 20 losing throws of 36; the hard ways and the Big bets; every bet ranked from least to most, and what 100 rolls of HK$100 on Any seven cost against 100 rounds of the pass line); on the floor (the words, Cantonese with Jyutping). Where it speaks of Macau it rests on the DICJ's rules. Not found, and so not claimed: any Macau table's limits, whether a table works its bets on the come-out roll (the rules are silent, so this one does), the printed Chinese on a Macau layout beyond the rules' own words, and the most odds a Macau table lets you take.

`tests/craps.mjs` checks the dice (36 ways), every bet the layout carries and none it doesn't, what each pays by Article 6, the lines and the point, a come bet travelling and a 7 taking every one, the odds and lays, every bet's edge worked out by formula and by playing it out through the rules from every state, that every stake in its step wins whole dollars on every throw, the limits, which bets may come down, a roll from its seed and 12,000 of them landing as two fair dice do, 1,000 rolls of random play never holding more than you do, and the call and the screen's counts. `tests/craps-course.mjs` checks every answer, payout, edge, count and odds figure a lesson states against the engine, every bet a picture puts on the layout and in a group the picture draws, the point after each throw it shows, and every word of the floor against the rules page.

### Texas Hold'em

Texas Hold'em (德州撲克) as Macau's regulation has it (October 2026), at a table of up to six: `holdem.html`, `holdem.js` and `holdem.css`; the rules in `holdem-rules.js` (a seats.js game: `deal`, `act`, `wake`, `over`), the hands' evaluator and the chances in `holdem-eval.js`, the computer players in `holdem-bot.js`, the rules on one page in `holdem-sheet.js`, and the course in `holdem-course.js` on the stage `holdem-stage.js`. Hold'em is the one casino game here where a decision changes the odds, so it is played against people (or the computer players) for chips, not against the house; the house only takes a commission. Its tile and logos are in `apps.js`, its entry in `rooms.js` (`GAMES`) and its place at a party's table in `party-core.js` (`ELSEWHERE`).

The game, by the regulation (Despacho 11/2008; the article numbers are its): one pack of 52, two hole cards face down for each player, five community cards face up in three stages, the flop (3), the turn (1) and the river (1), and a round of betting before the flop and after each stage. The button (指示器, Article 1) moves one seat clockwise every hand; the player left of it posts the small blind (小盲注) and the next the big blind (大盲注, Article 5); before the flop the player left of the big blind acts first and the big blind last (Article 6), and from the flop the first player left of the button acts first and the button last (Article 7). A pack is dealt from the small blind a card at a time with a card burned before each stage. A move is fold (蓋牌, Article 13), check, call or raise, a raise being at least the last bet or raise (Article 12), and all in (全押) a bet of every chip you have; there is no limit on a bet but your chips (Article 11). At the showdown the best five of a player's seven cards win (Article 8): suits never rank (Article 14), the other cards count as the regulation has it (Article 15: one kicker for four of a kind, two for three of a kind, one for two pair, three for a pair), equal hands split the pot and the odd chips go to the first of them left of the button (Article 10). The house takes 3 to 5% of the pot, never more than five big blinds (Article 16).

What this table decides, because the regulation does not say (the rules page says so too): the blinds are HK$50 and HK$100 and everyone starts with HK$10,000, a hundred big blinds; a game is 10, 20 or 40 hands (the menu), and a player who goes broke buys in again for the same, so the result is chips less every buy-in and the most ahead wins. Heads-up the button is the small blind, acting first before the flop and last after it, as casinos play it. A player all in can win only what the others matched: the pot is split in layers (the main pot and side pots) and the chips nobody matched go back to their owner and are neither contested nor raked (a last layer of its own, `back`); the odd chip of a layer goes to the first winner left of the button. An all-in bet short of a full raise can be called but does not let players who had already acted raise again. The commission is 5%, 3% or none (the menu; 5% by default), taken from the contested chips only, never more than five big blinds (HK$500) and rounded down to the dollar. After the last bet, when everyone left is all in, the board is turned up a stage at a time (the flop, then the turn, then the river, a beat each) with each hand's chance of winning beside it, counted over the boards still to come, and then the hands are shown with the five cards that win lit. The regulation's terms for check and call (過牌, 跟注) were not found in it, and neither were side pots, a numeric limit on a bet, the heads-up blinds, the short all-in rule or buying in again, so none of those is claimed to be its.

The page: the strip has the hand, the pot and the street. The other seats sit round the top and flank yours at the foot, in order clockwise from you, each with its name, chips, the bet this round (a chip beside the stack), the button, SB, BB or REBUY, its two cards face down (face up at a showdown) and what it last did; in the middle the pot, the five places of the board and what the narrator says (the same words are spoken, unless the voice is off). Under the table your cards, with the hand they make once there is a flop (or "Pair of kings", "Ace-king suited" before it) and whose turn it is; the size of a raise (Min, ½ pot, ¾ pot, Pot, All in, and a slider that is finer for small raises and snaps to HK$50); and the buttons, Fold (off when there is nothing to call, because a free fold is never right), Check or Call and Raise to. The menu's Odds line shows your chance against random hands for everyone still in and what the call needs. Between your cards and the size sits the coach's box (below), three lines high whatever it says, so nothing moves when it speaks. Held sideways the table is on the left and your cards, the size and the buttons beside it. A game played at a table of friends is a party's (the games screen's Invite players opens Party with Hold'em chosen) and computer players fill every seat nobody takes; alone, the menu sets how many seats (2 to 6, five by default) and how strong the computer players are.

The computer players (Easy, Medium and Hard; Hard by default) use only what their seat may see, the same as you: their cards and the board turned up so far. Before the flop they count the hand as Bill Chen did (the higher card A 10, K 8, Q 7, J 6, otherwise half its number; a pair double, five at least; 2 more if suited; less 1, 2, 4 or 5 for a gap of one, two, three or more; 1 more if the cards are close and both below a queen; rounded up) and open the betting with a hand worth 9 or more from an early seat, 6 or more from the button and between them in between, with fewer than fifteen big blinds going all in or out. After the flop they work out their chance of winning against hands dealt at random by drawing boards, trim it when the bet against them is large (whoever bets holds more than a random hand), and call when it beats the price of the call (the pot odds), raise a strong hand and, the harder levels, bluff and slow-play now and then. Easy plays looser, bluffs never and calls too much. In the tests Hard beats Easy and Medium beats Easy over 24 games of 20 hands, and heads-up Hard is no worse than Medium.

The coach (Menu → Coach, October 2026) speaks in one of four ways, as Big Two's, Hong's, Guan Dan's and Schafkopf's do. Hints, the default: nothing unless you ask; Hint, in the coach's box, gives its move for the decision you are at ("Raise to 200 · everyone folds 54% of the time · worth +HK$120 on average"; facing a raise, "Call 250 · about 73% to win, a call needs 36% · worth +HK$1,650 on average"), with Why? after it: every move it weighed, its own first, each with the chips it comes to beyond folding (and ±, the standard error: how far an average over that many deals may stray by chance), how often it wins the pot and how often everyone folds. Check: Hint too, and after each move of yours it would not have made, its word in oxblood so it isn't taken for advice: how many chips worse it reckons yours and what tells most against it ("Your fold: about HK$9,300 worse than all in for 9,900. You win about 100% against the hands that bet; the call needs 43%."; or that a smaller raise takes the pot as often for less, that a bigger one risks more than its extra folds are worth, that the hands that stay in beat yours more often than the folds pay). Always: the same, and at each of your decisions its move without your asking, ringed on the button that plays it, the raise slider set to its size. Off: nothing, and no Hint. A move counts as agreeing when it is the coach's or as good as near as its deals can tell: no more than a big blind (HK$100) worse, and not two standard errors clear of it, so a close decision is never called a mistake. The word stays until a move of yours it agrees with; a fold when there is nothing to call is not offered, because it is never right. The box keeps three lines' room whatever is said, a longer word is cut there, and a tap on the line opens Why? all the same. Under Check and Always a hand ends with how often the coach agreed with your moves, and Where it differed lists the three that cost most. A partner's frame, watched from the games screen, coaches nobody; at a table with friends each phone's coach is its own, and it sees only what its seat does.

How it weighs a decision (`holdem-think.js`; the words are `holdem-coach.js`'s, the work runs in `holdem-worker.js` so the table never stops, with 80 deals on the page's own thread where a browser has no worker). Hold'em has hidden cards, so the coach cannot search a tree of moves; it samples. It plays every move the seat could make (fold, call or check, each size of the raise buttons) out to the end of the hand, in the same deals of the cards it cannot see, and sets them side by side by the chips each comes to beyond folding, the commission taken. A deal is the others' two cards and the rest of the board, drawn from the pack less what the seat knows; everyone plays on by Medium's rules, and the seat too after its first move, but more steadily than Medium (it believes a bet a little more, raises only with a monster and never bluffs: Medium's own habit of bluff-raising and stacking off made every call look about 3,000 chips worse than it is). Every move is played in the same deals, so the moves are set against each other on the same footing, and a computer player's chances are counted once for a deal and shared by every move tried in it. A move goes once it is clearly behind the best (three and a half standard errors in the first hundred deals, two and a half after, and a twentieth of a big blind), so a decision takes about half a second on this machine, four seconds at most when it is close and all 320 deals are played; a backstop of six seconds (seven for a check) is for a slow phone. The seed comes from the position, so the same spot gives the same advice on any phone, and a Hint, Always's move and a check all agree. It knows only what the seat may: `known()` strips the others' cards and the board still to come before it starts, and `tests/holdem-coach.mjs` checks that changing either changes nothing.

What a seat has done says what it holds. The hand's `log` (`holdem-rules.js` keeps it in the state, and it survives the database's dropping of empty lists: every check, call and raise, with the pot, the bet to meet and the street) gives each other seat a weight for every two cards it could hold: a hand that bet is drawn from the stronger end of what it could be, the more so the bigger the bet; one that called from the middle upward (the price sets how far up from), without the very top, which would have raised; one that checked from the weaker end. The weights multiply over the hand, so a seat that raised before the flop and bet the flop is drawn from the top of the top. A hand is ranked among all that were possible on the board of that moment (before the flop by Chen's count, after it by the made hand with a bonus for a draw). The weights were set against what Medium's players really held when they acted, and the test keeps them there: for each kind of action the average rank of the hands that took it is within a tenth of the real one. This is a model of the computer players, not of people: the coach finds the computer players' habits (they fold to a re-raise; they pay a small bet with too little), which is the point at this table and nothing to rely on at another. One change to the computer players came with it: a seat facing an all-in it cannot raise now calls with a hand worth 12 or more by Chen's count before the flop (they used to fold aces to a 100-big-blind shove, so the coach found that shoving any hand was best), and `botMove` takes `samples`, `equity` and `tune` so the coach can play the others faster and itself more steadily.

How well it plays, measured (a scratch run, not kept in the repository): the coach in one seat of four against three Hard players, 1,600 hands from fixed decks, the same decks and seat for a Hard player and a Medium one in its place. The coach made 202 chips a hand (two big blinds), Hard −3 and Medium 13: ahead of Hard by 205 ± 46 chips a hand (162 over the first 800 hands, 248 over the second; ± is the standard error, how far an average over that many hands may stray by chance) and ahead of Medium by 189 ± 43. It ends 77% of its hands with chips won, Hard 25%: most of the gain is the computer players folding to a raise more often than a person would, which the coach has learned from their rules. So the figure says it reads these players well, not that it would beat people.

The course (Learn, in the header, and the menu's "Learn Hold'em, from the rules"): the hand (two cards and a board, with the best five tapped out of seven and a board that plays; the ten hands from the royal flush down and who wins between a flush and a straight, a full house and a flush, and the wheel and a six-high straight; kickers and ties); the betting (the button and the blinds, with the table seen from above and who acts first, first and last after the flop, and heads-up; fold, check, call and raise, and the least a raise may be; all in and side pots, with the layers, the most a player can win and a short all-in; the house's commission and its cap); counting (equity, a hand's share of the pot over every board still to come, for aces against kings, ace-king suited against queens, a small pair against two high cards and a dominated hand; outs, counted for a flush draw, an open-ended and an inside straight draw and two overcards, the quick rule of 4 and 2 against the exact chance); the price of a call (pot odds, and calling when the equity is more than the price); and which two cards (the Chen count, position and why the button opens wider). The last step is ten hands against Easy computer players, which sets those two choices (kept in the menu) and starts the game. The stage keeps back what a question asks for until it is answered. Where it speaks of Macau it rests on the regulation, as above.

`tests/holdem-eval.mjs` checks the evaluator (every hand's rank, the wheel, the best five of seven, ties, the equity counted over every board against known figures, and vs random). `tests/holdem.mjs` checks the deal (no card twice, the blinds posted, the first to act, the button moving), moves and why each is refused, raises (the least, the counter, a short all-in not reopening the betting), the board in stages, side pots and the chips going back, the showdown and split pots with the odd chips, the commission (3 to 5%, five big blinds, contested chips only), a game of so many hands with rebuys and chips adding up, the record through the database at every step with nulls and empty lists dropped, the computer players (always legal, never using hidden cards, the Chen count) and their strength against each other. `tests/holdem-course.mjs` checks every lesson's id, every choice's answer against its options, every card a real card seen once, every hand, winner, equity (counted over every board still to come, 1,712,304 of them before the flop), out and chance of hitting counted over the cards still to come, price, pot layer, commission, least raise, short all-in, blind and order of play against the engine, the Hard players' opening hands, the Chen counts, and every Cantonese word against the rules page. `tests/holdem-coach.mjs` checks the log (kept, and through the database's round trip), the moves offered (a short stack's all-in among them), that the others' cards and the board to come change nothing, that the advice is the same every time and always legal, that a move of yours is weighed on the same deals as the coach's, spots with one right answer (a royal flush never folds, jack high against a seat that raised and bet every street folds, aces under the gun raise, seven-deuce against an all-in folds), the words and the verdict, that every weight rises with the hand, the weights against what Medium's players really held, and a fuzz over every street. State is kept under `holdem:` (`holdem:opts`, with the coach's mode, `holdem:record`, `holdem:best`, the best result of a game, and `holdem:course`).

## Rooms
Two players link once, on the games screen, and stay linked: the partner chip there makes a four-letter code to give your partner, or takes theirs. The link is a room that is yours for good (crates/rooms/CODE), and it belongs to the player, not the device: the code and your player id sync with your solo code, so your phone and laptop both know your partner. Nothing about playing together is set up inside a game.

Every page you open tells the room what you're doing (live/PLAYER/DEVICE: the game, solo or together, online while the page is), so your partner always sees it: on the games screen's partner chip ("Sarah · Slate, solo"), and in a small line in every game's title (green while they're online). That line gives way to the header's buttons: where a game's title leaves little room (Cartel, with Rules beside the menu), it shortens to "…" rather than push the menu off the screen, and the games screen has it in full. From the partner sheet either of you can ask the other to play a game together, theirs or yours, among the games that have a duo match (Crates, Slate, Chart, Delta, Punt, Quote, Spot, Stow, Origin, Order, Manifest, Hong, Tribute, River, Gambit, and the head-to-heads on one deal: Gambit's puzzle rush, Brut, Blend, Arb, Survey, Pipes, Refinery): a banner appears over whatever the other is doing, "Korbi wants to play Slate together", Play or Not now, for the two minutes a request stands. It sits in the topmost open dialog when there is one, since a modal dialog (the games screen itself, a menu) leaves everything outside it out of sight and out of reach: on the page behind, the banner never showed to a partner on the games screen. Every answer is a transaction on the request (Play, Not now, or the asking device at two minutes), so only the first lands and the two of you can't part on it: a Play after the asker was told "No answer" says the request lapsed. Play takes both of you into the duo match, the game opened in the room at a new table (?room=CODE&new=, the request's time): whichever of you arrives first deals it over whatever was left there, and the other sits down at it (every game on `together.js`, and Slate, which keeps a room of its own; Crates, which keeps one too, carries on with the match in the room). The device that asked goes as soon as the yes comes in, from whatever page it's on by then (the page that asked may have closed, or the phone slept through the answer), once, and within ten minutes. Each solo game stays exactly where it was (Hong, Tribute and Stow don't deal or play on yours while you sit down); Back to solo (in the game's menu or the partner sheet) returns to it, and Join on the partner sheet sits you down again at the table your partner is at. The games' menus no longer start, join or share rooms: they point to the games screen. Tiles on the games screen always open a game solo. On `together.js` a seat belongs to a player, not a device: it keeps the player id of whoever sits in it, so a player who comes in on another device (phone to iPad, or the app on the home screen and the same site in Safari, which keep separate sign-ins) gets their own seat back, moves and all, without being asked, and the device they left goes back to solo; before, that device took the free seat, the one kept for the partner, or was asked which of the two players to be. Only when both seats belong to others (a device without your solo code has a player id of its own) do you choose which player to carry on as. Your partner's line follows you as you sit down at a table or leave one, not only when a page opens, and a page of yours left open elsewhere is never taken for your partner. A new partner code is a room from its first request: until October 2026 a room opened only if a game had made it (`createRoom`), so a pair linked with a new code got "No game with that code" on every match. `tests/rooms.mjs` checks the seating (`sitDown` in `rooms.js`, the joining transaction of every game on `together.js`: the first deals, the second sits across, your own seat on any device, a new table for each request and none without one, a third player changing nothing unless they chose a seat) and that a pair's room opens. A run with two players and a second device of one of them, separate browsers on one stand-in database, went through Tribute (the same table, the same side, the iPad taking its player's seat, Join, a new table for the next request), Hong (the asking device following from another game), Stow and Delta, and lapsed requests. You can watch your partner's solo game: the partner sheet offers Watch while they play solo. The game you have open is mirrored to the pair's room as you play (every key of its own: its run, its settings), at most every 1.5 seconds while you keep moving. Your partner opens the same game with ?watch: that page doesn't run the game itself (it holds it) but shows it in a frame, the same page with &frame, where every read of that game's keys returns your copy and every write goes nowhere, so their own solo state is never touched. When you move, a new frame loads behind the one on show and takes its place once drawn, at most every 1.2 seconds, so the view changes in place instead of reloading in front of them (the first version reloaded the whole page on every save, which in a game that saves every second meant a page forever reloading). The frames take no taps and connect to nothing; a bar says who you're watching, with Stop. It needs no code in a game, only that the game keeps what's on its screen in storage under its own prefixes (`PREFIXES` in `suite.js`: the game's id, but Slate's glyph:, its old name, and Blend's blend: and blend2:); Crates' run link and the suite's keys never go across. Where a game kept part of its screen only in memory, the frame drew a screen of its own: a scan of every game in October 2026 found Calibre's design, the chess rush's position, Parley's card and Pipes' board kept that way, and Blend's save under a prefix that wasn't copied. Each now keeps what it shows as it goes (see their sections), which also brings your own work back after a reload. Still not shown: Spot's targets, a Deck review under way, and a Harbour run (the plan shows, not the hours it has run). A frame never speaks (Parley says each card aloud, and every swap would say it again), and never ends or restarts a run because it's hidden. The swap was tested on Pipes with ten saves in five seconds: four swaps, the page never reloaded, the view ended on the latest save, and the watcher's own state stayed as it was.

What the database refuses (October 2026). Firebase refuses a whole write that has undefined anywhere in it (or NaN, or a key with . # $ / [ ]), and Tribute's every first move together failed so while the two-tab tests passed: their stand-in for the database (`tests/fake-sync.js`) quietly dropped undefined, and closed up lists where the database leaves gaps. It now refuses what Firebase refuses and keeps lists as Firebase does, and `tests/rtdb.js` does the same for the node tests (its rules are `memdb.js`'s, which an offline party's host keeps its database by), through which Tribute and Hong play whole matches move by move. `together.js` drops anything undefined from a state before saving it, with a warning in the console, so a slip of that kind costs a field, not every move. Joining is quicker on a slow connection (from Shanghai to the database in Belgium, each round trip is a large part of a second): a game reads what it needs of the room, its own branch, the request and the room's mark (`openBranch` in `rooms.js`), not the whole room, which holds every game's branch and what each of you has open mirrored for watching, and which it opened as a transaction, two round trips at least and run again for every write anywhere in the room meanwhile (each page saying where it is); the joining transaction runs with the branch already held by the page, one round trip instead of two; and the database's address, found once, is kept on the device (`suite:db`) rather than asked for on every connection. A two-page run on the strict stand-in went from Korbi's request on the games screen, through Pookie's banner ("Korbi wants to play Tribute together") and Play, to one table, with either of them arriving first: each named right on both screens, two whole hands of Tribute (tribute and its return included) and forty moves of Hong played, the computer players moved by one device only.

Joining fast, and on the same question from the first moment (October 2026). Korbi found joining a party or a duo match slow, often several seconds and never the same twice, and partners in Chart and Punt on different questions for the first ten seconds or so. From Asia each round trip to the database in Belgium is about a quarter of a second, and a page makes several before it's seated: the connection (its socket, Firebase's handshake, the sign-in's token), reading the room, sitting down. What made it slow and uneven was Firebase's fallback. It marks every socket it opens as failed until the server has answered twice, and a connection that starts while the mark is there begins on long polling (each message a plain request, seconds at a time from Asia), moving to a socket only once that's up; a page left before its socket had proved itself (the phone moving on to the game the party chose, a glance at another app, the camera to scan a code) left the mark for the next page. Now `sync.js` clears the mark as it connects (a network where sockets really fail costs one try before Firebase falls back by itself) and opens the connection at once, while the sign-in is read back, rather than when the first read asks for it; and a page opened to play with others (`?room=`, `?table=` or `?party=` in its address) starts connecting in its first moment, from `theme.js`, the first script every page runs, while the rest of the page loads. A page back from less than twenty seconds away (another app, the camera) keeps a connection that's still up, where it used to drop it and open a new one every time (several round trips, and the others seeing it away meanwhile); after longer, or offline, it reconnects as before, since Safari may have let the socket die while the page slept. Every game with a duo match now shows the rete, Joining your partner (Joining the table when a party sent you there: `joiningWords` in `rooms.js`), until the room's state is drawn, never your own game meanwhile, and goes to your own game only if the join doesn't come off: Chart, Punt, Quote, Spot, Delta, Slate and Crates drew your own game first, so each partner was on a question of their own until the room's arrived, and a tap meanwhile went to the solo game (Hong, Tribute, Stow, River and Gambit already held it back). Crates and Slate, which keep rooms of their own, now read only their part of the room to join, as the games on `together.js` do, with it held while joining so the transaction takes one round trip. A run of every duo game on a stand-in database answering each request after 700 ms showed the rete with no solo game under it, both partners on the same question once in, the name asked for over the rete, and your own game after a room that isn't there.

Three scores show on each tile: your solo best, your partner's (by initial), and your duo record: for a team game the pair's best result together (Punt's pot, Delta's fastest board, as a time), for a head-to-head one the wins each way ("Duo 2–1" on yours, "1–2" on theirs; a draw counts for neither). Each player's device reports a finished duo match from its own side (duo/GAME/MATCH/PLAYER: score, won, team or not), so a match counts once and both sides agree on the winner; games on `together.js` say what a match came to through its `result` option (Chart and Quote head to head; Delta, Stow, Order and Origin as a team; Punt and Manifest either, by the table's mode); Crates, Slate and Spot report from their own match code: Crates a board cleared together or not ("Duo 5/7", cleared of played), Slate a board solved together and its time (the fastest shows), Spot the pair's score in co-op and the winner in Versus. A game played both ways shows both on its tile, the wins and then the team's best ("Duo 2–1 · team 1,240"; `duo-records.js`, checked by `tests/duo-records.mjs`): until October 2026 a tile showed one or the other, and a game's first team match would have hidden every head-to-head. A team's best is in the game's own terms: a time for Slate and Delta, pieces for Stow (it showed as a time, 0:11 for eleven), and the pot for Punt (whose solo best is a return a question, so a pot of 1,240 showed as +124000%). `suite.js` holds the pair, the presence, the requests and the records; `rooms.js` and `together.js` the rooms themselves. A party of two plays a duo match in the party's room the same way (README, Party): its host writes the request, already said yes to, and both phones go; that match isn't the pair's, so it isn't recorded for them (`reportDuo` records only a match in the pair's own room). Tribute and Hong seat a party of three or four the same way: the request carries how many (`people`), the table keeps that many seats for people (`sitDown`'s `slots`, a function of the table), the first two across from each other and the others between them (`seatOfSlot` in `rooms.js`), and the computer players move on the device of the first to sit down who's here (`runsElsewhere`); `tests/rooms.mjs` seats four, turns a fifth away, and keeps a pair's table at two. A table of more than two (October 2026): `together.js` gives a game `others()`, everyone else seated with whether they're here, and `slot`, this device's seat; seats beyond the pair's cobalt and copper take verdigris, plum, ochre and olive (`--p2`…`--p5` in style.css); and `standings.js` draws a table's standings the same way in every game (each player's rank, a dot in their colour, what they did this round under the name, the round's change and the total, in duel.css's rows; `tests/standings.mjs`). A phone that sleeps is away. Each page holds a session in the room's here while its connection is up, and the database drops the list once the last one goes, which `enterRoom` read as online until October 2026, so a head-to-head's lobby could wait for the Ready of a phone that had gone to sleep; the seat's own mark (`presence`, which `together.js` keeps) now counts too.

## Together
Menu → Play together or Play hidden → send the link. Your partner opens it and joins. The team shares 4 lives and 4 clues per board. Opened in a room, the page shows the rete until the room's board is drawn, never your own board meanwhile (October 2026; your own board used to be drawn first, so for the first seconds you and your partner were on different boards); joining reads only Crates' part of the room, not the whole room, with that part held while joining so the joining transaction takes one round trip, not two.

One rule runs through both modes: you never see the result of your own action, your partner does.
- Your wrong guess: you see a life go, your partner sees whether it was one away (in Hidden, also whose pick is the odd one out). Everyone keeps their picks after a wrong guess.
- Your clue (Together): your partner reads the explanation and sees the crate colour. In Hidden, clues work differently (below).
- A crate you find: your partner names it.

**Together** — both of you see all sixteen words and each other's picks. Whoever started the game sets the settings.

**Hidden** — each of you sees only your own eight words; your partner's show as blank sealed tiles, and it's up to you to describe which one you mean. Both screens lay the tiles out identically. Every crate holds one to three words from each side, so a crate always needs picks from both of you.
- Before the first board you each set up your own side: your preset, difficulty and topics shape only the eight words dealt to you. Which countries and commodities can come up is shared and set by whoever started the game. The board is dealt once you've both pressed Ready.
- Clues come from the shared pool of four and are spent on tiles that are sealed on your screen: tap one, then its ?. You see that tile's explanation and crate colour (its ? takes the colour); your partner, who can see the word, only sees that a clue was used on it. The rest is up to the two of you.

## Tables
A table is one game played by several people in the same place, each on their own phone (October 2026): Party's games, and the card and dice games that seat friends at a table with the computer players. It is the duo match's cousin, without its pairing: a pair links once and keeps a room of its own, while a table is opened for an evening by whoever has the app and joined by anyone who scans its QR code, in a browser, with just a name.

A table is a room of its own, `crates/rooms/CODE/<game>` under a fresh four-letter code (`createRoom` in `rooms.js`), so it needs no new database rules: `{ v, app, created, host, rev, players: { uid: { name, pid, seat, joined, online } }, game, inputs: { uid: … }, say: { n, text, lang } }`. Seats belong to players, not devices, as at a pair's table: the same phone after a reload, or the same player on another device (their player id), sits back down in their own seat; a newcomer takes the lowest free seat, and a full table (a game's own limit: four for cards, more for a party) says so. One device runs the table, its computer players, its clocks and its narrator: the host's while it's online, else the lowest seat online; the next in line steps in only when nothing has moved for four seconds (the running phone asleep, or gone without a goodbye), and that phone keeps its screen on while it runs the table (the screen wake lock). Every change is a transaction numbered by `rev`, so a move worked out on a table that has moved on since is turned down rather than played twice; what everyone does at once (votes, clues) is written each to its own place, `inputs/<uid>`, with nothing to collide, for the running device to gather. A device running older code than the table is updated, as at a duo table. The table's code stays in the page's address (`?table=CODE`), so a reload sits down again. A party is a table too (`crates/rooms/CODE/party`, on Party's page), and the tables of the games it plays sit beside it in its room (`crates/rooms/CODE/bones`, or a duo match's branch): the party's branch says which is being played (`playing`), and a page in the party follows it (`party-link.js`, loaded only by a page with `?party=` in its address, through `menu.js`).

Sitting down at a table takes one round trip to the database for a player already in their seat (October 2026): the table is read first, and someone it already seats sits straight down, presence marking them here, with no transaction (`join` in `table.js`). That's every phone a party sends to a game its host started (the host deals that table with everyone in it: README, Party), and every reload; anyone else sits down by the joining transaction, on the table already read. The transaction alone took two round trips on a page that had read nothing yet: a transaction's first try on an empty cache is a blind guess the database turns down.

`table.js` is the table itself and has no page in it, so the tests can seat a whole party in Node (`tests/table.mjs`: seats, the running device and the next in line, two moves on the same table, inputs at once, leaving, a player's other device, newer code; on `tests/fake-db.js`, the database in memory with as many devices as a test needs, refusing and storing what the real one does: `memdb.js`, as an offline party's host keeps it). `table-ui.js` is what a page needs around it: the name a guest goes by (asked once, kept on the device), the invitation (the QR code, the code in large letters, Copy link and Share, who's at the table, the game's own Start), the wake lock and the narrator, whose lines (`say`, numbered) every page shows and the running phone speaks, queued so one line doesn't cut off the last. `qr.js` draws the QR code from `vendor/uqr.js` (uqr 0.1.3, MIT, after Project Nayuki's encoder), error correction M, black on white in every theme (a camera reads light-on-dark codes poorly), each row's dark runs joined into one path so it stays sharp at any size; the link it carries is the page's own with the table's code.

What's at a table can be read by anyone who has its code and a browser's developer tools, hidden cards and roles included, as with a pair's room: the database has no accounts to tell players apart. These are games among friends at one table; Quest's roles and the cards in a hand are hidden by the page, not by the database.

## Seats
The card, dice and board games for a few seats round one table (October 2026: Liar's dice, Big Two, Dou Dizhu, the trump games, dominoes, backgammon, Quest's hidden roles, Cartel, and Dandan's communal deck): each seat is a person or a computer player, and one runner, `seats.js`, plays any of them solo or at a table without the game knowing which. Solo, you sit in seat 0 and the computer players in the rest, on this device, with no connection needed; the game is kept as it changes (`<game>:game`), so a reload, another of your devices or a partner watching sees it as it stands. Invite players (the game's menu) opens a party with the game chosen (README, Party): when its host starts it, every phone comes to a table in the party's room, each in their own seat, and the host's page deals with its own options. A friend who joins mid-game sits down in the lowest free seat, which is a computer player's, and takes it over, hand and all; one who leaves gives it back to the computer, and one whose phone has been away for half a minute is played for until it's back, so nobody's sleeping phone holds up the table. The host deals the next game, with as many seats as asked and at least one for everyone at the table, and Back to the party (the menu) takes every phone back to the lobby; each phone's solo game stays as it was.

A game is a module of pure functions: `deal` (a fresh game for so many seats), `toMove` (whose decision it is), `act` (a move made, or why it can't be), `bot` (a computer player's move, from what its seat may know), `over`, and for a timed step (a trick left on the table a moment, the dice shown) `wakeAt` and `wake`; `restore` makes a state read back from the database whole again (it keeps no empty lists). Every move's randomness comes from the game's seed and the move's number, so a move worked out twice, as a transaction may be on a fresher copy, comes out the same. The device that runs the table (the host's, or the next in line) makes the computer players' moves, each a short while after the last move so the people can follow it (a game can set that by the step: Quest's secret votes go in at once), and checks the table hasn't moved on meanwhile; a person's move is checked on their phone and again in the transaction. A state may carry a line for the narrator, spoken by the phone that runs the table. `seats-ui.js` wires a page: the solo game, the table in the address, the invitation, the narrator and the menu's table actions. A page may make a move as it draws (a choice given before its turn, sent the moment it comes, as Banquet's throws and Quest's votes are): the runner then looks at the table afresh before setting the next computer player going. Until October 2026 it went on with the view it had drawn, in which it was still the person's turn, and cancelled the move the person's own move had just set going, so the table stood still. `tests/seats.mjs` plays a toy game both ways: solo, the computer players moving in turn until it's yours and waiting while a menu is open, a move made as the page draws not stalling it; at a table on the database in memory, two people and two computer players, only the running phone moving the computer, a friend leaving and another sitting down mid-game in the computer's seat, a phone gone away played for after its grace, a solo game carried to a table and back.

## Puzzle rush
