Eight games have a puzzle rush, and it has the same settings in every one, in the same words (October 2026, asked for when Rush moved into Gambit: `rush-core.js` has the rules of it, `rush-menu.js` the controls). Gambit's chess puzzles, River's Xiangqi, Hong's and Tribute's take the game's place on the page; Schafkopf's (Trumps), Gammon's, Banquet's and Bones' are a sheet over it (`rush-sheet.js`). Each opens from its game's menu as Puzzle rush (until then Schafkopf's was "Schafkopf puzzle rush" and Banquet's "Call it", and only the chess Rush and River's had a choice of clock or a today's run: the other six were five minutes).

A run is Random, or Today's: the same puzzles in the same order for everyone that day (a game may add kinds of its own after them: River's Patterns). Its clock is 3 min, each puzzle's first five seconds free as a chess clock's delay works (it waits, then runs, and never gains, so it never shows more than three minutes); 5 min; or Survival, no clock at all. Three misses end a run on any clock. Where the rush takes the game's place, the choices are in its menu while it's open (Start a run starts one), and the menu is the rush's: Start a run, the last run's results and Back to the game; the run and the clock; Reset my rating where the puzzles are rated (chess and Xiangqi: your rating moves Elo-style against each puzzle's, and a run serves puzzles round it); your rating and your bests. On the sheet they're on its first screen, before Start and again after a run, as the same controls, with your best on the clock chosen. A rush opens on the kind and clock of the last run started.

Your best is kept for each clock, and for a game's own kind of run, whether the run was random or today's; today's runs are kept by the day. A best kept before the clocks were chosen was a five-minute run's, and counts as one; the chess Rush's today's bests ("three:daily") count for their clock. A game's About gives its rush's bests in a line ("Puzzle rush bests: 3 min 12, 5 min 15, Survival 30"); the rush's own menu gives a line for each clock with today's run beside it. A run's results are headed Time, Three misses or Every puzzle done.

How a game deals and judges its puzzles stays its own: a board's (chess, Xiangqi) are a line of moves, the clock running through them and an easy warm-up starting it; the others are positions with one clearly best play, where the verdict says what each play was worth and the clock stands while you read it (and while the sheet is closed or the app is away). The positions are dealt rising, four at each of ten ratings, those seen lately last (`runOrder` in `rush-core.js`; Hong and Tribute deal theirs alike); today's run takes today as its seed and leaves nothing out, so every phone gets the same. `tests/rush.mjs` checks the clocks and their grace, a run's clock of either kind, the bests and what was kept before them, today's runs, the words, the rating's steps, and that every game's rush uses all of it: the rushes on a page the shared menu, the sheet the same controls, no rush a clock of its own, and every game's menu the name Puzzle rush and its About the bests.

## Head to head
A head-to-head on one deal (October 2026), for the games otherwise played alone: everyone at the table gets the same match (the game's seed and options), plays it through on their own phone, and sees the others' scores as they go. It's how Gambit's puzzle rush (a puzzle battle), Brut, Blend, Arb, Survey, Pipes and Refinery play together (Origin, Order and Manifest did for a day in October 2026, until Korbi asked for Origin and Order as one team and Manifest either way: see their sections). `duel.js` is the table, on `together.js` like every duo match: asked for from the partner sheet like any other (a new table for each yes), or started from a Party, where it seats up to six (the request's `people`); Brut, Blend and Refinery are for a pair only, kept off Party's list as too long and quiet for a table of friends. A lobby lists who's at the table, each saying Ready; once everyone at the table is, a three-second countdown to a start they all share (the database's time, through `serverOffset`), and the game deals the match on each phone at that moment, so nobody sees it early. Anyone ready can start without waiting for those who aren't; a player who comes to a match already under way plays it on a countdown of their own, and the match is over when every run that began is done and nobody at the table is still to play it. Each phone reports its run as it changes (`runs/s<slot>`, not by number, which the database would read back as a list with holes: the score, a few words for the others, done, the time), and a finished run stands, so a reloaded phone can't change it; a report that changes nothing isn't sent (Blend's every step; Survey's chips are sent at most every second and a half). The others' scores show in a line the game places (under the header, or in its own score line), which opens the sheet. The result ranks everyone by the game's order (the score, then the time, unless the game says otherwise; equal runs share a rank, and a draw is a win for nobody in the pair's record), with the game's own lines under it (the chess rush's missed puzzles), Rematch (a new deal, the one who asked already ready) and Back to solo. A game keeps its solo run out of the match: its run, bests and today's stay as they were, and the match's own run is kept apart (`<game>:duel`), so a reload picks it up where it was. `duel-core.js` is every change to the table, pure, tested in `tests/duel.mjs` through the database's own round trip; on the stand-in database two phones went through the lobby, the countdown, play, a reload mid-run, the result on both, the pair's record, Rematch and Back to solo, and a party of three through a draw.

## Offline, and installing it as an app
Every game works offline once the app has been opened online once, from the home screen too: `sw.js` (a service worker) keeps every file of every page on the device, about 13 MB. That's the pages and everything they load, the font (served from `fonts/`, not Google), the knowledge base Origin, Arb, Lexicon and Order read, every knowledge bank and all of Gambit's chess puzzles. Until October 2026 the list was every file of certain kinds outside `kb/`, so the four games that read `kb/` directly never opened offline. Now `tools/offline.mjs` finds what each page loads by reading it (its scripts and styles, every import, worker and fetch, any string naming one of the app's files), wherever it lives, and `tests/offline.mjs` fails if a page loads something that isn't there, or loads a file by a path built as the code runs that isn't accounted for. Offline, a page that isn't kept opens the games screen instead of the browser's error page, since an app on the home screen has no Back. What needs a connection: playing together, watching, syncing between devices, and Wikipedia's pictures not yet shown (each is kept once it has been, and Keep pictures offline in Settings keeps them all). Offline, solo play works fully and Play together says it can't reach the server.

An update downloads only what changed. `sw.js` lists every file with a hash of its bytes, and a file already on the device with those bytes is copied from the old version instead of fetched again (every update used to fetch the whole app, and it changes many times a day). A changed file is fetched at an address carrying its hash, so no cache on the way can hand back an older copy, and the old version is deleted only once the new one is whole: if a new version is ever left incomplete (an older one taking over while it filled can empty it), it's completed when it takes over, and offline the old copy stays and answers for what it lacks. The logic is in `tools/service-worker.js`; `tests/offline.mjs` runs it against a stand-in for the browser's storage and the network.

The site installs as an app called Almanac, with every game and the games screen inside:
- Windows: open it in Edge or Chrome, then the install icon in the address bar (or menu → Apps → Install). It gets its own window and a Start menu icon.
- iPhone: open it in Safari, Share → Add to Home Screen. (A home-screen app keeps its own saves, separate from Safari's.)
- Android: Chrome offers Install app.

The games screen (tap a game's name) has an Update button: it checks there's a connection, empties every stored copy of the app and reloads it fresh from the site, keeping saves, stats and settings (offline it changes nothing). Updates otherwise happen by themselves: a new version is fetched in the background and waits; it takes over when the app goes off screen and the page reloads onto it when it comes back (taking over mid-game could mix old and new code). When a partner's device is already on a newer version, the game switches straight away (`pwa.js`).

`sw.js` is written by the workflow `.github/workflows/pages.yml` and by nobody else: on every push to `main` it checks the banks against `kb/`, runs every game's tests and, only if they all pass, rebuilds `sw.js` (the file list, each file's hash and a version from them all) and commits it as github-actions[bot]. Pages serves `main` as pushed, but installed copies only move to a new version when `sw.js` changes, so a push that fails a test never reaches them; and since nobody else writes `sw.js`, nobody collides on it. `node tools/build-sw.mjs --local` writes one to try offline play.

Several Claudes can work at once, one app each: `CLAUDE.md` holds the rules (shared files one at a time, pull and rebase before pushing, never force-push, never commit `sw.js`, rebuild built files rather than merge them, test before pushing).

## Links
- `?cat=countries`, `?cat=commodities` or `?cat=mixed` opens straight into that pool.
- `?b=CODE` opens one specific board. Shared results include this link.
- `?room=ABCD` joins a game together.
- `?run=ABCDEFGH` makes this device follow a synced solo run.

## Setup for Together mode (once)
Together mode and synced runs use the same Firebase project as CroatiaQuiz (croatiabio). Its database rules need the `crates` block (game rooms and synced runs):
Firebase console → Realtime Database → Rules → replace with the contents of `firebase-rules.json` → Publish.

## The knowledge base
What the knowledge games know lives once, in `kb/`, and the banks the games read are built from it (`tools/build-kb.mjs`; edit `kb/`, never a built bank).

- **Entities** (`kb/entities.js`, 7,687): every thing a game asks about or uses as a clue: Crates' 69 countries and 90 commodities, the rest of the world's countries (from the flags; a clue named for one, like Namibia under uranium, is that country), 3,671 clue things (Fjords, Equinor, Trading Places, Gouda…), Chart's places and features, 201 paintings with their 127 painters, 218 museums and 34 movements. Each has its **sets** (what it is: country, commodity, group:eu, group:soft, city, trade-place, museum, painting, feature:river…; as many as apply) and its **facts** (position, note, region, year, picture…). Crates' answers keep their board position (`crates`, append-only) and the names accepted when typed.
- **Links** (`kb/links.js`, 9,855): how things connect. A **clue** link is one of Crates' pairs, from the clue to the answer: the hand-written "because" (hint), its **aspects** (the topics you weight in Crates' settings, which belong to the link, not the thing: Equinor is a company to Norway and a firm to natural gas), difficulty, the other answers it also fits, and its place in the answer's list (append-only). A word under several answers is one entity with several links, each with its own sentence (1,215 such words); a clue that is itself an answer links a country and a commodity directly (449). Typed links: **in** a country (835), **painted-by**, **hangs-in**, **movement**.
- **Items** (`kb/items/`): the questions, each pointing at what it's about. **Pins** (Chart: the place's name, position, note and picture come from the entity), **estimates** (Quote: a painting's year comes from the painting), **choices** (Punt's knowledge banks, word for word, tagged with the paintings and countries they're about). `kb/geometry.js` holds the features' shapes.

The move into `kb/` (October 2026) read every bank and wrote them back identical before anything was replaced; `kb/MIGRATION.md` records it. Things of the same name became one entity (130: Geneva the Chart city and the Crates clue, Lake Titicaca's point and shape, Namibia the flag and the clue under uranium); a natural feature only when something says it's the same thing (the clue is for a country it's in, or its name or hint calls it a river, lake, falls…), so the Orange River stays apart from the Dutch colour, and rivers from the countries named after them (5 kept apart). Also listed: 20 facts that two sources gave differently (the questions keep their own, nothing changed), 78 words that may be two things under one name (Java: Indonesia's island, coffee's nickname, Sun's language) to check and split, and 12 country strings that name no country (Svalbard, Antarctica…). Joining the sources showed three Chart places filed in the wrong country, which the clues contradicted: Geneva (France), Jurong Island (Malaysia) and Kiruna (Russia), now Switzerland, Singapore and Sweden. `tests/kb.mjs` checks the banks are current with `kb/`, every link and item points at a real entity, Crates' order is unbroken, and nothing has fallen below the counts at the move. Punt's skill banks (maths, reasoning, words, patterns) aren't knowledge and stay as they are; Brut, Refinery, Blend and Parley are parked outside it for now.

Knowledge travels between the games. Every question the games read carries the entities it's about (Crates' answers and clues too), and a miss goes into the pile with them. Each game's learning mode then deals, after its own due questions, its own questions about what the other games found you weak on, most-missed first: miss who painted the Mona Lisa in Punt and Quote's next set asks the year it was painted; miss Geneva as a clue in Crates and Chart's next set asks you to pin it, and Punt's Cities level asks Switzerland's capital. Punt adds up to five such questions a block; Quote and Chart fit them within the due half of a set. `pile.weakElsewhere` and `pile.dealDue` do it; `tests/pile.mjs` checks it. Punt's Easy, Medium and Hard questions (built from Crates' clues) carry their clue's and answers' entities too, so a miss there travels like any other.

Topics, the same in every game that deals from it (October 2026). Crates, Punt, Quote, Order, Origin, Arb and Chart deal by a mix of topics, the same control in each (`topicMix` in `menu.js`, the pieces in `topics.js`; Crates and Punt have their own of the same look): a preset (Balanced, Trader, Culture night) or your own, each topic tapped round out, in and more (twice as likely as in). Balanced deals exactly what the game dealt before it had topics, seed for seed; today's run is always Balanced, the same for everyone, so your topics are for random runs. A run on a preset counts for your best, your own mix doesn't (a single topic keeps a best of its own where a game kept one before: Quote's refining sets, Chart's old topics). The last topic in can't be tapped out (it goes round in and more), since a mix of nothing would deal nothing; a mix too narrow for a run deals from every topic and says so. A table deals from the mix of whoever deals. What a topic is follows what the game deals: Crates' clues and Origin's by their links' aspects (Origin the country ones), Arb's things by their links' topics or else their kind, Quote's and Order's questions by their category, Chart's places by theirs, Punt's questions by their bank. Two of Crates' clues had an aspect no game knew (history for hist) and fifty a word of the other list's for the same topic (a country's clue under Companies rather than Companies & brands, a commodity's under Money & economy rather than Markets), so your topics never reached them: they're corrected, and `tests/kb.mjs` fails on an aspect that isn't a topic. 123 commodity clues still carry a country topic (Energy, Metals & mining, Agriculture, Craft & style, Food & drink) where the commodity list has nothing quite the same (a gold mine is Production, a cotton jacket Uses, a pipeline Trade & logistics), and one country clue a commodity's (Production, for cashew shell liquid processed in Mangalore); they follow those topics, which Crates and Arb show beside each other, and are for a person to settle clue by clue.

Party's Who am I? deals its identities from the knowledge base: `kb/items/whoami.js` names the decks (lists of things well enough known to be guessed by yes-or-no questions, or sets such as every country, every city, every painting), and the build writes `party-whoami-bank.js`, each identity with the names a guess may use and its crib, what the knowledge base says of it, its own facts first (what it is, its note, its years, a god's domain and symbol, a painting's year), then its links, the most telling first (what it made, its typed links, the clues it gives to other answers, the things linked to it, the clues to it, easiest first, and what lies in it), six lines at most; a deck by set leaves out a thing the knowledge base says too little about. Its people are `PEOPLE` there, each marked for the party, for learning or both and dealt by the mark (Famous people, People worth knowing), with a crib written by hand and a picture's page: one the knowledge base holds keeps its id and names, the rest are Who am I's alone, and the build fails on one that's named as its own but is an entity, a mark it doesn't know, or a crib under four lines or over six. Party's Decoy asks its questions from it too: the build writes `party-decoy-bank.js`, the sayings' meanings with their wrong meanings, Quote's numbers each with three house lies set round the truth, and the odd facts of `kb/items/decoy.js`.

Deck's overview shows coverage by subject, across the games: for each subject of the knowledge base, how many of its things you're learning (and how many are due) and how many you've learned, of how many it holds, with a bar for the learned share; a thing counts once, whichever games asked about it. A thing with a set is in that set's subject (Countries, Commodities, Paintings, Cities, Physical features…); a clue thing in the subject of its links' commonest aspect, by Crates' topic labels, with the country and commodity versions of one topic joined (Companies & brands, Policy & institutions, Trade & shipping, Culture & arts): 35 subjects in all. The build writes them to `kb-index.js`.

What each clue thing is: `tools/kb-kinds.mjs` reads each of Crates' clue things' hand-written hints and their links' topics and gives it a kind (27 of them: person, company, organisation, place, nature, site, event, law, market, film, book, music, art, food, drink, animal, plant, disease, currency, vehicle, sport, custom, word, technology, material, craft, date) only when the wording clearly says so: the best kind has to score 3.5 or more and beat the next by 1.25 ("A soup thickened with it" is food; "The Cannibal of cycling", with the People topic, a person; "A currency whose name means crown" a currency before a word). That gave 1,047 of 3,356, each marked `kindBy: "words"` so a kind given this way can be told from one set by hand; a sample of 40 checked by hand had 35 right (the misses: the Red Sea filed under ships, oil fires under sites rather than events, call centres under companies, the Purity law under drink, coal exports under places). All the rest but 11, 2,298 in all, have since been settled by reading each one's hints in turn (`kindBy: "read"`: Copernicus a person, HS 0901 a customs code, La Tomatina a festival, Krone a currency, Troll a gas field). The 11 still open in `kb/KINDS.md` are left open on purpose: names that cover more than one thing (Java the island, the coffee and the language; Blue, Green and Grey as colours and as kinds of hydrogen or methanol) and bare descriptors (Ancient, Artisanal, Heavy, Lightweight). Kinds fold into the subjects (a company into Companies & brands, a festival into Culture & arts, a currency into Money & economy), so coverage by subject uses what a thing is where that's known and its links' topics otherwise.

Questions the knowledge base writes itself: a painting in `kb/` with its painter, museum, movement, year and picture gets Punt's who, where and movement questions and Quote's year from the build, unless it already has hand-written ones, and every museum with a position gets Chart's pin unless it has one (art museums under Art; history, archaeology, science, natural history, ships and memorials under Museums); so a painting added to `kb/` (one entity and three links) reaches all three games at once. Wrong options are things of the same kind, nearest first: painters of the same movement, then of the nearest years (The Lacemaker: Rembrandt, Velázquez, Frans Hals); movements of the nearest years; museums Chart can place, then the rest. Options' order and ids come from the entity, so every build writes the same questions and the pile keeps track of them. The first four came in this way: The Lacemaker, The Laughing Cavalier, Netherlandish Proverbs and The Blue Boy, with Frans Hals, Thomas Gainsborough, and the Gemäldegalerie and the Huntington as new pins in Chart. Before that the art side was tidied: Chart's art places that aren't museums (Machu Picchu, Stonehenge, the Taj Mahal) are art places now, the seven museums that came in twice (Punt's answer and Chart's pin) are one each, and every museum has the short name its questions show, Punt's own wording where it had one. A painting with no museum (Salvator Mundi, privately held; the stolen Storm on the Sea of Galilee; Sunflowers in its many versions) is asked who and when, never where. `tests/kb.mjs` checks every painting is asked in Punt and Quote and every placeable museum is pinned.

More paintings (October 2026): 116 more, to 201 by 127 painters, chosen to fill the story's gaps: the Gothic and early Renaissance (Giotto's Lamentation in the Scrovegni Chapel, Duccio's Maestà, Uccello, Piero, Mantegna), the Netherlandish masters (the Ghent Altarpiece, Rogier van der Weyden, Memling, Hugo van der Goes), Venice and Mannerism (Giorgione's Tempest, Titian, Parmigianino, Bronzino, El Greco, Arcimboldo), the Baroque (Caravaggio, Artemisia Gentileschi, Rubens, Velázquez, Poussin, Claude) and the Dutch beyond Rembrandt and Vermeer (Fabritius, Steen, Ruisdael, de Hooch), the Rococo, Neoclassicism and Romanticism (Watteau, Hogarth, David's Napoleon, Ingres, Fuseli, Friedrich, Turner's Slave Ship), the Hudson River School, the realists and the Russians (Courbet, Repin, Shishkin, Eakins, Sargent, Homer, Menzel), the Pre-Raphaelites, Impressionism and after (Caillebotte, Degas, Cassatt, Sorolla, Cézanne, Gauguin, Toulouse-Lautrec, Rousseau), and the twentieth century (Schiele, Matisse, Futurism, Mondrian, Kandinsky, Malevich, Magritte, Ernst, Kahlo, Miró, Pollock, de Kooning, Rothko, Newman, Lichtenstein, Hockney), with Hiroshige and more Hokusai. 22 museums came with them, each a new pin in Chart (the Scrovegni Chapel, Saint Bavo's in Ghent, Santo Tomé in Toledo, San Luigi dei Francesi in Rome, the Menil Collection, the Harry Ransom Center…), and three movements with their cards, Mannerism, Futurism and the Hudson River School; Gothic, until now architecture's, is a painting movement too, with a painting card beside its building one (`archCard`). A painting may carry a `note`, the one thing worth knowing about it (Washington Crossing the Delaware was painted in Düsseldorf, with American tourists as models; Fabritius was killed in the Delft gunpowder explosion the year he painted The Goldfinch), shown after its who question in Punt and its year in Quote; 132 do. Two facts keep the movement question fair, since a neighbouring movement isn't always wrong: a movement can be `within` another (the Rococo and the Dutch Golden Age within the Baroque, Pointillism within Post-Impressionism, the Hudson River School within Romanticism), and the parent is never offered as a wrong answer; and a painting can name movements it's `notWith`, ones it could fairly be said to belong to as well (View of Toledo is El Greco's Mannerism painted in the Baroque's years; L'Absinthe is Impressionist with a realist's subject), which aren't offered either. Dürer, a Crates clue before he was a painter here, is Albrecht Dürer in the art questions and stays Dürer on Crates' board (the link's `w`).

Next: Crates' learn mode taking in what the other games found you weak on, and more of these templates (a city's country, a country's capital) as their facts come in.


Architecture (October 2026) lives in kb/ as things and links, so every game draws on it: 21 styles, each with a card (what it rejected, what it sought, how to spot it, what lay behind it, what came next; the Baroque, the Rococo, Neoclassicism and Art Nouveau are the art movements' own entities, with a second card about buildings) and 'led-to' links from the Greeks to Deconstructivism; 55 architects with where they came from; 72 buildings with style, architect, country and city, a picture, a place and, where they're true and telling, the year it was finished and its height; and 36 parts of a building with definitions that never say their own name. The build makes of it Punt's Architecture level (arch-bank.js: styles as ideas, buildings by sight, which style and who designed it, and reading a building, each part both ways), Chart's Architecture pins (none whose name says the city), Quote's completion years and heights, so Order's Dates line mixes buildings with paintings and its metres family gains towers and spires, 27 clues for Origin and Crates (a hint never naming the country or a place in it), and Arb's routes through 'designed by', 'built in the Gothic style', 'stands in' and 'led to'. Years before 1000 stay out of Quote, whose years run from 1000.

Mythology (October 2026), the same way: the gods of Greece with their Roman names, the Norse and the Egyptians, each with what they ruled and their sign or form; heroes and monsters; the real places of myth (Delphi, Knossos, Troy, Gamla Uppsala, Karnak, Lake Avernus…). From it: Punt's Mythology level (myth-bank.js: the gods' questions written by the build, the others by hand, every wrong answer another myth's own: Prometheus's crime among Pandora's, Tantalus's and Sisyphus's), Chart's Myth pins, six clues for Origin and Crates, Order's Stories, and Arb's routes through 'son of', 'the Romans called her', 'beheaded' and 'depicts' (The Birth of Venus to Venus to Aphrodite to Eros to Cupid). The fashion house and the god are different things: Hermès keeps its id, the god is hermes-god.

Merchants and empires (October 2026): sixteen merchant powers, from the Sogdians on the Silk Road, the Hanse and Venice, through the Medici, the Fuggers and the Rothschilds and the Portuguese, Spanish and Ming fleets, to the chartered companies (the VOC, the East India, Hudson's Bay, Royal African, South Sea and Mississippi companies, Jardine Matheson). Each has a card: what it traded, how it did business, how it was run, its darkest chapter (stated as fact; the Rothschilds have none, since the only stories told about them are antisemitic myths), how it ended, what it left. From it: Punt's Mercantilism level (merchants-bank.js, in the Trader and Culture presets), Chart's History pins (Batavia, Dejima, Bryggen, the Steelyard, Elmina, Potosí…), fourteen dated events for Quote and Order's Dates, Order's stories of the East India Company, the VOC and the Hanse, six clues, and Arb's routes (nutmeg, monopolised by the VOC, ruled from Batavia, which is today Jakarta). Where the knowledge base already had a thing (Hudson's Bay, Clive, Coen, Samarkand), the card joined it.

Titles and etiquette (October 2026): eighteen orders of chivalry with cards (founder, year, motto with its translation, chapel), the Templars, the Hospitallers (the Order of Malta) and the Teutonic Order; the British peerage with its wives' titles; the German ranks and styles, glossed in English; seven dress codes, Highland evening dress and Tracht among them; the places of court and chivalry (St George's Chapel, Malbork, Aachen, the Hofburg, the Munich Residenz, Tuxedo Park…); and the eponyms (the Earl of Sandwich to the sandwich, Lord Raglan to the sleeve). From it: Punt's Etiquette level (titles-bank.js, in the Culture preset), History pins, fourteen dates and seven counts for Quote and Order, Order's stories of the peerage, the German ranks and the grades of the British Empire, eleven clues, and Arb's routes. No question about an order or a dress code names its answer: a founder of two orders, or one whose name echoes his order's, isn't asked, and the dress codes are described in other words (a cutaway coat, not a morning coat).

The art market (October 2026): auction houses (the Dorotheum, Sotheby's, Christie's, Bonhams, Phillips, China Guardian, Poly), dealers (Durand-Ruel, Vollard, Kahnweiler, Duveen, Castelli, Beyeler, Gurlitt) and forgers (van Meegeren, Beltracchi, Pei-Shen Qian, de Hory, Keating, Myatt) with cards; the fairs; the record works; the places of the trade (the salerooms, Messe Basel, the Geneva Freeport, Kunstmuseum Bern, Haus der Kunst, the Neue Galerie). From it: Punt's Art market level (artmarket-bank.js, in the Culture and Trader presets: auction mechanics, a guarantee as a put sold to the seller, provenance and loot, fakes, fairs), nine Art pins, twelve dates and nine prices for Quote and Order (the records join the '$ million' family), Order's stories of the Salvator Mundi and the Gurlitt hoard, seven clues, and Arb's routes. Houses are asked by where and when they began, since most bear their founders' names.

Bavaria (October 2026): the seven districts and their capitals; the kings and their dynasty; the towns, peaks, lakes and Ludwig's palaces; the companies and their home towns; the breweries; the Bairisch words; the people (Dürer, Röntgen, Wagner, Orff, Strauss, Brecht, Eisner). From it: Punt's Bavaria level (bavaria-bank.js, in the Culture preset), Chart's Bavaria region, seventeen dates and two heights for Quote and Order, Order's stories of the kings, of Ludwig II's life and of brewing, and Arb's routes. Germany already had its Bavarian clues (Oktoberfest, Servus, the purity law, Dürer, the Dassler brothers…), so the cards joined those entities and no new clues were needed.

Britain and Scotland (October 2026): Scotland's places, history, whisky (distilleries by region), customs, money, inventors and Scots words; the constitution, the City's markets (Lloyd's, the Baltic Exchange, the LME, the Bank of England), English history and Wales. From it: Punt's Britain level (britain-bank.js, in the Culture preset), Chart's Britain and Scotland regions, 32 dates, four peaks and Scotch exports for Quote and Order, Order's stories of the road to the Union, the Forty-Five, the Tudors, Henry VIII's wives and how Scotch is made, a clue for Panama (Darien) skipped because Panama takes no clues, and Arb's routes. Existing entities (the LME, the Good Friday Agreement, Culloden, Lloyd's…) took the cards.

Difficulty in the choice banks is on Punt's scale of 1 to 10, which the house prices by. The banks written in October 2026 (mythology, merchants, titles, the art market, Bavaria, Britain) were first graded 1 to 4 and priced too short; they now keep their easy questions at 1 and 2 and put the rest at 5 and 7, and `tests/punt.mjs` holds the long-run odds across all topics between 1.85× and 2.15×, with the easiest twentieth at 1.25× or less.

China (October 2026): the provinces and their capitals, the eight cuisines' dishes, the teas (kind and province), the futures exchanges and their contracts (iron ore at Dalian, PTA and methanol at Zhengzhou, yuan crude at the INE, lithium carbonate at Guangzhou), idioms, festivals, thinkers, the dynasties and the road to 1949 and beyond, the trade (teapots, the ports, the Canton Fair, Pingyao's first draft bank), and the places. From it: Punt's China level (china-bank.js, in the Culture and Trader presets), Chart's China region, dates and trade figures for Quote and Order (years after 1000 only, as Quote requires: the Tang and Song stay in the dynasties story), Order's stories of the dynasties, the zodiac, the road to 1949 and reform, and Arb's routes. China already had 115 clues, so none were added.

Skiing (October 2026): resorts (42 in the Alps, a dozen beyond), linked ski areas, races and runs, great skiers, history, technique and rules, avalanches, passes and food. From it: Punt's Skiing level (skiing-bank.js, in the Culture preset), Chart's Alps region, dates and figures for Quote and Order, Order's stories of the Streif top to bottom, of skiing becoming a sport and of avalanche rescue, and Arb's routes. An Alpine resort's wrong countries are the other Alpine ones; the 3 Vallées and the 4 Vallées are written in words (Trois, Quatre), so no option stands out by its digit.

Watches (October 2026): 26 manufactures with their founding, home town, owner and signature; complications, the parts of a movement and the finishes, each explained, with a picture where its Wikipedia page leads with one (the tourbillon, the balance wheel, the lever escapement, guilloché, the Reverso…); history, the icons, seals and standards. From it: Punt's Watches level (watches-bank.js, in the Culture preset; every question answerable without its picture), Chart's Lake Geneva and Watch Valley regions, dates and auction records for Quote and Order, Order's stories of the power's path through a watch, of watchmaking through time and of the lake's shore from Geneva to Villeneuve, and Arb's routes.

The maths bank is typeset (October 2026): its maths is LaTeX between $…$, rendered by Temml through rich.js, matrices as matrices; it was converted from plain text by tools/maths-tex.mjs, which leaves prose, units and anything Temml can't parse as text, and can be run again on new plain-text questions. `tests/tex.mjs` parses every maths span in every bank with Temml; `tests/banks.mjs` judges an option's length as it renders, not by its markup.
## Files
`bank.js` is the word bank as base64-encoded JSON, so answers aren't visible at a glance. It is append-only: board codes point at answer and word positions, so words are only ever added at the end of an answer and answers at the end of the bank. `gen.js` builds and checks boards, including the split boards for hidden mode. `run.js` keeps a solo run in step across devices; `net.js` loads the Firebase connection when either needs it. `tests/fake-sync.js` stands in for Firebase in local two-tab tests.

## Bests that compare
A best is only worth comparing if everyone played the same thing. So in the ranked games (Punt, Chart, Quote, Crates; `RANKED` in `suite.js`) the best on the games screen, and the one your partner sees, counts only comparable runs: the daily, and a run on one of the game's presets (Punt's with every difficulty, Chart's worldwide, Quote's with learning off; Crates counts its daily alone). Every daily is dealt on the device from the date (`today()` in `suite.js`, the UTC date, one for every game, so it works offline and rolls over at the same moment everywhere) with nothing of yours in it: no questions due from your pile, no name-it from your history, no narrowed selection. Punt has Today's run (the Standard settings: Balanced, every difficulty, 15 questions, name-it off), Crates Today's board (the mixed puzzles, one go, scored by points then time), Chart and Quote their sets of the day on everything. Other runs still play, learning mode keeps its review in them, and they keep their own bests in each game. `noteComparable(app, score, day)` keeps a game's comparable best and today's daily under `GAME:ranked`, synced like any best; the games screen shows both (Best +41% · Today +12%).

## Calibre

Learn watchmaking by drafting movements the way watchmakers do, on the calibre plan: the plate seen from above, each
arbor a point, each wheel and pinion a circle of its pitch diameter on a layer (1 to 5 the train's, D1 to D3 the dial
side). Choose a part from the tray: faint circles show where its arbor would have to stand to mesh with each wheel on
that layer; tap an arbor to add it there, or near a wheel to set a new arbor that snaps into mesh. Drag your own arbors;
tap one to see how it turns or take a part off.

3D (the button over the zoom, October 2026) pulls the movement apart: each layer at a height of its own, drawn with the
plan's own artwork under one axonometric map (turned by the azimuth, tilted by the elevation, lifted by its height), so
straight down it is the plan and the button swings the camera between the two. The main plate is the floor and the side
the level is worked on faces up (the train's layers rising, the dial side's hanging under it with the hands below, or
the other way up for a level under the dial), so nothing is mirrored and every wheel turns as on the plan. Each part
keeps its thickness, the arbors stand through the layers as steel staffs, a bridge's post stands up through the train,
and a rail at the left names each layer at its height: a tap on a name builds on that layer, a tap on a part picks it
and its layer, and with a part in hand a tap places it on its layer's plane, where the finger meets it. A drag turns the
movement the way the finger goes, as any 3D viewer does: across spins it about its staffs with the near edge following
the finger, down tips its top towards you, up tips it away and over to show the back. (Until October 2026 a drag moved
the camera instead, so the movement turned against the finger and down flattened it.) Pinch and the buttons zoom, and the
parts on the layer in hand are named where they sit. The view you chose is kept (`calibre:view`).

The engine (`calibre-engine.js`) runs the plan as it would really turn, at real size (one module, 0.1 mm a tooth, or a
gear's own finer module, which meshes only its own kind): ratios and directions through every mesh, a finger pushing a
star wheel or a date ring one tooth a turn, locked loops, no wheel over another arbor's pivot on its side of the plate,
nothing off the plate (round, or the Reverso's rectangle). The escapement is built from its real parts, a club-tooth
escape wheel, a pallet fork whose stones must reach its teeth and a balance in line at the end of the fork: no fork and
the spring runs away, a fork alone locks after one tick, both and the balance's beat sets the pace. Wind coils the
mainspring in its barrel; the watch then runs down over its power reserve, the balance swinging in real time and its
amplitude falling as the spring weakens, the escape wheel stepping half a tooth a beat at real speed.

Teaching (October 2026). Every level opens with its lesson (`calibre-lessons.js`), its ideas a step at a time, each
with a picture of the part it's about on its own and running (`calibre-scenes.js`): two wheels with the mesh between
them lit, a wheel and pinion on one arbor in 3D, a train turning in time-lapse with its turns or rates counted under
it, a mainspring winding and letting down, an escapement ticking in slow motion (or at its real beat) with its beats
counted, a balance's amplitude falling as the spring runs down, a finger stepping a star, a locked triangle straining.
A picture is a small mechanism of its own, the level's plate as it starts (its problem, never its answer), or an
earlier level's finished work, framed close on what it's about (the rest drawn faint or left out), with names of its
own on its parts; the demonstrations use other counts than the level's (an 80 driving a 40 where the level wants a
60 and a 30) so they show the idea and leave the arithmetic to you. A chapter's first level opens with what the
chapter is about. The last step is the task: the level's plate with what drives it and what each goal's arbor should
do written on it, what usually goes wrong ("watch out for"), and how to build, folded away; Build it closes it. Step
with Next and Back, the dots, the arrow keys or a swipe across the picture; Lesson, in the brief and the menu, opens
it again.

On the plate: the goals show live what each arbor does and what it should. Workings follows the train to the first
goal not yet met (all met: to the end of the longest path) as a chain, each arbor's name and between them the step's
ratio (×8 speeding up, ÷3 slowing down, a finger, a reverser's clutch, a jumper holding two together): a tap on a
ratio isolates that mesh, its two arbors drawn full and the rest faint, the mesh lit, the plan framed on them, and says
it in words ("The 80-tooth wheel on Centre wheel drives the 10-leaf pinion on Third wheel: 80 over 10 is ×8, faster,
and the other way round"); the last chip takes the whole path. Tap an arbor and the inspector says what drives it and
what it drives, mesh by mesh; Isolate shows it with just those, framed above the inspector (in 3D the stack keeps clear
of it too), and follows the arbor you tap next. Hint gives two or three graded nudges (a hint caps the stars at two); a
Glossary of the craft's words; Undo; keys (1–8 layers, W wind, Z undo, H hint, Delete, Esc). Stars for par (fewest
parts).

Tests (October 2026): once a level runs, It runs offers a test on what you made and what its lesson taught (Test, in
the brief and the menu, takes it again; the lesson's last step says it's coming). About ten questions of five kinds:
pick one of several, type a number or a word, tap a part on the picture, name the part in the ring (picked or typed),
put a train in order. The arithmetic comes from your own design, so its numbers are yours: a mesh you put in and its
ratio (asked so the answer is at least one: how many times the pinion turns for a turn of its wheel, or how many turns
of a pinion make a turn of the wheel it drives), how far apart two arbors stand, the train's ratio in all, how fast
the goal's arbor turns from the train's first, which way it turns, the beat, the power reserve, a finger's star. Then
reading the movement: a part to tap (one you put in, or one the goals are about, or the arbor that turns once a
minute), a part to name (its arbor drawn with that part alone, ringed, the rest faint), the train in order, the layer
two parts meet on, read off the 3D view. Then the level's own questions on its ideas (two to four a level, the history
and the arithmetic of the lesson), and a word or two the level brought in, from the glossary, asked one of three ways:
type it from its meaning, pick it, or pick its meaning. An arbor you added is named for what it is (the third wheel)
when it stands where the solution's does on the train, else for what it carries ("your 10/75 arbor"). A typed number
reads the English way or the European (7,5 is 7.5; 18,000 is eighteen thousand); a typed word forgives case, accents,
an article and a plural. After each answer: right or not, the right answer, and why; a tap that misses rings and names
both parts on the picture, what you tapped and what was asked for, so a wrong tap still teaches a name. Three in four
pass; the best is kept (in calibre:progress, so it syncs) and shown on the brief's Test link and in the course. The
glossary has grown to 75 words, and no definition says its own word or carries a figure: a test offers a definition
among others, and a lone figure would mark the right one out. Questions follow the subject banks' rule
(tests/banks.mjs): the right option mustn't stand out as the longest or the only one with a figure.

The course is a map of every chapter, folded, the one you're in open: how many levels run and the stars won, Continue
to the first level not yet running, and each level a picture of its plate (your design as you left it, the solution
once it runs, else its start), framed on where its work happens, with a line on what it teaches and its stars.

Your design is kept on each level as it changes (October 2026; the twelve levels last changed), so a reload, another
device on your solo code or a visit to another level brings the plate back as you left it, and a partner watching sees
each part go in (it used to see the level as it starts). Start this level again, in the menu, forgets it. A design
kept against an older version of a level (its fixed arbors or its tray since changed) isn't restored.

The course (`calibre-levels.js`, 55 levels): 1 Gears (two wheels, idlers, wheel and pinion, speeding up, a ratio hunt,
a locked triangle, clearance round a post), 2 The going train (the centre wheel, small seconds, choosing the counts, a
repair that keeps the tooth sum), 3 The escapement (the runaway and the pallet fork, the balance, the beat, the faster
beat, choosing the balance, the escape wheel's teeth), 4 The motion works (the minute wheel, twelve to one, the 24-hour
hand, the whole watch), 5 Power (more hours from the barrel ratio, a week with an intermediate wheel, force or hours: the balance's
amplitude from the torque reaching it against the reserve, constant force: a fusée or a remontoire keeping the
swing between 230 and 310 degrees over the whole run), 6 Winding and
setting (ratchet and click, the setting wheels), 7 Calendars and the moon (the date ring and its finger, the 59-tooth
moon, the 135-tooth moon good for 122 years), 8 Famous calibres (the Unitas 6497 with small seconds at nine, the
Reverso's rectangular calibre, the El Primero's 36,000 vph, the Valjoux 7750's oscillating pinion, Harrison's H4: steady force and regulation
to a second a day), 9 The chronograph (a module under the dial driven from the
fourth wheel: the chronograph wheel, start and stop with a coupling lever swinging about the driving wheel, a finger
counting minutes on a 30-tooth star, heart cams for reset, the flyback lever,
the Datograph's jumping counter against a creeping one), 10 Automatic winding (one reverser winds one way, a second
makes either swing of the rotor wind), 11 Regulating (the index on a balance whose beat comes from its inertia and its
hairspring's stiffness, to within five seconds a day; a free-sprung balance chosen by its inertia). 12 Pushers and
jumpers (the jumping hour: a twelve-point star held to the hour wheel by a jumper, a press jumping only the travel hand,
home time on a 24-hour hand; the column wheel, twice as many ratchet teeth as columns; the alarm, an hour trip and a minute trip letting it go at the
set minute, once in twelve hours), 13 Calendars that know (the
annual calendar's twelve-notch cam, the perpetual calendar's four-year cam with its leap year, cut month by month in an
editor, the equation of time on a kidney cam, a secular calendar that knows 2100 isn't a leap year), 14 Striking (the hour snail read by the rack from twelve o'clock, numbered in an editor and struck on a
synthesised gong; the quarter snail, and the whole repeater: hours low, quarters double, minutes high), 15 The tourbillon (Breguet's cage: the fixed fourth wheel and the escape pinion rolling round it).
Mechanisms with states (started and stopped, the rotor either way, running and pressed) are judged in each, with
buttons to switch between them. The chronograph levels' empty coupling arbors are under the dial, so they no longer
start with a clash over the train's pivots; the quarter snail is drawn with its four steps (it was drawn as twelve,
eight of them broken); a reverser is drawn gilt and steel (it was black); the workings follow a reverser's one-way
clutch, the way it passes. `tests/calibre.mjs` proves the engine's rules, that every level is unsolved at the start and
solved by its worked solution, from its tray, within par, that the workings multiply to the train's ratio, that
every lesson step builds its picture from parts it holds, names nothing missing, shows no solution of its own level or
a later one, and turns no faster than two turns a second, and that every level's test, dealt three times, asks five to
eleven questions whose pictures hold what they name and whose right answers are right (typed either way) and wrong
ones wrong, with no choice giving itself away; a test on 2.3 built the other way round from its solution (80 into 8,
then 72 into 12) asks those counts.

Held sideways (README, Bigger screens), the plan (or the movement in 3D) takes the left, all the height under the header, and the level's brief, the parts (wrapped into rows rather than scrolled past) and the controls stand beside it, the brief scrolling inside if it's long.

## The games screen's figures (October 2026)

Each tile shows one figure, the same way for you and your partner (suite.js: markOf, shared by shareBests): a win streak, the longest (Crates, Cartel: noteStreak); today's time (Slate, Delta: noteDayTime, each with a Today's puzzle, today's date its seed, a medium board, the first solve timed); today's count (Deck's cards revised, Parley's lessons: noteDayCount); stars collected (Calibre, Harbour, Blend, Refinery: noteStars, Blend and Refinery three a level by margin against par); or a best. A game with a daily (Punt, Quote, Origin, Order, Chart) shows today's when either of you has played it, and otherwise the bests: Punt's from today's run or a preset with every difficulty, Chart's from everything worldwide, Order's without its History runs (the stories, once Stories); Order has a daily for each of its modes, and its tile shows today's best of them. The chess puzzle rush's 3 min has five seconds' grace a puzzle (README, Puzzle rush): the clock waits, then runs, and never gains.

Today's comes first (October 2026). While a game's daily is still to play today (Slate, Delta, Punt, Quote, Origin, Order, Chart), its tile opens straight onto it (the tile's link carries `#today`, which the game takes once and clears with `arrivedForToday` from suite.js): a fresh day's puzzle or run, or today's where you left it; a board or run you'd started is left only after the game asks, as it does from its menu. Once today's is played the tile opens the game as it was. Inside a game, the menu's Random/Today's choice starts on Today's while it's still to play, and Origin and Order, after a finished run, deal today's next; a summary offers Today's when it's still to play. These came after a morning of dailies that didn't show: in Slate, Delta and Chart, today's was a choice in a list, and the board that opened was the last one or a random one, so it was easy to play a random board for the daily.

A daily counts the moment its result is final: Slate and Delta at the solve, Crates at the board's end, Order at its last card, Origin when its last country is found or given up, Chart at its last pin, Quote when its last quote settles, Punt when its last bet settles (an endless run when it ends). The summary that follows counts it again, which changes nothing; before, Origin, Chart, Quote and Punt counted only when that summary was opened, so leaving after the last answer lost the day.

Every daily goes by the same day: the UTC date, YYYYMMDD (days.js), the same set for everyone, rolling over at the same moment. Origin, Order and Arb once counted days on the phone's own clock (days since 1970 by its calendar, 20734 for 8 October 2026), so the games screen never found their dailies, and they weren't the same set for everyone; what they kept moves to its date once, on the first page that opens (suite.js, with `movedToDates`), so a run played that day still shows. `tests/days.mjs` checks the day, the move and a day's best across Order's modes, and that no game counts days of its own again.

The figures sync like any game's save: today's times, streaks, counts and stars used to be written past the sync (they never left the device, and an older copy from elsewhere could win), and now go up with your solo code. The games screen redraws its tiles when a sync brings in something newer from your other devices, and passes it on to your partner's tiles.
